## Translator's Foreword

[p. 13] Praise be to God, Lord of the worlds, and blessings and peace upon the best of His creation, Muhammad, and upon all his family and companions. To proceed:

This book originated as a series of lectures that our teacher, His Eminence Shaykh ʿAllāma Mufti Muhammad Taqi Usmani — may God the Exalted preserve and protect him — kindly delivered during a training course held by Jāmiʿa Dār al-ʿUlūm Karachi for scholars of the Sharia, teachers at religious institutes and seminaries, and those working in the field of fatwa in Pakistan. Its aim was to acquaint them with the most important topics discussed in political science, beginning with an explanation of its first principles, passing through an introduction to the various political theories and systems of government, and ending with a discussion of the principles and foundations of Islam that it has legislated in the political sphere, together with the forms of their practical application in the present age.

The lectures had been recorded on tape. Our two teachers, Shaykh ʿAbdullāh Memon and Shaykh Muhammad Muzammil Kāpāriyā — may God preserve and protect them both — undertook to transcribe and arrange them, and they had the good fortune of seeing them printed in book form after His Eminence Shaykh Mufti Muhammad Taqi Usmani — may God preserve and protect him — had revised them. He made changes to them, on the one hand by deleting some matters he considered unhelpful, and on the other by adding a number of important topics and issues, as well as correcting their wording and arranging their material. Thus the book has been presented in its final form to Urdu readers.

I considered myself very fortunate when His Eminence the Shaykh, may God preserve him, charged me with rendering it into Arabic, despite my weakness and shortcomings in this blessed language, its literature and its styles. That is on the one hand. On the other hand, a shiver would seize me at the magnitude of the responsibility that weighed on my shoulders whenever I pictured myself acting as interpreter between the Shaykh, may God preserve him, and Arab readers on so important a subject as politics. Nevertheless — through God's grace and the prayers of His Eminence the Shaykh, may God preserve him — I rolled up my sleeves and applied myself in earnest until the work of translation and revision was completed [p. 14] in about three months. What we present today to Arab readers comes with the hope that they will attribute to the translator — not to the Shaykh, may God preserve him — any error they find in it.

It should be noted that the book is divided into two parts. The first of them was devoted by His Eminence the Shaykh to introducing political science, its various schools, and the many systems of government arising from and based upon it. (He did not, however, withhold his critique and comments on them from an Islamic point of view wherever that was needed.) Naturally, he drew on a great many Arabic and foreign sources in preparing it, but he did not see much use in giving references to them or listing their titles: first, because of the difficulty of doing so, and second, because in that part he dealt only with the established, accepted questions of political science.

The second part contains an exposition of the rulings and teachings of Islam in the political sphere, accompanied by the opinions of the jurists (fuqahāʾ) and theologians (mutakallimūn) and their differing points of view. In it he made a point of citing the texts of the Qur'an and Sunna and the other sources on which he relied in setting out the positions of the jurists and theologians and their proofs.

Hence the book contains between its covers an introduction to the various political theories and prevailing systems of government on the one hand, and the premises and principles of Islam in the political sphere on the other — which will help, God willing, to clarify the basic features of Islamic government that are consistent with the demands of the present age and in harmony with its circumstances and needs.

Finally, I ask God the Exalted to accept this effort and to make it beneficial to the Umma, so that it may have the good fortune of contributing to guiding and correcting political efforts and to attaining the goal of establishing Islamic government in the full sense of the word. Amen.

**Kalimullah ibn Dāwūd Khān**
(One of those whom God has blessed with being a student of His Eminence Shaykh ʿAllāma Mufti Muhammad Taqi Usmani, may God preserve and protect him)
Karachi – Pakistan
11 Muḥarram al-Ḥarām 1435 AH / 16 November 2013 CE

---

## Foreword by His Eminence Shaykh Mufti Muhammad Rafi Usmani, may God preserve and protect him

[p. 15] We praise Him and send blessings upon His noble Messenger. To proceed:

It pleases and delights us — we, the teachers of Jāmiʿa Dār al-ʿUlūm Karachi and those affiliated with it — and it fills us with hope and expectation, that it has begun to take steps in the study of the burning issues that pose astonishing new challenges in most areas and fields of life for Muslims in general, and for the scholars of the Sharia among them in particular.

It is well known that with the passing of the fifteenth century CE, the Muslims were deprived of the land of al-Andalus — the lost paradise of Islam — which they had ruled for nearly a thousand years with all the splendour and power; and not only that, but they had attended to the sciences there with such care that it made them the leaders and guides of humanity in them. One never ceases to marvel that the West did not feel the stirrings of progress until the day the political star of the Muslims began to set. For the Muslims slid towards backwardness throughout the world as a result of the catastrophe of the fall of al-Andalus, while it gave Europe the opportunity to advance in the field of progress and development after it had been floundering in the darkness of ignorance and the black night of backwardness.

About a century after the catastrophe of the fall of al-Andalus, the countries of the West achieved great material development: on the one hand they managed to discover America, and on the other Vasco da Gama found a sea route to India. Although these were two simple events at first sight, they left a marked effect on changing the course of history and geography, and played a tangible role in bringing about a revolution in human society.

Thus began Europe's journey towards industrial development, and it made rapid strides in the field of the natural sciences and modern technology, drawing a great deal in this on the efforts of the scholars of al-Andalus, gleaning from the tables of their learning and harvesting the fruits of their labours. Through the products of their thought and the fabric of their efforts it attained what we Muslims did not. It seized [p. 16] the opportunity and devoted all its powers to exploiting it, which resulted in revolutions and clear, evident changes in thought, economics and politics at a pace that exceeded every measure. Among these were the countless modern issues that the Industrial Revolution in England gave birth to, in addition to the far-reaching changes produced by the French Revolution. These two revolutions changed the course of human life throughout the world.

It is our duty as Muslims — and especially as people devoted to the Sharia sciences — in the age in which we live, to offer solutions to the problems of the age and to its demands that arose from these two revolutions, and to confront the blatant challenges that face us in the light of current developments; all of this in the light of the lofty Islamic values and ideals, alongside the pure creeds of Islam and its sublime jurisprudence.

In short, many fields have opened up which look to the scholars of the Sharia to devote their efforts, exhaust their energies and apply their qualifications to them. The tragedy is that they have opened at a time when the Muslims are passing through the worst and lowest state in their history — enough to make the weeping weep — and they are, God forbid, on the verge of collapse and at the brink of ruin. Before now, Islam never faced a challenge without having among its sons those capable of resisting it. The philosophy of the Greeks did not penetrate the lands of the Muslims except when there were among them people able to resist it with argument and proof, such as the Greatest Imam Abū Ḥanīfa (may God have mercy on him) and other mujtahid imams. No sooner did the domain of the Islamic governments expand, their conquests widen and their demands and requirements consequently increase, than Islam carried within it those who undertook to resolve them and meet their needs — such as al-Māturīdī, Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī and al-Ghazālī (may God have mercy on them) and other Islamic thinkers who bore the marks of asceticism and the adornment of piety.

Today, however, the revolution is more widespread and more comprehensive, its issues more complex and its problems harder to solve; and the pressure has grown greater since the scholars among the Muslims now lack, first, the necessary scholarly qualifications and physical energies, and second, sincerity and piety. [p. 17] All of this has in turn contributed to making their situation more aggravated and their position more dangerous. Yet since God has guaranteed this religion permanence and survival until the Day of Resurrection, in every age He raises up from among the Muslims those who undertake to provide Islamic solutions to every question and problem that humanity encounters or suffers.

We can limit the most important issues facing Islam, on which it requires its sons to concentrate, to five fields:

**(1) Thought and philosophy.** Just as, when Greek philosophy was translated into Arabic and reached the lands of the Muslims with its predominant materialist tendencies and heretical spirit, the Muslim scholars confronted it with all competence and merit — first by learning it and excelling in its method of argument, and second by using that very method to refute its proofs and expose the falsity of its arguments — which may be regarded as an immortal achievement and a bright blaze on the brow of Islamic history;

so today, Western materialist philosophy and the intellectual misguidance that follows it pose a blatant challenge and an overwhelming assault on the beliefs and principles of the Islamic Umma. It has thus become the responsibility of its scholars to resist it by answering the objections it has raised and the doubts it has stirred up, following the same method their predecessors followed: first by mastering that philosophy, and second by using its own style as a weapon to refute and silence it.

**(2) Economics.** The English revolution brought about astonishing development in it, and it came to be dominated by two schools of thought and the two economic systems born of them, which the world has known and experienced under the names capitalism and its opposite, socialism. What concerns us Muslims in this respect is that we explain to the world the economic system of Islam, so that it may arbitrate between those two prevailing systems — especially since the issue of economics has taken first place among the burning, important issues of the world, and has raised countless questions from the point of view of the Sharia, and specifically from the perspective of Islamic jurisprudence. Praise be to God, who has enabled some Muslims to begin to fill [p. 18] the tangible gap in these two fields; work has also begun in other fields, but the need for still more attention remains pressing and urgent.

**(3) Politics.** The world has witnessed a number of modern political theories and the various kinds of systems and methods of government that followed from them. It thus falls on the shoulders of the scholars of Islam to study them and then to clarify the clear, decisive position of Islam on them: distinguishing what is in harmony with the principles of Islam — which may properly be called Islamic politics — from what contradicts and clashes with them, which we consider heretical and irreligious; in addition to revealing the merits of Islamic politics and the dividing line that separates it from what prevails in the world today under the name of the democratic system, monarchy, and others.

**(4) Medicine.** Modern inventions have brought about tremendous development in it, which makes it all the more necessary for Muslim scholars to attend to studying the mass of jurisprudential questions accumulating in it, so that they can respond to the challenge this progress and development poses to them, and to what is actually put before them from the east and west of the earth in the way of questions about the permissibility or prohibition of things, and about the position of the Sharia on methods of treatment and the ways of medication current in the world. As we mentioned earlier, many scholars and muftis around the world have turned their attention to this field as well, but it still needs more effort and care from them to meet its needs and fulfil its requirements.

**(5) Law and legislation.** This is a field in which the Muslims were the foremost champions and the winners of the race, for the world had never known a codified, systematic law like that which distinguished Islamic jurisprudence and which enjoyed codification, foundation-laying and derivation of a kind the world had not witnessed before. But in later ages Europe gave it attention and so advanced in it over the rest of humanity, Muslims included, becoming its pioneers and leaders. Although a group of scholars has had the good fortune to serve it, it, like the preceding fields, still needs those who will delve into it with distinction and skill.

At any rate, these are five areas and fields that face the Muslims. In each [p. 19] of them there are thorny issues and complex questions. Their due can be fulfilled, and they can then be harnessed and the rulings of Islam applied in them, only if a sufficient number and a great many of the sons of the Umma devote their lives to specializing and becoming expert in them — some in one, others in another, and so on.

In keeping with the principle "What cannot be attained in full should not be abandoned in full", the Dār al-ʿUlūm Karachi family held a training course on economics and its issues from an Islamic perspective. A considerable number of scholars took part in it with eagerness and energy, and showed how greatly they valued its importance and great usefulness. It produced the book *Business and the Contemporary Economy in the Light of Islam*, which can be taken as a basis for large-scale projects in this field. Dār al-ʿUlūm Karachi has had the good fortune of playing its full part and prominent role in performing the communal obligation (wājib kifāʾī) that weighed on the shoulders of the Muslims of Pakistan, represented by its Vice-President, His Eminence Shaykh Mufti Muhammad Taqi Usmani — may God grant him well-being and bless his life — who has delved deeply into the study of economic questions and become thoroughly versed in them, and then compared them with the rulings and teachings of Islam, after simplifying them and casting them in a modern form so that they have become easily accessible to all who seek them among ordinary people.

Today we are embarking on a course on politics, hoping, by God's grace and generosity, that He will grant it acceptance and make it beneficial to the Muslims, as with its sister course held on economics, and that He will raise up from among the participating scholars someone who will devote his life to one of those five areas, giving it his attention and dedicating his care to it.

You have come here from the remotest and nearest parts of the country, covering long and arduous journeys, and I therefore pray for you from the depths of my heart, as I find a hadith ringing in my ears and running through my mind again and again — the saying of the Prophet ﷺ: "Whoever follows a path seeking knowledge in it, God makes easy for him a path to Paradise."¹

> ¹ *Sunan al-Tirmidhī*, ed. Shākir (5/28), Chapters on Knowledge; he said: "This is a ḥasan hadith." Hadith no. 2646.

## Introducing the Subject

[p. 20] Praise be to God, Lord of the worlds, and blessings and peace upon our master and patron Muhammad, the trustworthy Prophet, and upon all his family and companions, and upon all who follow them in excellence until the Day of Judgement. To proceed:

Honoured scholars of the Sharia: as His Eminence the President of the University, may God preserve him, has explained to you, the modern age has directed at the Muslims diverse challenges in many fields and from various directions. He, may God preserve him, excelled in analysing them and limiting them to five fields, which call for exceptional attention from Muslim scholars and require mighty efforts from them. I personally believe that the fields of economics and politics are the most important and most dangerous of them, for they have left lasting effects on human life in this age of ours. In addition, they have very often given rise to questions and stirred up doubts among people: if we accept that the prevailing systems are impermissible according to the Sharia, because their foundations and principles clash with the foundations and principles of Islam, then what way is there to replace those foundations with these principles, and to run the helm of these rulings in accordance with them?

How often we are asked, and others are asked: if we purified our economic system of usury (ribā) on the grounds that God has forbidden it, and the scholars also consider bank interest to be part of forbidden usury, is there a Sharia alternative to it that could be adopted to run the economic system in this age? If we dissociated ourselves from modern political theories and abandoned the systems of government generated by them, on the grounds that they oppose Islam and clash with the Sharia, how could the helm of government be run according to the rulings of Islam in the present age and the demands of its political circles?! For various religious circles never cease to claim that Islam is free of monarchy and does not permit individual governments or dictatorships either, and that democracy too does not fully accord with its rulings — or so, at least, a serious and circumspect section of them maintains. So the question that arises in the mind of the child of the twentieth century is: what is the Islamic system of government? How can it be applied and implemented in the modern age while taking account of its demands, needs, circumstances and requirements?!

[p. 21] Since the fields of economics and politics are alike in facing this challenge and confronting this question in the first place, and because of their eminent position among those fields in the second place, we have devoted this course to the study of political issues, after the first one was devoted to economics and modern business. In it we aim, first, to review the various political theories and to pause over them; and second, to explain a plan and a picture of the actual application and real implementation of the rulings and principles of Islam. Although the period allotted to this course is ten days only — which is very short relative to the vastness and ramifications of the subject of politics, to the extent that we can say that political science has today assumed the character of an independent science that enjoys a great many works — we will nonetheless try, with God's help, to present to you an introduction to its important topics and a concise, summarized study of it, God willing. God, glorified be He, is the one whose help is sought.

### Politics and the First Works Written on It

Political science is not a modern science invented by Western men. Rather, it is an ancient science the world has known since the dawn of philosophy, since it is one of its branches and divisions. For the philosophers first divided wisdom — or say philosophy — into two basic parts: theoretical wisdom and practical wisdom. Then each of them was divided into three parts: the theoretical into the divine, the mathematical and the natural; and the practical into the refinement of character (ethics), household management (economics), and civil politics.

From this it is clear that politics is one branch of philosophy, which its practitioners have continued to treat in research and reflection alongside its other branches.

As for the first book on politics, I believe it is *The Republic* of the famous Greek philosopher Plato. He arranged it in the light of what he had learned from his teacher Socrates during his apprenticeship to him, merging his own personal opinions into it. This book of his has been translated into English under the title "Republic". It is most likely the first work on politics in the world, since the era of Plato goes back to around 400 years before Christ.

[p. 22] The book is worth reading, since it gives us a clear picture of the kind of writing and style of thinking that prevailed in that age. Although it is a large book, benefiting from it is somewhat difficult, especially in comparison with the style of writing prevalent in our age. For it is not arranged technically, and the topics are not set down in sequence and succession; rather he followed the method of dialogue in it — that is, conversation between two or more persons — and so presented all the topics in the form of a dialogue in which such-and-such a person met so-and-so, asked him about such-and-such, and he answered him thus. Although it can be regarded as the first brick and signpost in the field of politics, many of its discussions are shrouded in obscurity, or the allegorical style used in it makes them ambiguous. The researcher therefore faces a great problem and encounters extreme difficulty in extracting a conclusion from it or in identifying a position on something. At any rate, it takes pride in being the first book in the field of politics, and its author was the first to use the word "republic".

His most prominent student, Aristotle — who perhaps enjoyed more fame than his teacher in philosophical circles — then followed in his footsteps. He recorded the various portions of Plato's philosophy and presented them in the form of a book. To him goes the credit for founding all the fields of the natural sciences: you will hardly find a branch of them without finding him to be its first founder. Take, for example, medicine, zoology, geology, astronomy, optics and so on.

Among the works with which he enriched the human library is a book on politics entitled *Politics* itself, and this book of his is in truth an elevated and developed form of Plato's *Republic*, mentioned above. However, his book *Politics* surpasses Plato's *Republic* in the merits of its arrangement and composition, alongside its simplification of topics and clarification of issues, all of which the former lacks, as mentioned a little earlier.

His book has been printed in translation under the title "Politics", and fate has allowed me to read a large part of it. In the course of reading it, the reader soon gains a keen sense that philosophy, its methods and its ways of expression have undergone great changes and many upheavals, [p. 23] and that the philosophy studied by students in the scholarly circles of the Indian subcontinent, represented by the books written on it such as *al-Maybudhī*, *al-Hidāya al-Saʿīdiyya*, *al-Ṣadrā* and *al-Shams al-Bāzigha*, is not the philosophy of Aristotle. Rather, it is a branch of philosophy that the world knew centuries after Aristotle and is called Neoplatonic philosophy. Among the things that distinguish the two is that Aristotle's philosophy is marked by a focus on practical aspects more than theory, while the Neoplatonic philosophy is entirely its opposite, being dominated in its discussions by the theoretical side more than the practical.

At any rate, his book deserves to be read by people of learning so that they may come to know Aristotle's method and his arrival at forms of knowledge and philosophical theories that the world today calls "the most modern theories", wilfully blind to the clear, established facts, since Aristotle preceded them by thousands of years. What further increases the standing of his book and makes it a source of pride is that the scholars of politics have agreed that it is the main source and original reference for the theories that followed it throughout the history of humanity and over the course of human life.

In sum: politics is one of the ancient sciences that has enjoyed people's attention and scholarly service for a long time, beginning with Socrates and Plato, passing through Aristotle, and those political thinkers who followed in his footsteps. However, the basis of all of them and the centre of their discussions was nothing but pure theories that the toil of their minds and the labour of their thought produced alone. They therefore produced nothing but illusions built on mere speculation stripped of the light of revelation. But after the Muslims plunged into its depths, bearing a flame of faith and holding in their hands a torch of the light of revelation, they transformed its course and made of it an independent science which they called "Sharia governance" (*al-siyāsa al-sharʿiyya*). They devoted their care to it through research, writing and reflection until they reached its summit and attained its peak, so that it became capable of guaranteeing the soundness of the order of humanity and its safety if humanity took it into account and put it into practice.

However, in our course we shall refrain from exhausting its discussions and covering its subject matter, confining ourselves to its most important angles and practical dimensions which, in general, the scholars of the Sharia urgently need to know. Bear in mind that I am not one of the men of politics, nor one of the specialists [p. 24] and experts in its sciences; it is merely the extent of my knowledge from my study and reading of the books of these people. In the light of my survey and analysis of works on politics, I have chosen several headings to treat in this course of ours, devoting myself to explaining them and reaching sound conclusions about them with all my effort, God willing. These headings are as follows:

**(1)** How did the state (*State*) arise in this world? That is: how did it occur to human beings to form a state that divides society into a ruler who commands and a ruled who obeys? Since systems of government and practical political methods are closely connected with people's views on the beginning and rise of the state, it is fitting for people of Sharia knowledge to become acquainted with those theories, so as to be able to know these systems with insight and mastery.

**(2)** The second heading on which I would like to shed light is: an introduction to the various political systems and their philosophies and the ideas at work behind their rise and distinctiveness, such as monarchy, aristocracy, fascism, and democracy with its various branches and divisions.

**(3)** An introduction to systems of government and the kinds of powers. Under each political system the helm of government can be steered in many ways. Which method is preferable for forming the government? How many ministries and departments should it have, and how are powers distributed among them? We would like to present an introduction to the systems of government prevailing in the world today (as well as what prevailed in the past), such as the presidential system and the parliamentary system, for example, with their merits and the differences between them.

**(3)** [sic] In the course of introducing the systems of government, we shall move on to explaining the original sources of legislation and the basic sources of law-making. We shall likewise pass through the history of these systems from their origin to their actual implementation in the external world, and we shall cast a passing glance at the history of the two revolutions — the French and the Industrial in England — owing to their prominent, tangible role in the upheavals of the political world and in changing the course of history.

**(4)** Finally, we shall pause over the teachings of Islam and its rulings in the field of politics, and examine possible formulas and forms that could be adopted to apply and implement those rulings in [p. 25] this age of ours, while taking account of its needs and demands.

This, then, is a quick survey of the topics we shall treat through research and study in this course of ours, God willing.

---

# PART ONE

## Chapter One: The Origin and Development of the State

### Introduction

[p. 27] Since the study of government and its systems is among the most important subjects that political science undertakes to study, the first thing its scholars and philosophers attended to was the discussion of the origin and development of the state, answering in this several questions: How did authority appear in simple human societies? How did man join the ranks of government? In this regard, from the earliest time that humanity knew political science, several theories have been discovered, which we must review here — even if only briefly — because of their prominent role in creating and shaping the political systems in the world that followed them.

It should be known that the theories we are about to explain are merely the product of the ideas of people who were bare of divine revelation and deprived of heavenly guidance, relying in them on their pure rationalism and on whatever analogies and conjectures they found palatable. Then, when they arrived at some theory on the basis of analogy and conjecture, they searched for evidence for it, and so supported and strengthened it.

### The First Theory: The Social Contract Theory

The first of the theories that prevailed and became famous in political science in this regard is the theory of the social contract, designated in English by the term "Social Contract". In summary: that human beings, in their earliest period on earth, lived an individual life without being governed by a government, founded upon a state, or bound by a law or committed to a constitution; rather, their concern was concentrated on self-interest. This condition of theirs is called in the terminology of political science "the original condition" (State of Nature). (Those who advanced this idea had no evidence for what they said; but since they refused to submit to heavenly guidance, they discovered by the toil of their minds that human beings must have passed a period in which they were not divided into two groups, ruler and ruled — what they called the original condition.) And since this pervasive anarchic condition was a likely site of conflict and collision between [p. 28] the interests of human individuals — such that the interest of one required him to do something contrary to the interest of the other — disputes broke out among them and quarrels occurred. So they gathered to think about establishing a system that all of them would follow, which would protect them and their interests and protect them from conflict when interests clashed. Their thinking led them to agree to live under a mechanism that would impose laws through a ruling authority; and through this social agreement authority appeared in the world.

In sum, this theory holds that human beings themselves, by way of contracting among themselves, established the system of authority to protect their interests. Although this theory — the social contract theory — became famous through its interpreters mentioned below in the fifteenth century CE [sic], the researcher will find its conceptions in the writings and works of the ancient philosophers as well.

Curiously, however, on the basis of this one theory two political systems were later established that conflict clearly with each other and contradict each other explicitly, whose paths diverge and whose effects differ in every way: namely, the doctrine of absolute rule and the doctrine of popular sovereignty.

In detail: many scholars of politics interpreted the social contract theory to mean that when human beings delegated — for their own interest — all their rights, powers and authority to the [ruling] authority, it became, and ought to become, a despotic authority in managing their affairs, possessing absolute sovereignty and authority.

They held to this on the grounds that the social contract was concluded among human beings in the wake of a clash of interests and a struggle of demands; for each individual human being narrows the scope of his thinking and confines himself to what achieves his self-interest and what he considers it to be. So he should not be left free to follow whatever path he wishes in determining good and evil and distinguishing between what is sound and what is corrupt. Rather, since he has delegated authority over himself to the government, its head is the one who determines good and evil and distinguishes right from wrong — not with regard to his own self-interest, but with regard to the interests of the whole people. If he decrees something, then every one of his subjects is obliged and bound to act on it, [p. 29] even if against his will.

The greatest example of the application of this principle was presented by Socrates himself, the teacher of Plato, born in Athens. He had presented in the fields of religion and ethics many theories conflicting with the deep-rooted prevailing theories before him, to the point that he contradicted idolatry and refuted it (except that he did not believe in the Hereafter, as al-Qifṭī says in *Akhbār al-Ḥukamāʾ*, 1/87). So the government put him on trial and charged him with corrupting the youth and insulting religious traditions. Socrates relied on the fact that merely presenting new theories in ethics and opposing what people had previously believed was never considered a crime; yet the government paid no heed to that, but put him behind bars, and the court sentenced him to death.

His disciples and admirers — among them great wealthy men — also arranged for him to escape: first from the prison, and second from the state of Athens, so that its authority would have no power over him; for at that time the city-state system prevailed among the Greeks. But Socrates refused their initiative, explaining that — as a citizen of this city — he had covenanted to follow the commands of authority, whatever it might be, as holding absolute and despotic authority over him. He added: therefore, in fulfilment of that contract of mine, I prefer to remain in prison and submit to the process of execution, even if I consider it unjust and contrary to the demands of justice and the principles of fairness. Thus he carried out the death sentence on himself by drinking a cup of poison, according to the method of execution prevailing at the time. History has recorded his stance and remembered that he did not seize the opportunities to escape from prison despite their being easily available, regarding that as a betrayal of what he had covenanted with the government.

This, then, is an extreme interpretation of the theory of absolute rule on the basis of the social contract. It was its prevailing understanding until the fifteenth century CE, when France produced three specialist philosophers in the field of politics who have dominated mention and citation in the political sources written after them: Thomas Hobbes, Locke, and Rousseau. [Tr.: so in the original; Hobbes and Locke were English, not French, and they belong to the 17th century, Rousseau to the 18th.] Later authors in the field of politics relied on their books so heavily that they occupied first place [p. 30] in the list of political sources and references. Although their times were close to each other, their ideas and interpretations of the social contract theory were different and divergent. Hobbes and Locke held the view of the social contract as we have mentioned, while Rousseau interpreted it in an entirely opposite way. He held that authority did not arise by itself; rather, the people imposed it on themselves through social contracting among themselves. Therefore the source of legitimacy and sovereignty for every ruler is the people itself, and it is the people who granted the rulers authority, power and strength. Accordingly, the rulers must represent the people in managing the affairs of authority by laws expressing the general will of the people. If they do not do so, and manage the system contrary to what the people desire, or in a way that conflicts with its interests and demands, that is regarded as equivalent to breaking the social contract, and the people have the right to depose them and topple their government for breaking this contract.

He rejected the idea that the social contract theory leads to absolute rule, and emphasized that government ought to conform to the will of the people itself. His philosophy helped shape the political events that ultimately led to the outbreak of the French Revolution, whose details will come later, God willing.

### The Second Theory: The Theory of Force

Some philosophers advanced another theory on the origin of the state entitled "the theory of force". In summary: the state arose on the basis of force; that is, before forming communities, people quarrelled among themselves in the absence of authority and state, and some prevailed over others, so that by virtue of his victory one became the ruler and the other the ruled.

It is expressed in English by the phrase "wars begot states". In detail, as mentioned above: the establishment of the state and authority was not born of peaceful contracting; rather, it happened by chance, as a result of the victory of some people over others in the wars that broke out and the conflicts that flared up in the wake of clashing interests.

[p. 31] Of course, the adherents of this idea do not regard obtaining authority by the use of force as in any way unjust or tyrannical. Rather, it is attributed to Aristotle that whoever prevails over someone prevails over him only by virtue of qualities and merits he possesses that qualify him to prevail, and that force is in reality a manifestation through which the dust is cleared from those qualifications of his. Hence, since his acquisition of authority by prevailing in fact goes back to a quality inherent in him, it was in no way contrary to the demands of justice and reason.

### The Third Theory: The Patriarchal or Matriarchal Theory

Its gist: the basis of the state is the family; that is, every family has a head who leads it. Then several families come together to form a tribe whose head is its ruler; then several tribes come together to form a state, and one of their members is chosen to lead it and manage the system of its government.

If we examine this theory closely, we find that it does not differ much from the social contract theory. For people — whether within the scope of the tribe or the state — when they choose one of them as a leader for themselves, choose him only by contracting among themselves; except that this contracting may be concluded verbally, or may take place among them in meaning only.

### The Fourth Theory: The Theory of Divine Origin

If we let our gaze roam over the meaning of the theories just mentioned and the bases on which they are built, we find them bare of any reasonable basis apart from flimsy conjectures and false analogies. They had no evidence whatsoever, nor any proof, that this contract actually took place among human beings, or that wars and conflicts were what produced the origin of the state. Rather, they are theories in whose wilderness they wandered aimlessly, merely because they refused to believe that God is the one who created human beings and sent them into the world to realize certain aims under the shade of rulings and particular directives. Their refusal of faith in God and in His being the Creator made them stumble in the deserts of conjecture and guesswork, until their condition became the best illustration of the words of God, glorified and exalted: **"They have no knowledge of that; they only conjecture"** [al-Jāthiya, 45:24],

[p. 32] and of His words, mighty and exalted: **"They follow nothing but conjecture, and they only guess"** [al-Anʿām, 6:116].

As for those who believe in God — and that He is the one who created the universe and all within it, and sent human beings down to earth for a particular end — they have no need of such conjectures and analogies. Take, for example, the Muslims and the People of the Book, who believe that God created Adam — the first human being — and sent him down to earth only after making him a ruler, and the rest of humanity ruled by and subordinate to him. Hence humanity never passed through a period in which it lived an anarchic life not governed by a government; or, in other words, it never encountered a condition that could be called "the original condition" (State of Nature) according to the interpretation of the philosophers who held the social contract theory. In sum, their faith in God and His appointing of the first human being as His vicegerent (*istikhlāf*) saved them from running after the conjecture and the analogy latent in the social contract theory. This is what scholars of politics designate by the term: divine origin.

Since this theory belongs not only to the Muslims but also to those philosophers who believe in God, their word agreed on the principle that God is the one who created man and established government and authority for him. However, there are other schools that stumbled and wandered aimlessly in interpreting this theory, going in it to the point of extremism, saying: whoever assumes authority does so only on God's behalf, so human beings have no right ever to depose him. Indeed, some of them went even further, saying: when a tyrannical ruler is set over a people who abuses them and brings afflictions and calamities upon them, that is in reality a punishment from God that has descended upon them for their evil deeds. Since it is a punishment from God in which His will is manifested, the servants should not strive to remove him from the seat of government, even by safe, peaceful means; for that would amount to resisting what God is pleased with and opposing His will. From this they derived the rule that whoever ascends the throne of authority must be obeyed and submitted to, for in that lies God's will and by it His love is attained.

In truth, this problem arose only from their failure to distinguish between God's religious rulings (*aḥkām dīniyya*) and His cosmic will (*mashīʾa kawniyya*), although there is a great difference between the two. There is no doubt that God the Exalted sometimes [p. 33] sets a people of evil governors over them because of their evil deeds — as God mentioned at the beginning of Sūrat al-Isrāʾ, that when the Children of Israel spread corruption in the land and became exceedingly arrogant in it, twice God sent against them servants of His of great might, who disgraced them and utterly destroyed all that they had taken over.

Likewise, it is reported that the Prophet ﷺ said: "Your governors are [as] your deeds." [Tr.: i.e. your rulers are a reflection of your own deeds.]

However, this is God's cosmic will, and it does not mean that people should be content with it and not strive to replace them with righteous governors. Rather, the religious rulings legislated by God require them in such a case to turn back from their errant ways, trying to reform the system. They must depose the government and topple its system by whatever attempts are possible for them within the framework of safe, peaceful mechanisms. As for fighting (armed rebellion against him), it is forbidden in the general run of cases, because it leads to bloodshed among the Muslims and other evils that are among the most harmful to the Umma, and which the Sharia has forbidden to the Muslims so long as they can find a way to avoid it — as will come in detail in the second part of the book, God willing.

But removing him by peaceful means is among the duties of the Muslims. Its example is thus like an illness that befalls someone: there is no doubt that it is by God's will, and no doubt also that it is often a punishment for sins and evil deeds; yet treating it is in no way opposition to God's good pleasure or contrary to His will. Rather, God, glorified be He, Himself commands, in such a situation, seeking forgiveness and seeking treatment by whatever means are permitted by the Sharia.

So this has been a quick passing look at those political theories that are researched and debated in political science, and which later became the basis of the various systems of government.

---

## Chapter Two: An Introduction to the Various Political Systems

### Introduction

[p. 35] The second topic to which we would like to turn our attention is an introduction to the political systems the world has witnessed and that have prevailed in it, irrespective of the political theories explained in the first chapter. By political system we mean: the system that determines the course of political life in terms of the form of government — whether individual or collective — the merits and practices of the government, the method of its formation, and so on. We can divide all the political systems the world has known up to this day of ours into three categories: monarchy, aristocracy and democracy. From these three, other branches emerged as new forms of government, and we cannot avoid briefly introducing these three systems in order to reach the core of the main subject of the book.

### (1) Monarchy

Perhaps the oldest of the systems of government and the most influential and dominant in the world is the monarchical system of government, for it has continued in human societies from the beginning until this day of ours in its various forms and diverse shapes. Indeed, it had dominance and influence in most periods of human history. Although this word in reality means a kind of government headed by a king, whose government is individual and in which the king alone exercises rule, the patterns of its application in the external world have never been uniform; rather, they have differed and varied with differing times and countries, so that it too has come to have many types:

**(1) The system of absolute monarchical rule**

Among its most important features is that the king in it is not bound by any law or constitution; rather, his tongue becomes the source of law. Whatever he says takes on the character of law, without his needing in that any consultation, agreement or ratification by anyone (and even if he consulted someone in some [p. 36] cases, that was regarded as a favour from him, not an obligation). He is thus the holder of all powers in the state: he issues the laws, interprets them and undertakes their execution, and he himself undertakes judgement in resolving disputes and settling controversies, since the matter of judgement ultimately reverts to him. So his opinion is the [decisive] opinion and his word is the [final] word, whether in civil or criminal cases. In contrast to what prevails today — that the court must, in criminal cases, follow the laws that determine the punishment of the offender — the absolute ruler was never bound by such laws. Rather, if his anger flared against someone, he would order him sewn up in a donkey's skin, or order that someone be walled up alive in a wall, and that would become the law regarding that person.

This despotic absolute system is what bred tyrants such as Pharaoh and Nimrod, whose arrogance through their authority went so far that they claimed lordship!

**(2) Consultative monarchy**

Its meaning: that the king himself holds the legislative and executive powers in it, but he has a council (*shūrā*) which he consults in enacting and implementing laws and in other administrative affairs. The kinds of this council and its powers differ: there is what was called "Council" (al-majlis), and there is what was called the senate (majlis al-shuyūkh), and there is what was called by other names. Its forms also differ in terms of whether it is binding or not. In some states the king would consult the shūrā council without being bound to follow their advice as a matter of necessity or obligation, such that nothing deterred him from ruling against it and nothing prevented him from opposing it; while in other states the king was bound and strictly restricted to follow the shūrā and to issue whatever law or decision its members agreed upon.

It should be known that the cities of Greece were the most civilized and advanced cities in the world in ancient times, and the city-state system prevailed there at the time; that is, each city was an independent state with its own independent government. Cities at that time were fortified, surrounded by the walls of a strong fortress, so the inhabitants of one fortress regarded themselves as citizens of one state, [p. 37] and each city of them was self-governing and almost completely independent of the others, to the point that all the cities of Greece did not form a single federal state governed by one unified federal government. The kings of those cities followed at first the system of absolute rule, then converted it into the system of consultative monarchy, and some of them formed councils of shūrā to advise them in their various affairs. The shūrā was then binding in many of those cities, and not binding in some, while others set frameworks for it by dividing commands and issues into two kinds: those in which following the shūrā was binding and those in which it was not. This account goes back to 300–400 years before the Messiah, upon him be peace.

**(3) Religious monarchy**

It means: a monarchical system of government in which religion plays its role as a basic source of authority and rule. The king was chosen in it on the basis of religion, and was authenticated by the men of religion; so he remained throughout the period of his rule — and in the affairs of government — subject to them in general, in the sense that he was bound to follow the rulings of religion according to the interpretation they gave of them. This kind of monarchy prevailed among the Christians for a long period, whose beginning goes back to 300 [years] after the raising of the Messiah to heaven.

In detail: the followers of Jesus, upon him be peace, and his disciples continued to face war and aggression at the hands of their opponents, who had borne enmity towards them for three centuries. They used to propagate their religion and call people to it secretly and covertly, adhering to the pure doctrine of divine unity (*tawḥīd*) upon which Jesus, upon him be peace, had left them. During those three centuries, the hostile Jewish zealots entered their religion, led by Paul, who introduced into Christianity false doctrines such as the Trinity, redemption and the like. The struggle continued between the original Christians and the current led by Paul, until Paul's current prevailed and the followers of Jesus, upon him be peace, suffered a defeat that led to their extinction, or so few remained that they could barely suffice to resist the current. Thus Paul managed to triumph over true Christianity, distort its beliefs and corrupt [p. 38] its rulings, until the word "Christianity" came to be applied to this new religion that Paul brought into being, by which he opposed the call of Jesus, upon him be peace, and raided his teachings and his law.

In that period the Roman emperor Constantine (Constantine the Great, who founded the city of Constantinople named after him, and which became the capital of the Roman Empire) embraced the corrupted Christianity led by Paul — having previously been a pagan. His embrace of Christianity was the opening of Christianity's entry among the Romans. Pauline Christianity was led in his era by Athanasius, and it was he who conferred legitimacy on Constantine's government from the religious point of view — which was a beginning for religious monarchy among the Romans. This monarchy proceeded on the rule that purely religious affairs were delegated to the head of the Church — who was called the Pope — so that his word in them was regarded as final and decisive, while purely political affairs belonged to the king, so that his word in them was the final word. Nevertheless, the king was bound to follow the commands of the Church and was to some extent subject to its rulings, because the authority for determining the nature of affairs — whether religious or political — lay with the Pope. Since one scabbard cannot hold two swords, the question of the distribution of competences and powers between the Church and the state continued to be a pressing crisis and a great tragedy in the history of the Roman Empire, and so remained a burning, intractable problem in which the struggle raged, until democracy came and freed itself from the influence of the Church in state affairs by restricting its authority to the city of the Vatican only.

**The fourth type: Constitutional monarchy**

"It is a political system in which the king rules on the basis of powers defined by a constitution." It is the opposite of the absolute monarchy mentioned earlier, since the king himself in it is not the source of legislation, completely independent in issuing rulings and taking measures; rather, his powers, the work of his government and the force of his law are defined within the framework of that constitution, from which he may not deviate even a hand-span.

It too has existed in various eras of humanity and various parts of the inhabited world. It existed in Greece for a period, and Rome too witnessed it in some periods of its history, and especially [p. 39] in the periods after the Middle Ages, when monarchy was in most cases constitutional.

Among this type is what prevailed in several states: the monarchy subordinate to the parliamentary system, as in Britain, for example, where the monarch is Queen Elizabeth, but her functions are purely ceremonial and symbolic, while executive authority is exercised by the prime minister, the council of ministers or the house of representatives!

As you have noticed, "monarchy" is a polysemous general term (*kullī mushakkik*) that may be applied to every kind of monarchy, including absolute monarchy and ceremonial symbolic monarchy. Among kings there are those whose tongues are the source of law, and those who possess no authority at all except that they are crowned with the name of king. A brief introduction to its four types and their forms has passed before us, as they were embodied in various periods of humanity and their phases. On the other hand, the spread of democracy in the world has damaged the reputation of monarchy and distorted its image to the point that it has been regarded as an absolute evil in all its forms and shapes. Interacting with this false propaganda, we see some people negating its existence [in Islam] and rejecting it decisively from the Islamic point of view. In truth, a categorical ruling of rejection or acceptance on it absolutely is not correct; rather, it is a heading under which may fall both its sound and its corrupt kinds, and under whose concept is included what is beneficial and useful among its forms and what is harmful and injurious among its shapes.

As for the Noble Qur'an, we find it describing Ṭālūt (Saul) as king, mentioning his appointment as king in the context of bestowing favour and grace; as it also reminded the Children of Israel of His favour in making them kings: **"and made you kings"** [al-Māʾida, 5:20]. It likewise described David and Solomon as kings. Those who have turned their faces towards democracy — by their very nature of negating the existence of monarchy in Islam — have come with feeble interpretations and frigid, strained explanations in explaining this verse and interpreting the [word] monarchy in it, so as to make it agree with their purpose and suit their aim.

The truth is that the ruling on a system of government as right or wrong, or acceptance or rejection, does not turn only on its name and heading, but on its meaning and the powers of the king in it, and the foundations and principles he will follow in managing its affairs. If all this is free of anything that clouds it from [p. 40] the Sharia point of view, then the system is acceptable, and there is no justification for rejecting it in the Sharia, whatever its name and heading!

The upshot is that what prevails and has spread among us — that monarchy is an absolute evil — is nothing but the product of erroneous propaganda raised by modernist thinkers against it, out of partisanship for and preference for democracy; and we will come to its details in the coming pages, God willing. Islam does not make the ruling on a system depend on its name and heading only, but on its foundations and premises, which will be taken as basic principles in managing the helm of the system and running the wheel of government.

### (2) Aristocracy

A coined word taken from "the nobles" (*al-ashrāf*), meaning: a class of people of high status and prominent social position. In summary, this system [holds that] government and authority are not deserved by every Tom, Dick and Harry from among the rabble and riff-raff of the people; rather, they are the special preserve of a small number of citizens who are higher in status than others because of their family connections, their wealth and their particular abilities. Hence it is not the right of others to rule or seek rule.

There was also disagreement among the pioneers of this system in identifying the nobles who form the aristocratic class, and who therefore deserve rule over others by merit and worth. One said: they are those who possess lofty lineage distinguishing them from others of lowly lineage. Another said: they are those who occupy a prominent place in society from the religious point of view. Others defined them by their possession of abilities and noble human qualities, such as being the bravest and most intelligent of people. Thus their criteria for identifying the class of nobles among whom is confined the right to ascend the throne of authority and attain power and capability differed. However, all of them are united by this common measure: that authority is deserved only by a special group of people with a prominent social position distinguishing them from others; as for the rest, they must simply concede this right to them, submit to them and obey them.

It should be known that Aristotle and Plato were the originators of this idea and the inventors of this term, for they put forward the philosophy of intellectual aristocracy, holding that the right to rule should [p. 41] belong to those with prominent abilities and intellectual qualifications only. But this idea of theirs remained a wish and a fancy only, and was never actually realized in the external world, since no system has ever been established in which the rulers are chosen on the basis of their status and their mastery of knowledge.

It is worth noting here that aristocracy was originally applied to a form of government dominated by persons of prominent social standing. Today, however, it is also applied to the form in which this upper class does not directly exercise rule, but enjoys complete influence and full dominance over the rulers, such that it would be no exaggeration to regard them as the actual ruling party; in such a case too it is called aristocratic government.

What has become prevalent today in the use of [the word] "bureaucracy" in fact means this kind of system of government. Its gist is that administrative officials are able to dominate and take control of the system so that important government affairs are not concluded, and the state's important policies are not adopted, except in accordance with their wishes and the fulfilment of their demands; and they press the rulers so hard as to force them to follow them, to the point that they find no possibility of departing from their demands and wishes.

The word is compounded of bureau, meaning administrative officials, and (cracy) meaning rule; and the meaning of the compound is: that rule in reality belongs to the administrative officials and no one else.

With regard to identifying the upper class that deserves to rule people and lands, aristocracy has been divided into several types:

**(a) Intellectual aristocracy:** which is — as mentioned — an idea presented by Aristotle and Plato, but it never enjoyed actual application in external reality.

**(b) Racial aristocracy:**
It means: that the right to rule the country is confined to the members of one tribe or the members of a particular race, so that the king is only from among them.

It found currency and influence among a considerable number of nations and civilizations, and specifically in Hindu civilization, where the belief prevailed that rule was the special right of the members of [p. 42] the upper castes — Brahmans, Rajputs and Kshatriyas.

**(c) Religious aristocracy, or theocracy**
A word of Greek origin, compounded of two Greek words: "theo", meaning god (from it comes the word "Theology" for divinity, i.e. the science of God), and "cracy", meaning rule. So the meaning of the word is: the rule of God.

The motive behind the launching of this system was a blessed, splendid idea: that true rule in this universe belongs to God, glorified and exalted, and therefore whoever assumes authority over people and lands should be subject to and follow the rulings of God, glorified and exalted. The question these people faced was: who has the authority to interpret the law of God, glorified and exalted, and to specify His rulings? Christianity delegated this authority to the Pope and charged him with interpreting the divine law and specifying its rulings, then informing the ruler of them so that he would be compelled to act on them and implement them. The outcome was that theocracy came in fact to be confined in its meaning to the rule of the men of religion, after it had linguistically denoted the rule of God. Hence we see that the word is usually translated today in this second sense (the rule of the men of religion), not the first (the rule of God).

As we mentioned a little earlier, the sultan's subjection to the rulings of the Pope continued to be an intractable problem in the history of the Christian governments among the Romans. It caused, on the one hand, the outbreak of partisanships and the intensification of rifts between state and Church; and on the other, since the Pope enjoyed unlimited and unrestricted authority in specifying rulings and legislating them, he was not slow to exploit it many times, on various occasions, in what was illegitimate and not beneficial — especially when this authority passed into the hands of worshippers of personal interest and servants of selfish whims, whose care for it and greed for obtaining it drove them to choose harsh plans and practise ferocious policies that made the Romans live suffocated in an atmosphere of hardship, injustice and aggression for many centuries. This ultimately resulted in the people's rebellion against religion, and consequently in the establishment of democracy on the ruins of the refutation of the Church's authority and the ending of its interference in state affairs, until [p. 43] the word "theocracy" today became an insult and a disgrace that does not strike the ears except to bring to the mind the image of those hardships and calamities that the people of Rome suffered and endured during the authority of the popes and priests.

Since the word "theocracy" has today come to be a disgrace, many Muslims have come to apply to it the ruling of categorical rejection from the Sharia point of view, ignoring and feigning ignorance of the essential difference between its original philosophy, on whose foundation it was built, and what its practical application among the Christians came to. From this we see today that whenever scholars speak of the Islamic system of government, or demand it in Pakistan, or contribute their share in politics aspiring to establish Islamic government, from the first moment the modernist secular class sets about aiming arrows at them and launching raids on them, shouting at the top of their voices with all condemnation and denunciation that these people claim to set the state alight in the fire of theocracy, and that we shall resist them and fight them, and so on and so forth... — without any of the opponents or supporters directing their attention to understanding the reality of the word and the idea at work behind it: that it in truth denotes "the rule of God", which is a great principle in Islamic politics that God has explained in an eloquent, elegant style in His words, glorified be He: **"Judgement belongs to God alone"** [al-Anʿām, 6:57; Yūsuf, 12:40, 67]. Consider, then, the gravity of the position of one who denounces it and proclaims — without awareness or insight — that it has no share in Islam! Does not his denouncing it and his judgement of its absolute prohibition entail negating the rule of God in Islam?!

Once more we clarify that theocracy in its original concept and meaning — namely that true rule in the universe belongs only to God, and that government should be subject to the divine law — is a sound, correct idea that agrees with what is established in Islam and what the Noble Qur'an has declared more than once. But when it was actually realized in the external world, it was accompanied by many evils and attended by numerous abuses that led to the distortion of its image and the ruining of its reputation. In what follows we shall turn to clarifying the distortion and disfigurement it faced at the hands of the men of the earlier religions: Judaism, Hinduism and Christianity.

#### Jewish and Hindu theocracy

[p. 44] The first theocracy to appear on the face of the earth belonged to the Jews. All the Children of Israel — before the sending of Jesus, upon him be peace — were charged with following the law of Moses, upon him be peace, revealed to him in the Torah, which they called "the Law". However, most of its rulings concerned worship, purity and the like, and few of them concerned the civil and civilizational aspect of human life. So when they resolved to establish the state and run its system on the basis of the law of the Torah — which, as mentioned, was almost devoid of civil rulings — they very often faced an issue whose ruling the Torah had not stated. So, to learn the divine rulings and enact law in their light in such cases, they delegated authority and power to the scholars and men of religion. They had a particular way of performing this task: their rabbi would sit in a tent devoted to this purpose called "the Tent of Worship" [Tr.: i.e. the Tabernacle], in which was placed an ark of a special kind, covered with a covering of gold. The rabbi would watch for suitable weather in which the flashing of lightning in the sky could be hoped for. He would sit near this ark in an attitude of watching, reciting some of its verses and invocations; when the lightning began to flash, this rabbi would rise saying that Yahweh (meaning: God) had revealed to him through this lightning the ruling of such-and-such on this issue.

Thus the belief prevailed and spread among them that Yahweh informed the rabbi of His law on new cases by speaking to him directly.

They would resort to it whenever they faced such an issue or such a situation befell them. The rabbi would take up his place in the Tent of Worship to wait for the lightning that would lead him to revelation according to their corrupt claims. If it flashed — fine; otherwise he would continue his watch to claim a ruling on the issue, alleging that it had been revealed to him or inspired to him.

It is clear that the claims of unveiling (*kashf*) and direct inspiration from God to their rabbi were nothing but fancies and illusions not based on firm evidence or sound reasoning; rather, it was greed for preserving their leadership and continuing their headship that led them to invent them. Therefore

[p. 45] their alleged, fabricated law was nothing but an expression of their whims and desires, their personal inclinations and opinions, which they would enforce in the land as the rule and law of God — whereas in reality it was far removed from the law of God, His rulings and His good pleasure.

Something similar to this theocracy also prevailed in Hinduism, where the Brahman caste held the authority to know the law of God, glorified and exalted, through direct contact with Him.

What the two theocracies — Jewish and Hindu — have in common is that both imposed on the whole people the obligation of the alleged law as being from God, according to what the men of religion reported and enforced, without anyone having any possibility of challenging its standing on the grounds that it was not from God, and that it was nothing but what their own selves had invented and their desires had run after.

At any rate, this kind of theocracy, based on the claim of direct revelation from God to the priesthood and the men of religion, was prevalent and widespread in Judaism and Hinduism, and it still continues among the "Lamas", who are based in Tibet and Japan.

Since the kings among them were also bound to comply with this alleged divine law that was communicated to the priesthood and religious leadership — which amounted to subjecting them to the authority and control of these people — they tried to free themselves from it, until they managed to do so by way of alliance and collusion with some of the men of religion, who declared that the authority to know God's rulings and His law through direct revelation had passed to the king, in what God inspired him with. Thus authority was handed over to the king, resulting in the belief that what he says is nothing but an expression of the divine law. This was followed by another arbitrary philosophy: that the king is a creature created from Paradise, raised under special care from God, and sent down into the world bearing and possessing his authority directly from God. Therefore obedience to him is obligatory in all his commands and laws whatever they may be — even if he is tyrannical in rule, transgresses in command and oppresses the subjects — for all that is only according to God's good pleasure and entirely according to His will. Hence all the subjects must bear it and endure it, and never dare to take any step to remove him or topple his system. Thus theocracy blended with religious monarchy!

#### Christian theocracy

[p. 46] It should be known, first, that our master Jesus, upon him be peace, was sent to the Children of Israel specifically, to turn them away from polytheism and other corrupt beliefs and deeds that had crept into their religion at the hands of the falsifying deniers. His law was a copy of the Torah, entirely in line with the original except for some particular rulings (and this exception is only permissible according to our view as Muslims). As for the Bible, it is clear from it that the purpose of sending Jesus, upon him be peace, as a prophet was only to implement the Torah and apply its law, not to abrogate or change it; for many texts in it are reported from our master Jesus, upon him be peace, that convey this meaning. So the law of Jesus, upon him be peace, was not different in anything from the law of Moses, upon him be peace, according to the original belief of the Christians. But "Paul", among the distortion and falsification that befell Christianity, also introduced into it this deviant doctrine: that the Torah — with all the rulings in it — had been abrogated by the mission of Jesus, upon him be peace.

It should be known that Paul (Saint Paul) existed in the time of Jesus, upon him be peace, and was a Jew whose name was "Saul". No sooner had Jesus, upon him be peace, been raised to heaven and his disciples begun calling people to his religion than this man turned into a spiteful enemy of theirs, sparing no effort in harming and persecuting them. He continued thus until he suddenly claimed that — on his way back from Damascus — he had seen a light from heaven more brilliant than the sun; so he fell to the ground because of it and heard a voice speaking to him and saying to him in Arabic [sic: Acts 26:14 has "in the Hebrew tongue"]: "Saul, Saul, why do you persecute me?" He claimed that it was Jesus, upon him be peace, and that he meant by the persecution the persecution of his companions and disciples. He also claimed that after this incident he repented of what he had been engaged in during his past life, and left Judaism as a believer in the Lord Jesus Christ. So this Paul arose and introduced much distortion into original, pure Christianity, and brought into being another Christianity, which has no connection to the first, its beliefs or its practices. The prevailing beliefs that Christians embrace today, and which are regarded as the foundations of the Christian religion, are nothing but the product of the thought of Paul, who racked his mind and laboured his thought and so invented these [p. 47] deformed, false doctrines in order to harm Christianity, corrupt it and distort it. Even in this day of yours, the "Holy Bible" is in your hands — and it has received an abundant share of distortion — yet you will not find in it anything about the doctrine of the Trinity, nor the doctrine of redemption and atonement.

Among Paul's distortion of and tampering with Christianity in its beliefs was that he regarded the Torah as a curse, and claimed that Jesus had been sent to deliver people and save them from the curse of the Torah. As for the account of the means he adopted as a mechanism to achieve all this — attaining this influence and interference by eliminating the authority of the followers of Jesus, upon him be peace, and his disciples — that is a long account, which you can find laid out and detailed in my book *What Is Christianity?*.

At any rate, his claim that the Torah had been abrogated resulted in Christian beliefs and practices being confined to what is mentioned in the four Gospels only. If you let your eyes roam and look closely in them, you will not find in them any ruling relating to political and civil life, except for the saying: "Whoever strikes you on your right cheek, turn to him the other also", and the like, which focuses on moral values such as tolerance, forbearance and patience. As for rulings and laws, it is entirely empty-handed of them!

So it follows from the foregoing detail that if a government is established on the basis of the Christian religion, the four Gospels have nothing to supply it with in any matter of state affairs and government; for they are entirely devoid of it! Here a question arises: what will Christian government adopt as a religious law to follow in managing the helm of government and running its wheel? This was a question the Christians faced for the first time when Christianity became the official religion of Rome following the Roman Emperor Constantine's embrace of it. Their situation in this became worse than that of Judaism, for the Torah carries between its covers six laws of the taʿzīr [discretionary punishment] type, in addition to many criminal laws, while the Bible was devoid even of this slender number of rulings and laws. So, to free themselves from this thorny position and to rescue themselves from this crisis, the Christians resorted to inventing another philosophy which meant delegating legislative authority to [p. 48] the Church.

It should also be known that the Church in Catholic Christianity is not merely a place of worship, like the mosque among the Muslims; rather, it is regarded among them as an independent organization playing a special role. It is also enveloped in a cloud of complexity and intractability, beginning with its being regarded as the deputy of the disciple Peter, whom Christianity regards as the best of the disciples, the highest of them in standing and the most eminent in rank, and claims that he is the one to whom the knowledge of Jesus, upon him be peace, passed. So he had in the souls of the Christians the same standing held by our master Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq, may God be pleased with him, among the Muslims (the comparison being in their rank among their respective peoples only, not in every respect) — indeed higher and loftier than that, since he — like the other disciples — holds among them the rank of prophethood. Christianity also says: Peter enjoined the men of religion to continue his work and carry out his task after him. Since establishing the Church and administering it was in the hands of those men of religion and that religious leadership, which represented its role on behalf of Peter, it alone should have authority in legislating rulings and enacting laws.

It should be known that the Church is headed by the Pope, and that Christianity has an organized ranking of the leaders and men of religion, with the powers and duties of each rank defined, beginning with the deacon, followed by the archdeacon, then the bishop, then the archbishop, then the cardinal. When seventy of them agree on a person, he becomes the Pope. As for the word "priest" (*qassīs*), it is more general than these words absolutely, being applied to the holders of all these ranks; and there may also be a rank other than those mentioned whose holder may properly be called a priest. This system concerned with the ordering of their degrees and positions is called the priestly system (the hierarchy), and the body of those holding these various positions is called the men of religion (the clergy).

The ability to enter the Church's system and the aforementioned ranks did not turn only on the scholarly and practical qualifications and abilities a person had attained; rather, it may [p. 49] have been due to nationalist considerations, and sometimes it took place through election carried out by a special council set up for this purpose (choosing persons to hold these various positions). But their election was not free of the tricks and methods of forgery that usually accompany political elections. Very often it took place under the constraints of racial discrimination: for example, if there was a region inhabited by a group of Christians, white and black, it was possible that the position of bishop would always and absolutely be occupied only by whites, while blacks were not, whatever their degree of knowledge and mastery of learning.

Then, since they believed the Pope to represent the Church, which is the deputy of Peter, they naturally believed him to be infallible, and this became the doctrine of the Roman Catholic Church: that the Pope is infallible and free from error in religious affairs. The English term (infallible) is specifically used for him, meaning: the person who does not err and whose foot does not slip. However, his infallibility among them was confined to legislative matters only; that is, whenever he issued a ruling of the Sharia or enforced one of its laws, he was infallible in that, free from error and mistake. It should also be known that his role among them was not confined to explaining and interpreting the law; rather, he had the authority to legislate and to enact laws as well, and this was the area in which they believed him infallible from error.
