It has attained such fame and acceptance in political circles that it has become the original source to which the discussion of civil fundamental rights is always attributed, and England regards it as a source of pride, claiming that it was the first to lay the first brick in the edifice of the call to fundamental rights. But on the other hand, if we examine the text of this document, we find it devoid of focus on civil rights, except for what appears in it [p. 117] in the clause providing that no citizen shall be detained except in the cases defined by law, and no one shall be convicted until a regular judicial body establishes his conviction. This brief sentence is what relates to civil rights among the clauses of the document called the Magna Carta, which clearly shows the worsening situation regarding human rights before the signing of that document, as it was common to arrest and detain a person without there being a pressing need for a legal justification that would allow and permit it! As for the remaining clauses, some of them concern the independence of the Church, and some aim at explaining the government's entitlement to impose taxes on the people and so on. So although there is nothing in it — apart from that single clause — relating to civil fundamental human rights, they have raised an uproar claiming to have preceded all humanity in protecting and safeguarding human rights. By contrast, the fundamental rights the Prophet ﷺ declared in the Farewell Sermon are marked by a comprehensiveness and clarity that the Magna Carta cannot match or even come near — let alone surpass or rival!

On the other hand, the Noble Qur'an too has declared the civil fundamental rights of man since the first dawning of Islam, and its teachings began to fill the universe with light and brilliance; but they close their eyes to it, trying to conceal it out of what has taken hold of them of tendencies of feigned blindness and feigned ignorance born of blind religious partisanship.

I am seized with astonishment when I see their false propaganda about attributing the credit for protecting civil rights to the Magna Carta, while it is a document that does not exceed 42 pages and does not provide for any of the fundamental rights except the limitation of detaining a person and convicting him without legal justification; whereas the American Declaration of Independence opened its statement of the other rights it contains with the sentence: (Every human being is born from his mother's womb free, so no one may enslave him) — and it conveys the same meaning that our master ʿUmar al-Fārūq, may God be pleased with him, made clear long centuries before the independence of America, when addressing ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, may God be pleased with him: "Since when have you enslaved people, when their mothers bore them free?"¹

> ¹ *Kanz al-ʿUmmāl*: (12/660), hadith no. 36010, Ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam.

[p. 118] Likewise what Ribʿī ibn ʿĀmir, may God be pleased with him, said when Rustam asked him what he had come to them with; he said: "God has sent us to bring out whom He wills from the worship of servants to the worship of God."¹

These clear historical facts, as clear as the sun at midday, establish with all explicitness and decisiveness that these [Muslims] have the credit of preceding all humanity in attending to fundamental human rights and emphasizing their protection and actually providing them to every human being. Therefore Western propaganda and its claims, and its granting the Magna Carta the credit for acquainting humanity with human rights, are nothing but fabrications by which it is intended to obliterate established facts and distort the image of pure Islam by accusing it of violating and contradicting these rights!

Add to that that the liberal democratic states still cry out at the top of their voices and raise their throats in declaring these rights and calling to them, while you will not find a state in which the people exercises its freedoms in expression and otherwise with full freedom and independence. Rather, every state has imposed restrictions on its freedoms and laid down conditions on them; no state has ever allowed its citizens to violate the sanctity of a person's life, property and honour on the basis of the freedoms that the civil rights have granted them. So it is known that these freedoms are not absolute and fully independent; rather there must be restrictions imposed on them that prevent a person's exercise of his freedom from leading to causing harm and injury to others. So the question that arises here is: what are the restrictions that can be imposed on man in exercising those freedoms? Is there a sound criterion for them that we take as a standard for specifying those restrictions and defining those conditions? Who is it that restricts these freedoms and specifies which restrictions it is correct to impose and which not? Which freedoms admit of restriction and which do not? The truth is that these irreligious secular bodies will never succeed in presenting a satisfying, convincing answer to these questions that arise in the mind of the researcher who looks at man's right to exercise these freedoms on the one hand, and feels the pressing need to restrict them to guarantee the protection of the rights of individuals on the other. Since they have no straight balance to take as a regulated criterion for restricting these freedoms, they have been compelled to delegate the authority in it sometimes to the law of the state, and sometimes to

> ¹ *Al-Bidāya wa-l-Nihāya*, Iḥyāʾ al-Turāth ed. (7/46).

[p. 119] the general policies of the state, and at other times to moral ideals and virtuous principles. But it is self-evident that however much they try, the outcome among them ultimately reverts to man, his making and his thought: for the law is legislated among them only by man, the policies of the state are adopted among them only according to the will of the people, and moral values are defined among them only by human societies. Hence the question posed still stands as a formidable challenge to them. It is thus established that subjecting man's freedoms to these things means nothing but a trick to strip them from him, and enabling those in authority to use the umbrella of the subordination of rights to the rule of law — which reverts to them in laying it down and implementing it — as an instrument for depriving man of them whenever they wish.

Hence we must acknowledge this established fact: that the world has no philosophy forming a decisive, definitive criterion for imposing restrictions and laying down conditions on human freedoms. So no means remains for it other than for man to submit to that Power capable of granting him his creation and endowing him with the power of opinion and depositing in him the faculty of thought; and consequently he must resort to Him concerning the restriction of the freedom of his opinion and his thought and the narrowing of its scope. It is thus established from all this that the freedoms granted to man by the documents of civil rights must be restricted in the light of the divine rulings and in subordination to His revealed law. Whatever agrees with His law and rulings, there is no harm in expressing; and what does not agree with them is the true site of restriction by which the opinion ought to be bound.

As for human philosophy and human law, they will never succeed in establishing a decisive, definitive criterion that can be followed always and absolutely.

At any rate, this has been a brief introduction to civil / fundamental rights, which are regarded as among the most important components of democratic constitutions in our age.

After the preamble and the statement of rights, the third part of the constitution usually sets out the three powers of government (legislative, executive and judicial), the competences of each of them, the methods of their formation and so on.

As for the legislature, we have spoken about it before in detail, so we refrain from [p. 120] mentioning it again, turning to cast a passing glance at the two powers, the executive and the judicial, and the constitution's position on them.

**The executive**

The executive is divided into two types:

(1) The political executive: meaning the executives who ascend the seat of government through elections and obtaining voters' votes, and who undertake to steer the wheel of government affairs throughout a specified electoral cycle — such as the prime minister, and likewise the ministers themselves.

(2) The non-political executive: it comprises government employees, such as secretaries of ministries and directors of government departments. They are not employed on the basis of elections, and hence not limited by an electoral cycle; they are government employees who manage its affairs before the elections and after them as well, without revolving with the governments and the parties forming them.

The constitution provides the principles and rules for both types of executives, such as the formation of the government and the definition of its authorities and powers, the employment of administrative officials and the definition of the conditions of their employment, and other necessary matters.

The constitution usually guarantees administrative officials that they will not be removed from their positions except for a reasonable cause justifying that for the government. Accordingly we see them remaining in their posts while the government is overturned and passes from one person to another and from one party to another; and sometimes they acquire complete control over the government such that they influence government policies and commands. This is what is called bureaucracy (in English: Bureaucracy).

**The judicial system of the state**

Among the essential characteristics of democratic government is the existence of an independent judicial body. From this, the constitution in the democratic system gives it attention by specifying its activities in the manner of selecting judges, and mechanisms that greatly strengthen its independence, so that it is able [p. 121] to exercise its tasks with complete freedom and without being affected by any pressure from any direction. From this, democratic systems focus a great deal on freeing the task of appointing judges and installing them — and specifically for judges of the higher courts — from any political or governmental element. Take America, for example: it stipulated for the appointment of judges the ratification and approval of the Senate, while many states do not stipulate this condition; rather, they allow the head of state or the head of government to appoint a judge on their own authority without needing to obtain the approval of the legislature. This is regarding the appointment of judges and installing them in judicial positions. As for their removal from judicial positions, it has become firmly established in all states that the ruling body has no authority at all to remove them and depose them from their positions — so that the judge does not deviate from ruling in cases out of fear that the government will remove him and depose him from his position if it needs to rule against the government and its policies. Accordingly, the constitution usually includes lawful procedures to remove the judge and dismiss him from his position if need calls for that. America has delegated the authority to remove them to the Senate, and Pakistan has formed for this purpose a special council named "the Supreme Judicial Council", which undertakes investigation into the case of judges if accusations are brought to it against them; if it ascertains the soundness of the accusation, it can remove the accused judge and dismiss him from his position. These are attempts that democracy makes in order to preserve the integrity of the judicial system and protect it from veering right and left under political pressures or interferences of the executive.

The constitution also provides the principles and rules relating to the affairs of the military forces, and the state's expenditures and budget.

---

## Chapter Four: Examples of Democratic Systems of Government

### Introduction

[p. 123] Having finished introducing the constitution and its contents in a brief glance, we follow it with a look at the forms that democracy has assumed in its actual application in various states of the world. We single out among them four states that have played a tangible role in drawing the picture of democracy and strengthening its principles and supporting its ideals, so that their systems are regarded as the most solid and well-established systems of democracy, namely: Britain, America, France and Switzerland.

### The parliamentary system in Britain

Since we, the sons of the Indian subcontinent, spent a period of time under the control of British colonialism and in the shadow of subjection and servitude to it, British principles became rooted in our minds, and hence we began trying to follow its method, imitate its model and follow in its footsteps. Proceeding from this, and as an imitation of the British system of democracy (called the parliamentary system: Parliamentary System), the parliamentary system has also become widespread among us, and our constitution was laid down along the lines of the constitution followed in the parliamentary system.

By the parliamentary system is meant: the system of government that regards parliament as the sole supreme power in whose hands lies actual rule of the country. [Such that the two powers, executive and legislative, are intertwined in such a system: the head of government is, by the nature of the case, both the executive head and the chief of the parliamentarians alike.]

The researcher never ceases to wonder when he sees, on the one hand, that the theory of "separation of powers" — which requires separation between the two powers, legislative and executive — is one of the most important principles of democracy, and sees, on the other hand, their fusion in a state that has a leading position in democracy and in founding its structure and strengthening its principles, Britain! For parliament, which represents the legislative power, in reality performs the task of enacting laws, just as it is what forms the executive power [p. 124] and possesses actual rule in the state, through its forming the government and appointing the council of ministers and its head first, and its holding the authority to remove him from the presidency of the government and dismiss his ministers whenever it wishes and wants second. So, from the point of view of the unity of the source of these two powers, it can rightly be said that they have intertwined and actually united — which amounts — despite the separation of the judicial power from them — to demolishing and undermining the democratic principle concerned with the separation of powers.

The parliamentary system is built on the theory of separation between the head of government and the head of state. In Britain the head of state is a king or queen, and the government is headed by the prime minister. The element at work behind this theory separating the two heads is: that the head of government is usually from the political party with the majority in parliament; so he represents his own party and implements its particular policies and programmes; whereas the head of state does not belong to a political party, so he does not represent a particular faction nor a defined party nor take its side; rather he represents the whole people, and hence he undertakes to supervise the government and urge it to implement the plans and policies aimed at taking account of the whole people impartially, away from political affiliation, and offers it advice and counsel if he sees a need for it. If the ruling party's government falls, he directs the invitation to form the government to the party that follows it in terms of seats in parliament. To this the functions of the head of state are limited.

Although the system of government existing in the United Kingdom is the constitutional monarchy headed by the king or queen, it possesses no actual authority except offering advice and counsel to the government that the policies it wants to implement are not, for example, in the interest of the people and the state. The saying of Queen Victoria became famous in this regard, addressing the prime minister: "Go ahead with what seems right to you; I will sign it even against my will — except that I want to alert you that this decision is not right."

The system of this monarchy in Britain is based on inheritance, not election. Some may have criticized this system on the grounds that it is unnecessary after authority has been transferred entirely to parliament. But [p. 125] it is the habit of the English not to touch the existence of their organizations and their ancient circles as long as they do not form an obstacle in the way of their progress. (From this we see Europe almost devoid of towering buildings in the manner of the American style.) So just as they left the "Big Ben" clock as it was, since it does not stand in the way of their progress, likewise they left the queen and the monarchy as they were; for it has never happened within three centuries that the queen refused to sign one of the decisions of parliament, in keeping with the prevailing custom among them — otherwise there is no clause in their constitution obliging the queen to it and compelling her to it. So since they were safe from the queen's opposition and her lack of interference in their affairs, they regarded it as not harmful and kept its system as it was. Hence the monarchical system does not harm them in anything except the meagre percentage (1%) of the budget the queen costs them; but they do not regard it as anything in comparison with the opportunities to benefit from the wisdom and counsel that the queen offers them, by virtue of her experience and her being adorned with the experiences she has acquired after long occupation of her office and exercise of its functions. From this they revere the queen and regard her as their elder who strokes their heads with the hand of affection and tenderness.

It is curious to mention that the monarchy, although deprived of any authority in rule and the system, yet all the matters concluded in the kingdom and the decisions taken in it are taken only in the name of the queen and on behalf of her government. So every law that is legislated and implemented in the kingdom must be legislated and implemented in the name of "the British Crown" (which is a name for the aforementioned monarchical system). The queen used in the past to undertake herself the signing of the law so that it became law; but now she has appointed a representative for herself to undertake the task of signing on her behalf, and at the end of every law he signs is written: "This law has been issued by order of the Crown of Britain [the British royal throne]."

This organization enjoys great sanctity among them, to the point that it has become a current proverb that "the king of Britain does not die" — meaning by the king here: the aforementioned monarchical system — just as their saying in this regard has become famous: "The king is dead, long live the king." The first means the departed king, and the second refers to the monarchical system.

[p. 126] This organization (the British Crown) enjoys many ceremonial entitlements, such as presiding over celebrations and occasions, corresponding with the heads of the states subordinate to the kingdom, and other symbolic, ceremonial matters that have no connection with authority and actual rule.

### The British Parliament

The representative authority in Britain consists of two chambers, one called "the House of Commons" (House of Commons) and the other "the House of Lords" (House of Lords). As for the House of Commons, it resembles our National Assembly in Pakistan, whose members are elected on the basis of voting by every citizen who has reached the age of 18 years, and which undertakes to elect the executive head of the kingdom from among its members. Its method is that the queen asks the head of the party with the majority in the representative chamber to undertake the formation of the government; if he obtains the confidence of the majority of the House of Commons he becomes prime minister. Then he appoints for himself ministers of both types: the ordinary minister and the minister who is also a member of the council of ministers. So he appoints 70 ministers, and the council of ministers is formed from 20 or 25 of them. The council of ministers is what determines government policy in general and takes most government decisions, and its members are like the right arm of the prime minister in running the wheel of government and managing its affairs, in addition to the administration of the government departments whose ministries have been entrusted to them. From this each of them has the right to give his opinion on any matter of government affairs, whether inside the framework of his ministry or outside it.

As for the other ministers, they have no share in government decisions; rather their functions and responsibilities are confined to what has been entrusted to them of government departments, and from this they are not allowed to attend the meetings of the council of ministers (unless one of them is sent a special invitation). (This category of ministers is called among us the federal minister.)

When the Speaker of the House of Commons has been elected, he is the one who presides over meetings in the British representative chamber, where the members of the ruling party sit on his right and on his left [p. 127] the members of the opposition. (From this arose the naming of political parties as right and left [the right wing and the left wing]. It happened at the beginning that the parties calling for capitalism were in government, and so they filled the seats on the right of the Speaker of the chamber, while the parties calling for communism were in opposition, and so they sat on his left. So capitalist parties were named "right-wing parties" even if they were in opposition, and communist parties "left-wing parties" even if they became the government. The term has developed today, so that the name "right-wing party" is applied to the party that calls for the old governmental order, while "left-wing" is applied to the party that opposes the old order and supports change in the prevailing order.)

### Legislation in the British system

The House of Commons occupies a high position in legislation and law-making. The order of that is that the government presents a draft law in it, which then passes through three readings:

(1) The first reading: in this round the text of the law is read out before the members of the chamber so that they may become acquainted with it, and also so that they may express their general opinions on it.

(2) The second reading: after initial approval of the draft is obtained, in the second stage it is referred to committees, which study it professionally, show its aspects of correctness and weakness, and propose the amendments necessary to reform it. Among these committees: the standing committee and the select committee. These committees prepare reports on the draft and present them to the chamber, whose members study it to propose amendment to it in the light of those reports; whatever a member proposes is presented in the chamber for voting, and thus the draft of the bill is ratified — amended or unamended — according to the opinion of the majority of the chamber.

(3) The third reading: in this round only the correction of verbal errors in the draft is allowed, and no other amendment is allowed besides that; then the bill is finally decided in this [p. 128] stage (the stage of the third reading). (It should be known that the law, as long as it is under discussion in the representative chamber, is called a bill, and after being decided by the chamber it becomes: the law (Act).)

At any rate, after the bill has passed through those three stages in the House of Commons, it is referred to the House of Lords.

### The House of Lords

It is one of the two chambers entrusted with the task of enacting laws in the parliament of Britain, and its system and powers are among the things that arouse wonder and astonishment in the mind of the researcher. For a large number of its members are chosen by inheritance: if a person is made a member of it, this membership extends in his family for long centuries, the successor in it becoming a member in place of the predecessor, and so on generation after generation, like the monarchy established among them on the basis of inheritance. The one who obtains it in the family of the deceased member is the one who deserves his inheritance from the Christian point of view; and since the woman does not inherit anything among the Christians, she cannot obtain this membership on the basis of inheritance.

Part of this chamber consists of nobles whom the queen chooses for this membership, and they are called "Peer" (meaning: the nobles), since the queen among them enjoys the authority to choose members of this chamber whatever their number. Usually those who enjoy this right are men who have distinguished efforts in the fields of business, civil service and the like, and the word (Technocrat) is applied to them. They also especially attract men who are specialists in the field of law, because of the pressing need for them, since this chamber is regarded as equivalent to the final court of appeal as well. These two types of members remain members for life, and proceeding from this concept it is said: "This chamber has an entrance and no exit" — that is, there are ways to enter it, but there is no way to get out of it!

Its original function was to review the laws approved by the House of Commons: if it approved them they were implemented; otherwise they were returned to the House of Commons a second time to review and reconsider them, and it was not possible to implement a law as long as it had not been approved by the House of Lords. In this regard [p. 129] a curious story occurred, namely that the queen sometimes used her powers in choosing the members of this chamber as a weapon to pressure them: if the House of Lords postponed approving a law that she wanted to hasten in implementing, she would threaten them by adding other members equal to them in number, since there was no numerical restriction on her regarding the choice of members. She would say: "If you do not approve this law I will add to the chamber other people equal in number to you, who will undertake to approve this law."

Accordingly, this is regarded as a strange organization with no parallel in the world, and it is said that a number of the nobles have never attended the meetings of the chamber. It had at first broad powers almost equal to the powers of the House of Commons, but they began to be stripped from it one after another, until the matter came to the shrinking of its powers, which made it a symbolic, ceremonial chamber. At first it was able to postpone a law approved by the House of Commons; but today it has been deprived of that — rather the law is regarded as automatically in force one year after it is put before the House of Lords.

Usually what is criticized about this chamber is that it has become a burden on the state without having any usefulness or benefit, so it ought to be ended and its system dissolved. But the British people still keep it for the sake of benefiting from the experiences of its members and the studies they present in its meetings. For these people find opportunities available for study and reading, then they present the result of their wide reading and the essence of their deep study in the discussions that take place in the meetings of the chamber about some subject. There is no doubt that the studies of this chamber usually distinguish themselves with an excellent scholarly and literary standing.

What is striking in the political system of Britain is that it has no written constitution; rather it follows a set of customs, habits and beliefs. Let us give an example of that with the queen's signing of a law approved by the House of Commons: there is no clause in the constitution obliging her to it; but the traditions and customs have proceeded thus, so the queen is committed to signing every law approved by the House of Commons. Likewise there is no text in the constitution obliging the queen to ask for the formation of the government [p. 130] from the head of the majority party in the representative chamber; but she commits to that according to the customs and traditions that have proceeded, continued and been followed in practice. These customs and traditions have taken on a constitutional character, and their standing in the kingdom does not fall short of the standing of any constitution in any state; they are the matters on the basis of which the courts decide.

In sum: Britain has no written constitution like other states, apart from some constitutional laws approved by parliament, such as the Bill of Rights. Since it has no constitution, and on the other hand parliament enjoys extremely wide powers, no law approved by parliament or measure taken by parliament can be challenged. Accordingly, if parliament approved a law that allows the prime minister to detain someone and arrest him without needing to explain its justification, it is not possible to object to this law in court on the argument that it contradicts fundamental rights; rather the judge himself is compelled to rule by it, by virtue of the subjection of the judiciary among them to the laws of parliament. This is entirely the opposite of what is in America, since it allows every citizen to challenge any law he regards as contrary to human rights. But despite that, it has never happened that the British parliament implemented a law the people regarded as a violation of civil rights, and therefore demanded its cancellation on that basis¹ — whereas America has encountered that more than once, as parliament issued a law the people opposed in the Supreme Court on the basis of its contradicting civil rights, and so the court proceeded to cancel and nullify it.

> ¹ But this is a story of the past. Today, and specifically after the incident of 11 September 2001, the parliament of Britain issued several laws that allowed the government to arrest and detain a person on the basis of an accusation directed at him concerning terrorism.

The British representative chamber has continued to be dominated by two parties (the Conservative Party and the Labour Party), one of which forms the government while the other forms the main opposition. The opposition between them has continued for a long period: if the Labour Party is in government, the Conservative Party must occupy the seats of the opposition, and conversely, if the Liberal Party [sic: presumably the Conservative Party] were [in government], it would in turn undertake its opposition. But all this [takes place] in the shadow of observing the established traditions and continuing customs [p. 131] and the principles of respect for others and tolerance, such that neither of the two sides touches them with violation however severe the opposition becomes and however many the conflicts. From this, Britain has the right to be regarded as the pioneer of the parliamentary system and the leader of the world in it.

Many states have taken to following the parliamentary system, and it is the system preferred by the political parties among us in Pakistan, which seek to imitate Britain in it — despite its being surrounded by a series of contradictions and inconsistencies. Among them is that it grants the opportunity to form the government to the political party that has obtained the confidence of the majority of members in parliament; and this confidence can be obtained only in one of these two ways: that the party wins the majority of seats in parliament, i.e. a percentage of 51% and above. In such a form this party must form that government. This is the case in which the two powers, legislative and executive, are intertwined, since parliament legislates with this party's members forming its majority, while the executive power too is made up of some of those same members. When these two powers have intertwined and merged in this way, the result is that parliament endorses the government's policies and programmes, whatever they may be. For it is parliament that has the right to hold the ruling party to account and review its policies; but since the ruling party has become the voice that is heard and the word that prevails in parliament by virtue of holding the majority of its seats, it is only natural that parliament should endorse the policies its own party is implementing, and not obstruct it or stand as a barrier in the way of achieving them. Thus there remains no path that can be followed to change government policies and its programmes.

The second form: that none of the political parties obtains the majority of votes or seats that would enable it to form the government, such that no party has been able to obtain 51% of the seats. Then the coalition government is formed, meaning that the dominant party in parliament unites with other political parties or independent members who do not belong to any political party. The attempts at union to form the coalition government sometimes result in astonishing situations, where [p. 132] two parties unite that differ vastly in policies, programmes, visions and orientations, after their failure to obtain the majority of seats; so they have no choice but to unite to form the government. How can they accompany each other when one of them faces east while the other has headed west?! Even if they make peace on mutual support and solidarity between them, this too does not result in great benefit and cannot be sustained for a long period, by virtue of the difference of the policies, programmes and plans they follow. So no sufficient solution can be found in such a case except in one of two forms: either that one of them gives up its policies for the interests of the other, or that it proceeds on the principle of "take some and leave some", such that each of them gives up some of its demands. Thus, usually, the wheel of the coalition government cannot be run except by sacrificing some principles and giving up some policies and programmes. Proceeding from this, forming the coalition government takes a long time, and after the process of formation it still remains on the verge of falling and dissolving, as a series of conspiracies begins in its wake to dismantle this government and seek to create a gap between the two parties. Take Italy, for example: 300 governments were formed in it in the period between 1954 and our day [sic], and the reason for that is the change in the state of confidence in parliament over the days and limited periods, alongside the failure of political parties to win the majority of seats. Conditions with them deteriorated to the point that a government had hardly been formed before the movement of withdrawal of confidence began to confront it — which is regarded as sufficient to eliminate the prime minister's government even if it is not based on a serious and acceptable accusation. All it amounted to was that the members' preference had shifted to another person, so they tabled a motion of no confidence in the prime minister, which ultimately brought down his administration and toppled his government.

But Britain has been able to continue the parliamentary system with all precision and solidity, owing to the factor of awareness and political insight which the British people possessed, which we can appreciate through the fact that Britain has no written constitution; its affairs are run only according to customs and habits, yet there is no conception there of violating those customs and contradicting them!

### The presidential system in America

In contrast to the parliamentary system in Britain, America follows the presidential system, which differs in [p. 133] its form, its characteristics and its features greatly from the parliamentary system. The first difference between the systems of the two states lies in the fact that while the constitution of America is federal, Britain has a unitary one. Second, Britain does not give the principle of separation of powers — which is regarded as one of the most important characteristics of democracy — its due, as we discussed in detail a little earlier: that the two powers, legislative and executive, are intertwined in it to some extent; while America still clings to it tenaciously, since it classified the powers into three powers, each power regarded as entirely independent of the other two powers. The three powers are: the executive power, headed by the head of state; the legislative power, represented by Congress, which consists of two chambers: (1) the House of Representatives and (2) the Senate; and the third power: the judicial power, entirely independent of the first two powers.

The head of state is the head of the executive apparatus, and the legislature or parliament does not elect him; rather he is elected by a system surrounded by much ambiguity and complexity. First, people in each state elect several members forming a body known as the electoral body (Electoral College), which is made up of representatives of each state; the number of members of the electoral body for each state is equal to the number of that state's representatives in Congress. (America is regarded as a federal state comprising fifty-two [sic: fifty] states, so there must be representation of each state in the election of its president, this being one thing; and because the American president actually enjoys extremely important powers, which naturally requires that his election not be by the opinion of every Tom, Dick and Harry from the rabble and riff-raff of the people, but that he be elected by men with qualifications and virtuous competences from each state — from this source the idea of indirect election in appointing the president of America emerged.)

It is stipulated for whoever stands for the office of the American presidency that he be an American citizen, aged 35 years, who has lived in America for at least 14 years. When [p. 134] such a man is elected as head of state, all executive responsibilities fall on his shoulders by virtue of his being head of the executive apparatus, and hence he enjoys wide powers. He appoints the council of ministers — in contrast to what passed in the British system, that the party winning the majority of seats forms the government and appoints the prime minister, who appoints the council of ministers from among the members of parliament. The presidential system does not require that a member of the council of ministers be a member of Congress as well; rather the president appoints for his council of ministers whom he wishes, whether a member of Congress or not. That person then becomes director of the government department whose ministry has been entrusted to him. These are not called ministers among them; they only call them secretaries: among them the secretary of the interior, another of foreign affairs, another of the treasury and transport and others. The secretaries of these government departments in America are equivalent to ministers in other states, and the collection of these is called "the council of ministers" [the Cabinet], without its having those powers it enjoys in the parliamentary system, as the ruling body that runs most government policies and implements its laws by consultation among its members. Rather its standing in the presidential system does not go beyond being equivalent to a consultative council for the president, while the president has the right to exercise the right of veto (Veto power) over its decisions, and to take the decision by his opinion alone — meaning that the council of ministers, if it unanimously agreed on an executive matter contrary to the opinion of the president, the president may reject their opinion and rule by his opinion alone. It is thus established that the president has the supreme authority in the executive affairs of the state, and he is the one who appoints the leaders and senior officials in the civil and military fields; but in enacting laws and policies he needs to present them to Congress and obtain its ratification of them.

The American president is regarded as head of both the state and the executive apparatus, in contrast to the parliamentary system concerned with distinguishing between the presidency of the state — which is occupied by the head of state — and the presidency of the government — which is occupied by the prime minister.

The manner of removing the president and the prime minister also differs in the two systems, parliamentary and presidential. [p. 135] In the parliamentary system the prime minister is removed if the parliament agrees by majority on a decision of withdrawal of confidence from him. Indeed Britain has gone further: if the prime minister proposes a bill to the representative chamber and did not succeed in obtaining its agreement to it, but the majority of the chamber rejected it, his failure is equivalent among them to the withdrawal of the members' confidence in him. Hence it is incumbent on him from the moral point of view to resign from his office; if he does, [well and good]; otherwise he and his council of ministers are regarded following that as removed and stripped of the presidency, even if there was no coordinated movement for withdrawal of confidence from him. So it is not necessary for removing him from his office that he have committed a crime or engaged in treason or done anything else; rather merely the majority of members' showing withdrawal of confidence from him, explicitly or implicitly, suffices to topple his system and depose him from the seat of government. The presidential system, by contrast, never rules the president removed merely by Congress deciding to withdraw confidence from him. (For his election did not take place on the basis of the opinion of Congress, but by the direct vote of the people, according to the detail just given.) Rather, the removal of the president among them has a special method, namely that if the president becomes exposed to accusation of negligence, treason or violation of the constitution and the like, then in such a case the constitution allows a movement of impeachment (impeachment) to be undertaken against him. In detail: the majority of the votes of Congress, if they issue a decision that his trial be conducted by the Senate, the Senate undertakes it in its capacity as the supreme court (and in the presence of the Chief Justice of the Supreme Court of the United States as its president). The president is informed of the charges directed at him, and given the opportunity to explain his position on them; and after hearing his position and the testimonies against him, the Senate decides the case: if two thirds of the members of the Senate vote for his conviction, it rules that he be removed from his office; otherwise it declares that the charges directed at him are not correct, and he remains president of the state. One case of impeachment of the president has occurred throughout America's history, and it too did not result in the removal of the president, on the grounds that the charges against him were not correct. [Tr.: In fact two presidents had been impeached and acquitted by the time of writing: Andrew Johnson (1868) and Bill Clinton (1999).] There are decisions to which the Senate agreed against the vice-president [sic: no vice-president has been removed by impeachment] and judges, so they were removed following that from their positions; as for the head of state, it has never happened that he was removed from his office as a result of charges directed at him. Nixon almost faced this ugly fate had he not submitted his resignation himself.

[p. 136] The upshot of what we have presented is that the administration of the government, the management of foreign policies, and the declaration of war — all these responsibilities are entrusted to the American president, and from this he is regarded as possessing extreme power and might; yet in all his policies and laws he is responsible before Congress, in the sense that he needs, in their implementation, their adoption by Congress.

The legislative power in America (called Congress) is divided into two chambers:

**(1) The lower chamber:** it is called the House of Representatives, which consists of 435 members, and the representation of these members is subject to the number of inhabitants in each state; they are elected for a period of two years only, and it has the same powers that the lower representative chamber usually has, of imposing taxes and fees and enacting the laws relating to them.

**(2) The upper chamber:** it is the Senate, which consists of one hundred members. Each state has two members representing it regardless of the size of the state and the number of its inhabitants. The period of service of each member is six years, and they are elected on the basis of voting by every adult citizen. The American system differs greatly from the British system regarding the upper chamber, since the British House of Lords has come to be an organization that no longer has a large role in government or state policies; rather it has become "a discussion forum", to the extent of what some have said about it. The American Senate, by contrast, enjoys wide powers not matched by any other senate in all the states of the world. Usually its members are elected on the basis of their qualifications and abilities, and the general public gives this aspect in electing them more attention than in electing the members of the House of Representatives. In enacting laws it has powers equal to the powers of the lower chamber, except legislation in imposing taxes and arranging the budget, as in other states of the world, where it is established among them that this power is exclusive to the House of Representatives, not the Senate. Many states see no need for presenting the budget to the upper chamber and obtaining its adoption. On the other hand, it is the only body that ratifies the appointment of the ministers and heads of departments whom the head of state recommends for occupying federal leadership positions, civil or military; and [p. 137] no one is officially appointed except after the ratification of the Senate. It is also the only body that approves or rejects all treaties concluded by the United States of America.

There is another important aspect in the American system, namely that while Britain regards parliament as the highest official authority — in the sense that the decisions parliament has agreed to are not subject to any other authority, and no court can review, cancel or nullify that law whatever it may be — in America the courts are regarded as the body that has the authority to consider any legislative works and executive orders and declare their conformity or contradiction with the constitution. This is what is called judicial review; it is an additional right granted to every citizen to undertake objection to any law issued by Congress in the Supreme Court.

There is another feature by which the American system is distinguished from its British counterpart, and this feature concerns political parties. As you have noticed, political parties have a large role and effective activities in the administration of government in Britain, since the head of the majority party undertakes the formation of the government; whereas in America the president is elected directly, and political parties have no interference in forming the government. So political parties do not have a large role; rather their task is confined to registering candidates and carrying out mass propaganda campaigns for elections. It is not necessary that the helm of government be steered by the party with the majority in Congress; rather it is possible — and has actually happened — that the president belongs to one political party while the majority of members in Congress are from another party. The president is also not a member of Congress, nor is he obliged to attend its meetings; consultations between them take place by correspondence. But nevertheless, in such a critical situation the divisions and conflicts between the president belonging to a political party other than the party that has obtained the majority in Congress do not rage to a degree that obstructs the course of government or stands as a barrier before its activities.

The reason for that is that the method of the members of Congress and their manner differ entirely from the members of parliament in the parliamentary system. For the member of parliament in the parliamentary system is completely compelled [p. 138] to submit to the policies of his party and its programmes, without deviating from it a hand-span, whether in expressing opinion or casting his vote; whereas the member in Congress can express what is in his conscience and express his opinion even if it departs from the party to which he belongs and its policies. Rather, the representatives link their connection with their electoral districts more than with their political parties; from this they take account of the interests of their districts more than the interests of their parties, and are concerned with the policies they see as in the interest of the state in general, whoever their proposer and presenter may be. From this it is no longer necessary that if the president is not from the party dominant in Congress he will face problems and obstacles, just as there is no conception of opposition like what is prevalent in the parliamentary system.

### The parliamentary system in France

The third system that I would like to undertake a passing look at to introduce is the parliamentary system of France. It has passed before us that the credit for strengthening democratic principles and consolidating its pillars in Europe goes only to France and its revolution arising from the source of the ideas of its three famous philosophers, which first produced the birth of the idea of liberal democracy, and second its consolidation and influence in the other European states. But despite its role, France continued to suffer constitutional conflict for a long period, which resulted in the laying down of many constitutions over short periods. The reason for that was that at first they disputed over the type of constitution — whether it should be parliamentary or presidential — until their choice settled on the parliamentary constitution like that in force in Britain. This account goes back to before 1958 CE: all executive authority was in the hands of the prime minister, while the head of state's presidency was only symbolic and ceremonial, like the queen in Britain, and lacked authority and powers to the extent that he no longer could use his powers in which he was regarded as independent without obtaining the attesting signature of the prime minister or one of the ministers. From this it became famous in describing him that he needed attestation in everything except the request for his resignation — meaning that he could not execute anything without [p. 139] its being attested by the signature of the prime minister or one of the ministers, except his resignation: it took effect merely by the executive head's acceptance of it, even if not accompanied by an attesting signature.¹ Just as another anecdote became famous: that the queen in Britain, even if she had no authority, at least enjoys royalty; and the president of America enjoys power without royalty; while the president of France is deprived of both: he neither enjoys wide authority like his American counterpart nor enjoys the privileges of monarchy as the queen in Britain has.

> ¹ It is the system that Zulfiqar Ali Bhutto followed in Pakistan following his assumption of the reins of power there in 1973; the president at the time was Chaudhry Fazal Elahi.

At any rate, this parliamentary system prevailed in France for a long period. But it is known that the parliamentary system is built on the distinction between the presidency of the state and the presidency of the government; from this it can be applied in a good manner only if the powers and authorities of both presidents are balanced. If one of them possesses the authorities and uses the powers on a wide scale while the other is deprived of them except for a minimum, that results only in bitter rivalries and continuing rifts between the two offices. The situation in France was like that, until de Gaulle took up the reins of the presidency of the state, and saw that the source of anarchy and constitutional collapse in the state was nothing but the lack of balance between the powers of the two presidents, since the presidency of the state had remained a merely ceremonial post, and the president had only to see what the prime minister implemented of policies without criticizing or opposing them. So the idea of laying down a new constitution was born in his mind, and he actually undertook it in 1958, striving with his effort to create balance between the two powers. Instead of presenting his project in the National Assembly, he put it before the people for a general referendum, and the results showed that it enjoyed wide popular support. It has remained the constitution in force in the state from 1958 until this day of ours, and is called the constitution of balance and equilibrium ("checks and balances").

He included in it something of the merits of both systems, presidential and parliamentary: in its origin and spirit it is a parliamentary constitution, emphasizing that the government is formed by the political party with the majority in [p. 140] parliament; but it did not leave the president neglected and abandoned either; rather it granted him something of powers and authorities too. First, it established concerning him that he is regarded as head of the state and of the states subordinate to it (which are the African states that France occupied, and Algeria and many territories were of this type; France later withdrew from them, but some of them are still under its control and occupation, and hence they are subject to its system and its constitution), and the president of the Republic is elected by the members of parliament. [Tr.: Under the 1958 text the president was chosen by an electoral college of parliamentarians and local councillors; since 1962 he has been elected by direct popular vote.] Among the powers the new constitution granted him is that he presides over the meetings of the council of ministers. From all this he came to have interference and influence in running the executive apparatus as well.

On the other hand, if he saw in a law issued by parliament a defect or deficiency, and then felt the need to amend it, he can send it again to parliament for review, just as he can present it to the Constitutional Council to undertake examining the law and declare the extent of its conformity or non-conformity with the spirit of the constitution.

The president has also been delegated authority in appointing the commanders of the military forces and some heads of civil government departments, without his needing in that the counsel of the executive head; and he was also granted the right to dissolve the National Assembly if he saw it deviating from the constitution — and if he consulted the executive head in that, that is his right, but such consultation is not obligatory for him. He was also granted other supreme powers: if the Republic is struck by a state of emergency and exposed to danger to its security and stability, he may take whatever steps to resist that situation he sees as appropriate and sufficient to guarantee the peace of the Republic.

These, then, are the differences between the systems of Britain and France, and between the French presidential system and its counterpart in other states, embodied in delegating many executive matters to the president of the Republic. As for other matters relating to politics and government, the system of France in them resembles the systems of other states: the legislature consists of two chambers, and between the two chambers are the same ties and connections as between them in other states.

### The Swiss system of government

[p. 141] The fourth state whose system of government I wanted to acquaint you with is Switzerland, whose system of government differs from the systems of all states of the world entirely. Researchers and authors in political science have declared that democracy in all its meanings and import is in force only in Switzerland. It is a state small in area, perhaps not exceeding the area of the province of Sindh in Pakistan, and its inhabitants number no more than five to six million souls. This small state is divided administratively into 22 [Tr.: 26 today] units, each called a canton, and each canton enjoys self-government, with its own local assembly and special council of ministers.

The inhabitants of this small state belong to three nations (*aqwām*): German (71%), French (21%) and Italian (6%). As for religion, 57% belong to the Roman Catholic Church, 41% to the Protestant Church, and 0.5% are Jews. From this its inhabitants differ from one another in every way in terms of ethnicities, religions and languages; but they did not divide the cantons on the basis of language, ethnicity or religion; rather they divided the state into 22 administrative parts, and from their collection they formed a federal republic in which political authorities are divided between the central government and the governments of the cantons (the states). It is also built on the basis of clear distinction among the three powers: legislative, executive and judicial. As for the executive power, it is run by a federal council of ministers consisting of seven members whom the legislature elects for a period of four years. They are a substitute for the head of the single executive body, and this council elects from among its members a president for one year only, to preside over its meetings throughout the year; but beyond that his authority is equal to the authority of the other members. Usually in electing them an attempt is made, as far as possible, that they be from the various cantons and belong to the different ethnicities and religions. These seven are the ones who run the wheel of the executive apparatus for the whole Republic; but since it is divided into 22 states, each of which enjoys broad self-government, [p. 142] federal affairs are few among them and the burdens on the federal council are light. To observe the principle of separation of powers, no sooner are these seven elected as members of the federal council than they are deprived of their seats in the National Assembly; but they are compelled to obtain the assembly's ratification of their policies and plans, so they attend its meetings without having in them the right to vote.

Women too were granted the right to vote in elections some years ago; before that, women did not enjoy this right.

Among the most important features of the Swiss system: the popular referendum on issuing many laws and ratifying government policies and programmes. Accordingly referendums are numerous among them, and there are matters on which the government must undertake a popular referendum before concluding them, such as amending the constitution for example, while in some matters it is optional and not obligatory.

Likewise, if one hundred thousand persons propose holding a referendum on the constitution, the government must then undertake it.

This is the situation that the constitution of the Republic imposes; as for external reality, the people often does not know its president, since he is elected for one year and followed in the next year by another; and whoever has tasted the sweetness of this presidency usually does not wish to ascend its seat another time, because of the grave responsibilities it involves, alongside the paltry financial and material benefits he receives in return for this service.

Usually this president does not enjoy the pomp and might that the presidents and members of government in other states have, to the extent that the minister is not allowed to put the flag of his political party on his house or his car, nor do ceremonial rites accompany his reception. One of the citizens told me this during a visit I made to it a few months ago: that they have no conception that the minister is accompanied by his servants, his entourage of guards and others; he lives only as one of the subjects. From this people were seized with astonishment and bewilderment when they saw Yasser Arafat and with him

[p. 143] a company of the army!

In truth, this is among the teachings of Islam and its guidance to the ruler: that he should mingle with the subjects and live as one of them, so that his might and pomp do not cause harm, injury and discomfort to the subjects. This is what these people have followed while they are unbelievers, whereas Muslim rulers have cast it behind their backs!

### Democracy weighed in the balance

We have presented earlier a brief introduction to the various political schools and many of the political systems prevailing in this age of ours. You noticed, at the end of the discussion of democracy, its basic ideas, its philosophy, the institutions arising from it, and the kinds of governments that appeared and took shape as a result of activating it and putting it into effect.

Here we single out — from among those systems — secular democracy for commentary only, since it has become — after the other political systems failed in practical reality and no longer remain in force — the ideal modern political point of view and the best system of government and authority, or so it is said and held in the world today — to the point that it has been said: "It is impossible for there to occur any system of government better than it." A book has recently been issued by a senior official of the American State Department entitled "The End of the History and the Last Man" [Tr.: Francis Fukuyama, *The End of History and the Last Man*], whose summary is that history — having passed through the stages of development and progress — has reached its end; and that after the discovery of secular democracy first, and the production of liberal democracy second, no better system than them can exist or be born.

This is nothing but like Karl Marx's prophecy of the eternity of socialism and that it is the final developed system for the world, which would not be followed by a better or more ideal system.

The same expression has come to be used for democracy and capitalism today, as they — especially after the fall of the Soviet Union in Russia — have begun to claim that democracy and capitalism in the fields of politics and economics have actually achieved a brilliant victory, such that it has become impossible for [p. 144] any other system to rival them.

So let us study this system with precision and close attention, to discover whether there is anything of solidity and reasonableness in it, or whether it is merely a false illusion and a counterfeit reality?

There is no doubt that democracy has contributed considerably to spreading and activating many principles that strengthened the freedom of the individual and his independence to some extent; it has also contributed not a little to rescuing humanity from the persecutions and afflictions it suffered at the hands of individual governments and despotic authorities that prevailed before it in the world. We also grant here that it gave the individual human being freedom of opinion and thought, after dictators had stripped him of it, and brought him out of the state of stagnation and constriction he had lived in under those tyrannical, despotic authorities.

But despite all that — if we examine closely its intellectual structure and its theoretical foundations, we find that they are not built on serious, sober theories; rather, they are nothing but the product of a reaction to the man-made systems that ruled humanity unjustly, relying on what they had invented of erroneous ideas and values.

It is known that the West was ruled by dictatorial governments in most of its past ages, as was explained in detail. Whatever of them was formed in the name of religion or on religious bases was subject to theocracy, with all the evils that beset it, which I explained earlier. As an example of that, take Roman theocracy: it was bare of any spiritual and religious foundation; rather, nothing was implemented in it except the personal opinions of the Pope as religious rulings, since they believed him immune from error and free of mistakes — which naturally caused the destruction of the rights of the people and unfairness to them. So the reaction to that was that the advocates of democracy threw the yoke of religion off their necks entirely, and founded it on the principle of delegating absolute sovereignty and supreme authority to the people. This becomes clear from the fact that democracy is a translation of the English word (Democracy), meaning "rule of the people". From here the principle of the authority of the people emerged. Then, when they granted sovereignty to the people and believed that there was no [p. 145] authority above its authority, it was natural that they should regard it as free in its affairs and matters, not subject to any heavenly law. Hence they resorted to tying and linking this principle to secularism, so democracy and secularism became inseparable, such that liberal democracy cannot be applied without secularism. In sum, the meaning of democracy is that religion has no connection with the affairs of the state; rather it is a personal matter of the individual relating to his individual life, while the affairs of authority are to be conducted without submission to religion and its teachings.

The first thing taken against this idea is that rule, for it to be conceived, needs both its ends: the ruler and the ruled together. When it regarded the whole people as ruler, and it is granted that the ruler requires the existence of a ruled under him, here a question arises in the mind of every rational person: what then remains to play the role of the ruled and occupy its function? The land of the state and its buildings, its inanimate things and the plants that grow in it?! Delegating rule to the whole people results in nothing but the pervasive anarchy and absence of government with which running the state's system becomes impossible; for the existence of two rulers in one region leads to corruption and ruin — so how if the whole people were rulers?!

The saying has become famous in defining democracy: "Government of the people by the people for the people", i.e. it is the rule of the people by the people and for the people. This indicates that the people itself is ruler and ruled at the same time — which is nothing but a sophistry that cannot be valid on any standard; for it is self-evidently impossible for the one person to be ruler and ruled and an instrument of delegating rule at one and the same time. So with this detail the whole foundation of democracy — I mean the principle of rule for the people — has been undermined.

The democrats have answered it that the people is ruler in the sense that at the time of voting it voluntarily elects for itself a representative from among the candidates; the representatives collectively become rulers, while the rest of the people remains ruled by them.

But we say: first, it is not a true representation, as we shall clarify shortly, God willing. Second, if we look at it precisely we find it to be the rule of a small group of the people, not its whole nor its majority; for the choice of the people and its sovereignty are confined to the period [p. 146] of elections only; then sovereignty and authority all pass to the representatives and members of parliament, and the majority of the people becomes in their hands entirely stripped of choice. So it is not correct, then, to regard it as the sovereignty of the majority of the people.

Further, sovereignty according to the scholars of politics is that a person should have absolute authority and power, disposing by it in the affairs of the state and in determining right and obligation as he wishes, such that he is not subject to anyone besides him and there is no other authority above it. In view of their definition of it, then, how can the sovereignty of the people be straight, while the authority of the representatives and the members of the legislature is above it?!

Second, after delegating complete authority to the people, another question arises in the mind of the researcher himself: what is democratic government aiming at, or what is the hoped-for purpose behind establishing democratic government? The scholars of politics are bewildered in answering this question, and more than one of them has tried his hand at defining and specifying the aim of democracy; but all of them were unable to find a satisfying, convincing answer to this question. Rather, they offered nothing that satisfies. One said: "Government itself is the aim sought from democracy." This is merely an opinion and pure theory, which we do not see as worthy of comment. Another said: "The ambition of democracy is to satisfy the majority of the people, and to provide comfort and spread ease in it; for the people does not issue a ruling except if it sees it as harmonious and consistent with what it desires."

But we say: satisfaction and comfort are entirely relative matters. What satisfies some people may be considered, in relation to others, disliked and not preferred; and among things are those that ease some people and gladden them while at the same time causing the disturbance and anxiety of others. So what rule, then, is applied in preferring the satisfaction of some and giving precedence to their comfort over the satisfaction and comfort of others?

The democrats have no convincing answer to this question except to resort to making the rule of the majority the arbiter, saying that its satisfaction is given precedence over the satisfaction of others.

But we say: it contradicts the requirement of democracy itself, since the whole people has not obtained what it desires; and second, it entails that a vice be included among the aims [p. 147] of democracy if the majority of the people is pleased with it. That alone is enough to show its blindness and the ugliness of its error.

The last idea in this regard — and it is the most accepted in modern democratic circles — is that it aims at protecting the rights of the people.

This idea is refuted and falsified by the fact that there is no regulating criterion defining the rights of the people that they aim to protect under democracy. Nor do they have any refuge in that either except the principle of regarding the rule of the majority of the people — to which they have often resorted, yet it did not avail them and benefited them in nothing, little or much. So they said: "What that majority sees as among the rights of the people, democracy sets itself to protect; and what it does not, it does not."

We say here too what we said about the previous ideas: that delegating the definition of rights to the majority of the people results only in their becoming a target with which the whims of that majority play, without there being a complete definition and full conception of the rights that democracy sets itself to protect. Often the people today sees something as a right, so it strives to obtain it or defend it; then when tomorrow comes, that same majority soon rejects it and refuses to count it among the rights worth striving for.

At any rate, you will not find among its alleged aims and declared goals any effort to spread good and deter evil, and spread virtues and remove vices from human circles; likewise you will not find among them a sound criterion nor a balanced measure for estimating good and evil. Rather, they now assert — in a way that exceeds all bounds — that good and evil are relative matters, so it is possible — in view of this theory — for the goodness and evil of things to differ with the difference of time, place and environment. So a matter that was once all evil has become, by virtue of this arbitrary philosophy, possible to regard today as all good, and likewise conversely.

Proceeding from this, we see the West sliding into the torrents of indecency and sexual perversion, a pervasive slide the like of which it did not witness under the individual governments and Christian theocracy. The situation has deteriorated to the point that there remains no vice of the most evil, nor [p. 148] act of the most hideous, but it has been granted a certificate of legitimacy to be practised under democracy, or at least voices have been raised demanding it be permitted legislatively. All of that because democracy has thrown the yoke of religion off its neck entirely, and rejected following moral values categorically, and the whim of the people has become the prominent factor and the sole element among them in legislation and law-making.

The most hideous example of that is what the British House of Commons issued as a decision permitting sexual perversion [homosexuality] legislatively under thunderous applause, which was followed by some Western states recognizing same-sex marriage as legitimate and lawful.

The issuing of the aforementioned decision by the British House of Commons has an astonishing and bewildering story, whose summary is that at the beginning a disagreement occurred among the members of parliament concerning this decision, since not all of them were in agreement with this barbaric decision. To settle the disagreement between the two parties, a committee was formed named the "Wolfendern Committee" [sic: Wolfenden Committee], whose task was to learn the direction of public opinion, and especially of the thinkers and experts among them, and then to present to the House a report on the basis of which a proposal would be adopted by the House in this regard. The committee prepared a report containing clear lessons for these people deluded by democracy; the gist of what came in it: "Although sexual perversion is an evil and a vice among vices, we find ourselves in agreement with issuing this decision, since we have not founded our system on moral principles and values; rather we have granted the people all freedom in what it adopts for itself by way of legislation and law-making. So the framework of both legislation and morality and their domains have become entirely different, since morality is a personal matter, while legislation is a social phenomenon that goes along with public opinion regardless of moral values. So long as no attempt is made to equate sin with crime, our hands are tied in the face of legislation that follows public opinion, whatever it may be."

In the light of this committee's report, the House of Commons of Britain took a decision of the legitimacy of sexual perversion; then the United States followed in its footsteps. Following that, associations were formed [p. 149] dedicated to calling for this hideous vice and spreading this idea among the masses openly before the eyes of witnesses. The men among them are called (Gay), meaning fun and recklessness, i.e. that they are cheerful in this hobby of theirs; and the women among them are called (Lesbian), meaning: a woman engaged in sapphism.

There is another association by the name of "Swap Union", meaning linguistically: exchange, by which is meant wife-swapping; it has special clubs. It raises its voice against the law that prohibits wives from having a sexual relationship with a man other than her husband, since it was legislatively forbidden because it causes the destruction of the husband's right.

As a result of all this, most of the inhabitants of Europe and the United States — or at least a large number of them — have become of unknown lineage, as is clear from statistics published by several states, while other states have not published their statistics. A few days ago the famous newspaper "Time" published an article in which the writer expressed his anxiety about this outrageous phenomenon — but not from a moral or religious perspective, but from the perspective that it causes economic problems, in that children of unknown lineage do not find anyone to provide for them and bear their expenses.

Likewise women have begun to raise their voices against the law prohibiting abortion, and have begun demanding its legitimacy. It has actually been permitted in some states, and in others these calls find a suitable and paved atmosphere from which it is known that it will soon be permitted legally.

Previously indecency was forbidden by law; today it has smashed all restrictions and has become prevalent and widespread such that there is no barrier preventing the raging torrent of films and obscene images that are broadcast throughout the world, including the Islamic states within it. How could that be forbidden legislatively, when by it the goal of democracy, which lies in pleasing the people and feeding its whims, is achieved?!

In sum: there is no evil among evils but democracy has moved to legitimize it under the sovereignty and dominion of the people, which has led to the destruction of the family system and the uprooting of the seeds of family ties of marriage and childbirth entirely.

[p. 150] Among what makes one appreciate how far the situation has worsened in their overstepping of human limits: when they were afflicted with the torment of AIDS — whose main cause is regarded as illegitimate sexual contact, of sodomy and fornication, according to what specialist doctors are agreed on — they refused to take heed and desist from their errors and follow the path of chastity. Rather they persisted in remaining on their conduct, and began practising sexual relations under more caution and prevention, and undertook preparing educational courses for practising sex and offering its practical education through television and through curricula in all universities. To prevent infection with AIDS, they placed in the universities and colleges populated by unmarried people of both sexes vending machines that dispense condoms to whoever of them wishes to practise sex. While I speak to you of this I find a motive of modesty and distaste holding me back from it; but despite that I have related it before you to remove the veil from the face of the truth. So thus they tried to treat the disease from the point where they were afflicted, and strove to prevent it by directly engaging in the causes of infection with it — how far, how far is that for them!

What astonishes and bewilders us is that America still suffers from the occurrence of cases of rape of women in numbers exceeding all other states, despite opportunities to practise sex with the woman's consent being available with all freedom and without any obstacle confronting it in the way of achieving it; and prostitution is prevalent to the point that it has become a commercial profession with its own companies and special clubs. Yet nevertheless the rise in the rate of cases of forced fornication in this state and this permissive society is an astonishing matter for which unaided human reason can find no explanation.
