> ¹ *Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim* (1/69), Faith, chapter on forbidding wrong being part of faith, hadith no. 78 – (49).
> ² *Majmaʿ al-Zawāʾid wa-Manbaʿ al-Fawāʾid* (7/274), [chapter on one who fears harm to others and to himself], hadith no. 12169.
> ³ *Al-Fatāwā al-Hindiyya* (5/353), Book of Disapproved Matters, chapter seventeen.

[p. 182] of complete authority, absolute sovereignty and full influence. So it is not permissible for him to content himself with admonition and advice only; rather he must put an end to the wrong by using all his authorities and his apparatuses.

Since the government is the one charged with changing by the hand, which is the highest of the ranks of commanding right and forbidding wrong, the Qur'an mentioned them among the purposes of government, its aims and its duties. Accordingly, they form a great basis for legislation and the administration of authorities in Islamic government.

As I mentioned more than once, secular democracy is empty-handed and devoid of the absolute fixed criterion that estimates right and wrong for it and distinguishes good from evil for it. So no value of life remains among them that enjoys being good and right absolutely, fixed forever. Rather its standing is subject in every age and city to the opinion of the majority of people in it: whatever they approve is right whatever it may be, and whatever not, not! Even if it were granted that they too have some fixed principles and values that do not accept change — like non-violence as a principle, respect for the human being, and equality between man and woman, and others — we say: they are ambiguous terms that have not yet been defined with a consistent definition, and their explanations and interpretations differ from time to time. So nothing remains among them, then, that could be regarded as good absolutely and permanently.

As for right and wrong from the Islamic point of view, they are of two kinds: a kind that divine revelation — recited or otherwise — has specified and defined and expressly stated; this remains in its character through the passage of ages and the succession of eras without there being any possibility in it of change with the change of time or place or people or anything else. The second: what revelation has not expressly stated; rather its matter was entrusted to the social conscience of the Muslims — and it is that in which the current saying came: "Whatever the Muslims see as good is good with God, and whatever they see as bad is bad with God."¹ And although the hadith scholars regarded it as mawqūf [i.e. a saying of a Companion, not of the Prophet] on Ibn Masʿūd, may God be pleased with him, and said: the chain by which Ibn al-Jawzī narrated it in *al-ʿIlal al-Mutanāhiya* has in it a fabricating narrator, so it carries no weight — the jurists and the scholars of uṣūl nevertheless usually mention it as a saying of the Prophet ﷺ, perhaps because it is not something attainable by analogy, since it is unlikely that

> ¹ Narrated by Aḥmad and others from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd, may God be pleased with him, as mawqūf, no. 3600 (*Musnad Aḥmad*, al-Risāla ed., 6/84); and Aḥmad said in the chapter on the night prayer of the month of Ramaḍān of his *Muwaṭṭaʾ* [sic: presumably Muḥammad al-Shaybānī, in his recension of the *Muwaṭṭaʾ*]: narrated from the Prophet ﷺ.

[p. 183] the Companion said it merely by his own opinion; rather he must have heard it from the Prophet ﷺ. The scholars of the Umma have agreed on accepting it as an accepted principle, whose scope of operation is: the matters not expressly stated in the Book and the Sunna. As for the matter on which these two basic sources, or one of them, have stated expressly that it is right or wrong, the Muslims all together will not be able to change its condition and move it from one category into the other.

So it has become clear from that that Islam has divided matters in terms of their being good and evil into two parts:

**The first:** what of them is absolute good (Absolute virtue) or absolute evil; they are the matters concerning which a text has come in the Qur'an or the Sunna. So they remain forever in the character which the Qur'an or the Sunna specified for them, of being good or evil, right or wrong; there is no room for human reason to legislate concerning changing their position. That is like the obligation of prayer and the prohibition of wine, pork, usury and so on.

**The second:** the permissible matters concerning which no text has come from the Book or the Sunna; rather the Sharia has entrusted that to the Umma all together, to undertake in every age, through consultation among its children, to choose what is most beneficial for it and most fitting for its condition. Whatever they approve of these matters is good, and whatever they do not approve is not. That is like the ways of construction and building, agriculture and irrigation, traffic rules and the instruments and equipment of war, and other matters in which preference may differ, and the specification of what is most appropriate among them, according to the difference of the situations of a time and the requirements of a place and the psychology of human generations. In this category fall the matters in ruling on which the Sharia has taken human customs and traditions into account.

This makes clear the difference between Islam and democracy: democracy has entrusted the ruling on all things to human reason, to choose in every age by its majority what it sees as appropriate for it and achieving its interests; whereas Islam — as is its way — has put matters in their proper places: it enacted explicit legislation in the matters that are beyond the grasp of human reason, and which would be likely sites of slip and error for it if it were left to itself in ruling on them — this on one hand. On the other, it did not bind it with explicit rulings in the matters that fall within the scope of its experience; rather it gave it free rein [p. 184] in them to choose by consultation what it sees as appropriate to it and to its circumstances and interests.

#### 5. Establishing justice and fairness

The fifth function of Islamic government in the light of the teachings of the Qur'an is: establishing justice and fairness. God the Exalted said to David, upon him be peace: **"O David, indeed We have made you a vicegerent upon the earth, so judge between the people in truth and do not follow [your own] desire, as it will lead you astray from the way of God"** [Ṣād, 38:26].

So He commanded David, upon him be peace, in his capacity as a vicegerent, to judge among people in truth — which indicates that judging among the subjects in truth and establishing justice and fairness among them is among the duties of Islamic government and the aims sought from it. Judging in truth covers observing justice and fairness both in judicial rulings and administrative matters together.

It is necessary to draw attention here to what we have presented more than once: that justice and fairness are two terms which each person can explain according to what he desires. But the truth is that justice in the terminology of the Qur'an is nothing but what God and His Messenger regarded as justice. As for the interpretations of justice other than that which clash with the rulings of the Sharia, they are not justice; rather injustice, aggression and the following of desire only. From this God the Exalted mentioned — in the same verse — the following of desire in the context of contrast with judging in truth, and drew attention to its leading astray from the way of God.

So the judicial ruling, if it clashes with a ruling of God's rulings and contradicts it, is not justice; rather it is injustice and tyranny, as God, glorified and exalted, said: **"And whoever does not judge by what God has revealed — then it is those who are the wrongdoers"** [al-Māʾida, 5:45].

He commanded the Prophet ﷺ to judge with equity (*al-qisṭ*), saying: **"And if you judge, judge between them with equity. Indeed, God loves those who act equitably"** [al-Māʾida, 5:42]. After that He commanded him to judge by what God revealed, saying: **"So judge between them by what God has revealed and do not follow their inclinations"** [al-Māʾida, 5:48] — which indicated that judging with equity is nothing but judging by [what] God [revealed], and whatever is other than that is only the following of desire.

The gist of the discussion: you do not see a government among governments but it openly claims [p. 185] to establish justice and equity and fulfil their requirements according to its own invented notions of them. The truth is that justice and judging in truth and equity and fairness — all these words convey one meaning, namely: that the ruling be in accordance with the rulings of God the Exalted which He revealed Himself in the Qur'an, or the Prophet ﷺ taught them, so they reached us by way of his hadiths.

As an example of that let us take democracy: it does not cease claiming that it strives to establish justice and fairness, and that all are equal before it in the implementation of the law on them without difference between one and another. But it is a claim contradicted publicly by the observed external reality: at the same time there are many democratic societies that still grant the head of state, by an exception in the constitution, that he not be tried nor be a litigant at all in any court. So what justice is this, then?! But they still defend this position, which contradicts all the requirements of justice, and do not regard it as anything of injustice!

It also clearly and explicitly contradicts the concept of justice from the Islamic point of view; for Islam does not reserve for anyone — head of state or other — the right to exception from the law, as the Prophet ﷺ said: "Those before you were destroyed only because when a noble among them stole, they would leave him, and when a weak one among them stole, they would apply the ḥadd to him. By God, if Fāṭima the daughter of Muhammad stole, I would cut off her hand."¹

The radiant Islamic history is full of many cases in which the head of state was taken to court and the judge ruled against him without fear or regret. Among that is what is reported of ʿAlī, may God be pleased with him, that the judge Shurayḥ ruled against him — while he was Commander of the Faithful — in favour of a Jewish dhimmī. So let us leave history to relate to us this splendid story with its astonishing details. Wakīʿ narrated in *Akhbār al-Quḍāt* with his chain from Shurayḥ; he said: "When ʿAlī, may God be pleased with him, set out to fight Muʿāwiya, he missed a coat of mail of his. When he returned, he found it in the hand of a Jew who was selling it in the market of Kufa. He said: O Jew, the coat of mail is my coat of mail; I did not give it away nor sell it. The Jew said: It is my coat of mail, and it is in my hand. He said: Between me and you is the judge.

> ¹ *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (4/175), before the Book of Merits, hadith no. 3475; *Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim*, Ḥudūd, hadith no. 4378; *Jāmiʿ al-Uṣūl*, hadith no. 1879.

[p. 186] He said: So they came to me. ʿAlī sat beside me and the Jew in front of me. He said: Were it not that my opponent is a dhimmī, I would have sat level with him in the session; but I heard the Messenger of God ﷺ say: 'Belittle them as God has belittled them.' [Tr.: so in the source.] Then he said: This coat of mail is my coat of mail; I did not sell it nor give it away. So he said to the Jew: What do you say? He said: It is my coat of mail, and it is in my hand. Shurayḥ said: O Commander of the Faithful, do you have evidence? He said: Yes — al-Ḥasan my son and Qanbar testify that the coat of mail is my coat of mail. Shurayḥ said: O Commander of the Faithful, the testimony of the son for the father is not permissible. ʿAlī said: Glory be to God! A man of the people of Paradise — his testimony is not permissible? I heard the Messenger of God ﷺ say: al-Ḥasan and al-Ḥusayn are the two lords of the youth of the people of Paradise. The Jew said: The Commander of the Faithful brought me before his judge, and his judge rules against him! I bear witness that this religion is in the right, and I bear witness that there is no god but God and that Muhammad is His servant and His messenger, and that the coat of mail is your coat of mail, O Commander of the Faithful. It fell from you at night. He set out with ʿAlī fighting with him at al-Nahrawān, and was killed."¹

The presentation of this splendid model of observing the requirements of justice, and of trying the commander and the judiciary ruling against him, was not confined to the era of the Rightly-Guided Caliphate; rather it continued and followed in the Islamic governments after it too. Among that is what al-Kindī narrated in *Kitāb al-Wulāt wa-Kitāb al-Quḍāt* with his chain from ʿUbayd Allāh; he said: my father told me, he said: "When the black-clad ones [the Abbasids] came, they returned Khayr ibn Nuʿaym to the judgeship. ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān came to him in a dispute with a cousin of his, and sat on his [the judge's] carpet. He said: Get up [and sit] with your cousin."²

Likewise the Caliph al-Manṣūr's wife sued him before the judge Ghawth ibn Sulaymān, and she appointed an agent for herself; when the time of the ruling came, the judge asked the Caliph to sit level with the wife's agent in the session, and the Caliph's answer was nothing but submission, as is reported in history: he came down from his carpet and sat with the opponent.³

Add to that that in most modern democratic empires the head of state has been granted the right of pardon for criminals and of reducing their punishments whatever their crime,

> ¹ *Akhbār al-Quḍāt* (2/200), what was narrated from Shurayḥ the judge from the Musnad.
> ² *Kitāb al-Wulāt wa-Kitāb al-Quḍāt* of al-Kindī (1/257).
> ³ *Kitāb al-Wulāt wa-Kitāb al-Quḍāt* of al-Kindī (1/271).

[p. 187] to the point that he is not constitutionally obliged to disclose its justification. We can estimate the extent of the suitability of this situation to the question of justice and fairness if the court sentenced a murderer to execution by hanging, for example, after examining the necessary testimony, taking the required measures and observing the established judicial rules, and the higher courts — the court of appeal and others — upheld it, consuming many years and the skills and time of government officials, and exorbitant judicial fees and expenses were spent in the way of arriving at it. When the matter reached its end and the criminal was about to receive his punishment, the head of state surprised [everyone] by using his authority to pardon the criminal and cancel his punishment — disappointing the hopes of the victim's family and nullifying the labours of judges and lawyers. By God, tell me: what justice is this and what fairness is that?!

Islam never acknowledges this principle, as is clear from the hadith which we mentioned part of earlier; and here it is for you in its text with Imam al-Bukhārī as follows: "From ʿĀʾisha, may God be pleased with her, that Quraysh were concerned about the matter of the Makhzūmī woman who had stolen. They said: Who will speak about her to the Messenger of God ﷺ? They said: Who dares [to do so] except Usāma ibn Zayd, the beloved of the Messenger of God ﷺ? So Usāma spoke to him. The Messenger of God ﷺ said: 'Do you intercede concerning one of the ḥudūd of God?' Then he stood and gave a sermon, then said: 'Those before you were destroyed only because when a noble among them stole, they would leave him, and when a weak one among them stole, they would apply the ḥadd to him. By God, if Fāṭima the daughter of Muhammad stole, I would cut off her hand.'"¹

Add also that most of the states belonging to secular democracy implement on all citizens, in disputes and family matters, unified laws without taking into account the schools (*madhāhib*) they follow and the religions they profess. So a great number of those who inhabit those states — including the Muslims — are compelled to act by what does not agree with their school and law in the individual affairs of marriage, divorce, inheritance, bequest and so on. That is nothing but a disgrace and stigma on the brow of secularism, which loudly proclaims that every individual is permitted to follow whatever schools and religions he chooses! How then does the poor man act according to his school while he is fettered in the restrictions of following the man-made family laws of the state absolutely, even if they are contrary to the teachings of

> ¹ *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (4/175).

[p. 188] his school?! What justice is this — or is it injustice and aggression?

Whereas Islamic government is obliged to permit every citizen to act, in these individual affairs, by the schools he believes in and the religions he follows; so the following principle was established among them: "Leave them and what they profess."¹

The upshot of this question is that non-Muslim citizens in the Islamic state are obliged to follow its general laws only. As for the individual affairs and private matters related to a person's religion and his creed, they are not obliged in them to follow the laws of the state; rather they are entirely free in them to follow the laws of their schools to which they belong. This is the true justice that Islam presents, unlike what secular democracy merely claims!

> ¹ *Al-Taqrīr wa-l-Taḥbīr ʿalā Taḥrīr al-Kamāl ibn al-Humām* (2/310), question of specifying the Sunna by the Sunna.

### Authority is a responsibility, not a right!

Authority (*al-imāra*) in the light of the teachings of the Qur'an and Sunna is a responsibility that weighs heavy on the shoulders of whoever undertakes its burdens, and not a right worth striving and expending effort to obtain. The Prophet ﷺ said: "The imam is a shepherd and is responsible for his flock."²

Hence naturally a person should deal with it as with other responsibilities: to be wary of it and to try to escape from it as far as he can. But if it comes to that and he is compelled to accept it and undertake its burdens, let him devote his effort to performing it and carrying out its functions as a heavy trust and a great responsibility, not as a source of pride, distinction and honour.

Among what came in the hadith is that Abū Dharr, may God be pleased with him, showed his desire for authority before the Prophet ﷺ; so the Prophet ﷺ said to him: "O Abū Dharr, you are weak, and it is a trust, and it is on the Day of Resurrection a disgrace and regret — except for one who takes it by its right and fulfils what is upon him in it."³

> ² *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (2/5), chapter on the Friday prayer in villages, hadith no. 893.
> ³ *Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim* (3/1457), chapter on the dislike of authority without necessity, hadith no. 4683.

[p. 189] In a version narrated by Imam Abū Dāwūd, the Prophet ﷺ said to him: "O Abū Dharr, I see you are weak, and I love for you what I love for myself; so do not take command over two, and do not take charge of the property of an orphan."¹

Abū Hurayra, may God be pleased with him, narrated from the Prophet ﷺ that he said: "You will be eager for authority, and it will be regret on the Day of Resurrection; how excellent is the wet-nurse and how evil is the weaning one."²

The meaning is that authority at first pleases a person and delights him; but when a person is held to account for it in this world or the Hereafter, he discovers the gravity of its position and the weight of its responsibility. From this the Prophet ﷺ said to al-Miqdām ibn Maʿdīkarib, may God be pleased with him: "You have succeeded, O Qudaym, if you die and have not been a commander, nor a scribe, nor a chief (*ʿarīf*)."³

So look at the great difference between what the Qur'an and the Prophet ﷺ say — that authority is an enormous, grave responsibility, not a bed of roses but a bed of thorns and briars — and between what people today are disposed to regarding it: that whoever obtains it has been granted a great happiness that brings him its benefits and realizes his interests for him; and so deserves the warmest congratulations and the brightest words of blessing, while the one denied this great good fortune is the one to be pitied. How far apart are the two ideas!

The Rightly-Guided Caliphs, aware of the gravity of the position, trembled with fear of falling short in performing the duties of this responsibility, as is reported of ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him, that he said:

"If a camel died lost on the bank of the Euphrates, I would fear that God would ask me about it."⁴

Shortly before his death, when people began to praise him, he said: I wish that I came out of it even, neither against me nor for me.⁵

> ¹ *Sunan Abī Dāwūd* (3/114), chapter on what came about entering into wills, hadith no. 2868; *Jāmiʿ al-Uṣūl* (4/56), hadith no. 2036.
> ² *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (9/63), Book of Judgements, chapter on what is disliked of eagerness for authority, hadith no. 7148; *Sunan al-Nasāʾī*, Book of the Pledge, what is disliked of eagerness for authority, hadith no. 4216.
> ³ *Sunan Abī Dāwūd* (3/131).
> ⁴ *Al-Ṭabaqāt al-Kubrā*, Dār Ṣādir ed. (3/305), mention of the appointment of ʿUmar as successor.
> ⁵ *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (5/16), Book of Merits, chapter on the story of the pledge, 3700.

[p. 190] Indeed, al-Ṭabarī narrated in his history that a man proposed to him at that time that he appoint his son ʿAbd Allāh as successor. He was angry with him and said to him:

"Woe to you! How could I appoint as successor a man who was unable to divorce his wife [properly]!"¹

He continued, saying:

"It is enough for the family of ʿUmar that one man of them be held to account and asked about the affair of the Umma of Muhammad. As for me, I have exerted myself and deprived my family; and if I escape even, neither burden nor reward, I am indeed fortunate."²

> ¹ An allusion to what happened with ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar once, that he divorced his wife while she was menstruating.
> ² *Tārīkh al-Ṭabarī* = *Tārīkh al-Rusul wa-l-Mulūk*, and *Ṣilat Tārīkh al-Ṭabarī* (4/228), the story of the Shūrā.

---

## Chapter Three: A Study of the Issues of Forming a Government from an Islamic Perspective

### Introduction

In this chapter we would like to set out the basic principles that Islam has granted concerning the formation of the government that will aim at its goals and perform its functions mentioned a little earlier. We shall study in it many of the issues that relate to the aforementioned heading from an Islamic perspective.

### 1 – Islam's position on seeking authority

Since it has become clear from what we mentioned at the end of Chapter Two that Islam sees authority as a grave responsibility and not a right, it does not permit a person to seek authority and ask for it and exert his efforts and spend his attempts in attaining it. As is indicated by the prohibition in a hadith which Imam al-Bukhārī included in his *Ṣaḥīḥ* from Samura ibn Jundab, may God be pleased with him, that the Messenger of God, God bless him and give him peace, said to him: "Do not ask for authority; for if you are given it as a result of asking, you will be left to it [alone]; and if you are given it without asking, you will be helped in it."³

> ³ *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (8/127), Book of Oaths, 6622.

[p. 191] A hadith has also come concerning seeking the judgeship, which is like an explanation and clarification of this hadith mentioned above, namely what al-Tirmidhī included from Anas, may God be pleased with him, as marfūʿ [attributed to the Prophet]: "Whoever seeks the judgeship and asks for intercessors in it is left to himself; and whoever is compelled to it, God sends down upon him an angel who guides him aright."¹

So this hadith indicated that the meaning of "he is left to himself" is that whoever obtains these posts and reaches these ranks by seeking and attempts on his part through seeking intercession for them and other things, God does not help him nor support him in performing the tasks of his post in anything; rather He entrusts his affair to himself, so that it takes him to whatever watering place it wills and leads him astray in whatever valley it wills! May God protect us from it. It is a severe threat, the full severity of which — and hence the seriousness of this man's predicament — can hardly be grasped except by one who believes in God, glorified and exalted, and believes that a person does not conclude a matter nor accomplish a work except if success accompanies him in it and help from Him, mighty and exalted, goes with him in it.

It is narrated in another hadith from Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī, may God be pleased with him, that he said: "I entered upon the Prophet ﷺ, I and two men of my people. One of the two men said: Make us commanders, O Messenger of God; and the other said the like of it. He said: 'We do not appoint over this one who asks for it, nor one who is eager for it.'"²

Here is another hadith which al-Bukhārī included from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr, may God be pleased with him, that the Messenger of God ﷺ said: "And you will find among the best of people the most intensely averse of them to this matter until he falls into it."³

So these hadiths indicated clearly and explicitly that asking for authority and seeking office are not permissible, and that it makes whoever asks for it and aspires to it a person unfit for it.

> ¹ *Sunan al-Tirmidhī*, ed. Shākir (3/606), Book of Judgements, hadith no. 1324; *al-Sunan* of Abū Dāwūd, Book of Judgements, hadith no. 3578; *Musnad al-Imām Aḥmad*, hadith no. 12184.
> ² *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (9/64), chapter on what is disliked of eagerness for authority, hadith no. 7149.
> ³ *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (4/196), Book of Merits, hadith no. 3588.

[p. 192] While some held the permissibility of asking for authority and seeking office, arguing by what the Noble Qur'an mentioned of Joseph, upon him be peace — that he sought it from the king of Egypt, saying: **"Appoint me over the storehouses of the land. Indeed, I will be a knowing guardian"** [Yūsuf, 12:55].

The matter went further with some, in that they argued by it — in view of Joseph, upon him be peace, having sought it and expressed his competence and fitness for it by explaining his qualities and qualifications — that it is permissible for a person today to stand as a candidate in elections and disclose before people what he has of qualities, virtues and qualifications, so as to draw them to cast their votes for him at the time of balloting and voting.

The answer to this argument is from two aspects:

**The first of them:** that Joseph, upon him be peace, came to know of the king's resolve to appoint him — on the king's own initiative, not at Joseph's request — to one of the posts of government; he knew this from the king's words which God mentioned just before this verse: **"And the king said: 'Bring him to me; I will appoint him exclusively for myself.' And when he spoke to him, he said: 'Indeed, you are today established [in position] and trusted with us'"** [Yūsuf, 12:54]. So when he knew that the king was determined on accomplishing that resolve of his, the question remained about specifying the post which he would entrust to him. So at this stage he asked him to make him over the storehouses of the land. So it is not correct, then, to say that he asked for office or post in the meaning that the Sharia has forbidden; for he did not send the king a request from prison asking to be appointed to a post, nor did he spend money for it or seek intercession for it. So how then is it correct to argue by it for the permissibility of what goes on today in political circles at the time of elections?!

**Second:** what we presented of the prohibition of asking for authority and seeking office is the original ruling of the Sharia, while the Sharia itself permitted seeking it in some special exceptional cases, as we shall clarify shortly, God willing. So it is possible to interpret the action of Joseph, upon him be peace, as having been in that exceptional case in which seeking office is permitted.

There is a hadith by which some may argue for the permissibility of seeking office, namely what Abū Dāwūd narrated in his *Sunan* from Abū Hurayra, may God be pleased with him, from the Prophet ﷺ: he said: "Whoever seeks

[p. 193] the judgeship of the Muslims until he obtains it, then his justice prevails over his injustice, for him is Paradise."¹

But we say: leaving aside what is in its chain from the words of the hadith scholars, it can also be interpreted as that exceptional case in which the Sharia permits seeking office.

The detail of that is that the hadiths have conflicted in this issue: some of them show that asking for authority is not permissible — and it is what we presented earlier; and among them is what indicates its permissibility — and it is the hadith we mentioned just now. The scholars of the Sharia have put both these kinds into effect by reconciling them: that the principle is the prohibition of asking for authority and the prohibition of seeking office. But perhaps there arises a case in which a person feels with certainty that if he did not seek office, those who are not competent in it would take charge of it, which would result in usurping the rights of the Muslims and wronging them. So in such a case it is permissible for him to ask for it, on condition that he have a desire in it for establishing the truth, and fear that someone undeserving will take it on, and that his intention not be status and display. This is the position which al-Qāḍī Abū Yaʿlā al-Ḥanbalī (may God have mercy on him) held in *al-Aḥkām al-Sulṭāniyya*, and a group of the later scholars supported it, among them ʿAllāma Ẓafar Aḥmad al-ʿUthmānī (may God have mercy on him) in his valuable book *Iʿlāʾ al-Sunan*.

The upshot of what we presented is that in the general run of cases it is not permissible to seek authority; and excepted from it is when it has become incumbent on a person to seek it, namely if he fears that if he did not seek it, it would go to one who does not deserve it nor is qualified for it, which would lead to corruption and the loss of the rights of the Muslims. So in this exceptional case it is permissible for him to seek it with the aim of reform and establishing the truth, not of obtaining status and rank.

### The alternative form to candidacy in elections

The foregoing detail has raised in turn a question in the mind of every reader: that since it is established that seeking authority is a matter not permissible in the Sharia in the general run of cases, what then will fill the place of candidacy for parliamentary membership, on which the electoral structure in this age of ours stands? For if there is no candidate, then in favour of whom will the voting be, and how?

> ¹ *Sunan Abī Dāwūd* (3/299), chapter on the judge who errs and is correct, hadith no. 3575.

[p. 194] Before going deep into answering this question, it is fitting for us to draw our attention to an important point which may remove the veil from our eyes in many issues that have a connection and similarity with this issue of ours, namely that whenever a system or method has prevailed on the international scope and found currency and acceptance in all or most of the world, it becomes rooted in people's minds such that they do not look to other than it — what could be made an alternative that substitutes for it and takes its place in its work; rather they see it as not worthy of thinking about and not capable of implementation at all. Whereas if they freed themselves in thinking about it from the influence of the environment in which they live, they would find it as capable of implementation as its original, with all ease and simplicity.

Candidacy for elections is likewise. First, it is not universal throughout the world; rather there are several other formulas which — even in this age of ours — flourishing states have adopted for elections with ease and success: in one state there is the system of proportional representation; in another two-round voting (Second ballot); and in another: the transferable vote. So it is not correct, then, to regard the prevailing method as a necessity whose place nothing else can fill at all. Rather, if it were rooted in the mind once that seeking authority — besides its being impermissible in the Sharia — is an evil that has brought down our political system, then offering an alternative to it would no longer seem as difficult as people imagine.

We propose here an alternative to the system of contemporary candidacy, which can be adopted with ease on one hand, and which will eliminate the social and economic evils that human society faces as a result of it on the other. Its detail: that the inhabitants of every electoral district be asked to nominate in their turn, to represent them in the membership of parliament, persons possessing specified qualifications for membership: scholarly attainments, social service, practical experience, moral conduct and so on. Let the elections committee undertake to specify a special number — of 500 or 1,000 or other — so that if the number of nominators from the inhabitants of an electoral district in favour of one of the candidates reaches that specified number, let the committee review whether this nomination is genuine or falsified, through examining and discovering several matters: do the nominators

[p. 195] actually inhabit that electoral district or not? Did they truly reach the specified required number or not? Is the person whom they nominated qualified to stand in the elections, having the required qualifications, or not? After ascertaining these matters, let the committee accept his candidacy papers for membership. So if those for whom the nomination reached the specified number were more than one, then for choosing one of them let it call people to vote and ballot in favour of one of these.

Instead of the candidate himself undertaking to call people to cast their votes — which usually drags him into self-praise, false promises about his future after success, disparaging the competitors among the other candidates, and spending exorbitant sums in that, as is prevalent today — let the elections committee itself undertake the task of publicizing them and introducing them to the voters using the media of all kinds, and let it arrange interviews and meetings for them if it feels a need for them. Thus people will become acquainted from close by with the candidates in their electoral districts with a true acquaintance not mixed with praises and claims; and their scholarly qualifications, and their conduct in their past, and the extent of their understanding of the matters of their present and the issues of their people, and their being adorned with the noble qualities of truthfulness, trustworthiness and so on.

As for today, the candidate does not content himself with deceiving people and raising their hopes with false promises only; rather he spends on publicizing himself and introducing himself to the voters exorbitant sums and vast amounts of money. So its consequence was that it no longer remained possible for anyone to stand as a candidate except one who has surplus of these exorbitant sums beyond his needs on one side; and on another side, the one who spends it usually sees it as a profitable investment that yields him, after winning, more than what he spent, by fraud and embezzlement of state funds.

In view of this, in our prevailing political environment and system it has become almost impossible for truthful, trustworthy men of sound thought and sincere intention to ascend the thrones of government through elections.

From this I am confident that if this proposed alternative to the system of contemporary candidacy were accepted, and publicity were carried out by the elections committee alone, without allowing anyone

[p. 196] to use its prevailing widespread means, it would go a long way towards eliminating the evils we have just mentioned.

On the day of balloting, let it likewise be entrusted to it — not to the candidates — to run the camps outside the polling stations from which voters collect their papers, so that the last door is closed through which public opinion could be negatively influenced by the mechanisms of pressure on voters, encouragement and inducement.

The system (CONVINCING) [sic: canvassing] has actually been abandoned in some states; rather they have come to content themselves, after registering the names of the candidates, with conducting interviews and dialogues with them and broadcasting them on the visual and audio media only, without conducting election campaigns and meetings, nor blackening the walls of the public and distorting them with advertisements and banners full of praise and false promises.

It will also serve, as a successful alternative system, to enable the people to exercise their right to vote with full freedom and independence — a right the feudal lords had taken from them, so they became subject to them and to their wills, such that no role remained for them in voting except using their fingers like a machine programmed to put the ballot paper in the special ballot box only, without that being by consent from them and voluntarily!

What adds insult to injury is what has prevailed in our country of the single-member system, in the sense that from an electoral district — regional or national — only one candidate is elected, represented by whoever obtains the largest number of votes even if they are very few relative to all the voters in that district. An example of that: if the candidates from an electoral district were ten persons, and nine of them obtained a thousand each, while one of them obtained two thousand votes, he is regarded as the winner, even though the opinion of most voters in that district goes to the opposite of it. By contrast, if wide electoral districts were taken, and the system of multi-member representation were chosen — that from a large district four members are elected according to their ranks in obtaining votes, whether by the transferable votes or otherwise — that would go a long way towards ending this evil.

[p. 197] We repeat here what we presented earlier: that Islam in the field of politics contented itself with legislating basic principles only, and entrusted the forms of their application and the formulas of their practical implementation to the scholars of the Sharia and those expert in it in every age and city. So the principles of Islam in the field of forming the government are embodied in these two principles: the prohibition of seeking authority, and founding it on consultation (*shūrā*). But it gave them flexibility that makes them fit to keep pace and go along with every age and every time. So there is scope for the scholars of the Sharia and the people of insight among the Muslims to elaborate on the alternative we have just proposed and to work out more of its details and particulars in the light of their seasoned experiences and their expertise in the conditions of contemporary politics.

At any rate, it is clearly not impossible to present a practical alternative capable of implementation for the system of candidacy in contemporary elections, which agrees with the principles of the Sharia on one side and is free of the evils that are born of the prevailing system on another side.

### The conditions and qualities that qualify a ruler for authority

Modern democratic systems usually do not specify special descriptions and conditions that must be present in the head of the country or the members of the legislature; rather they usually content themselves in that with what has been written in the constitutions: that he be a citizen of the country, holding its nationality, registered by name in the list of voters with their conditions, including that he has reached a certain age. They do not concern themselves with other conditions and qualities such as scholarly qualifications, practical experiences, moral conduct or anything else. Rather obtaining the largest number of votes is the only element among them that qualifies a person for the presidency of the country. From this we have often seen an ignorant person of bad character and conduct who ascended the throne of the presidency merely by his obtaining the majority of the voters' votes; so he becomes head of state even though his immersion in immoral activities is no secret; indeed, stories of it continue to circulate openly long after he has left office. Take for that, for example, John Kennedy, the first American president who made Russia surrender to his policies: the stories of his interest in sex and his immersion in its activities are still printed and prevalent to this day.

[p. 198] Whereas Islam is distinguished among those systems in that from the very beginning it attends to specifying conditions and descriptions that must be present in a person who intends the presidency of the country, and does not permit anyone to take charge of the presidency of the country and the authority over the Muslims as long as those conditions are not present in him and he has not obtained those descriptions. The scholars have in this subject extensive studies and long discussions; we spare you their trouble by presenting a brief summary of them in what follows, God willing.

#### The first condition

That he be of sound mind, adult, with sound senses.

#### The second condition

That he be a Muslim. That is because God regarded polytheism as a great wrong, saying: **"Indeed, association [with God] is great injustice"** [Luqmān, 31:13]; and He mentioned in another verse that the wrongdoer does not attain God's covenant latent in the authority over the Muslims and the government of their country, saying: **"My covenant does not include the wrongdoers"** [al-Baqara, 2:124]. The upshot of this is that the polytheist does not attain the covenant of God, glorified and exalted, at all; so it is not correct to make him Commander of the Faithful and ruler of their country.

It is self-evident that the Islamic state must have a Muslim head; because government from an Islamic perspective has its special functions and duties, and the aims which Islam seeks from establishing it — and these are matters and works which it would be futile to expect an unbeliever to perform and carry out properly.

Despite its being a self-evident matter, those who have wrapped themselves in the cloak of democracy and have come to look at all matters with a democratic view only have raised doubts and objections about it too, as was witnessed at the time of laying down the constitution for Pakistan. For the clause that means stipulating Islam for the office of the presidency of the country was among the clauses that stirred up discontent and poured out anger in democratic circles, as being an injustice to the non-Muslim citizens. These doubts are nothing but the offspring of the democratic and nationalist ideas (*al-afkār al-dīmuqrāṭiyya wa-l-qawmiyya*) which were discussed

[p. 199] in detail earlier. But the matter at which the researcher's wonder never ceases is that many states — and they are among the first generation of the pioneers of democracy and the vanguard of its champions — have actually stipulated for the head [of state] the embrace of a particular school and belonging to a particular religion. Take Britain, for example: it has provided in its constitution that its headship is held only by one who belongs to the Protestant sect of Christianity. How astonishing! A state that has taken the slogans and garments of democracy, like Britain, provides in its constitution for a clause that contradicts the principles of democracy in an explicit contradiction and clashes with them in a clear clash! For democracy calls — and its principles require — that it allow the headship of the country to be held by any person whatever his religion. Britain contradicts it by restricting it with restrictions: that he be a Christian, and specifically of the Protestant sect, and in particular one who has a connection with the Church of England!

There are many other states — like Argentina, Colombia, Costa Rica and Malta — which claim to follow secular democracy while they have actually stipulated for their head that he be among the followers of the Roman Catholic Church. Denmark, Norway, Iceland and Sweden stipulate that he belong to the Lutheran Church. As for Greece, it did not lag behind others in this; rather it undertook and stipulated for its head that he be of the school of the Orthodox Church (Orthodox Churches). Likewise Israel committed itself to stipulating Judaism for its head, and the Kingdom of Nepal to stipulating Hinduism.

So if we presented these examples to the democrats, they would disown them by justifying that these states are not purely secular; rather they are states built on religious foundations.

So we say to them: just as these states mentioned above have not stipulated any religion for the post of head of the administration represented by the prime minister — rather they have allowed everyone to hold it regardless of his religion and school — reasoning that stipulating a particular religion for the prime minister means injustice to the citizens not belonging to that religion; this same reasoning can be used to argue for the prohibition of stipulating a particular religion for the head of state as well. For if it is granted that stipulating a particular religion

[p. 200] for the prime minister is an injustice, then by what is it taken out of the realm of injustice for the head of state?!

Further: if it is permitted among them for a state built on religious foundations to stipulate for its headship a particular religion, how much more so Islam, which declares with all clarity that the state is Islamic and its government an Islamic government built on religious foundations and principles — that it should naturally stipulate that its head belong to the religion of Islam and embrace its teachings! From this it was provided in the constitution of Pakistan — after fierce discussions in the days of laying down the constitution — in its capacity as an Islamic state, that Islam is stipulated for both the head of the country and the prime minister. So praise and thanks be to God for that.

#### The third condition: being male

The third condition for the head of the country is that he be male, not a woman. The evidence for that is the saying of the Prophet ﷺ: "A people who make a woman their ruler will never prosper."¹ He ﷺ said it when it reached him that the people of Persia had made the daughter of Chosroes queen over them.

It is narrated in another hadith which al-Tirmidhī included from Abū Hurayra; he said: the Messenger of God ﷺ said: "If your commanders are the best of you, and your rich are the generous of you, and your affairs are [decided by] consultation among you, then the surface of the earth is better for you than its belly. If your commanders are the worst of you, and your rich are the stingy of you, and your affairs are [handed] to your women, then the belly of the earth is better for you than its surface."²

In addition to that, the Sharia regards authority as an imamate, as was detailed earlier. The imamate is divided into two kinds: the greater, which is this, and the lesser, which is the imamate in the prayers. The scholars are agreed that the woman is not fit for the lesser imamate (i.e. to become an imam for men in the prayer); so how could it be said that she is fit for the greater imamate, with what it has of gravity and standing?!

It is a question on which the opinions of the scholars of the Umma have agreed, such that I do not know of any person

> ¹ *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (6/8), chapter on the letter of the Prophet ﷺ to Chosroes and Caesar, hadith no. 4425.
> ² *Sunan al-Tirmidhī*, ed. Shākir (4/529), chapter 78, hadith no. 2266; and he said: This is a gharīb hadith.

[p. 201] among them who disagreed. But some attribute the saying of its permissibility to the Sage of the Umma, Shaykh Ashraf ʿAlī al-Thānawī (may God have mercy on him), relying on a fatwa of his within his collection of fatwas called *Imdād al-Fatāwā*. But it does not in fact go beyond being a fallacy in which these people have become entangled by error in their understanding of his fatwa. The detail of that is that His Eminence Shaykh al-Thānawī (may God have mercy on him) agreed in it with the majority of the Umma that the woman is not fit for the imamate of the Muslims; but he permitted her being prime minister, with the reasoning that the prime minister in the prevailing democratic system is not the head of the country and the holder of its affair in reality; rather he is part of the legislative council around which authority revolves in the democratic system. So since he denied that this post is regarded as the headship of the country and the obtaining of complete authority, he therefore permitted a woman to hold it.

The upshot is that his disagreement was not in an agreed Sharia matter; rather in a matter related to external reality. So in it there is room for talk and latitude for disagreeing with him; for the prime minister, even if he is not the head of the country, is nevertheless the head of administrative affairs and the executive authorities. My respected brother, His Eminence Shaykh Mufti Muhammad Rafi Usmani, may God preserve him, has an extended article in which he has investigated this question thoroughly, which is printed and in circulation.

They have called today to the philosophy of equality between man and woman with force and intensity, and have given it currency and acceptance in contemporary human society, such that anyone who opposes it simply makes himself a target for the well-aimed arrows of those who imitate secularism blindly and sympathize with it foolishly. So when we speak of the woman's unfitness for authority, we know precisely that those deluded by the propaganda of renewal will attack us and stir up doubts and suspicions about this question. But instead of plunging with them into a fruitless debate, we would like to content ourselves here with relating a story that happened to me: a woman journalist representing the famous American magazine *Time* visited me, without prior notice, to interview me. She asked me to answer her questions on the question of the woman's headship and her fitness for that. So I said to her: it is futile for me to reply to your questions on this subject while you do not possess a sound idea nor a correct conception of government and its gravity

[p. 202] and of the woman and her standing in Islam. She was astonished at that and perplexed, and refused to go away until she heard from me the detail of this statement of mine. So I said to her: as for your conception of government, it is wrong; because you look at it as a privilege on which a person's happiness and prosperity turn; so the happy one among you is whoever obtains it, and the deprived is whoever is denied it. Hence if it were granted concerning someone that he will never obtain it, you would have the right to regard him as a person whose right has been usurped and whose benefit has been seized. As for Islam, it does not look at it as a privilege and an interest, but as a grave responsibility, such that whoever is burdened with it deserves compassion and sympathy. As becomes clear from what the Prophet ﷺ said to al-Miqdād [sic] ibn al-Aswad: "You have succeeded, O Qudaym, if you die and have not been a commander, nor a scribe, nor a chief"¹ — since he tied success to keeping far from this responsibility, not to obtaining it and bearing it!

Then I related to her the story of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, may God be pleased with him, when it was suggested to him — while in the illness of his death — that he appoint as successor his son ʿAbd Allāh; he said: "It is enough for the family of ʿUmar that one man of them be held to account and asked about the affair of the Umma of Muhammad. As for me, I have exerted myself and deprived my family; and if I escape even, neither burden nor reward, I am indeed fortunate."²

So if it were granted that it is not a bridal bed of roses but a bed of thorns and briars — not a necklace with which one is adorned but a collar with which one is strangled — then by God tell me: which of the two kinds is more fitting to be regarded as happy: the one who risks being strangled by this noose, or the one who is safe from falling into this net?! Indeed, in view of the responsibility and gravity of government in Islam, we may rightly say that Islam has done women a great favour by keeping them safe from becoming entangled in this responsibility.

As for your idea of the woman's equality with the man, which drives you and your like to strive to achieve it, it is also wrong; because you look at the woman with the same view with which you look at the man, and you do not see any difference between their functions and the purposes of their life; while merely reflecting on the creation of the woman and her constitution will reveal the matter clearly before you, that God created her for the purpose

> ¹ *Sunan Abī Dāwūd* (3/131).
> ² *Tārīkh al-Ṭabarī* = *Tārīkh al-Rusul wa-l-Mulūk*, and *Ṣilat Tārīkh al-Ṭabarī* (4/228), the story of the Shūrā.

[p. 203] other than that for which He created the man. As for Islam, it sees her as a basis for civil life, beginning with her role in forming a secure, calm family from which many family bonds are born, which multiply so that from them clans, tribes and peoples are formed. Undermining this foundation by removing the woman from her original functions for which God created her results only in the scattering of these bonds and the destruction of the family system — a destruction the West faces today and can find no way to repair, despite the anxiety it causes.

No sooner had she heard the words than astonishment took hold of her entirely. Since I had made clear to her before that I would not allow her to publish this dialogue of mine except in full, and that if it were cut I would reserve the right to sue her, it seems she has not published it in the magazine she represented to this day. At any rate, the West has stumbled in defining the functions of the woman and the purposes of her life, as some of the men of thought from the West have also acknowledged, among them Gorbachev, the last president of the Soviet Union; for he said in his book *Perestroika* that although the West, by taking women out of the home, gained some economic benefits, this has utterly destroyed its family system. The question of how to bring them back home has become widespread in its circles, in search of a convincing answer — for which perhaps no way remains!

#### The fourth condition: being from Quraysh

The evidence for that is what ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar, may God be pleased with them both, narrated from the Prophet ﷺ; he said: "This matter will remain in Quraysh as long as two of them remain."¹

What Muʿāwiya, may God be pleased with him, narrated from the Messenger of God ﷺ that he said: "This matter is in Quraysh; no one shows enmity to them but God throws him on his face, as long as they establish the religion."²

Likewise Abū Hurayra, may God be pleased with him, narrated that the Prophet ﷺ said: "People follow Quraysh in this matter: their Muslim follows their Muslim and their unbeliever follows their unbeliever. People are [like] mines;

> ¹ *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (4/179), chapter on the merits of Quraysh, hadith no. 3501.
> ² *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (4/179), chapter on the merits of Quraysh, 3500.

[p. 204] the best of them in the Jāhiliyya are the best of them in Islam, if they gain understanding."¹

What al-Tirmidhī narrated, that a man of Bakr ibn Wāʾil said in the presence of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, may God be pleased with him: Quraysh had better desist, or God will place this matter in a multitude of Arabs other than them. ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ said: You have lied; I heard the Messenger of God ﷺ say: "Quraysh are the rulers of people in good and evil until the Day of Resurrection."²

Jābir ibn ʿAbd Allāh, may God be pleased with them both, narrated from the Prophet ﷺ; he said: "People follow Quraysh in good and evil."³

By these hadiths it has been argued that the commander and caliph is required to be from Quraysh, as becomes clear from the prevailing style in the books of jurisprudence and creeds in research on the subject of the imamate, where they mention it as a condition for the validity of the imamate. Al-Qāḍī ʿIyāḍ (may God have mercy on him) said: "Stipulating that the imam be from Quraysh is the school of all the scholars, and they have counted it among the questions of consensus, and no disagreement in it has been transmitted from any of the predecessors, and likewise from those after them in all the cities. He said: And no account is taken of the saying of the Khawārij and those of the Muʿtazila who agreed with them, because of what is in it of opposing the Muslims."

Al-Ḥāfiẓ Ibn Ḥajar, however, discussed it in *Fatḥ al-Bārī*, the commentary on *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī*; and al-Māwardī mentioned it in *al-Aḥkām al-Sulṭāniyya* in the context of the conditions concerning which the imams differed; and ʿAllāma Anwar Shāh al-Kashmīrī (may God have mercy on him) transmitted in *Fayḍ al-Bārī*⁴, attributing it to *Mawāhib al-Raḥmān*, a report from Imam Abū Ḥanīfa (may God have mercy on him) that it is not a condition. ʿAllāma al-Rāfiʿī (may God have mercy on him) also transmitted that in *al-Taḥrīr al-Mukhtār*.⁵

What appears to me is that this means: if a non-Qurashī took power such that he acquired influence

> ¹ *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (4/178), Book of Merits, hadith no. 3495, 3496.
> ² *Sunan al-Tirmidhī*, ed. Shākir (4/503), chapter on what came [to the effect] that the caliphs are from Quraysh until the Hour comes, hadith no. 2227; *Jāmiʿ al-Uṣūl*, hadith no. 2020.
> ³ *Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim* (3/1451), Book of Leadership, hadith no. 4667.
> ⁴ (4/498).
> ⁵ (1/681), Book of Prayer, chapter on the imamate.

[p. 205] and obtained control and dominance, his imamate would be validly concluded, exactly as it is concluded by seizure. It does not mean that the bodies that appoint the imam and choose the leadership are not obliged to observe this condition in whomever they choose for the imamate.

Some contemporary scholars attributed to ʿAllāma Ibn Taymiyya (may God have mercy on him) the saying that it is not stipulated¹; but I did not find it in any of his books. Rather I found in them texts contradicting it, in which he mentioned what means that Islam did not base rulings on lineage except in several places, in consideration of what is generally the case; and he mentioned among their examples stipulating Qurashī descent for the imam.

As for the Imam of the Two Holy Sanctuaries [al-Juwaynī], he counted it among the points of disagreement and matters for consideration in his book *al-Irshād fī Uṣūl al-Iʿtiqād*, while he spoke about it in *Ghiyāth al-Umam fī Iltiyāth al-Ẓulam* (and it is called: *al-Ghiyāthī*), which he composed at the request of Niẓām al-Mulk al-Ṭūsī and deposited in it splendid discussions on the subject of Islamic politics not found in other books on the subject; and he wrote in a way which indicates that he does not see it as one of the qualities and conditions necessary for the imamate, but requires it only because of the continuous, uninterrupted practice of the Umma.² And likewise ʿAllāma Ibn Khaldūn attributed to al-Qāḍī al-Bāqillānī the saying that it is not stipulated; but al-Qāḍī al-Bāqillānī (may God have mercy on him) himself supported the position of stipulating it in his book *Tamhīd al-Awāʾil* (see pp. 174–373).

Al-Ḥāfiẓ Ibn Ḥajar (may God have mercy on him) spoke at length about it in *al-Fatḥ*, and showed in it the possibility that ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him, did not see it as a condition for the imamate, in view of what Aḥmad included from him with a chain of trustworthy men that he said: If my appointed time comes while Abū ʿUbayda is alive, I will appoint him successor — and he mentioned the hadith, and in it: If my appointed time comes and Abū ʿUbayda has died, I will appoint Muʿādh ibn Jabal as successor — the hadith. Muʿādh ibn Jabal was an Anṣārī who had no lineage in Quraysh.

Similar to that is what al-Ṭabarī narrated in his history from ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him; he said: If Sālim

> ¹ See the commentary of Dr. ʿAbd al-ʿAẓīm al-Dīb on *al-Ghiyāthī*, p. 82.
> ² As he said (*Ghiyāth al-Umam fī Iltiyāth al-Ẓulam* 1/80): "The aspect in proving what we are attempting in that is that those who have passed continued to be convinced that this office is specific to Quraysh, and no one other than Quraysh ever aspired to the imamate…"

[p. 206] the client of Abū Ḥudhayfa were alive, I would appoint him successor; and if my Lord asked me, I would say: I heard Your Prophet say: "Sālim is intense in love for God."¹ And Sālim the client of Abū Ḥudhayfa was not from Quraysh — unless the kinship-bond (*al-ʿaṣabiyya*) is considered for him through clientship (*al-walāʾ*).

Al-Ḥāfiẓ (may God have mercy on him) also did not accept that the practice of the Umma was uniform on this, saying: "Some who were not of Quraysh took the title of caliph, like the Banū ʿAbbād and others in al-Andalus, like ʿAbd al-Muʾmin and his descendants in the lands of the Maghrib — all of these resembled the Khawārij in this, and did not hold their sayings nor follow their opinions; rather they were of the people of the Sunna, calling to it."²

As for those who deny stipulating Qurashī descent, they relied in that on the apparent meanings of hadiths, like what al-Bukhārī included from Anas ibn Mālik, may God be pleased with him; he said: the Messenger of God ﷺ said: "Hear and obey, even if an Abyssinian slave whose head is like a raisin is appointed over you."³

But it does not stand as an argument in the matter; because taking charge of leadership is one thing, and appointing the imam and investing him is another thing. For whoever seizes control of the affair, or takes charge of it at a time when Quraysh lack anyone in whom the necessary qualities are combined — his imamate would be concluded in these two forms by consensus. So it is possible that the hadith refers to one of these forms, [or that it came out as a parable, with the aim of exaggeration in making hearing and obedience obligatory]. At any rate, arguing by it that the Muslims may appoint a non-Qurashī by capability and choice on their part is far-fetched and weak. Admittedly, what we mentioned earlier from ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him, does constitute an argument on the subject; for if he had seen it as a condition he would never have shown his desire to appoint non-Qurashīs.

These deniers answer the hadiths in which came the saying of the Prophet ﷺ: "The imams are from Quraysh" — that it came out as a report (*khabar*), not as a command (*inshāʾ*), in the sense that the Prophet ﷺ did not command taking commanders

> ¹ *Tārīkh al-Ṭabarī* = *Tārīkh al-Rusul wa-l-Mulūk* (4/227).
> ² *Fatḥ al-Bārī* of Ibn Ḥajar (13/119), Book of Judgements.
> ³ *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (9/62), Book of Prayer, chapter on the imamate of the slave, hadith no. 1693; and also chapter on hearing and obedience, hadith no. 7142.

[p. 207] from Quraysh; he only told that they would be from Quraysh. This is as came in another hadith that the Prophet ﷺ said: "The religion will remain upright until the Hour comes, or twelve caliphs are over you, all of them from Quraysh."¹

ʿAllāma Ibn Khaldūn spoke at length on the subject in the *Muqaddima*, and presented there another point of view; we transmit it to you in its text as follows. He said: "The Sharia rulings all must have purposes and wisdoms which they contain and for which they are legislated. We, if we search for the wisdom in stipulating Qurashī lineage and the purpose of the Lawgiver in it, [find that] it is not confined to blessing by connection with the Prophet ﷺ as is well-known — even though that connection exists and blessing by it is obtained; but blessing is not among the Sharia purposes, as you know. So there must then be an interest in stipulating lineage, and it is what is intended by its legitimacy. If we probe and divide, we do not find it except in the consideration of group feeling (*al-ʿaṣabiyya*), by which protection and claim occur, and disagreement and division are removed by its existence for the holder of the office; so the community (*al-milla*) and its people are at ease with him, and the bond of harmony in it is ordered. That is because Quraysh were the group feeling of Muḍar and their root and the people of dominance among them, and they had over the rest of Muḍar might through numbers, group feeling and nobility. So the rest of the Arabs acknowledged that for them and submitted to their dominance. So if the matter were put among others than them, the splitting of the word would be expected through their opposition and their non-submission, and no one else of the tribes of Muḍar would be able to turn them back from disagreement nor bring them against their will; so the community would be divided and the word would differ.

The Lawgiver warns against that, eager for their agreement and the removal of dispute and dispersion among them, so that cohesion and group feeling are obtained and protection is improved — unlike if the matter is in Quraysh, because they are able to drive people with the stick of dominance to what is wanted of them; so no disagreement against them is feared from anyone, nor division, because they are guarantors then of repelling it and preventing people from it. So He stipulated their Qurashī lineage in this office, and they are the people of strong group feeling, so that it be more effective in the ordering of the community and the agreement of the word. If their word was ordered, by its ordering the word of Muḍar was ordered altogether, so the rest of the Arabs submitted to them, and the nations other than them yielded to the rulings of the community, and their armies trod the furthest of lands."²

> ¹ *Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim* (3/1453), Book of Leadership, chapter on people following Quraysh, hadith no. 4669.
> ² *Tārīkh Ibn Khaldūn* (1/244), the *Muqaddima*, chapter twenty-six on the disagreement of the Umma on the ruling of this office and its conditions.

[p. 208] Let it be noted that he used here the word "*al-ʿaṣabiyya*" (group feeling), from which the mind may rush to the nationalist and tribal partisanship (*al-ʿaṣabiyya al-qawmiyya wa-l-qabaliyya*) which the Sharia forbade with the most severe prohibition. But a careful reading of the passage above makes clear that this is not what he intends; by group feeling he means only the authority and influence a group comes to have in people's hearts, so that it carries them to submit to it and to obey it voluntarily from them and with consent.

Some supported this point of view of his by what is narrated from Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq, may God be pleased with him, that he said on the day of the Saqīfa of Banū Sāʿida: "And this matter will not be recognized except for this clan of Quraysh; they are the most central of the Arabs in lineage and abode"¹ — since he did not mention the imamate of Quraysh as a Sharia ruling, but as an interest he saw: that the Arabs will not submit and yield to others than them.

Those who affirm this condition answered that Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq, may God be pleased with him, did not content himself that day with this statement; rather he brought along with it the text "The imams are from Quraysh" also as evidence for their imamate, as al-Ḥāfiẓ mentioned from Aḥmad (may God have mercy on him) in the explanation of the aforementioned hadith.²

The upshot is that the views of the scholars on this question conflict; and we have known from what preceded that the majority of the jurists and theologians chose the saying of stipulating it. [So it appears that it is the preponderant one.] But there are points that must be borne in mind in studying this subject, and they are:

**The first:** this is stipulated only if there is found in Quraysh one who is qualified for the imamate by combining the rest of the conditions and qualities necessary for it. But if there is not among them one qualified for it, then it is permissible to appoint a non-Qurashī by consensus. What indicates that is what al-Bazzār narrated from Anas, that the Prophet ﷺ said: "The imams are from Quraysh, as long as they act by three: if they are asked for mercy, they show mercy; and if they make a covenant, they fulfil; and if they rule, they are just."³ So it is known from it that they are not qualified for the imamate if the mentioned qualities are not present in them.

> ¹ *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (8/170), Book of Ḥudūd, chapter on stoning the one pregnant from fornication, hadith no. 6830.
> ² *Fatḥ al-Bārī* of Ibn Ḥajar (12/152).
> ³ *Kashf al-Astār ʿan Zawāʾid al-Bazzār* (2/228).

[p. 209] **The second:** it appears to me that this also covers the case where lineages have become mixed or lost, such that a Qurashī is not known from another — as the jurists mentioned of the non-Arabs that they lost their lineages and did not preserve them. So it is fitting to choose the saying of the permissibility of appointing a non-Qurashī in such a form by consensus.

**The third:** stipulating Qurashī descent is — among those who hold it — for the imam and caliph, not for others such as provincial governors and other government officials. So if the imam wished to invest someone of non-Qurashīs and appoint him over a town of the lands of the Muslims, or put him in command in war, that is also permissible by consensus. That becomes clear from al-Ḥāfiẓ Ibn Ḥajar's (may God have mercy on him) restricting the imamate for which Qurashī descent is stipulated with "the greatest" among the kinds of imamates and commands.¹

#### The fifth condition: knowledge

The scholars of politics have agreed on stipulating knowledge for the imam, and no disagreement on this has been reported from anyone; the disagreement is only over how much knowledge suffices for the imam. Some — among them al-Māwardī and al-Juwaynī — held that it is stipulated that the imam be a mujtahid who has reached the level of the mujtahids; while the later ones among them denied stipulating his reaching the rank of ijtihad, and rather contented themselves in that with the amount that enables the sultan to administer his government according to the rulings of the Sharia only.

#### The sixth condition: probity (al-ʿadāla)

What is meant by it is that he be upright in conduct, following the rulings of Islam, avoiding the deeds and states that necessitate transgression (*fisq*) and dissoluteness. al-Qāḍī Abū Yaʿlā al-Farrāʾ (may God have mercy on him) said: "That he be of the description of one who is fit to be a judge, of freedom, adulthood, sanity, knowledge and probity."²

For the judge, too, it was originally stipulated that he have reached the level of ijtihad, being of probity

> ¹ See *Fatḥ al-Bārī* of Ibn Ḥajar (13/119).
> ² *Al-Aḥkām al-Sulṭāniyya* of Abū Yaʿlā al-Farrāʾ (1/20).

[p. 210] with its comprehensive conditions. Indeed, Imam Ibn Taymiyya (may God have mercy on him) has stated at the opening of his book *al-Siyāsa al-Sharʿiyya fī Iṣlāḥ al-Rāʿī wa-l-Raʿiyya* that it is obligatory to appoint the fittest of those he is able [to find] among the Muslims, arguing by what al-Ḥākim narrated from Ibn ʿAbbās, may God be pleased with him; he said: the Messenger of God ﷺ said: "Whoever appoints a man over a group, while he finds in that group one more pleasing to God than him, has betrayed God and His Messenger and betrayed the believers."¹

Imam Abū Ḥanīfa (may God have mercy on him) used to content himself in probity with the outward state of the witnesses among the Muslims if they were not openly transgressing; while the two imams Abū Yūsuf and Muḥammad (may God have mercy on them both) held that that does not suffice; rather their vetting (*tazkiya*) is necessary secretly and openly. It is the saying which the later Ḥanafīs preferred and gave fatwa by.

Then, with the passage of time and the decline of the state of the Muslims in attending to religious matters, this condition was relaxed, so the definition of probity among them became as follows: "Whoever's good deeds prevail over his bad deeds."²

The relaxation continued until they said: it is impossible to find a person in whom all the conditions of probity are combined; so it should be limited to what pertains to the office he holds, and the criterion in his case would be as follows: "Justice in the case of the judge is judging by the truth, and in the case of the governor is fairness without treachery."³

But by close examination of the subject and the details of it that have passed, it becomes clear that it is necessary here to distinguish between two matters: the duty of the bodies that appoint the imam and confer leadership, and the ruling on one who was invested or took on the governorship or judgeship with a defect in these conditions. As for the first, it remains the duty of these to undertake appointing the fittest and most just of those they are able [to find], and not to prefer other than

> ¹ *Al-Siyāsa al-Sharʿiyya fī Iṣlāḥ al-Rāʿī wa-l-Raʿiyya* (1/7); *al-Mustadrak ʿalā al-Ṣaḥīḥayn* of al-Ḥākim (4/104), and he said: sound in chain; and al-Dhahabī did not mention it in the *Talkhīṣ*.
> ² *Durar al-Ḥukkām fī Sharḥ Majallat al-Aḥkām* (4/406), article 1705.
> ³ *Durar al-Ḥukkām fī Sharḥ Majallat al-Aḥkām* (4/580), article 1792.

[p. 211] the fittest over the fittest, in view of the hadith which we mentioned just now and which al-Ḥākim narrated in it from Ibn ʿAbbās, may God be pleased with them both; he said: the Messenger of God ﷺ said: "Whoever employs a man from a group while in that group there is one more pleasing to God than him has betrayed God and betrayed His Messenger and betrayed the believers."

But if there is not found among the Muslims one who combines these conditions and qualities, or someone took on the governorship or judgeship by seizure and domination, his caliphate is concluded in these exceptional cases of necessity. This is as ʿAllāma Ibn al-Humām transmitted from Imam al-Ghazālī that he said: "The combining of these conditions — of probity, ijtihad and other than them — is impossible in our age, because of the age's being empty of the mujtahid and the just. So the aspect [to take] is the implementation of the judgeship of everyone whom a sultan with power appoints, even if he is ignorant and transgressing; and it is the apparent [position] of the school with us. So if the ignorant transgressor were appointed, it is valid and he rules by the fatwa of others; but he should not be appointed."¹

> ¹ *Fatḥ al-Qadīr* of al-Kamāl ibn al-Humām (7/253).

### 3 – Appointing the imam and investing him

Then Islam commands its followers to undertake — by consultation among them — handing over the office of the imamate over them to one of them in whom these qualities are combined and these conditions are present. This principle comprises two matters: the obligation of appointing the imam over the Muslims, and that it be from consultation among them.

As for the detail of the first, let it be known that appointing the imam is obligatory on the Muslims by the consensus of those of the Muslims on whom the sun has risen and set, and the agreement of the schools of all the scholars. The meaning of that is that it is imposed on the Muslims as a communal obligation (*ʿalā al-kifāya*) that they make one of them commander and appoint over them one of them in whom those conditions are combined and those qualities are present.

But let it be noted that this ruling is directed to the Muslims only when they have no imam and no commander, and they have come to live without a ruler in anarchy, with no one to gather them on the truth, and no restrainer restrains them, and no deterrent deters them from following the footsteps of Satan. Then it is obligatory on them

[p. 212] to appoint one of them as an imam who performs his functions through establishing the ḥudūd, defending the sanctity of Islam and so on. As for if they have an imam — even if he took on the imamate by seizure and taking control, and against their will — and even if he does not combine the qualities required for the imam, as is the case in most Islamic states today: what then should the Muslims do in this form? It is a question related to the subject of deposing the imam, whose details we leave to its place in Chapter Six, God willing.

As for the second part of this principle, it is that the appointment of the imam must be by way of consultation among the people who loosen and bind (*ahl al-ḥall wa-l-ʿaqd*) among the Muslims. It is not permissible for a person to seize the affair and so take it over without consultation, or upon the consultation of some of those not counted among the people who loosen and bind. The evidence for that is the saying of God, glorified and exalted: **"And whose affair is [determined by] consultation among themselves"** [al-Shūrā, 42:38].

The greatest evidence for that is that the Prophet ﷺ left this world and did not appoint anyone as successor in it; rather he entrusted his affair to the generality of the Muslims to choose from among them for this matter whoever of them is the fittest and most just. That suffices as evidence that the authority in this matter rests with the generality of the Muslims, not others; otherwise he would have spared them the trouble of appointment by appointing one of them as successor.

More explicit than that is what al-Bukhārī narrated from ʿĀʾisha, may God be pleased with her, that the Messenger of God ﷺ said to her while he was in the illness in which he died: "I was about — or I wanted — to send to Abū Bakr and his son and make a covenant [of succession], lest speakers say — or wishers wish —; then I said: God refuses [otherwise] and the believers repel [otherwise]."¹

So this hadith indicated with all clarity and explicitness that the Prophet ﷺ left the matter of appointing his successor to the Muslims; and since he was confident that they would not be pleased except with Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq, may God be pleased with him, he therefore did not see any need for appointing him personally.

There is another hadith which al-Bukhārī included too with its details, from which the standing of consultation in the appointment of the caliph and the designation of the imam becomes clear; and its summary: that ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf heard, in the last days of the pilgrimage which ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, may God be pleased with him, performed, [a man] saying: "If ʿUmar had died I would have pledged allegiance to

> ¹ *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (7/119), Book of the Sick, hadith 5666.

[p. 213] so-and-so; for by God the pledge of allegiance to Abū Bakr was nothing but a sudden thing (*falta*) and it was completed." So ʿUmar became angry at that and said: "I will, God willing, stand this evening among the people and warn them against these who want to usurp their affairs from them." ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said: "So I said: O Commander of the Faithful, do not do it; for the season gathers the riff-raff of people and their rabble, and they will be the ones crowding closest to you when you stand among the people. I fear that you will stand and say something that will be carried off and spread in every direction, that they will not understand it, and that they will not put it in its proper place.
