So wait until you come to Medina, for it is the abode of emigration and the Sunna, and you will be alone with the people of understanding and the nobles of the people, and you will say what you said with ease; and the people of knowledge will understand your statement and put it in its proper place." ʿUmar said: "By God — God willing — I will certainly do so on the first occasion I stand up in Medina." Ibn ʿAbbās said: So we came to Medina at the end of Dhū al-Ḥijja; and when it was Friday I hurried to go when the sun declined, until I found Saʿīd ibn Zayd ibn ʿAmr ibn Nufayl sitting at the corner of the pulpit; so I sat beside him, my knee touching his knee. I did not wait long before ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb came out; and when I saw him coming I said to Saʿīd ibn Zayd ibn ʿAmr ibn Nufayl: he will say this evening a statement he has not said since he was appointed successor. He disapproved of that from me and said: What do you expect him to say that he has not said before? So ʿUmar sat on the pulpit; and when the muezzins fell silent he stood, and praised God with what He is worthy of, then said: "To proceed: I am going to say to you a statement which has been decreed for me to say; I do not know; perhaps it comes shortly before my death. So whoever understands it and retains it, let him relate it wherever his mount ends with him; and whoever fears that he will not understand it, I do not permit anyone to lie about me… Then it has reached me that a speaker among you says: By God, if ʿUmar had died I would have pledged allegiance to so-and-so. So let no man be deceived into saying: the pledge of allegiance to Abū Bakr was only a sudden thing and it was completed. Indeed it was so; but God protected [us] from its evil; and there is no one among you to whom necks are stretched like Abū Bakr. Whoever pledges allegiance to a man without consultation of the Muslims, he is not to be followed, nor the one who pledged allegiance to him, lest both be killed. It was part of what happened to us when God took His Prophet ﷺ…"

He continued, telling them the story of the pledge of allegiance to Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq, may God be pleased with him: "The Anṣār opposed us and gathered all of them in the Saqīfa of Banū Sāʿida, and ʿAlī and al-Zubayr and those with them opposed us, and the Muhājirūn gathered to Abū Bakr. So I said to Abū Bakr: O Abū Bakr, take us to

[p. 214] these brothers of ours of the Anṣār. So we set out wanting them … until we came to them in the Saqīfa of Banū Sāʿida… When we sat a little, their speaker gave witness [of faith] … Then Abū Bakr spoke and said: As for what you have mentioned of good among you, you are worthy of it; and this matter will not be recognized except for this clan of Quraysh; they are the most central of the Arabs in lineage and abode. I have chosen for you one of these two men, so pledge allegiance to whichever of them you wish. He took my hand and the hand of Abū ʿUbayda ibn al-Jarrāḥ, while he was sitting between us… So the clamour increased, and the voices rose, until I feared disagreement; so I said: Stretch out your hand, O Abū Bakr. So he stretched out his hand and I pledged allegiance to him, and the Muhājirūn pledged allegiance to him, then the Anṣār pledged allegiance to him. We set upon Saʿd ibn ʿUbāda; and a speaker among them said: You have killed Saʿd ibn ʿUbāda. So I said: May God kill Saʿd ibn ʿUbāda. ʿUmar said: And by God, we did not find, in what we attended of matters, anything stronger than the pledge of allegiance to Abū Bakr. We feared that if we left the people and there was no pledge, they would pledge allegiance to a man of them after us; so either we would pledge allegiance to them on what we are not pleased with, or we would oppose them and there would be corruption. So whoever pledges allegiance to a man without consultation of the Muslims, he is not to be followed, nor the one who pledged allegiance to him, lest both be killed."

It is narrated in a report with Ibn Abī Shayba in the *Muṣannaf* from Abū Hurayra that ʿUmar, may God be pleased with them both, said: Whoever pledges allegiance to a commander of the Muslims without consultation, there is no pledge for him.¹

So these proofs all suffice to stand as an argument for what we claimed: that the appointment of the imam and the investing of the caliph must be from consultation of the Muslims. So after this is established, this question poses itself in the mind of the researcher: how does this consultation among the Muslims take place? Is it by voting on the basis of the ballot of every adult, or by election by the members of a specific, defined body or circle, or in another way besides that? We say: it is among the secondary matters for which Islam did not specify a special formula to act by; rather it entrusted its matter to the majority of the Muslims to choose for it a form that agrees with the principles of the Sharia on one side and harmonizes with their external reality on another. But the matter on which the opinion of the scholars has settled is that what is meant is not the consultation of every sane, adult Muslim, whatever his qualifications and his insight and the extent of his understanding of the realities of the Muslims; rather what is meant by it is the consultation of the people who loosen and bind. The evidence for that is that it was the prevailing method in the era of the Rightly-Guided Caliphate, where the governorship

> ¹ *Muṣannaf Ibn Abī Shayba* (6/454), what they said about obligations and the codification of the registers.

[p. 215] and the pledge of allegiance for anyone in it took place from consultation of the people who loosen and bind, not every Tom, Dick and Harry of the Muslims. So the pledge of allegiance to Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq, may God be pleased with him, was not as a result of the voting of every one of the Muslims in his favour, but from the consultation of a group of the Muhājirūn and Anṣār, people of opinion and insight only. Likewise ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him, entrusted the matter of the caliph to a committee consisting of six of the notables of the Muhājirūn and Anṣār, who chose our master ʿUthmān, may God be pleased with him. When ʿUthmān, may God be pleased with him, was martyred, a group of people came to our master ʿAlī, may God be pleased with him, and asked him to stretch out his hand to them so that they might pledge allegiance to him. He said to them: "That is not for you; it is only for the people of the Shūrā and the people of Badr. Whoever the people of the Shūrā are pleased with is the caliph; so we will gather and look into this matter."¹

For the story of his pledge of allegiance there is a detail which al-Ṭabarī mentioned in his history from Muḥammad ibn al-Ḥanafiyya; he said: I was with my father when ʿUthmān, may God be pleased with him, was killed; so he stood and entered his house. The Companions of the Messenger of God ﷺ came to him and said: This man has been killed, and the people must have an imam, and we do not find today anyone more deserving of this matter than you, nor of older precedence, nor closer to the Messenger of God ﷺ. [He said: Do not do it; it is better that I be a minister [i.e. counsellor] than a commander. They said: No, by God, we will not do [it] until we pledge allegiance to you. He said: Then in the mosque; for my pledge of allegiance will not be hidden, and it will not be except by the consent of the Muslims.] Sālim ibn Abī al-Jaʿd said: ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās said: I disliked that he should come to the mosque, for fear that there would be rioting against him; and he refused except the mosque. When he entered, the Muhājirūn and Anṣār entered and pledged allegiance to him, then the people pledged allegiance to him.²

So these texts indicated that he did not content himself in his taking up the imamate with the opinion of the generality of people; rather he said: "It is only for the people of the Shūrā and the people of Badr." So the scholars of Islamic political jurisprudence argued by it that the decisive voice and authority in choosing the caliph belong to the people who loosen and bind among the Muslims.

What is meant by the people who loosen and bind: the leaders who have opinion and standing in the Umma, to whom people turn in their needs and interests, and whose opinions are therefore accepted in the Umma.

> ¹ *Al-Imāma wa-l-Siyāsa* of Ibn Qutayba 1/43.
> ² *Tārīkh al-Ṭabarī* = *Tārīkh al-Rusul wa-l-Mulūk*, and *Ṣilat Tārīkh al-Ṭabarī* (4/427).

[p. 216] In the era of the Rightly-Guided Caliphate the term was understood to mean the notables of the tribes, the scholars of Islam, the jurists of the Sharia and the other leaders. People knew well who they were — the ones who represented them in their affairs and administered their matters. So the term was not so vague that identifying who it covered would lead to dispute; and hence there was no need to designate them by name.

The choosing of the caliph took place by the pledge of allegiance of the people who loosen and bind among the Muslims to him; and usually they used to say at the pledge: "We pledge allegiance to you, pleased, on establishing justice and undertaking the obligations of the imamate according to the Book of God and the Sunna of His Messenger [God bless him and give him peace]."¹ And it does not require clasping hands; rather the statement suffices in it.

But with the progress of time the situation changed from what it had been entirely; so the people who loosen and bind no longer remained accepted in all circles without dispute. So the Umma came to feel the pressing need to specify them and define their descriptions, to protect it from being encroached upon and toyed with in the affairs of the Muslims by every Tom, Dick and Harry. So it is reasonable that you pose to me the question of how to choose them today. The answer to that is that the consultative council (*majlis al-shūrā*) whose mention will come in what follows, God willing — or both of the councils of legislation, regional and national together — their members can be regarded as embodying "the people who loosen and bind" and as the body with authority to elect the caliph today. As for choosing the members of these councils, it can be by casting votes from every adult citizen, as its detail will come shortly in the discussion of consultation, God willing. It also appears to be permitted in the Sharia that the caliph be elected by a direct election by the Umma on the basis of voting by every sane, adult Muslim citizen; for there is no text contrary to it in anything.

What has passed was a detail of the straight method fulfilling the conditions for appointing the imam and choosing the caliph, while there are two other methods which the Sharia has considered for the validity of the imamate and the conclusion of the caliphate, namely: designation of a successor (*al-istikhlāf*), and seizure or taking control (*al-taghallub aw al-istīlāʾ*). For

> ¹ *Taḥrīr al-Aḥkām fī Tadbīr Ahl al-Islām* of Ibn Jamāʿa (1/57).

[p. 217] the completeness of the research, we see it necessary to cast a glance at them here, even if passing and quick.

### 4 – Appointment by designation of a successor (istikhlāf) and covenant

Its meaning: that the rightful imam who combines all the conditions of the imamate appoint someone as successor in his lifetime to be the imam of the Muslims after his death. The term "covenant" (*al-ʿahd*) in royal circles is synonymous with the word. The Sharia also considers it, with its conditions, as a method for the conclusion of the imamate. They argued for that by Abū Bakr's appointing ʿUmar, may God be pleased with them both, as successor, and by what ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him, said shortly before his death: "If my appointed time comes while Abū ʿUbayda is alive, I will appoint him successor." So it indicated that designation of a successor is permissible in the Sharia. But three points must be noted here with precision and care:

**The first:** it is not permissible for the imam to appoint one of his children or relatives as successor merely for his kinship and blood relations with him, regarding the caliphate as an inheritance as property and goods are inherited. The evidence for that is that Abraham, upon him be peace, when he asked for the office of the imamate for his descendants, God, glorified and exalted, answered him, saying: **"My covenant does not include the wrongdoers"** [al-Baqara, 2:124].

And when Abū Bakr, may God be pleased with him, appointed ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him, as successor, he spoke the words history has preserved from him, which became a bright lamp lighting the way for whoever seeks to follow it: "For by God I have spared no effort in forming my judgement, and I have not appointed a relative."

It is a matter on which the Umma has agreed. ʿAllāma Ibn Ḥazm (may God have mercy on him) said: "And there is no disagreement among anyone of the people of Islam that inheritance is not permissible in it."

**The second:** the imam who wants to designate a successor or covenant to someone must in the Sharia choose the fittest of those he is able [to find] among those in whom the qualities of the imamate are combined and the conditions of the caliphate are present; and that his election of him be from consultation of the people who loosen and bind and of knowledge, as Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq, may God be pleased with him, did in his appointing al-Fārūq, may God be pleased with him, as successor.¹

In such a form, if he appointed someone of his kin as successor on the basis of his qualification for the imamate

> ¹ See: *Tārīkh al-Ṭabarī* = *Tārīkh al-Rusul wa-l-Mulūk*, and *Ṣilat Tārīkh al-Ṭabarī* (3/428).

[p. 218] by combining its conditions, then even if it is permitted in the Sharia, it is better to avoid it also, guarding against a position of suspicion — as it was the followed Sunna in the era of the Rightly-Guided Caliphate. Here is Abū Bakr, may God be pleased with him, declaring with all explicitness that he did not appoint a relative. Here is ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him, flaring up in anger when it was suggested to him that he appoint his son ʿAbd Allāh as successor; rather it came in a report that he said to the one who suggested it: "May God fight you! By God, I did not want God by this."¹ As for ʿUthmān, may God be pleased with him, he was martyred before he found an opportunity to think about the designation of anyone as successor. Here is ʿAlī, may God be pleased with him, being asked about pledging allegiance to al-Ḥasan after his death; he answered them, saying: "I neither command you nor forbid you; you are more discerning."² And it came in a report that he said: "But I leave you to what the Messenger of God ﷺ left you."³

Thus the Rightly-Guided Caliphs left this world without appointing in it as successor anyone with kinship or blood relation. The first to follow this way was our master Muʿāwiya, may God be pleased with him, when he appointed his son Yazīd as successor — seeking thereby, in his own view, nothing but the truth, sincere counsel and the interest of the Umma, as ʿAllāma Ibn Khaldūn said: "And likewise Muʿāwiya's covenant to Yazīd, for fear of the splitting of the word, given that the Banū Umayya would not be pleased to hand over the matter to anyone other than them. So if he had covenanted to someone other than him, they would have disagreed with him, even though their opinion of him was that he was righteous, and no one doubts that in that; and Muʿāwiya is not suspected of other than this. So he would not have covenanted to him while believing what he was upon of transgression — God forbid that of Muʿāwiya."⁴

What supports that is what is narrated from Muʿāwiya, may God be pleased with him, that he said one day in his sermon: "O God, if You know that I only appointed him because, as I see it, he is worthy of that, then complete for him what I have appointed him to; and if I appointed him because I love him, then do not complete for him what I have appointed him to."⁵

Al-Dhahabī and al-Suyūṭī transmitted it from ʿAṭiyya ibn Qays in the following words: "O God, if I

> ¹ *Al-Ṭabaqāt al-Kubrā*, al-ʿIlmiyya ed. (3/261).
> ² *Majmaʿ al-Zawāʾid wa-Manbaʿ al-Fawāʾid* (9/142).
> ³ *Musnad Aḥmad* 2, hadith no. 1078; *Majmaʿ al-Zawāʾid wa-Manbaʿ al-Fawāʾid* (9/137), and he said: narrated by Aḥmad and Abū Yaʿlā, and its men are men of the Ṣaḥīḥ except ʿAbd Allāh ibn Subayʿ, and he is trustworthy. And al-Bazzār narrated it with a good chain.
> ⁴ *Muqaddimat Ibn Khaldūn*, Book One, chapter three, section 28, p. 258.
> ⁵ *Al-Bidāya wa-l-Nihāya*, Dār al-Fikr ed. (8/80).

[p. 219] covenanted to Yazīd only because of what I saw of his merit, then bring him to what I hoped for and help him; and if it was only love of a father for his child that carried me, and he is not worthy, then take him before he reaches that."¹

The truth is that this action of his had dire consequences that brought woes upon the Umma; for his action became a followed precedent in the Umma, which those who followed him of the imams of tyranny and the caliphs of seizure and greed held fast to, and the caliphate came to be inherited like property and goods. The Umma was deprived of that blessed Sunna adopted for choosing the imam in the days of the Rightly-Guided Caliphate, except in very rare exceptional cases. I have spoken at length on this discussion in my Urdu book *Ḥaḍrat Muʿāwiya aur Tārīkhī Ḥaqāʾiq* (Our Master Muʿāwiya in the Light of Historical Constants).

**The third point:** the scholars of Islamic political jurisprudence differed concerning the status of this covenant and designation of a successor: is it binding on the Muslims after the death of the imam, or does it depend on the approval of the people who loosen and bind? Some held that the covenant by which the rightful imam who combines the conditions of the imamate covenanted is regarded after him as binding on the Muslims, with the reasoning that it is among the totality of the rulings which he issued in his lifetime in the period of his imamate; so obedience to him in it is obligatory like his other rulings. While others held that his covenant is nothing but a proposal and counsel, and its implementation depends on the approval of the people who loosen and bind for it: if they approve it, it is in force; otherwise not. It is what ʿAllāma Abū Yaʿlā al-Farrāʾ (may God have mercy on him) chose in his book *al-Aḥkām al-Sulṭāniyya*, saying: "That the imamate of the one covenanted to is concluded after his death by the choice of the people of the time."²

ʿAllāma Ibn Taymiyya (may God have mercy on him) clarified it, saying: "And likewise ʿUmar, when Abū Bakr covenanted to him, only became imam when they pledged allegiance to him and obeyed him; and had it been supposed that they did not implement the covenant of Abū Bakr and did not pledge allegiance to him, he would not have become imam."³

What Abū Bakr, may God be pleased with him, did also supports it: when he wanted to conclude the caliphate for ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him,

> ¹ *Tārīkh al-Islām*, ed. Tadmurī (4/169); *Tārīkh al-Khulafāʾ* of al-Suyūṭī, p. 157.
> ² *Al-Aḥkām al-Sulṭāniyya* of Abū Yaʿlā al-Farrāʾ (1/26).
> ³ *Minhāj al-Sunna al-Nabawiyya* (1/530).

[p. 220] after him, he called first ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf and second ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān, may God be pleased with them both, and consulted them in the matter. When they advised him to [do] it, he looked out upon the people from his balcony and said to them in what he said: "And I have appointed ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb as successor, so hear him and obey." They said: "We hear and we obey."¹

Thus Abū Bakr, may God be pleased with him, did not act by his opinion alone in the designation of a successor; rather he covenanted to ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him, after consultation on it and the consent of the people who loosen and bind with it, seeking in it justice, truth and the interest. And even if we granted that his designation alone was binding on the Muslims, does anyone today reach his level in the soundness of opinion, the correctness of judgement and the sincerity of intention? No!

So what is correct is that designation of a successor does not go beyond being a proposal from the imam and counsel from him to the Muslims that they appoint the one he appointed as successor as imam over them after his death, and the reference in implementing it or not is to the people who loosen and bind among the Muslims; and they, concerning this proposal, have the two choices: if it seems right to them to implement it, they implement it; otherwise they reject it and choose someone else as imam.

The answer to what the people of the first position said — that the designation of a successor must be implemented as being a ruling of the rightful imam — is: that what is most correct is that the caliphate of the imam and his imamate end with his death; so if he died and his caliphate ended by it, his commands no longer have to be obeyed. ʿAllāma Ibn ʿĀbidīn (may God have mercy on him) devoted an independent heading to this discussion in *Radd al-Muḥtār*, and among what came in it: "It is inferred from the words of *Sharḥ al-Munya*, 'it was in their time…', that the command of the caliph does not survive his death or his removal, as was stated explicitly in *al-Fatāwā al-Khayriyya*; and built on it is that if he forbade hearing a claim after fifteen years, his prohibition does not remain after his death."²

### 5 – Seizing power by force (taghallub)

The third form for the conclusion of the imamate is: that a person seize power and authority and prevail over it against people's will by using force.

> ¹ See: *Tārīkh al-Ṭabarī* = *Tārīkh al-Rusul wa-l-Mulūk*, and *Ṣilat Tārīkh al-Ṭabarī* (3/428).
> ² *Al-Durr al-Mukhtār* and the gloss of Ibn ʿĀbidīn (*Radd al-Muḥtār*) (2/172).

[p. 221] Its ruling: that whoever seizes the affair by rebelling against the rightful imam and fighting him is a sinner, and a rebel (*bāghī*) also, as long as power has not been obtained for him and authority has not come under his control. It is obligatory on the Muslims to resist and oppose him as far as they are able, acting by the words of God the Exalted: **"But if one of them oppresses the other, then fight against the one that oppresses until it returns to the ordinance of God"** [al-Ḥujurāt, 49:9].

But if his authority is established and his control is consolidated and his power stands firm, then he — even if he committed a great, heinous sin like rebellion and insurgency against the rightful imam, concerning which severe threats have come, like what the Prophet ﷺ said: "Six I have cursed, and God curses them, and every prophet [whose prayer] is answered… and the one who seizes power by tyranny to exalt thereby whom God has humbled and humble whom God has exalted"¹ —

nevertheless the jurists regarded this imamate of his as concluded and that leadership as standing; so his commands take on the character of commands issued from the caliph in terms of the necessity of obeying them and the obligation of complying with them, with their established conditions. The upshot is that his affair fluctuates between two aspects: as for one aspect, the Muslims should try to remove him and turn him away from the imamate on condition that there not be in it the stirring of a strife (*fitna*) that cannot be borne; and from another, they regarded his rulings as in force on condition that they not clash with the Sharia nor contradict it. The evidence for this second matter is the saying of the Prophet ﷺ: "Hear and obey, even if an Abyssinian whose head is like a raisin is appointed."²

The wisdom in that is that if we did not recognize those of his commands which do not contradict the Sharia, that would lead to sweeping anarchy among the Muslims. This is as is established in the world today, that rulers are of two kinds:

The legitimate constitutional ruler (De Jure): he is the one to whose authority the constitution of the country has given legitimacy, so he has become a legitimate ruler of it.

The actual ruler in reality (De Facto): i.e. the ruler who has actually taken up the reins of rule,

> ¹ *Sunan al-Tirmidhī*, ed. Shākir (4/457), hadith no. 2154.
> ² *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (1/140), Book of Prayer, chapter on the imamate of the slave, hadith no. 693; and also chapter on hearing and obedience, hadith no. 7142.

[p. 222] even if his rule has no legitimacy.

The meaning of that: that if a person took up rule and took charge of command over the state by rebellion against the legitimate ruler, or seizure, or other than that of unconstitutional ways, then the commands which he issued during his rule and the days of his command are granted legal validity, to protect the state from being swept away in the torrent of disturbance and sweeping anarchy which floods a state only to sweep it away and destroy it utterly. For rejecting his commands entirely and denying them totally entails that there be no weight for the judges whom he appointed and the governors who took charge from him of rule — from which follows the cancellation of their rulings by which they judged in the disputes of the subjects and their trials. So the consequence of all this would not be other than toying with the state and throwing it into the mill of anarchy, disturbance and disorder.

So to protect the Muslims from disturbance and anarchic life, the scholars of Islam held that the imamate is concluded in the case of seizure and domination, and consequently that his commands are valid and to be implemented as long as he is in control of authority — even though his audacity in this is among the major sins, and removing him by peaceful means is a duty incumbent on the Muslims.

### 6 – On the unity and multiplicity of the caliph

The majority of the scholars have agreed that the imam should be one for all the Muslims, and that there not be for every region of the regions of the Islamic state and every territory of its territories an independent imam. Al-Māwardī (may God have mercy on him) said: "And if the imamate is concluded for two imams in two lands, their imamate is not concluded; because it is not permissible for the Umma to have two imams at one time — even if a people deviated and permitted it."¹

They also argued — besides other proofs — by his ﷺ saying: "And there will be caliphs, and they will be many." They said: What do you command us? He said: "Fulfil the pledge of allegiance to the first, then the first."²

But Islamic government, if the areas of its influence widened and the extent of its authority stretched such that it became impossible for one imam to manage its affair, then in such a form it is permissible to distribute

> ¹ *Al-Aḥkām al-Sulṭāniyya* of al-Māwardī (1/29).
> ² *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (4/169), Book of the Prophets, chapter on what was mentioned about the Children of Israel, hadith no. 3455.

[p. 223] the Islamic Umma into different units and states, and hence the conclusion of the imamate for one of the people of each state. ʿAllāma ʿAbd al-Qāhir [Ar.: ʿAbd al-Qādir] al-Baghdādī (may God have mercy on him) stated this explicitly, saying: "It is not permissible for there to be at one time two imams whose obedience is obligatory… unless there is between the two lands a sea preventing the arrival of the help of the people of each of them to the other; then it is permissible for the people of each of them to conclude the imamate for one of the people of his region."¹

ʿAllāma al-Māwardī (may God have mercy on him) said: "As for in scattered lands and distant cities, a deviant group has held the permissibility of that; because the imam is appointed for interests. If there were two in two lands or two regions, each of them would better manage what is in his charge and better control what lies near him. And because, since it was permissible to send two prophets in one era without that nullifying prophethood, this is all the more so for the imamate, and it does not nullify the imamate."²

It is what the Imam of the Two Holy Sanctuaries, ʿAllāma al-Juwaynī (may God have mercy on him), showed his inclination to, saying: "My own view is that concluding the imamate for two persons in one district with narrow boundaries is not permissible, and consensus has occurred on it. But if the distance is far and a vast remoteness lies between the two imams, then there is room for [the other] possibility, and the matter falls outside the definitive questions."³

Just as ʿAllāma al-Qurṭubī (may God have mercy on him) supported it in his commentary, and transmitted the aforementioned statement of al-Juwaynī word for word⁴; and Shaykh al-Farhārwī (may God have mercy on him) preferred it in his commentary on *Sharḥ al-ʿAqāʾid*.⁵

Despite these sayings and what they contain of proofs, the majority of the scholars still hold their position that the imam of the Muslims in all parts of the world at one time can only be one.

> ¹ *Uṣūl al-Dīn*, p. 274.
> ² *Adab al-Dunyā wa-l-Dīn* (1/136), what the condition of the world is set right by.
> ³ *Al-Irshād ilā Qawāṭiʿ al-Adilla*, the last chapter, p. 358.
> ⁴ *Tafsīr al-Qurṭubī*, Sūrat al-Baqara, verse 30, vol. 1, p. 273.
> ⁵ *Al-Nibrās ʿalā Sharḥ al-ʿAqāʾid*, p. 513.

[p. 224] What appears to me after looking into the subject and deliberating in it is: that Islam, as being a universal call that strives to unify its children from the furthest parts of the inhabited world and its nearest, and to unite them in a single rank, requires that the position of the majority be the preponderant one, such that the saying of the unity of the imam for the whole Islamic world is chosen however far their abodes are apart and however distant they are and however wide the distances between them. The clearest example of that is what was of the unity of the imam in the era of the Rightly-Guided Caliphate, when Islam and its followers had reached one third of the inhabited world or half of it, at a time when communications and means of transport had not reached the zenith of development, flourishing and capability. How much more so today, when these have developed so astonishingly that the whole world has become like one village! So let a model Islamic state strive and devote its efforts to gathering the Muslims from all parts of the world on one imam. This is the original Sharia ruling on the question. But the Islamic world has today been divided — regrettably — into more than fifty independent states which have their own kings and rulers; so it has become impossible to gather them on one imam except by one of two matters: that all of them gather on the imamate of one person and his leadership — and how far, how far that is! — or that they fight each other and strike one another until some are able to take control of others by force. That is nothing but like one who tried to build a palace and demolished a city, or like one who fled from the rain and settled under the gutter. So there remains for us then no alternative to acknowledging these states and their governments as Islamic states and governments, to protect the Muslims from anarchy and disorder and the dividing of their word and the dismantling of their unity. Nor is this an innovation of ours or a new view we have invented; rather the scholars of the Umma preceded us to that in its past when the imams multiplied with the multiplicity of governments, so they acknowledged them and regarded the commands of every government as in force among its people. So for the necessity of implementing commands and protecting the Muslims from anarchy, the second saying (the saying of the permissibility of the multiplicity of imams) is chosen — to this extent only.

---

## Chapter Four: Islamic Rules for the Administration of Government

### Introduction

[p. 225] Islam has wise teachings and right directives which are like the bright lamp, lighting the way for the government that has been formed on the basis of the principles we explained earlier. The Muslims must take them with regard and compliance in administering the helm of government, so that it is of an Islamic character and is dyed with a religious dye — not tyrannical and despotic, nor wrongful and oppressive. We would like to devote this chapter to explaining those principles and foundations which the imam follows in administering administrative affairs and social questions.

### 1 – Submission to the rulings of the Qur'an and Sunna

The basic principle for the imam of the Muslims is that he be, in all his commands, his affairs and his measures, subject to the rulings of the Book of God and the Sunna of His Messenger ﷺ and following what the Umma agreed upon and its practice continued upon. He has no scope whatever to lay down a law or take a measure that contradicts the clear rulings established by the Book and the Sunna; and that is because — proceeding from the principle of faith in rule belonging to God, glorified and exalted, alone — he is a vicegerent of God in His rule over His servants, so he is subordinate to what He revealed and legislated for him in His firm Book and the purified Sunna of His Messenger. The vicegerent may not oppose the one who appointed him as vicegerent, and the branch is not permitted to contradict its root. Hence it was natural that the first constitution and original source for the imam — while he is in the course of administering his command over the Muslims and performing his functions — be these two primary sources of Islam: the Book of God and the Sunna of His Messenger ﷺ. Hence any command he issues or legislation he enacts that contradicts the Sharia or its fundamentals carries no weight; rather it is the right of the Muslims to try to cancel that by way of the court or other safe constitutional ways.

### 2 – Consultation with the people who loosen and bind

The role of shūrā (consultation) is not confined to its being a means for appointing the imam and designating the caliph only;

[p. 226] rather Islam emphasizes it as an obligation on whoever takes the office of the imamate: that he administer its affairs and perform its obligations on the basis of consultation with the people who loosen and bind — to the extent that the jurists regarded the imam as deserving removal if he did not attend to consultation in the affairs of government. Its standing and importance become clear from God's commanding the Prophet ﷺ with it, saying: **"And consult them in the matter. When you have decided, then rely upon God"** [Āl ʿImrān, 3:159] — despite his being infallible from error, guided by revelation directly. So it indicated that it is obligatory, all the more so, on others than him among the commanders of the Muslims and their rulers.

The Prophet ﷺ kept this matter constantly in view in public affairs and the tasks of government, attending to it carefully, as Abū Hurayra, may God be pleased with him, said: "I never saw anyone who consulted his companions more than the Messenger of God ﷺ."¹

ʿAllāma Ibn Kathīr said in the commentary on the aforementioned verse: "And for that reason the Messenger of God ﷺ used to consult his companions in a matter when it occurred, to please their hearts, so that they would be more energetic in what they did."

He listed for that several examples of the Prophet ﷺ's consulting them on the day of Badr about going to the caravan, and fighting the polytheists of Mecca and ransoming their captives in it; and he consulted them at Uḥud about whether to stay in Medina or go out to the enemy; and he consulted them in the Battle of the Confederates about digging the trench; and he said in the story of the Slander: Advise me, O assembly of the Muslims.

He transmitted, attributing it to Aḥmad, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ghanm that the Messenger of God ﷺ said to Abū Bakr and ʿUmar: "If you two agreed in counsel, I would not oppose you."²

ʿAllāma al-Haythamī commented on its chain, saying: "Narrated by Aḥmad, and its men are trustworthy, except that Ibn Ghanm did not hear from the Prophet ﷺ."

But that does not impugn it in anything; for it does not go beyond being mursal [with a missing link], and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ghanm is a Companion³, and the mursal of a Companion is an argument by consensus.

> ¹ *Musnad Aḥmad*, al-Risāla ed. (31/244), Musnad of the Kufans, hadith of al-Miswar ibn Makhrama and Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam, 18928.
> ² *Tafsīr Ibn Kathīr*, ed. Salāma (2/150).
> ³ *Al-Iṣāba* 4/350, biography no. 5185.

[p. 227] Here too the Sharia has not specified, for the manner of consultation, its people, and whom the imam consults, and what its basic structure will be, a special method that must be taken always and absolutely in every age and city. Rather it entrusted its details also to the people of insight among the Muslims in every age, to choose to act by it a formula that agrees with the requirements of their age and harmonizes with their reality. So it is not necessary that there be a special committee or a council made up of specific, limited members whom the imam is charged with consulting in every matter; rather anything that fulfils its purpose and meets the need is permitted in the Sharia, whatever its form. So a special council for shūrā may be adopted in one age, just as the imam may be left free to choose for it people of insight and opinion in his time without forming a special council. It is also permitted that he consult some at one time and others at another; just as it is permitted that his consultants differ with the difference of the matters on which he consults them: in one matter he consults expert specialists in it, and in another other people who have connection, expertise and skill in it.

In the era of prophethood and the Rightly-Guided Caliphate it was practised with complete simplicity, without there being a parliament or a legislative body as prevails in this age of ours. Rather the caliph used to consult the notables of the people and the eminent among the Muslims in the matters of the Umma and the affairs of government, and would proceed with a matter which they advised him [upon] after his ascertaining and assurance that it represents the social conscience of the Umma. But nevertheless, the founding of a special council for shūrā also falls within its general meaning and its comprehensive Sharia concept.

It is in truth a feature by which the rulings of Islam in the field of politics are distinguished: where it contented itself in it with legislating many principles without specifying their details and particulars; rather it entrusted that to those of opinion and insight among them in every age and city. The wisdom at work behind that is: that Islam is a general and comprehensive religion which all humanity, in all places and times until the Hour comes, has been called to embrace; its teachings must be implemented and its laws are binding on everyone at every time, without confining them to one people to the exclusion of another, nor narrowing their scope to one place to the exclusion of another —

[p. 228] this on one side. On another, it is tangibly witnessed that needs and requirements differ and change with the change of places, times and temperaments. So if the Sharia had legislated concerning it a special method and a specified manner, it would not have been far-fetched that its adoption and acting by it would be, in relation to a society, impossible or nearly so, or contrary to the interests of the Umma in view of the nature of that society and its circumstances and so on; while another Islamic society would be in the most pressing need to be obligated with this specified formula, because of what is feared of it, that leaving it entirely to its own devices in the matter of shūrā would result only in harming its purposes and the interests of the Umma.

The upshot is that the Sharia, has left this door wide open for everyone to enter according to the requirements of his circumstances and the nature of his age, we are nevertheless confident that if the ruler today were entrusted with its matter wholly such that the choice would be his, and the reference to his will in whom he consults in matters and to what extent, that would make us fear that it would drag woes and evils upon the Umma and dangers and many evils concerning its interests. So it appears that it is necessary today that a special council for shūrā be formed whom the imam is charged with consulting. It has details that will come shortly, God willing.

### 3 – The powers and competences of the shūrā

The second important issue in this discussion revolves around the extent of the competences of the shūrā and its powers, and the ruling of what they advised the imam with: is it binding on him to follow it at all times, or is it permissible for him to disagree with it? In the form of disagreement, by which of the two matters does he judge: by the counsel of the people who loosen and bind, or by his own opinion opposing them? The issue is of considerable importance in the discussion of Islamic politics; we summarize for you its explanation in what follows.

The position which the generality of the jurists chose, and especially those of them who attend to political jurisprudence, is that obeying the shūrā is not obligatory on the imam; for its import and meaning are nothing but that all the aspects of the issue be uncovered before the imam, and all its dimensions become clear such that no hidden aspect of it is concealed from him nor does a far corner of it remain, so that he becomes acquainted with all

[p. 229] the possible courses he could take in undertaking a matter, with their proofs and details, from the mouths of their proponents, and so arrive at the soundest and most correct decision — a decision in which he is independent, not necessarily bound by the shūrā nor obliged to submit to what they said.

This is what the majority of the jurists held: that the imam has the choice in choosing whatever saying or opinion he wishes if he saw it as the strongest of them in argument and the clearest of them in proof, even if it is an opinion which the minority of them saw, or a saying which none of them held. They have in that the following arguments:

**First**, the saying of God, glorified and exalted: **"And consult them in the matter. When you have decided, then rely upon God"** [Āl ʿImrān, 3:159] — since He did not restrict in it the decision on the matter in which consultation has taken place to its being in accordance with the shūrā and its agreement entirely.

**Second**, the practice of the Prophet ﷺ and his Rightly-Guided Caliphs: that they were not committed to the matter of the shūrā in every case; rather it is established from them that they decided by a matter contrary to what the majority of those consulted [advised]. Thus the Prophet ﷺ consulted his companions concerning the captives of Badr: ʿUmar advised him to kill them, and Ibn Rawāḥa to kill and burn them, and Abū Bakr to spare them; and the people divided in that into two groups: people said: he will take the saying of Abū Bakr, may God be pleased with him, and people said: he will take the saying of ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him.¹

So the Messenger of God ﷺ decided by the opinion of Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq, may God be pleased with him, and ordered taking the ransom from them, even though it was not — as it appears — the opinion of two thirds of his companions. Even if the Qur'an reproached [him] for that, its reproach was not for rejecting the opinion of the majority, but because God was not pleased with releasing them at that time.

Likewise Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq's dispatching of the army of Usāma was, it appears, contrary to the opinion of the majority at that time. The story of that is as al-Ṭabarī narrated it in his history from al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī; he said: "The Messenger of God ﷺ raised, before his death, an expedition from the people of Medina and those around them,

> ¹ *Al-Durr al-Manthūr fī al-Tafsīr bi-l-Maʾthūr* (4/105).

[p. 230] among them ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, and appointed over them Usāma ibn Zayd. The last of them had not passed the trench when the Messenger of God ﷺ was taken. So Usāma stopped the people, then said to ʿUmar: Go back to the successor of the Messenger of God and ask his permission; let him permit me to return with the people; for with me are the leading men of the people, all of them, and I do not feel safe for the successor of the Messenger of God, the household of the Messenger of God and the families of the Muslims, lest the polytheists snatch them away. The Anṣār said: If he refuses except that we proceed, then convey [this] from us, and ask him to put over our affair a man older than Usāma.

So ʿUmar went out by the order of Usāma, and came to Abū Bakr and told him what Usāma said. Abū Bakr said: If dogs and wolves snatched me, I would not reverse a decree which the Messenger of God ﷺ decreed! He said: The Anṣār ordered me to convey to you that they ask of you that you put over their affair a man older than Usāma. So Abū Bakr jumped up — and he had been sitting — and took ʿUmar's beard and said to him: May your mother be bereaved of you and lose you, O son of al-Khaṭṭāb! The Messenger of God appointed him and you order me to remove him! So ʿUmar went out to the people, and they said to him: What did you do? He said: Go! May your mothers be bereaved of you! What I met on your account from the successor of the Messenger of God! Then Abū Bakr went out until he came to them, and saw them off and walked with them, walking while Usāma rode… until when he finished he said: If you see fit to help me with ʿUmar, then do! So he gave him permission."

So the army set out and returned with overwhelming success; it was not long before it cast terror, awe and astonishment into the hearts of the people, to the point that it came in what al-Dhahabī narrated in his history that news of the death of the Messenger of God ﷺ and of Usāma's raid on a region of his land reached Heraclius together, and the Romans said: What is the matter with these? Their master dies and then they raid our land!¹

Just as many of the Companions — at their forefront ʿUmar al-Fārūq, may God be pleased with them — did not agree with him on fighting those who refused zakat and warring against them; yet he faced the position with all strength, firmness, resolve and courage, and swore to fight them, rejecting their counsel to make truce with the apostates and conciliate them. In his position on that he said to ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him: Mighty in the Jāhiliyya, weak in Islam? ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him, after that used to mention it among the merits of al-Ṣiddīq, may God be pleased with him; for history narrates

> ¹ *Tārīkh al-Islām*, ed. Tadmurī (3/20).

[p. 231] to us that he used to say: By God, a night of Abū Bakr and a day are better than ʿUmar's [whole life] — the night of his going out with the Messenger of God ﷺ from Mecca on the resolve of emigration to Medina; and as for his day, when the Arabs apostatized I said: O successor of the Messenger of God, conciliate people and be gentle with them; so he said: Mighty in the Jāhiliyya…¹

These, then, are the arguments of those who hold that the imam is not bound to choosing the saying of the people of the shūrā in every case; rather he is charged with sifting the opinions and probabilities and following the strength of the argument and the proof.

While the others relied on [the fact] that consultation is only completed, and its benefit only obtained, if we obligated the imam to choose the saying on which the people of the shūrā agreed definitively, without there being for him room for deviating from it. It is also supported by a hadith which ʿAllāma Ibn Kathīr mentioned in the commentary on this verse, attributing it to Ibn Mardawayh from ʿAlī, may God be pleased with him, that he said: the Messenger of God ﷺ was asked about the decision (*al-ʿazm*); he said: "Consulting the people of opinion, then following them."²

So this hadith — after granting its soundness and its freedom from what impugns it — indicates that the meaning which the people of the first position mentioned for the decision in His words (*then when you have decided*) — that in his ruling the imam follows only the strength of the argument and the proof, not the saying of the people of the shūrā — is not definite and decisive; rather it also admits that its meaning be: that the imam decide on a matter on which the people of the shūrā have agreed, and not turn away from what they unanimously agreed upon. Indeed, if the chain of this hadith were sound, this would be its definite meaning, not merely a possibility.

Al-Ḥāfiẓ Ibn Kathīr here also brought from Imam Aḥmad what we mentioned earlier, that the Prophet ﷺ said to Abū Bakr and ʿUmar, may God be pleased with them both: "If you two agreed in counsel, I would not oppose you."

In addition to that, the Prophet ﷺ himself left his opinion in many places for the opinion of the majority of the Companions, may God be pleased with them, teaching the Umma to take consultation with regard; for if the Prophet ﷺ, though guided by revelation, set aside his opinion and acted on the shūrā, then the other rulers and commanders of the Muslims are all the more obliged to do so.

> ¹ *Tārīkh al-Islām*, ed. Tadmurī (1/321).
> ² *Tafsīr Ibn Kathīr*, ed. Salāma (2/150).

[p. 232] An example of that is what happened in the Battle of Uḥud: that the Prophet ﷺ consulted his companions whether to go out to Quraysh or stay in Medina and fight them in the streets; and his opinion was that they not go out of Medina (and the text of Ibn Hishām: "And the Messenger of God ﷺ disliked going out").¹

But many of the Companions, and especially the young among them, insisted on going out; so he resolved on going out, leaving his opinion for their opinion.

Al-Dārimī narrated in his *Sunan* a hadith in which Maymūn ibn Mihrān spoke at length about the conduct of Abū Bakr, may God be pleased with him, in what came to him of disputes; and among what came in it: "So if it was too difficult for him to find in it a Sunna from the Messenger of God ﷺ, he gathered the heads of the people and their best and consulted them; and if their opinion agreed on a matter, he decided by it"² — which clearly indicates that he used to decide by a matter on which the opinion of the people of the shūrā agreed.

It does not contradict what we presented of his dispatching the expedition of Usāma despite the opposition of most of the Companions to him; for their disagreement was in the stage of consultation only. As for after they saw his steadfastness in his position and his firmness in his opinion with all resolve and strength, it was not long before they were all convinced by it; and it was not long before their opinion turned to his opinion, as is narrated from ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him, that he said in the issue of fighting those who refused zakat — and he had at first seen in it an opinion other than the opinion of Abū Bakr: "By God, it was nothing but that God had opened the breast of Abū Bakr, may God be pleased with him, and I knew that it was the truth."³

So what Abū Bakr carried out was not his opinion alone; rather it became in the end the opinion of all after their being convinced by it when they saw his steadfastness and firmness upon it. So it is not correct to argue by it that he was despotic in his opinion against the opinion of the Companions, and especially the opinion of the people of the shūrā among them.

Here we have finished the brief explanation of the two positions and their proofs in this issue; we follow it with explaining what is preponderant of them, so we say: what appears to us is that the principle is what the people of the position

> ¹ *Sīrat Ibn Hishām*, ed. al-Saqqā (2/63).
> ² *Sunan al-Dārimī* (1/262), the Introduction, chapter on giving fatwa and the severity in it, no. 163.
> ³ *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (2/106), Book of Zakat, hadith no. 1400.

[p. 233] first said: that the imam is not obligated to choose the saying of the people of the shūrā absolutely and in every case. For the word "*al-shūrā*" itself indicates that the saying of the adviser is not necessarily binding on the one consulted; and it is so self-evident that even a slave-woman of the Muslims, Barīra, may God be pleased with her, grasped the difference between it and a command: when the Prophet ﷺ asked her to go back to Mughīth — after she had left him by exercising the option she gained on her emancipation — she asked him: "Are you commanding me?" The Prophet ﷺ said to her: "(I am only interceding)." So she refused to accept his intercession, saying: "I have no need of him."¹ And the Prophet ﷺ did not reproach her; which established it as a Sharia rule that intercession does not have to be accepted by the one to whom intercession is made.

The gist of the words: that God commanded the commander with consultation by the text of the Noble Qur'an, and it was established from this hadith that counsel by its nature requires that it not be binding such that he must accept it; rather its role lies in enabling the imam to know all the dimensions of the issue and its aspects and its probabilities, so that he undertakes, consequently, sifting them and choosing what he sees as soundest and most correct in view of the strength of its argument and the firmness of its proof — even if it was the opinion of the minority, or was not the opinion of any of them definitively and at all. Here too it is necessary to note several points:

**The first:** what we mentioned of not regarding the saying of the shūrā as binding is only in relation to the imam of the Muslims and their commander who was chosen by the people who loosen and bind among them on the basis of his qualification for the office of the imamate by combining its qualities and the presence of its conditions; and not for a democratic ruler bare of those qualities and conditions, whom obtaining the majority of votes enabled to ascend the throne of authority with defects and deficiency in his character and his conduct. Such a ruler is outside the scope of our discussion here, which concerns permitting the imam to carry out matters by his opinion alone, even at the cost of opposing a matter on which the opinion of the people of the shūrā has agreed.

**Second:** the imam of the Muslims and their caliph is not an absolute ruler, despotic in his opinion and independent

> ¹ *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (7/48). This point was explained by the Shaykh, the Sage of the Umma, ʿAllāma Ashraf ʿAlī al-Thānawī (may God have mercy on him).

[p. 234] in his command like kings and emperors; rather he is a believer in the principle of rule belonging to God, so he is subject to His fixed rulings and His revealed laws, restricted necessarily to following His Book and the Sunna of His Messenger ﷺ and the way of the believers. All this forms an impregnable barrier for him that blocks him from deciding by a matter that contradicts these original sources of reference or opposes them in anything.

Since undertaking justice and judging among people with equity is among the totality of the rulings which those sources have strongly emphasized, if the imam transgressed it and ruled with tyranny, that would not be accepted from him; because it is disobedience to the Creator in which obedience to a creature, whoever he may be, is not obligatory. Then it is possible to cancel and nullify it by way of the judiciary and the court. If the imam persisted in his errors without desisting from what he does, he would be a transgressor (*fāsiq*) deserving his removal from this grave office. So, then, not obligating him to choose the saying of the people of the shūrā does not mean that he has become an absolute ruler and despotic in his opinion; rather he is restricted by following the rulings of the Book and the Sunna. In the form of his not fulfilling this commitment and his opposing them or one of them in anything, his subjects may sue him, and then remove him and turn him from this office of his.

The upshot is that what we said of not obligating him with the saying of the shūrā — its scope is limited only to his consultation of the people who loosen and bind in the permissible matters in which Islam has not decided by a ruling. So in these alone he is permitted to depart from the opinion of the people of the shūrā in favour of what his own opinion settles upon as a result of the strength of its argument and its proof. This is what distinguishes him from kings and emperors, since they are so independent and despotic that they do not see anything else — not even divine revelation and heavenly law — above them in sovereignty and authority!

**Third:** since among the most important things that are obligatory on the imam is consultation in the general matters of the Umma, and especially in its political affairs — and the shūrā is only completed if the imam attended it and listened to what is presented and discussed in it of opinions and their proofs, not biased towards one side or a special opinion — it is therefore not correct what has prevailed today: that the shūrā takes place in the absence of the imam, and what was decided in it of opinion is only presented to him; and he cancels it with a single stroke of the pen, without looking closely at what

[p. 235] went on in it and without deliberation in what was presented to him.

In sum: the principle is permitting the imam who combines the conditions of the imamate and its qualities to disagree with the people of the shūrā by his special opinion and to decide by other than what their opinion agreed on — on condition that it be within the scope of these three points and with their observance.

On another side, it is permitted also that the imam and caliph be restricted — in special exceptional cases and circumstances — to taking the opinion of the shūrā within the limits of a special scope. The reason for that is that rarely is there found among the Muslims one who combines in completeness and perfection those conditions and qualities necessary for the imamate in the Sharia, because of the weakness of the religious motive in them and the decline of their state in attending to religious matters — which compelled the scholars to lighten the conditions of probity until its concept among them was confined to a person's good deeds being predominant over his bad deeds only! For witnesses, strict and severe conditions were originally stipulated; then when Imam Abū Yūsuf took the judgeship and found that a witness meeting those conditions had become all but impossible to find, he relaxed them, to the point of accepting the testimony of the transgressor if he had manly virtue (*murūʾa*) that deters him from lying. Likewise among the conditions of the imamate first was that he had reached in knowledge the level of ijtihad; then when attaining this became far harder than before, the condition was relaxed, and it ceased to be a condition for the imamate. So it became possible for someone who is not a mujtahid scholar to take up the reins of the imamate. It is entirely unreasonable that such an imam — not a mujtahid, and not meeting the other conditions of the imamate — be given the powers and authority that the Rightly-Guided Caliphs had — among them independence of opinion and rejection of the saying of the people of the shūrā.

So in such a case there does not appear any harm in choosing the second saying and not permitting the imam to reject the saying of the people of the shūrā definitively and decisively; rather, given the lack of confidence in his expertise and trustworthiness, he should be bound to adopt the view on which the shūrā council has agreed, without there being for him room for opposing it or deviating from it.

There is a formula which can be chosen to define the scope of restricting him in this regard, namely that

[p. 236] rulings and commands are of two kinds: among them what is like legislation and law-making, whose nature is that as soon as they are issued they take the character of law; and among them what are commands related to administrative and executive affairs. As for the first part, it has been established concerning it definitively that the imam is never allowed to legislate what contradicts the Sharia or opposes it in anything. As for permissible matters (*mubāḥāt*), in which the Sharia has left the door of legislation open to the imam, he can be bound in these to follow the opinion of the people of the shūrā.

As for executive and administrative commands, if we charged him in all of them with following the opinion of the shūrā council, that would threaten to obstruct the administration of the helm of government entirely; so here the original principle applies: the imam may follow his own opinion, without being bound at all to follow the opinion of the people of the shūrā.

An example of that is "the emergency tax" (*al-ḍarība al-nāʾiba*): the jurists permitted the government to impose a tax on the subjects if the public treasury fell short of the expenditures necessary for the state, or if a calamity befell the Muslims. The method of that is that the imam put the draft decision concerning it before the shūrā council; so if it did not agree with him on it and did not see a pressing need justifying burdening the subjects with taxes, then the imam may never impose the tax as long as he has not obtained the agreement of the people of the shūrā on it. The task of specifying the matters in which he is bound by the opinion of the shūrā council, and those in which he is not, can be entrusted to the constitution of the Islamic state today.

### 4 – Taking decisions by majority opinion

Among what is worth mentioning here is the discussion of the standing of the majority of opinions and its status in Islam: in the sense that taking [the outcome of] consultation — does it depend on the people of the shūrā being unanimous, or is it possible to take the decision in it by the majority of opinions also?

It has passed before you gentlemen earlier that democracy regards the majority of opinions as its only basis for judging things and distinguishing what is true from what is false. How excellent is the great poet Muhammad Iqbal, saying:

[p. 237]

> *Jamhūriyyat ik ṭarz-e ḥukūmat hai ke jis men*
> *Bandon ko gina karte hain, tolā nahīn karte*

Democracy is a kind of government in which people are merely counted and not weighed. (What counts in it is mere number, without distinguishing among the voters between the dull and the able, the foolish and the sound of judgement, etc.)

While a passing look at the rulings of the Qur'an and Sunna clearly shows us that Islam has never regarded the majority as a criterion that judges things to be lawful or unlawful, defines truth and falsehood, and distinguishes good from evil. For the Qur'an has rejected that categorically, saying: **"And if you obey most of those upon the earth, they will mislead you from the way of God. They follow nothing but conjecture, and they are only guessing"** [al-Anʿām, 6:116].

So it has no weight at all, then, in the matters on which the Islamic Sharia has decided with a ruling. Of such, the poet Muhammad Iqbal also said:

> *Ke az maghz-e do ṣad khar fikr-e insānī namī āyad*

The thought of a single human being does not come out of the brains of two hundred donkeys.

As for what is besides that, Islam has not rejected it outright; rather it can be considered as a Sharia criterion in two matters of religion:

**The first:** in the permissible matters in which there are before the imam many options, and the people of the shūrā differ over them; so the majority can be considered in them as a factor giving preponderance to one of those forms and options, all of which are permissible. The evidence for that from the side of transmission is the action of ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him: shortly before his death he formed, for choosing the caliph after him, a committee consisting of six of the senior Companions — ʿUthmān, ʿAlī, ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf, Ṭalḥa, al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām and Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ, may God be pleased with them all — and he directed them to the plan of action which they would take in their affair, saying: "Consult [one another] in your affair; and if it is two and two, then go back in

[p. 238] consultation; and if it is four and two, then take the side of the majority."¹

This makes clear that al-Fārūq, may God be pleased with him, considered the majority of opinion in this issue — which can be used as evidence for considering it in others among the permissible matters.

**The second:** in the issues open to ijtihad (*al-masāʾil al-mujtahad fīhā*), which are the matters concerning which no decisive ruling has come from the Lawgiver, and the opinions of the mujtahids have differed in their ruling on the basis of differences in their understanding in explaining what is general in the Qur'an and the Sunna and clarifying what is ambiguous of them, and so on. So the majority of opinion can be considered in them as a factor giving preponderance to one of those probabilities and concepts, in the sense that the saying held by most of the imams be chosen. Some jurists have expressed this position by saying: the majority of opinion is not in itself evidence; rather we can take it as an indication, in the sense that probability prevails that the evidences of the position which most of the imams chose are preponderant; for if it were not preponderant, those eminent imams and great scholars would not have held it.

This does not mean that whenever the majority of the imams is on one side, that is the preponderant one necessarily and definitively; for there are many issues in which Imam Abū Ḥanīfa differed from the rest of the imams, standing alone on one side, with all the other imams and mujtahid jurists on the other. But I heard my father, His Eminence Shaykh Mufti Muhammad Shafiʿ (may God have mercy on him), relate from Shaykh Maḥmūd al-Ḥasan al-Deobandī (may God have mercy on him) that he said: I strongly suspect in such issues that the position of the Imam and his argument are of the utmost strength; for if it were not so, he would not have dared to oppose all the imams definitively.

The upshot is that although majority opinion is not in itself a conclusive argument or a decisive proof, there is no harm in taking it as a factor of preponderance for one of the positions when Sharia evidences conflict concerning it. From this it is correct that the constitution of the shūrā council provide that, in case of disagreement over matters permissible in the Sharia, decisions are taken by majority vote.

What deserves attention in this field is that many councils and bodies have provided

> ¹ *Al-Ṭabaqāt al-Kubrā*, al-ʿIlmiyya ed. (3/45).

[p. 239] in their constitution that if the opinions of the two positions are equal, preponderance belongs to the side in which the president is; and sometimes this is expressed by saying that the president gives a casting vote. It too has a basis in the action of ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him, since he said in the plan of action which he instructed the people of the shūrā to proceed by in the field of choosing the caliph after him: "And if the opinion of three and three agreed, then follow the side of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf."¹

Likewise it has become prevalent in the world today that they sometimes allow persons to attend the shūrā council and give opinion in it without their having a vote; and this is what is called in contemporary terminology the non-voting member (Non-Voting members). It is also permissible in the Sharia, relying on what ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him, did: that he allowed his son ʿAbd Allāh to witness the council of the people of the shūrā as a mere witness, without his opinion having weight in disagreement among them; and among what came in the plan of action which we briefly described earlier is that he said: "ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar will witness you, and he has nothing of the matter."²

### 5 – Qualities of the members of the shūrā

The third discussion in the matter of the shūrā is: are there conditions that must be present in the members of the shūrā who advise the imam in the affairs of the state, or not? We say in that: the shūrā council consists of the people who loosen and bind among the Muslims, a brief introduction to whom and to their important functions has passed before us earlier, among them the choosing of the imam and the appointment of the caliph for the Muslims. From this it becomes clear that the shūrā council does not consist of every Tom, Dick and Harry of the riff-raff of people and their rabble; rather they must have qualities, some of which are self-evident, like truthfulness and trustworthiness, alongside knowledge and insight in the affairs of the state and the tasks of government. While the Sharia has not specified any of their other qualities; rather it left that to the Muslims to choose in every age what agrees

> ¹ *Al-Ṭabaqāt al-Kubrā*, al-ʿIlmiyya ed. (3/45).
> ² *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (5/17), Book of Merits, chapter on the story of the pledge and the agreement on ʿUthmān, hadith no. 3699.

[p. 240] with its requirements and meets its needs.

But the amount we can define, even from the word itself, is that they should be people whom the Umma trusts to some degree as to their truthfulness, their trustworthiness and their insight into the interests of the Umma and its political issues.

In tribal life there was no need for electing them; rather the leaders of the tribes and the notables of the people were almost designated for this purpose. As for today, the situation has not remained as it was; so a coordinated election of them has become necessary. The Sharia has also not specified for it a special method among the methods of election prevailing today, whether by way of the electoral body (Electoral college), or on the basis of the right to vote for every adult citizen. What is preponderant with me is that if the rate of learning and political insight were high in the state, they should be elected by way of voting from every adult citizen; just as there does not appear any harm from the Sharia point of view in the elections being in stages and according to ranks, if that is what is appropriate to the condition of the people and the interest of the Umma.

### 6 – Membership of women in the consultative council

The opinions of the scholars of Islamic political jurisprudence have conflicted on whether a woman may according to the Sharia be a member of the shūrā council or not. Some hold it permissible, arguing by what came from the Prophet ﷺ: that he often consulted the women even in the general matters of the Muslims, as was so in the Treaty of al-Ḥudaybiya when the Prophet ﷺ ordered his companions to slaughter [their sacrificial animals] and shave [their heads]; and although he repeated this command three times, none of them did it. So astonishment took him when he saw this slowness in complying with his command, from those who had been eager to comply with his commands, and he had never known that from them before. So he entered upon Umm Salama and mentioned to her what he had met from the people. Umm Salama said: "O Prophet of God, would you like [them to do] that? Go out, then do not speak to any of them a word until you slaughter your sacrificial animals and call your shaver and he shaves you." So he went out and did not speak to any of them until he did that: he slaughtered his sacrificial animals and called his shaver and he shaved him. When they saw that, they stood up and slaughtered, and some of them began to shave

[p. 241] others."¹

Commenting on this argument we say: the hadith, even if it indicated his consulting the woman, at the same time is not fit by itself — and merely by his consulting a woman on some issue — to be used as evidence for permitting the woman to be a member of the shūrā council, as is obvious.

The second evidence for this position is what was of the action of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf, may God be pleased with him, as a member of that committee which ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him, formed for choosing the caliph after his death: when he withdrew and gave up his own claim, he set about striving to find the best of the Muslims to appoint. So it is mentioned that he asked whoever it was possible for him to ask of the people of the shūrā and others, and that continued three [days]; and among what history narrates to us about him is that: "Then ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf, may God be pleased with him, rose up consulting people about them [the candidates], gathering the opinion of the Muslims with the opinion of the heads of the people and their leaders, all together and scattered, in twos and singly, and gathered, secretly and openly, until he reached the secluded women in their seclusion."²

These scholars cite his going as far as the secluded women, consulting them about the caliphate, as evidence that there is no harm in the woman becoming a member of the shūrā council of the Muslims.

But I say: "This argument is weak; for in the same story it is reported after that that he even asked the children in the schools"; so just as it is not correct to argue by this sentence to make the children of the schools members of the shūrā — as is judged self-evident — likewise it is not correct to argue by it for the right of women to membership of the shūrā council either.

As for the people of the first position — the prohibition of granting them the right to membership in the shūrā council — they argued for it by what al-Tirmidhī narrated of the saying of the Prophet ﷺ: "And your affairs are [handed] to your women, then the belly of the earth is better for you than its surface."³

> ¹ *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (3/196).
> ² *Al-Bidāya wa-l-Nihāya*, Dār al-Fikr ed. (7/146).
> ³ *Sunan al-Tirmidhī*, ed. Shākir (4/529), Chapters on Trials, chapter 87, hadith no. 2266; and he said: this is a gharīb hadith.

[p. 242] But arguing by this hadith for their position is open to question; for the meaning of the hadith — and it is in the context of blame — is that authority be entrusted to them in all matters such that their word in it is the [final] word and their opinion in it is the [final] opinion, and the man becomes merely a follower of her in all affairs and issues. That does not entail that consulting them be categorically forbidden to men!

Finally, I have presented before you gentlemen these two positions and their proofs in detail with brief comments on them. Since there is no text decisively prohibiting their participation in the shūrā council of the Muslims, [membership is not ruled out]; but no two would disagree that, if they were granted membership of the shūrā, they must observe the rulings of the ḥijāb in all the details laid down by the Sharia.

### 7 – Membership of a non-Muslim in the consultative council

The fourth discussion in this regard is granting the unbeliever membership in the shūrā council of the Islamic state. Some held that it is not permissible, arguing by the following verse: **"O you who have believed, do not take as intimates those other than yourselves, for they will not spare you [any] ruin. They wish you would have hardship. Hatred has already appeared from their mouths, and what their breasts conceal is greater"** [Āl ʿImrān, 3:118].

Commenting on it we say: "This argument is not clear; for the verse aims at explaining the ruling of the unbelievers hostile to Islam, by forbidding the Muslims from taking them as intimates in their special affairs; and its restriction to the hostile among them is indicated by the events which ʿAllāma al-Ālūsī (may God have mercy on him) mentioned as the occasion of revelation of this verse, saying: From Ibn ʿAbbās, he said: Men of the Muslims used to keep connections with men of the Jews because of what was between them of neighbourliness and alliance in the Jāhiliyya; so God the Exalted revealed this verse concerning them, forbidding them to take them as intimates for fear of their being led astray. ʿAbd ibn Ḥumayd included that it was revealed concerning the hypocrites of the people of Medina: the believers were forbidden to take them as friends."¹

> ¹ *Tafsīr al-Ālūsī* = *Rūḥ al-Maʿānī* (2/253).

[p. 243] It is a clear matter in which there is no doubt: that the Muslims are forbidden from making the hostile among the unbelievers their intimates and the keepers of their secret, and from granting them membership in their shūrā council or bringing them into it. As for the non-hostile among them, who inhabit the Islamic state in non-hostility to it and live in it a safe, peaceful life, the jurists allowed them to attend the shūrā council, relying on the action of ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him: that many of the dhimmīs once attended a shūrā council of his, as ʿAllāma al-Sarakhsī mentioned in *al-Mabsūṭ*; and he said in one place in the wake of the story of a shūrā of ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him, which a Christian attended: "And in it is evidence that there is no harm in bringing some of the People of the Book into the shūrā council; for the Christian who said what he said had attended the council of ʿUmar — may God be pleased with him — for the shūrā, and this was not objected to."¹

Perhaps that was only in relation to some matters that related to the dhimmīs specifically, so he consulted some of them in it; which indicates that there is no weight for their opinion and their counsel in the public affairs of the Muslims and their social issues. As for matters related to the dhimmīs specifically, or what is of the kind of permissible matters, there is no harm in bringing them into the shūrā for it and seeking opinion from them about it.

### 8 – Legislation and law-making

Parliament or the house of representatives is what performs the task of legislation in contemporary political systems; and from this the term "legislative council" or "legislative body" is applied to it also. It enjoys in the democratic system absolute authority and complete power concerning legislation, without being committed in it to a matter or restricted by anything or confined to a limit — except for the restrictions which a constitution may impose on it in some state; but they too do not remain restrictions in the end; for parliament itself is able to lift them by amending the constitution by the majority of the opinions of the representatives and members. So it is correct, then, to say that democracy grants it complete freedom and absolute independence to legislate whatever it wishes and make law as it wants.

> ¹ *Al-Mabsūṭ* of al-Sarakhsī (24/7).

[p. 244] While Islam looks at the Qur'an and the Prophetic Sunna as a basic constitution and a fixed principal source — not capable of replacement and change — for the Islamic state; and from this it subjects to them both all that it undertakes of legislation and law-making. Having established this rule and principle, we can divide legislation in Islamic government into three kinds:

**The first:** that Islamic government undertake the implementation of the clear, decisive Islamic rulings from the Book and the Sunna, on which there is no room for disagreement and no place for ijtihad in explaining them, as a law for it. This can take one of two forms: that those rulings be in the form of written law (codified law) and the courts be obligated to judge by what is in it. The second: that the courts be obligated to judge in all disputes and cases by the rulings of the Qur'an and Sunna without those rulings being handed to them in the form of a written law or a codified constitutional document.
