So it would be among the functions of the courts then, and the duties of their judges, to refer to them in every case to learn its Sharia ruling. So that they can arrive at that, it is necessary in this form that the judges of the courts be knowledgeable about this kind of rulings of the Qur'an and the Prophetic Sunna, so that they are able to judge by them.

**The second kind:** cases whose rulings these two sources do not state; so they need, in arriving at their ruling, the faculty of ijtihad and the qualification for derivation from them; or what is general and ambiguous among them, in explaining and clarifying which two may differ. So the Islamic state undertakes their implementation in the form of law; and since they are a place for ijtihad and a scope for derivation, they therefore need a rule for preferring one of the positions and opinions when disagreement occurs in them. It is possible to solve them also by one of these two forms: that those rulings be codified, so they are implemented as a written law of the country (statute law); or the government issue a brief law requiring the courts either to choose among these positions themselves, or to follow a particular school of Islamic jurisprudence — for example, requiring those courts to judge in all disputes and pleadings according to Ḥanafī jurisprudence. To learn the Ḥanafī position on a given case, the court will need

[p. 245] to refer to its sources. It is clear that it will not actually be applied and will not be implemented in practice except if the judges of the courts are specialists versed in the Islamic sciences; otherwise that would lead to gross errors and heavy losses, as is not hidden!

In the early period of Islam the first method prevailed, by virtue of the deep learning of the judges in the Islamic sciences and their qualification for ijtihad from the Qur'an and the Prophetic Sunna directly. Later, when the judges' mastery of the Islamic sciences had declined, the government would assign them a number of muftis whom they were required to consult before deciding anything.

The most appropriate, in view of our circumstances and our contemporary situations, is that the law be implemented after codifying its texts and its articles in writing; and not leave the rulings for them as an unwritten law, in which the fears of fraud, cheating and forgery increase more — and especially in our time, in which these blameworthy traits have spread and prevailed even in judicial circles! But it is known that this enormous work needs a group of eminent jurists of the Sharia and experts, who undertake by consultation among them codifying those rulings and writing them in the form of law, as was so in the last eras of the Ottoman Caliphate, where "the Majalla of Judicial Rulings" was arranged and codified, which became a law for it and was considered a source for judicial rulings in Islamic lands for a not short period. As for today, until the codification of rulings — which needs time and the efforts of a large body of the Umma — it is possible temporarily and for a transitional period to act by the first form of the two forms mentioned just now.

There has continued to be a proposal put forward in political circles with intensity and force, namely that legislation concerning both these kinds of rulings be entrusted to parliament alone, so that it performs, by all of its members, the role of ijtihad and derivation in clarifying these rulings from the Qur'an and Sunna and defining their concepts and so on.

Our reply is that this proposal could have been accepted if the election of members of parliament among you took place on the basis of their knowledge and familiarity and their connection with the sciences of the Qur'an

[p. 246] and the Prophetic Sunna — which is impossible today; for they have become such that most of them have no connection with them, let alone having qualifications for understanding them and insight into them and ijtihad and derivation from them. So if the situation is as bad as this, then how are they to be trusted in understanding their meanings and clarifying what is general in them and specifying what is ambiguous in them?!

**The third kind:** legislation concerning the permissible matters on which the Sharia has not decided anything. Legislation in parliament today is usually about these permissible matters: so a law about the employees of the government and their privileges, and another about the municipal council and its departments, or the organization of armies and their arrangement. To guarantee its freedom from what contradicts the Sharia, it should be presented first — and before it takes on the character of law and is put into implementation — to experts of the Sharia and the scholars, so that they review it with a precise scholarly review, so that there is not found in it anything — even a clause — that contradicts its rulings.

The prevailing method today is that legislation and law-making about something usually take place in parliament and the house of representatives; then, after parliament has adopted and approved it, it is presented to the head of state or the king to approve it by signing it if he sees fit. But if he disagrees with it, the most he can do is send it to parliament to undertake sifting it another time; then if parliament held firm on its adoption after reviewing it at the direction of the president, it is presented to him another time so that he signs it, approving it whether he likes it or not!

As we have just seen, the first two kinds of legislation in the Islamic state are only completed by mastering the Islamic sciences and specializing in them, which the members of parliament today lack, as is tangibly witnessed. So they are not to be trusted in this field; rather there must be a body formed of the scholars of the Sharia who have mastered its sciences and are specialists in it, to undertake clarifying the rulings of the Sharia and interpreting them in the light of its fixed principles and its established rules. There is no harm in there being with them experts from various fields of life and specialists from its various branches, to inform them and help them understand the realities on the ground (*fiqh al-wāqiʿ*), so that they form a correct picture of them, which makes it easier for them to rule on them. Their role will be confined to informing the scholars about these realities

[p. 247] only, without their having any hand in specifying the rulings of the Sharia and interpreting them; rather the reference in it, first and last, is to those scholars and jurists only, who possess complete skill and full mastery and precise view of the principles of derivation from the four sources: the Qur'an, the Prophetic Sunna, consensus and analogy.

So this body itself undertakes the task of legislation and law-making concerning the first two matters, either directly and without need of anyone, or it performs the function of specifying rulings only, and so presents it as a proposal to the shūrā council or parliament to undertake implementing it officially if it agreed with it. But if parliament disagrees, it sends it back to the body with a clarification of what it found problematic and an explanation of the justifications for its rejection, so that it reconsiders it and reviews it with more precision and mastery, taking note of parliament's objections to it and its justifications for rejecting it. After all that, either it undertakes amendment in the draft law according to parliament's direction, or it answers its observations with a satisfying, convincing answer, so that parliament then undertakes implementing it officially and finally.

A doubt has been raised today around this proposal: that it means nothing but subjecting parliament to this body of scholars — which is a form of theocracy and papacy. I answered it earlier, specifically in the discussion of theocracy, in detail sufficient to remove this doubt and uproot it from its roots, just as I spoke at length on it in my Urdu book *Nifāẕ-e Sharīʿat aur us kā ṭarīqa-e kār* (Implementing the Sharia and its method), in which I clarified prominent essential differences that distinguish between what we are concerned with — the body of scholars — and what theocracy was upon. In brief, it is self-evident that what counts in every art is its men who are specialists in it; so if our discussion is about insight into the sciences of the Qur'an and the Sunna and explaining and interpreting it and deriving rulings from it, why then not rely on the scholars in it, when they have spent their lives in the way of acquiring its sciences, sparing nothing, however precious, until they mastered them and grasped of them the amount with which sufficiency is achieved in leading the Umma and steering its wheel according to the rulings of the pure, tolerant Sharia, without distortion, forgery, flattery or fawning? For more detail on the difference between the role of the scholars of Islam

[p. 248] in government — and especially in the field of legislation — and what Christian theocracy was upon in the field of authority and rule, the reader may refer to what we presented in the discussion of theocracy in the first part of this book.

What has prevailed today in the patterns of contemporary government — that the head of state or the king is entirely subject to the house of representatives such that they are compelled to sign the decision which parliament issued even against their will and their non-agreement to it — contradicts the principle of respect for the freedom of conscience altogether, and makes the president merely a symbolic head. So he ought to be allowed to reject it and not sign it if he did not agree with it in principle; and in this form, let the decision be taken and the legislation completed by the signature of the speaker of parliament and the legislative council — avoiding the occurrence of the situation which Queen Victoria faced when a law was presented to her with which she did not agree, yet she approved it, saying: "I sign it while I believe it to be wrong."

On another side, the law is not completed in its final form except after its passing through many stages in which some deficiencies may creep into it from the Sharia point of view. So it appears necessary that every citizen be allowed to challenge a law on the basis of its contradicting the Sharia and its clashing with it, in the supreme constitutional court which includes the specialists in the Sharia sciences with experience and insight in the rulings of the Qur'an and Sunna, so that they undertake to hear the position of both parties: the complainant and the representative of the government bodies, and to rule on the soundness of the complaint or not in the light of its proofs. So if the complaint was sound and it was established to the court that the law or a clause of it contradicts the Sharia and clashes with its principles, let it order the government to cancel it or amend it so that it agrees with the requirements of the Sharia. The court's competence to rule in such a case will resemble what is established in America: that the court is distinguished by the authority which permits it to cancel a law which parliament adopted and approved.

Pakistan has established the two constitutional institutions "the Federal Shariat Court" and "the Shariat Appellate Bench of the Supreme Court" for this purpose; and we would

[p. 249] have found them sufficient in this field were it not for many evils and deficiencies that exist in their administrative structure — among them the shortage of the number of men of knowledge and experts in the Sharia in them; and second, that the power to appoint the scholars to them and remove them rests entirely with the head of state. So the executive can obtain rulings to its liking by removing members who will not endorse a government law that they regard as contrary to the Sharia — as has actually happened in the past. If these two defects were removed, these two institutions would play a major role in giving laws a religious form and casting them in an Islamic mould.

### 9 – The scope and limits of obedience to the ruler

The Islamic Sharia makes it obligatory on the subjects to obey the imam who was chosen and designated as imam of the Muslims and their caliph on the basis of the details which we mentioned earlier with elaboration and clear explanation — provided [his command] is within the framework of the rulings of the Qur'an and the Prophetic Sunna, not contrary to them in anything. The evidence for that is what God, glorified and exalted, said: **"O you who have believed, obey God and obey the Messenger and those in authority among you"** [al-Nisāʾ, 4:59]. So God commanded in this verse obedience to those in authority (the caliphs and their officials) alongside obedience to God and obedience to His Messenger ﷺ. So it became clear from it that whatever was of the commands of God, glorified and exalted, or the commands of His Messenger ﷺ is obligatory on all the Muslims, including the imam and the holders of authority and others. As for the ruling of obedience to the commander, it is directed to the Muslims in two cases:

**The first:** if the imam issues a specific order to do something permissible in the Sharia, it is obligatory on the Muslims to do it because the imam has ordered it. That is as if the imam commanded his subjects to fast a day, for example: it is obligatory on them to fast that day in performance of the obligation of obeying the imam — as ʿAllāma Ibn ʿĀbidīn al-Shāmī (may God have mercy on him) said, attributing it to more than one of the jurists: "That obedience to the imam in what is not disobedience is obligatory; so if he ordered fasting, it became obligatory."¹

> ¹ *Al-Durr al-Mukhtār* and the gloss of Ibn ʿĀbidīn (*Radd al-Muḥtār*) (5/422).

[p. 250] Likewise if the imam forbade a permissible matter: it is obligatory on the subjects to desist from it merely by the imam's prohibition concerning it.

The meaning of that is not that the state of that Sharia-permissible thing has changed; rather it still remains, from the Sharia point of view, in its character, I mean permissibility; but its ruling [for the subjects] has changed after the imam's command or prohibition, solely because of the obligation to obey the imam.

Proceeding from this it is obligatory on the subjects to comply with the traffic rules issued by the governmental bodies and administrative authorities, as being commands of the imam or his officials in permissible matters.

But alongside that, the jurists have also established this principle with all explicitness and clarity: that "the disposal of the imam over the subjects is tied to the interest (*maṣlaḥa*)"¹ — in the sense that obedience to him is only obligatory in the commands which he issued out of regard for the interest of the subjects and concern for them. As for orders issued unjustly and tyrannically against the subjects, or for the purpose of exhausting them and constraining them, obedience to them is not obligatory in anything. Its basis is what God commanded David, upon him be peace, saying: **"O David, indeed We have made you a vicegerent upon the earth, so judge between the people in truth"** [Ṣād, 38:26]; and what is narrated that the Prophet ﷺ sent an expedition and put over them a man of the Anṣār, and ordered them to obey him. He became angry with them and said: Did not the Prophet ﷺ order you to obey me? They said: Yes. He said: I resolve upon you that you gather firewood and kindle a fire, then enter it. So they gathered firewood and kindled a fire; and when they were about to enter, they stood looking at one another. Some of them said: We only followed the Prophet ﷺ fleeing from the fire; shall we enter it? While they were like that, the fire died down and his anger subsided; and it was mentioned to the Prophet ﷺ, and he said: "Had they entered it they would never have come out of it; obedience is only in what is right (*al-maʿrūf*)."²

**The second case:** that the imam, if he commanded something in the issues open to ijtihad in which the opinions of the jurists have differed and diverged, then it becomes incumbent on all to comply with what the imam commanded, even if it is

> ¹ *Al-Ashbāh wa-l-Naẓāʾir* of Ibn Nujaym (1/104).
> ² *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (9/63), Book of Judgements, hadith no. 7145; and Book of Military Expeditions, chapter on the expedition of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ḥudhāfa, hadith no. 4340.

[p. 251] contrary to his opinion and his school. This is what the jurists expressed by saying: "The ruling of the ruler removes disagreement" (*ḥukm al-ḥākim rāfiʿ li-l-khilāf*). To explain: if an issue is disputed among the jurists, with divergent opinions and conflicting positions, and the Sharia permits the followers of each jurist to follow him in it, and then the imam issues a specific decree choosing one of those positions — his ruling is implemented on the subjects in their entirety, such that it becomes incumbent on all to act by it regardless of what is preponderant with him and with his imam whom he follows and imitates in the partial issues. As an example of that we present the issue of the number of additional takbīrs in the ʿĪd prayer: it is narrated from Ibn Masʿūd, may God be pleased with him, that they are three in every rakʿa — and it is the position which Imam Abū Ḥanīfa (may God have mercy on him) chose — while it is narrated from Ibn ʿAbbās, may God be pleased with him, that they are seven in the first rakʿa and five in the second — and it is the position which Imam al-Shāfiʿī (may God have mercy on him) chose. Despite that, it is authentically established from the two eminent imams, pillars of Ḥanafī jurisprudence, Abū Yūsuf and Muḥammad (may God have mercy on them both), that they did takbīr in the ʿĪd seven and five according to the Shāfiʿī school; and that was in the wake of the command of the Caliph Hārūn al-Rashīd with it because it was the school of his ancestor Ibn ʿAbbās, may God be pleased with him. ʿAllāma Ibn ʿĀbidīn (may God have mercy on him) said: In *al-Ẓahīriyya* he said: "And the interpretation of what is narrated from Abū Yūsuf and Muḥammad, that they did that, is that Hārūn ordered them to do takbīr with the takbīr of his ancestor; so they did that in compliance with him, not as a school and belief."¹

All that has passed of the obligation of obeying the imam is only in permissible matters and those open to ijtihad. As for if he ruled by something that contradicts the established rulings of the Sharia by consensus, then his ruling is not implemented and obeying him in it is not permissible; rather the following principle applies: no obedience to a creature in disobedience to the Creator — which is taken and derived from the following verse: **"But if they endeavour to make you associate with Me that of which you have no knowledge, do not obey them but accompany them in [this] world with appropriate kindness"** [Luqmān, 31:15].

And especially what is narrated from the Prophet ﷺ concerning obedience to the commander, that he said: "Hearing and obeying is upon the Muslim person in what he loves and dislikes, as long as he is not commanded with disobedience; so if he is commanded with disobedience, then there is no hearing and no

> ¹ *Al-Durr al-Mukhtār* and the gloss of Ibn ʿĀbidīn (*Radd al-Muḥtār*) (2/172).

[p. 252] obeying."¹

The imam deserves his removal for the like of issuing this ruling, according to the details which we shall explain in their place from the discussion of removing the imam and deposing him in Chapter Six, God willing.

The same ruling as that of the imam, just explained in detail, applies to parliament, the legislative council and the legislative body if they adopt such a law. So it is established from this what we said earlier: that there must be a constitutional body that undertakes cancelling the like of these laws and commands or amending them so that they agree fully with the principles of the Sharia.

### 10 – Freedom of criticism in Islam

The ruling of the obligation of obeying the commander does not mean that criticism of a step the imam took or a work he undertook is categorically forbidden. Rather Islamic government founded on straight foundations grants the people complete freedom in criticizing the imam — and especially if they saw from him what contradicts the rulings of the Sharia and opposes its fixed principles; and in this case they are also allowed to take him to court, to be questioned about it, and to have those of his commands that contradict the Book and the Prophetic Sunna annulled. For the imamate is not a rank that makes its holder exceed the limits of human nature and become infallible from error in undertaking and free from mistake in the matter. Accordingly came the command to consult the believers, so that each gives his opinion before the imam with all freedom and firmness without fear, blame or regret.

The Prophet ﷺ forbade the generality of the Muslims from following the imam in what contradicts the Sharia; rather he commanded them to strive to set him right as far as they could, as came in the hadith from Kaʿb ibn ʿUjra; he said: The Messenger of God ﷺ came out to us and we were nine; he said: "There will be after me commanders; whoever believes them in their lying and helps them in their injustice, he is not of me and I am not of him, and he will not come to me at the Pool. Whoever does not believe them in their lying and does not help them in their injustice, he is of me and I am of him, and he will come to me at

> ¹ *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (9/63), Book of Judgements, chapter on hearing and obedience to the imam, hadith no. 7144.

[p. 253] the Pool."¹

Likewise what al-Bukhārī narrated from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī, from the Prophet ﷺ; he said: "No caliph is appointed but he has two intimate circles: a circle that commands him with good and urges him on it, and a circle that commands him with evil and urges him on it; and the protected one is whom God protects."²

What al-Haythamī mentioned in *Majmaʿ al-Zawāʾid*, saying: And from Ibn ʿUmar he said: the Messenger of God ﷺ said: "Whoever attends an imam, let him say good or be silent."

Al-Ṭabarānī narrated it in *al-Awsaṭ*, and in it is Ṣāliḥ ibn Muḥammad ibn Ziyād, whom Aḥmad and others declared trustworthy and a group declared weak; and the rest of its men are men of the Ṣaḥīḥ.³

What al-Tirmidhī included from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī, that the Prophet ﷺ said: "Among the greatest jihad is a word of justice before a tyrannical sultan."⁴

What Ibn ʿAbbās, may God be pleased with him, narrated from the Prophet ﷺ that he said: "It is not fitting for a man to stand in a place where there is a word of truth to be said without saying it; for it will not bring forward his term nor deprive him of provision that is his."⁵

What is narrated from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī, that the Prophet ﷺ said: "Let none of you belittle himself, seeing a matter concerning which there is a word due to God upon him, and he does not say it; so he meets God, mighty and majestic, having lost that, and He says: What prevented you? He says: I feared the people. He says: It was Me whom you more rightly should have feared."⁶

> ¹ *Sunan al-Nasāʾī* (7/160), Book of the Pledge, mention of the threat for one who helps a commander in injustice, hadith no. 4212.
> ² *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (8/125), Book of Judgements, chapter on the intimate circle of the imam, no. 7198.
> ³ *Majmaʿ al-Zawāʾid wa-Manbaʿ al-Fawāʾid* (5/231), Book of the Caliphate, chapter on speaking the truth before the imams, hadith no. 9167.
> ⁴ *Sunan al-Tirmidhī*, ed. Shākir (4/471), Book of Trials, hadith no. 2174.
> ⁵ It came in different wordings in the *Sunan* of Abū Dāwūd, Book of Battles, hadith no. 4344, and of Ibn Māja, no. 4011.
> ⁶ Narrated by Abū Dāwūd al-Ṭayālisī in his *Musnad* with a sound chain, and this wording is his; and Abū Yaʿlā al-Mawṣilī and from him Ibn Ḥibbān in his *Ṣaḥīḥ*; and narrated by Aḥmad ibn Manīʿ and ʿAbd ibn Ḥumayd and Ibn Māja in abridged form; *Itḥāf al-Khiyara al-Mahara bi-Zawāʾid al-Masānīd al-ʿAshara*, Book of Trials, chapter on commanding right and forbidding wrong, hadith no. 7402, Dār al-Waṭan in Riyadh.

[p. 254] So in view of these hadiths, the Rightly-Guided Caliphs called on the subjects to direct criticism at them, and accepted it with broad-mindedness whenever it was in fact directed at them. So here is Abū Bakr, may God be pleased with him: no sooner had he taken over the command of the believers and received the reins of the imamate over them than he stood among them delivering his sermon, which is the first brick and the basic step for founding the Rightly-Guided Caliphate and the white lighthouse for whoever follows it and tries to walk on its course. History has preserved for us that he said: "To proceed: O people, I have been put in charge of you and I am not the best of you. If I do well, help me; and if I do wrong, set me right. Truthfulness is trustworthiness, and lying is treachery. The weak among you is strong with me until I remove his grievance, God willing; and the strong among you is weak with me until I take the right from him, God willing… Obey me as long as I obey God and His Messenger; and if I disobey God and His Messenger, there is no obedience owed to me by you."¹

How excellent he was: he declared in it with the utmost concision and eloquence the landmarks of the way for the Islamic caliphate, and clarified the status of the Commander of the Faithful and his basic qualities, and called the Muslims to direct criticism at him and at his commands — while he is their imam and the first successor of their Messenger ﷺ. So by that he established a good precedent which those of the just Rightly-Guided Caliphs after him followed with completeness and fidelity. So look at ʿUmar al-Fārūq, may God be pleased with him, saying in one of his sermons before the heads of the masses: "For I am one like you; and today you are to speak the truth, whether that means disagreeing with me or agreeing with me; I do not want you to follow this whim of mine."²

This was not merely a phrase he tossed off or a sentence he embellished and threw to the winds; he also put it into practice in the most splendid way — for though he was the imam of the Muslims in the furthest and nearest parts of the inhabited world, and a man of formidable power and firm opinion, he accepted with broad-mindedness every stinging criticism directed at him. Here is one story of this, which al-Qāḍī Abū Yūsuf narrated from al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī: that a man said to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb: "Fear God, O ʿUmar"³ — and he repeated it much to him. A speaker said to him: Be silent, you have said too much to the Commander of the Faithful. ʿUmar said to him: Leave him; there is no good in them if they

> ¹ *Al-Bidāya wa-l-Nihāya*, Dār al-Fikr ed. (5/248).
> ² *Al-Kharāj* of Abū Yūsuf (1/36), section on the fayʾ and the kharāj.
> ³ An expression usually used to rebuke sharply someone who has erred.

[p. 255] do not say it to us, and there is no good in us if we do not accept [it]. And he came close to rebuking the one who had silenced him."¹

As for his story in which he retracted his position upon a woman's objection to him and regarded her as more knowledgeable than him, let the books of hadith narrate it to us with its splendid, shining details. It came in *al-Sunan al-Kubrā* of al-Bayhaqī: ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, may God be pleased with him, addressed the people, praised God the Exalted and extolled Him, and said: "Do not exceed bounds in the dowries of women; for it does not reach me that anyone gave more than what the Messenger of God ﷺ gave or what was given to him, except that I put the excess of that in the public treasury." Then he came down, and a woman of Quraysh presented herself to him and said: O Commander of the Faithful, is the Book of God the Exalted more deserving to be followed, or your statement? He said: "Rather the Book of God the Exalted; what is that?" She said: You forbade the people just now from exceeding bounds in the dowries of women, while God the Exalted says in His Book: **"And you have given one of them a great amount [qinṭār], do not take [back] from it anything"** [al-Nisāʾ, 4:20]. So ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him, said: "Everyone is more knowledgeable than ʿUmar" — two or three times; then he returned to the pulpit and said to the people: "I had forbidden you to exceed bounds in the dowries of women; let a man do with his property whatever seems right to him."²

Al-Bayhaqī also narrated from Abū Hurayra; he said: When ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, may God be pleased with him, wanted to enlarge the mosque of the Messenger of God ﷺ, his enlargement fell upon the house of al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, may God be pleased with him. So ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him, wanted to bring it into the mosque of the Messenger of God ﷺ and compensate him for it; but he refused, and said: [It is] a grant (*qaṭīʿa*) of the Messenger of God ﷺ. They differed, so they made Ubayy ibn Kaʿb, may God be pleased with them, [judge] between them. So they came to him in his house, and he was called the master of the Muslims; he ordered a cushion for them and it was thrown for them; so they sat before him, and ʿUmar mentioned what he wanted, and al-ʿAbbās mentioned the grant of the Messenger of God ﷺ. Ubayy said:… [He ruled for al-ʿAbbās, so] al-ʿAbbās said: "O God, I do not take a reward for it; I have given it in charity to the community of the Muslims." So ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him, accepted it from him and brought it into the mosque of the Messenger of God ﷺ.³

More astonishing than that is what Ibn Saʿd narrated in the *Ṭabaqāt* with his chain from Yaʿqūb ibn Zayd: that

> ¹ *Al-Kharāj* of Abū Yūsuf (1/22).
> ² *Al-Sunan al-Kubrā* of al-Bayhaqī (7/380), Book of the Dowry, no. 14336.
> ³ *Al-Sunan al-Kubrā* of al-Bayhaqī (6/277), before the Book of Gifts, no. 11937; and *al-Ṭabaqāt al-Kubrā* of Ibn Saʿd 1914, biography of al-ʿAbbās, may God be pleased with him.

[p. 256] ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb went out on a Friday and the gutter of al-ʿAbbās dripped on him — and it was on ʿUmar's way to the mosque — so ʿUmar pulled it out. Al-ʿAbbās said to him: You have pulled out my gutter, and by God it was not put where it was except by the Messenger of God ﷺ with his hand. ʿUmar said: Then certainly it shall not be that you have any ladder other than me, and no one shall put it [back] except you with your hand. He said: So al-ʿAbbās climbed on ʿUmar's neck, put his feet on ʿUmar's shoulders, then put the gutter back where it was and placed it in its place.¹ And the gutter remained there on the western side of the mosque until this day of ours.

This splendid, beautiful moral conduct was not confined to the era of the Rightly-Guided Caliphate; rather the shining, bright Islamic history is full of the like of these events which the just among the caliphs of the Muslims wove over the passage of ages and the succession of eras. To give evidence of that we shall content ourselves here with relating two of them:

The summary of the first story: that in the year four hundred and twenty-nine, in the month of Ramaḍān, the Caliph ordered that there be added to the titles of Jalāl al-Dawla ibn Buwayh: "Shāhanshāh al-Aʿẓam Malik al-Mulūk" [the Greatest King of Kings, King of Kings], and the Friday sermon was pronounced in his name with them. Some of the jurists gave a fatwa forbidding that, and that "King of Kings" is not said except of God; and the common people followed them and threw bricks at the preachers.

It was written to the jurists about that … and al-Māwardī gave a fatwa forbidding [it] and was strict in that; and al-Māwardī was among the close associates of Jalāl al-Dawla. When he gave the fatwa forbidding, he cut himself off from him. Then Jalāl al-Dawla summoned him, and he went to him in great fear. When he entered, he said to him: I have become certain that if you favoured anyone you would have favoured me, because of what is between me and you; and nothing carried you except religion. and by that your standing with me has only increased.²

The second story al-Subkī also mentioned in the biography of Shaykh ʿIzz al-Dīn ibn ʿAbd al-Salām, whose summary: that, regarding a group of the state's Turkish commanders, it had not been established to the Shaykh that they were free, so the ruling of slavery still applied to them, as property of the public treasury of the Muslims. So that reached them, and the matter became serious with them over it, and the matter flared; and the Shaykh was determined that neither sale nor purchase is valid for them

> ¹ *Al-Ṭabaqāt al-Kubrā*, Dār Ṣādir ed. (4/20), biography of al-ʿAbbās, may God be pleased with him.
> ² *Ṭabaqāt al-Shāfiʿiyya al-Kubrā* of al-Subkī (5/271), biography of ʿAlī ibn Muḥammad ibn Ḥabīb Abū al-Ḥasan al-Māwardī.

[p. 257] nor marriage, and their interests were suspended by that. Among them was the deputy of the Sultanate, so he flared up in anger. They gathered and sent to him; he said: We will hold a council for you and you will be called out [for sale] on behalf of the public treasury of the Muslims, and your emancipation will be obtained by a Sharia way. So they raised the matter to the Sultan, and he sent for him, but he did not retract. A word burst out from the Sultan in which there was harshness, whose upshot was disapproval of the Shaykh's entering into this matter, and that it does not concern him. So the Shaykh became angry, carried his belongings on a donkey, and put his family on another donkey, and walked behind them, going out from Cairo heading towards Syria… So the Sultan rode himself and caught up with him and sought to please him and soothed his heart; so he returned, and they agreed with him that he would call out [for sale] the commanders. So the deputy of the Sultanate sent to him with gentleness, but it did not avail in him; so the deputy was disturbed and said: How does this shaykh call us out and sell us when we are the kings of the earth? By God I will strike him with this sword of mine! So he rode himself in his company and came to the house of the Shaykh with the sword drawn in his hand, and knocked on the door. The son of the Shaykh — I think [he is] ʿAbd al-Laṭīf — came out and saw from the deputy of the Sultanate what he saw; so he returned to his father and explained the situation to him. He did not care about that nor change, and said: O my son, your father is too insignificant to be granted death in the way of God. Then he came out as if the decree of God had descended upon the deputy of the Sultanate; when his gaze fell on him, the hand of the deputy dried up and the sword fell from it and his joints trembled. So he wept and asked the Shaykh to pray for him, and said: O my master, what news? What will you do? He said: I will call you out and sell you. He said: And in what will our price be spent? He said: In the interests of the Muslims. He said: Who will take it? He said: I. So what he wanted was completed for him, and he called out the commanders one by one and raised high their price, and took it and spent it in the ways of good. This is something the like of which was not heard of anyone — may God the Exalted have mercy on him and be pleased with him.¹

### 11 – Between criticism and insult

But despite that, the Islamic Sharia has drawn a clear dividing line that distinguishes between what is criticism of the sultan and what is regarded as an insult to him. It permits and indeed urges the former, while forbidding the latter with the most severe prohibition. That is as al-Tirmidhī included with his chain from Ziyād ibn Kusayb al-ʿAdawī; he said: I was with Abū Bakra under the pulpit of Ibn ʿĀmir while he was giving a sermon, and he was wearing thin clothes.

> ¹ *Ṭabaqāt al-Shāfiʿiyya al-Kubrā* of al-Subkī (8/216).

[p. 258] Abū Bilāl said: Look at our commander wearing the clothes of the transgressors. Abū Bakra said: Be silent; I heard the Messenger of God ﷺ say: "Whoever insults the sultan of God on the earth, God insults him."¹

This makes clear that criticism of the sultan must be within its special framework and its limits established for it in the Sharia, without exceeding them and going beyond them so as to turn from it into an insult that is categorically forbidden in the Sharia.

Aḥmad narrated it in his *Musnad* in a fuller wording than this, saying: From Abū Bakra; he said: I heard the Messenger of God ﷺ say: "Whoever honours the sultan of God in this world, God honours him on the Day of Resurrection; and whoever insults the sultan of God in this world, God insults him on the Day of Resurrection."²

Although there is some criticism of the hadith's chain, many of the hadith scholars have narrated it with different chains and many routes, which makes it fit to be used as evidence that insulting the sultan is not permissible in the Sharia. The essential difference between them lies in the aim which its doer wants behind undertaking it: if it was counsel and reform and seeking the pleasure of God — not for humiliation nor for display — then it is constructive; otherwise it is insult or showing off. as is clear from what Aḥmad narrated with his chain from ʿIyāḍ ibn Ghanm, may God be pleased with him, that he heard the Messenger of God ﷺ say: "Whoever wants to counsel a holder of authority on a matter, let him not show it openly to him; but let him take him by his hand and be alone with him; if he accepts it from him, so be it; otherwise he has fulfilled what was upon him."³

### 12 – Spending the funds of the public treasury

The government, whatever it may be, needs, in administering public affairs and performing its duties, money and exorbitant sums — what is called "the state treasury" in the general political terminology, while Islam assigns to it the term "*bayt al-māl*" (the public treasury). The treasury of the state before the dawn of Islam was usually regarded by kings as their purely personal property; so they dealt with it as they dealt with their own property,

> ¹ *Sunan al-Tirmidhī*, ed. Shākir (4/502), and he said: this is a gharīb hadith; Chapters on Trials, hadith no. 2224.
> ² *Musnad Aḥmad*, al-Risāla ed. (34/79), hadith no. 20433.
> ³ *Majmaʿ al-Zawāʾid wa-Manbaʿ al-Fawāʾid* (5/229), Book of the Caliphate, chapter on counsel to the imams and its manner, hadith no. 1961, 1962.

[p. 259] collecting it however they wished by imposing heavy taxes and crushing levies on the subjects, and spending it on whatever object they wished; and it no longer had a role except to guarantee them luxury of living and provide them with the satisfaction of desires.

Islam from the very beginning regarded it as a public trust in the hands of the imam which he must spend on the subjects and expend in their interests. Then it did not leave the imam independent and despotic in collecting funds and spending them; rather it imposed heavy restrictions on him in this regard: it defined first the special resources and sources for them — namely zakat, the tithe (*ʿushr*), the land tax (*kharāj*), the *fayʾ*, lost property and so on. Then it divided them into kinds and specified for each kind of them a special object of expenditure second. So the funds of zakat and charities have with Islam their special object of expenditure, and it is not permitted to spend them in other than it; and likewise for the land tax, the jizya and the other sources.

Proceeding from this, the jurists of Islam undertook codifying its rulings from both these aspects — collecting funds and spending them — with all elaboration and clarity in the light of the rulings of the Qur'an and the teachings of the Prophetic Sunna and the conduct of the Rightly-Guided Caliphs. So you see the Islamic library flourishing with many books in which the eminent scholars devoted works to this chapter — among them *Kitāb al-Kharāj* of each of the two eminent imams Abū Yūsuf and Yaḥyā ibn Ādam (may God have mercy on them both), and *Kitāb al-Amwāl* of Imam Abū ʿUbayd Qāsim ibn Sallām (may God have mercy on him) — which have remained original sources and principal references for whoever came after them of jurists and scholars.

It is narrated from ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him, that he said in one of his sermons, explaining how the public treasury should be run: "And I do not find that this wealth is set right except by three traits: that it be taken by right, and given in right, and withheld from falsehood. I and your wealth are only like the guardian of the orphan: if I am rich I abstain, and if I am poor I eat in what is right (*bi-l-maʿrūf*)."¹

In a report of Ibn Saʿd this addition also came: "If I become well-off, I repay."²

> ¹ *Al-Kharāj* of Abū Yūsuf (1/130).
> ² *Al-Ṭabaqāt al-Kubrā* of Ibn Saʿd (3/256), mention of the caliphate of ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him.

[p. 260] The caliphs always put before their eyes that the funds of the Muslims are a trust in their hands which they must administer with all caution and scrupulousness. So there issued from them, concerning dealing with them, splendid stories which are a white blaze on the brow of humanity and a shining pearl on the breast of humanity over the passage of ages and the succession of eras. Come, let us turn our gaze to a story of them which explains to us how the caliph began to take a stipend from the public treasury of the Muslims. Ibn Saʿd narrated in his *Ṭabaqāt* that when Abū Bakr was appointed successor, he set out in the morning to the market with garments on his neck which he traded in. ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb and Abū ʿUbayda ibn al-Jarrāḥ met him and said to him: Where do you want [to go], O successor of the Messenger of God? He said: The market. They said: What will you do, when you have been put in charge of the affair of the Muslims? He said: Then from where will I feed my dependants? They said to him: Go, so that we assign something for you. So he went with them, and they assigned for him every day half a sheep, and what clothes him on the head and the belly.¹

Thus he began to obtain his stipend from the public treasury. But let us advance a little and cast a look at what al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī (may God have mercy on him) narrates, which indicates that he did not make it a pleasant morsel which he and his dependants enjoyed; rather he accepted it against his will, amid the fears and anxiety that beset him, to the point that he used to say: "Woe to ʿUmar! I fear that it will not be permissible for me to eat anything of this wealth."

Al-Ḥasan continued, saying: "So he spent in two years and some [more] eight thousand dirhams. When death came to him he said: I had said to ʿUmar: I fear that it will not be permissible for me to eat anything of this wealth, but he prevailed over me. So when I die, take from my property eight thousand dirhams and return them to the public treasury. When they were brought to ʿUmar he said: May God have mercy on Abū Bakr; he has wearied those after him with a severe weariness."²

As for ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him, his manner of dealing with the public treasury becomes clear from what he once said in a sermon he delivered before the people:

"I will tell you what I consider permissible of it for me: two garments are permissible for me — one for winter and one for summer — and what I perform the Ḥajj and the ʿUmra on of mounts, and my food and the food of my family like the food of a man of Quraysh,

> ¹ *Al-Ṭabaqāt al-Kubrā*, Dār Ṣādir ed. (3/184).
> ² *Kanz al-ʿUmmāl* (5/600), no. 14062.

[p. 261] not of the richest of them nor of the poorest of them. Then I after that am a man of the Muslims; what befalls them befalls me."¹

But he did not sit at ease on this small amount either; rather it is narrated that when the people were struck by a year [of famine] in which ghee became expensive, ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him, ate oil, and his belly rumbled. In a report of Yaḥyā he said: "ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him, used to eat it; when it became scarce he said: I will not eat it until the people eat it." He said: So he used to eat oil, and his belly rumbled. Ibn Mukarram said in his report: so he said: Rumble as you wish, for by God I will not eat ghee until the people eat it.²

So these are splendid models of the scrupulousness of the Rightly-Guided Caliphs and their caution in the funds of the Muslims, the like of which the world did not witness before them nor after them. But nevertheless the Islamic Sharia does not obligate every caliph to follow them exactly; rather, looking to what motivated them, it holds that the purpose of all this is that the stipends of caliphs and rulers should not make them a burden on the public treasury, and should not exceed their needs through extravagance and waste. Otherwise the Prophet ﷺ himself assigned as provision to ʿAttāb ibn Asīd, when he appointed him over Mecca, forty ūqiyya [of silver] every year.³

This amount equals one thousand four hundred dirhams annually — which shows that the Sharia did not specify a special amount for stipends; rather decrease and increase in it are possible with the difference of time and place and in view of circumstances and situations, on condition that that not be taken as a means of extravagance and an instrument of waste.

### The ruling on taxes in Islam

The Islamic Sharia has regarded imposing taxes on the subjects in the general run of cases as injustice and tyranny, and severe threats have come against it — contenting itself with what it specified for the public treasury of sources of revenue, like zakat, charities, the land tax, the jizya and so on. But the Muslims, if a calamity struck them and emergency states befell the Islamic state, so that its funds

> ¹ *Al-Ṭabaqāt al-Kubrā*, Dār Ṣādir ed. (3/275), mention of the appointment of ʿUmar as successor.
> ² *Al-Sunan al-Kubrā* of al-Bayhaqī (9/73).
> ³ *Al-Sunan al-Kubrā* of al-Bayhaqī (6/578).

[p. 262] hardly sufficed the needs of the Muslims and their social interests — then in such a special exceptional case and for the pressing necessity of the Islamic state, the jurists permitted, with extreme caution, burdening the subjects with a tax to the extent of its necessity and its needs only, observing in that gentleness with the subjects to the utmost, and scrupulously avoiding injustice and oppressive burdens. It is called in the terminology of the jurists "the emergency tax" (*al-ḍarība al-nāʾiba*). It is stipulated for that, first and last, that it be for necessity, and so limited to the extent of that necessity; and that the people not be charged with it except after the rulers have abandoned what they are upon of the life of luxury and extravagant living; and on condition that they be truthful of disposition and sound of intention in that. The jurists set out this question amid real fears that tyrannical rulers would use it as a means to achieve their ambitions and as a cover for collecting tolls (*mukūs*) and unjust levies from the people — as ʿAllāma Ibn ʿĀbidīn (may God have mercy on him) said: And Abū Jaʿfar al-Balkhī said: What the sultan imposes on the subjects as an interest for them becomes an obligatory debt and a deserved right, like the land tax. Our shaykhs said: And everything the imam imposes on them for an interest for them, the answer is thus — even the wage of the guards for guarding the road and [against] thieves, and the setting up of gates and the gates of streets. This is to be known [by the scholars] but not made public, for fear of strife. Then he said: So on this, what is taken in Khwarazm from the common people for repairing the dam of the Oxus or the outpost and the like of it of the interests of the common people is an obligatory debt; it is not permissible to refuse it, and it is not an injustice. But this answer is to be known so as to act on it and to refrain from speaking against the sultan and his agents in this — not to be publicized, lest they dare to increase beyond the deserved amount. End.

He followed it by saying: I say [the speaker: Ibn ʿĀbidīn]: "And it is fitting to restrict that to [cases] where there is not found in the public treasury what suffices for that."¹

What is worth noting here is that there is a hadith which Abū Dāwūd narrated with his chain from ʿUqba ibn ʿĀmir; he said: I heard the Messenger of God ﷺ say: "The collector of tolls (*ṣāḥib maks*) will not enter Paradise."² And the word is usually explained as tax; so from this some sometimes imagine that imposing a tax on the subjects

> ¹ *Al-Durr al-Mukhtār* and the gloss of Ibn ʿĀbidīn (*Radd al-Muḥtār*) (2/337), Book of Zakat, before the chapter on the object of expenditure.
> ² *Sunan Abī Dāwūd* (3/132).

[p. 263] is forbidden absolutely and in every case, including the special exceptional case in which the jurists permitted it, and specifically what passed in the words of Ibn ʿĀbidīn (may God have mercy on him). The truth is not so; for the hadith scholars and the jurists explained the word in different ways. Here is Imam Abū ʿUbayd Qāsim ibn Sallām saying: It had a root in the Jāhiliyya, which the kings of the Arabs and the non-Arabs all did; and it was their practice to take from the merchants one tenth of their goods when they passed through their lands with them.¹

ʿAllāma al-Ṭaḥāwī explained it as follows: that the tithe which the Messenger of God ﷺ lifted from the Muslims is the tithe that used to be taken in the Jāhiliyya, and it is other than zakat; and they used to call it *al-maks*, and it is what ʿUqba ibn ʿĀmir narrated concerning it from the Prophet ﷺ.²

There is no doubt that what prevailed in the circles of kings and emperors in the Jāhiliyya — of burdening the subjects with heavy taxes and accumulated tolls — was injustice and tyranny and an injury from them against the subjects. The ambitions behind imposing them were nothing but saving money and piling it up in their personal treasuries, so that luxury of living, extravagance and indulgence in their lives became possible for them. The two passages quoted above make clear that the threat in the hadith is aimed at imposing this kind of tax; and it also covers what governments impose today without a Sharia necessity to justify it. As for the emergency tax which the jurists permitted and which ʿAllāma al-Shāmī mentioned, it is not correct to draw an analogy with that, by virtue of its aiming at meeting the pressing need of the Islamic state and observing the clear interest of the subjects; so it does not enter into "*al-maks*", and hence the threat of the aforementioned hadith does not apply to it.

Imam al-Ghazālī (may God have mercy on him) spoke at length on it: after he finished denouncing the rulers who impose taxes merely to pamper the army and make their own living luxurious, he followed it by saying: "As for if we supposed an obeyed imam in need of increasing soldiers to block the frontiers and protect the kingdom after the widening of its extent and the expansion of its territory, and the public treasury became empty of money, and the needs of the army pressed to what suffices them,

> ¹ *Al-Amwāl* of al-Qāsim ibn Sallām (1/636), chapter on the mention of the tithe-collector and the collector of tolls, in the explanation of hadith no. 1462.
> ² *Sharḥ Maʿānī al-Āthār* (2/31), Book of Zakat, chapter on zakat: does the imam take it.

[p. 264] and their hands became empty of the amount of their sufficiency — then the imam may impose upon the rich what he sees as sufficient for them at the time, until money appears in the public treasury. Then it is up to him to consider imposing that upon the yields of crops and fruits, so that singling out some people with it does not lead to the filling of breasts with rancour and the alienation of hearts; a small amount taken from much, which does not ruin them and by which the purpose is achieved."

The upshot of that is that Imam al-Ghazālī (may God have mercy on him) permitted imposing this kind of taxes with the following conditions:

- That the imam be among those whose obedience is obligatory.
- That there be a real necessity for protecting the state and defending the territory of the country and its heartland.
- That the public treasury be empty of money that fulfils that.
- That the taxes be in the amount that suffices for their needs at the time, until money appears in the public treasury.
- That imposing that on people be with justice and equity, and that he not differentiate in it between two men of one kind as to profession and work.

The jurists discourage rulers from imposing such taxes only because of what is plainly seen of rulers: that rarely do they observe the Sharia limits in it in completeness and perfection. So we see that the ruling of permissibility in the case of exceptional necessity has been surrounded by a mass of restrictions and a set of conditions, to protect the subjects from the commanders taking it as a prey for their ambitions and a means to attain their ease and their luxury.

Take for that, for example, the story of Shaykh ʿIzz al-Dīn ibn ʿAbd al-Salām (may God have mercy on him): when the Tatars began their attacks on the Islamic lands, the Sultan wanted to take money from the people to help finance jihad against them; and he gathered the jurists, judges and notables to consult them in this matter. Among those who attended was Shaykh ʿIzz al-Dīn ibn ʿAbd al-Salām… and they went at length in the talk; and the reliance was on what Ibn ʿAbd al-Salām said, and the summary of what he said: that when the enemy attacks the lands of Islam, it becomes obligatory on everyone to fight them, and it is permissible for you to take from the subjects what you seek help with in your jihad,

[p. 265] on condition that nothing remains in the public treasury, and that you sell what you have of gilded belts and valuable equipment, and that every soldier confine himself to his mount and his weapon, and they and the common people be equal. As for taking money from the common people while there remain in the hands of the soldiers money and luxurious equipment, then no.¹

Similar to that is what is narrated that when the Sultan al-Ẓāhir Baybars went out to fight the Tatars in Syria, he took the fatwas of the scholars that it is permissible for him to take money from the subjects to seek help with in fighting the enemy. So the jurists of Syria wrote to him about that. He said: Is anyone left? It was said: Yes, Shaykh Muḥyī al-Dīn al-Nawawī remains. So he summoned him and he attended. He said: Add your signature to the jurists'. He refused. He said: What is the reason for your refusal? He said: I know that you were in slavery to the commander Bunduqdār, and you have no money. Then God favoured you and made you a king. I heard that you have a thousand slaves, each slave having a belt of gold; and you have two hundred slave-girls, each slave-girl having a box of jewellery. So if you spent all that, and your slaves remained with woollen bands instead of the belts, and the slave-girls remained in their clothes without jewellery, I would give you a fatwa that you may take money from the subjects.²

The upshot is that all of these permitted on the whole imposing the tax in real necessity and only to the extent of it. Imam al-Juwaynī went a step further in *al-Ghiyāthī* and permitted that on a permanent and fixed basis also, if the need of the public treasury was fixed and permanent; for he said after discussing this subject in sections: So I say — and God is the one whose help is sought — there must be the imposing of funds which the imam sees as fulfilling the regular provisions, or close to them…

So if there is need to draw on funds to support the religion and [protect] the Muslims, and what may be expected to come in unforeseen from the unbelievers does not suffice, and it is established that a continuous flow of funds is needed for maintaining strength and defending the lands — then if the imam singled out certain wealthy people, that would breed resentment in souls [and ill thoughts] in minds and conjectures; but if he imposed a modest amount on surpluses, fruits and crops, that would be a way of

> ¹ *Al-Nujūm al-Zāhira fī Mulūk Miṣr wa-l-Qāhira* (7/73), and *Ṭabaqāt al-Shāfiʿiyya al-Kubrā* of al-Subkī (8/215).
> ² *Ḥusn al-Muḥāḍara fī Tārīkh Miṣr wa-l-Qāhira* (2/105).

[p. 266] caring for the soldiers and the subjects that is moderate and acceptable.¹

If the jurists were beset by fear on the part of the commanders of tyranny that they would take the ruling of permissibility in itself as a means of burdening the subjects with tolls and unjust levies, how much more must we fear today, when fraud and embezzlement have spread through governmental bodies, state funds are squandered recklessly, a considerable part of the state treasury is devoted merely to the rulers' luxury and comfort, and exorbitant costs and huge expenditures are spent on conferences that yield no benefit and visits that bring the state no return. Add to that the many enormous expenses of the royal palace which take an ample share of the budget, in which — regrettably — the Islamic states have preceded many non-Islamic states!

So if a government undertook reducing these extravagant, empty expenditures, and imposed strict, continuous oversight on the officials of the government and its employees to protect the funds of the state and safeguard its treasury from fraud and embezzlement, that would go far towards making it unnecessary to burden the subjects with crushing taxes and charging them with heavy levies.

Nevertheless, it is an undeniable fact that modern civilization has generated many expenditures which have become virtually necessary for living on earth with honour and dignity, and which cannot be included among luxury, extravagance and indulgence — in addition to governments today undertaking to provide many services to the subjects which in bygone times they did not provide and were not committed to, like the service of electricity and gas for example. There are fields whose expenditures have increased today over what they were before by much, like arming the defence sector and supplying it with modern war technologies and equipment, and constructing paved streets organized on a wide scale, and acquiring the media alongside transport and communications, and the costs of the education and health sectors. All this has come to be among the functions and duties of the government today, and they are expenditures which it appears difficult to cover with the established resources of the public treasury of zakat, the tithe, the land tax

> ¹ *Ghiyāth al-Umam fī Iltiyāth al-Ẓulam* (1/283).

[p. 267] and the *fayʾ* and booty money. As for zakat and the tithe, in most of these matters it is not possible to spend them from the Sharia point of view for lack of the aspect of transfer of ownership (*tamlīk*) in them; and how would a large amount of the land tax be obtained, while the principle in the lands of Dār al-Islām is that they be tithe-paying [lands]? As for the jizya — on granting its application — its amount is small and will not fulfil these enormous expenditures. As for booty and the *fayʾ*, funding these sectors from them is almost, if not entirely, impossible; because they are only obtained in the wake of success in wars with the unbelievers, while wars today in themselves leave whoever plunges into them — even if he returned with success — empty-handed, exhausted of strength.

So it is concluded from all this that Islamic government founded on straight foundations will need — at least at the beginning of its era — to cover its necessary expenditures by burdening the subjects with "the emergency tax"; but this tax, in view of the details just presented, will be low and easy to bear, if it undertook imposing it with caution and scrupulousness and succeeded in reducing the royal expenditures and the empty governmental burdens that have no meaning except making the commanders' living luxurious and comfortable. On another side, if it followed the divine rulings and submitted to the law of His Book and the Sunna of His Messenger in complete and perfect submission, it would find blessings from heaven and earth beyond all counting. Is it not enough as evidence of that what the Mighty One said: **"And if only the people of the cities had believed and feared God, We would have opened upon them blessings from the heaven and the earth"** [al-Aʿrāf, 7:96].

### 13 – The rights of non-Muslim minorities in Islam

There has been broadcast in the world today propaganda against Islam that it does not protect the rights of the non-Muslim citizens who inhabit the Islamic states as minorities; and it is only fabricated propaganda that has no share of truth nor portion of seriousness. For Islam, even if it is a universal call to divine unity addressing all the children of Adam to embrace it and submit to it, it calls people to that on the strength of evidence and the soundness of proof, so that they reflect and deliberate on its teachings and accept it with insight and understanding from them; and it has never permitted its followers to compel anyone to embrace it and belong to its religion. So here is the Noble Qur'an

[p. 268] calling out with that openly, saying: **"There is no compulsion in religion; the right course has become clear from the wrong. So whoever disbelieves in Ṭāghūt and believes in God has grasped the most trustworthy handhold"** [al-Baqara, 2:256].

Human history bears witness that the Muslims held fast to this divine ruling with the utmost tenacity; they did not use the weapon of force in compelling people to accept Islam, even in the most flourishing of its ages and the most advanced of its eras. Rather the most splendid example of tolerance is established from them: their judge permitted those who entered Islam compelled to return to their religion if they wished. Look at what Ibn Kathīr narrates to us in his history in the events of the year six hundred and eighty, saying: "And in Shawwāl a council was held because of the People of the Book of the dhimmīs who had become Muslim against their will; and a group of the muftis had written for them that they were compelled, so they were entitled to return to their religion; and the compulsion was established before the judge Jamāl al-Dīn ibn Abī Yaʿqūb al-Mālikī; so most of them returned to their religion."¹

From this, the unbelievers who inhabit the Islamic state — after they have taken upon themselves the covenant with the state — become like the rest of its Muslim citizens: they have of civil rights what they [the Muslims] have, and it is upon the state to protect them and defend them as it does for the Muslims. Indeed, it is right for us to say: that the protection of rights and the safeguarding of interests which Islam has granted to citizens not belonging to it and who inhabit its state as a minority are such that no other religion has ever equalled or approached them. The duty of each of the two parties — the state and the non-Muslim minority — is clear from the terms Islam has assigned to the latter: "*al-muʿāhad*" (the one under covenant) or "*al-dhimmī*" (the one under protection). *Al-muʿāhad* expresses the duty incumbent on him to live in the state peacefully and safely, without causing any harm to the state or its interests; and *al-dhimmī* reflects the guarantee (*dhimma*) the state has given him for the protection of his life, his property and his honour.

Come, let us cast a look at what has come concerning the dhimmīs of the wise, right Prophetic teachings. Among that is what Imam Abū Dāwūd narrated with his chain from the Messenger of God ﷺ that he said: "Indeed, whoever wrongs one under covenant (*muʿāhad*), or diminishes [his right], or charges him beyond his capacity, or takes something from him without the consent

> ¹ *Al-Bidāya wa-l-Nihāya*, Hajar ed. (17/578).

[p. 269] of [his] soul — I will be his opponent on the Day of Resurrection."¹

Abū Dāwūd also narrated with his chain from Abū Bakra; he said: the Messenger of God ﷺ said: "Whoever kills one under covenant without just cause, God forbids Paradise to him."²

What al-Bukhārī narrated with his chain from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr, may God be pleased with them both, from the Prophet ﷺ; he said: "Whoever kills one under covenant will not smell the scent of Paradise, and its scent is found from a distance of forty years."³

The best document clarifying the rights of non-Muslims in the Islamic state is what the Commander of the Faithful ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, may God be pleased with him, covenanted upon with the Christians of Jerusalem; and among what came in it: "In the name of God, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful. This is what the servant of God ʿUmar, the Commander of the Faithful, has given to the people of Īliyāʾ of security: he gave them security for themselves and their property, and for their churches and their crosses, and their sick and their healthy and the rest of their community: that their churches shall not be inhabited nor demolished, nor shall anything be diminished from them nor from their precinct, nor from their crosses, nor from anything of their property; and they shall not be compelled concerning their religion, and none of them shall be harmed."⁴

Among his concern for preserving the rights of non-Muslims is that he used to inspect their conditions and ascertain that harm from the Muslims did not reach them. Al-Ṭabarī (may God have mercy on him) narrated that he said to the delegation of Basra: "Perhaps the Muslims are causing harm to the people of protection (*ahl al-dhimma*), and doing things that lead them to break with you!" They said: "We do not know [anything] except fulfilment and good treatment."⁵

Notice also what Imam Abū Yūsuf (may God have mercy on him) narrated from Abū Bakra: that ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, may God be pleased with him, passed by the door of a people where there was a beggar asking: an old man, blind of sight.

> ¹ *Sunan Abī Dāwūd* (3/171), Book of the Land Tax…, chapter on taking the tithe from the people of protection, hadith no. 3052; and let it be known that there is talk about its chain.
> ² *Sunan Abī Dāwūd* (3/83), Book of Jihad, chapter on fulfilment towards the one under covenant, hadith no. 2760.
> ³ *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (4/99), Book of Jihad, chapter on the sin of one who killed one under covenant, hadith no. 3166.
> ⁴ *Tārīkh al-Ṭabarī* = *Tārīkh al-Rusul wa-l-Mulūk*, and *Ṣilat Tārīkh al-Ṭabarī* (3/609), events of the year fifteen.
> ⁵ *Tārīkh al-Ṭabarī* = *Tārīkh al-Rusul wa-l-Mulūk*, and *Ṣilat Tārīkh al-Ṭabarī* (4/89).

[p. 270] He struck his upper arm from behind and said: Of which of the People of the Book are you? He said: A Jew. He said: What has compelled you to what I see? He said: [I beg] to meet the jizya, out of need and old age. He said: So ʿUmar took his hand and took him to his house and gave him something from the house; then he sent to the keeper of the public treasury and said: See to this man and those like him; for by God we have not treated him fairly that we ate [the fruit of] his youth and then abandon him at old age. "Alms are only for the poor and the needy" — and the poor are the Muslims, and this is of the needy of the People of the Book. And he removed the jizya from him and from those like him.¹

Shortly before his death, he advised the caliph after him with many matters, among them: "And I advise him with the protection of God and the protection of His Messenger ﷺ: that their covenant be fulfilled for them, that he fight in their defence, and that they not be charged except with their capacity."²

Abū ʿUbayd al-Qāsim ibn Sallām narrated with his chain from Khālid ibn al-Walīd, may God be pleased with him, that he said: "Do not take three steps to put yourself in command over three people, and do not take from one under covenant so much as a needle or anything more…"³

He also narrated: from Ṣaʿṣaʿa; he said: I asked Ibn ʿAbbās; I said: We travel in the land of the people of protection and we take things from them. He said: Without a price? I said: Without a price. He said: What do you say? I said: We say: it is lawful, there is no harm in it. He said: You say as the People of the Book said: **"There is no blame upon us concerning the unlettered.' And they speak untruth about God while they know"** [Āl ʿImrān, 3:75].⁴

So in compliance with the rulings of the Qur'an and the Prophetic Sunna, and following the model of the Rightly-Guided Caliphs, may God be pleased with them, the jurists gave great attention to explaining the rulings of the people of protection and their rights, and strongly emphasized observing them and performing them. Among that is what Imam Muḥammad ibn al-Ḥasan al-Shaybānī (may God have mercy on him) said: Because the Muslims, when they gave them protection (*dhimma*), committed themselves to repelling injustice from them, and they became of the people of Dār al-Islām.⁵

> ¹ *Al-Kharāj* of Abū Yūsuf (1/139).
> ² *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (5/17), Book of Merits, hadith no. 3700.
> ³ *Al-Amwāl* of al-Qāsim ibn Sallām (1/13).
> ⁴ *Al-Amwāl* of al-Qāsim ibn Sallām (1/197), no. 415.
> ⁵ *Sharḥ al-Siyar al-Kabīr* (1/209).

[p. 271] To the extent that the jurists regarded calling them "unbeliever" as a sin if that pained them. It came in *al-Fatāwā al-Hindiyya*: "If he said to a Jew or a Zoroastrian 'O unbeliever', he sins if that is hard on him; thus in *al-Qunya*."¹

Even more remarkable is what is narrated from the jurists that they regarded injustice to the dhimmī as more dangerous than injustice to the Muslim, with the reasoning that no way remains, after his death, for being absolved of it.²

### The jizya in Islam

We grant that Islamic government imposes a kind of taxes on its non-Muslim citizens called "the jizya" — which Western researchers sometimes take as [grounds] for impugning Islam and degrading it, as being a conduct involving discrimination among citizens on the basis of their religions and their schools, with the reasoning that the unbelievers alone are singled out for imposing it and burdening them with it, to the exclusion of the Muslims. The truth is that if they had looked at it with the eye of fairness, far from blind religious partisanship, they would have discovered what is in imposing it of the interests and privileges which the unbelievers obtain to the exclusion of the Muslims. That is because Islamic government receives from the Muslim citizens the zakat of their wealth, whose percentage ranges between 2.5% minimum and 10% maximum — and it is higher than the percentage of the jizya which the unbelievers are committed to paying by much. Second, in return for this small amount, the Islamic state undertakes the responsibility of protecting them — their lives, honour and property; just as it exempts from the jizya the insolvent, needy among them; indeed it provides for them from the public treasury their provisions, as passed in what we narrated of the story of ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him, with an old Jew, and his giving him provision, and his ordering the removal of the jizya from him and his like.

There is another point which explains what we are saying more fully and clearly, namely that the Islamic state, if the unbelievers attacked it, then it is permissible for the imam to command all his Muslim subjects to contribute to jihad against the enemy and to defend the heartland of Islam and the sanctity of the lands; and it is not permissible for him to charge the unbelievers with defending themselves and their lands — that is, protecting it and defending it; rather their protection and defence of them in such a case is obligatory on the Islamic government towards them, in return for what it receives from them of the sums of the jizya. This appears to be the meaning of the saying of ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him,

> ¹ *Al-Fatāwā al-Hindiyya* (5/348).
> ² *Al-Nawāzil* of the jurist Abū al-Layth al-Samarqandī, p. 207.

[p. 272] which we mentioned earlier: "that he fight in their defence." ʿAllāma al-Ālūsī (may God have mercy on him) also held it, saying: "That it [the jizya] is a substitute for help in fighting on our behalf; and for this reason it varies [in amount]: because everyone who belongs to Dār al-Islām is obliged to support the abode with his person and his wealth; and since the unbeliever is not fit for it because of his inclination to Dār al-Ḥarb by belief, the jizya taken was put in its place, spent on the fighters."¹

The most splendid example of their implementing this principle and observing it is that they returned to the dhimmīs their jizya if they were prevented from defending them. To illustrate this, read with me the following story, which alone suffices as a convincing, decisive answer to that propaganda broadcast against Islam concerning the ruling of the jizya, which does not benefit the Muslims with the like of what the unbelievers paying it enjoy, as passed in detail just now. It came in *Futūḥ al-Buldān* of al-Balādhurī: when Heraclius gathered the masses for the Muslims, and the Muslims learned of their coming to them for the battle of Yarmūk, they returned to the people of Ḥimṣ what they had taken from them of the land tax, and said: We are too occupied to help and defend you, so you are in charge of your own affairs.

The meaning of handing over their affair to them was that it was possible for them to open the gate of the city for Heraclius also.

The historian al-Azdī narrated the text of Abū ʿUbayda, may God be pleased with him, as follows: "And we have only returned to you your wealth because we disliked to take your wealth and not defend your lands; but we shall withdraw to some land and send to our brothers so that they come to us; then we shall meet our enemy and fight them; and if God gives us victory over them, we shall fulfil for you your covenant — unless you do not seek that."

Al-Balādhurī added that the people of Ḥimṣ replied to him as follows: "Your rule and your justice are more beloved to us than what we were in of injustice and deceit; and we shall certainly repel the army of Heraclius from the city together with your governor."²

At any rate — has the world presented a parallel to this splendid moral performance and this just compensation, where they receive from the Islamic state those great services like the protection of their lives and their defence, in return for paying a small sum — at most forty-eight dirhams a year? In addition to that, Islam exempted kinds of them from this responsibility, and provided

> ¹ *Tafsīr al-Ālūsī* = *Rūḥ al-Maʿānī* (5/273), under verse 29 of Sūrat al-Tawba.
> ² *Futūḥ al-Buldān* (1/162), and *al-Ṭarīq ilā Dimashq* of Aḥmad ʿĀdil Kamāl, p. 410.

[p. 273] for them the mentioned services entirely free of charge — namely their women and their children, and the insolvent and the elderly of their men.

So, after establishing these three matters:

- That the Islamic state grants the dhimmīs all civil rights, including protection and defence of them — like the rest of the other citizens among the Muslims alike.
- That it also grants them exemption from the commitment to providing war and defence services if the enemies attacked it.
- That the percentage of the jizya imposed on them is less, by much, than the percentage of zakat which the Muslims pay.

— is there any fairness, then, in regarding the ruling of the jizya — specifically in the light of these three points — as injustice and bias? Is calling it a usurpation of the rights of the unbelievers and an injury to them anything but false, lying propaganda and a patent blindness to clear, established facts?!

This is what many of the Western researchers who are known for their fairness have acknowledged — among them Montesquieu, among the founders of modern democracy, for he said:

> It was the excess of the taxes that occasioned the prodigious facility with which the Mohametans carried on their conquests. Instead of a continual series of extortion devised by the subtle avarice of the Greek emperors, the people were subjected to a simple tribute which was paid and collected with ease. Thus they were far happier in obeying a barberous nation than a corrupt government in which they suffered every inconvenience of lost liberty.
> (Montesque [sic], *The Spirit of Laws*, Book 13, Chapter 16, Great Books, v. 35, p. 100)

[Tr.: The English is quoted in the original as given. The author's Arabic paraphrase that follows it reads:] "The increase of taxes (in non-Muslim states) was the element that helped the Muslims in widening the extent of their conquests; for Islam charged people with the jizya, which was easier to pay and collect than that plunder and robbery which the arrogant emperors of Greece used to impose on them. So they were happier in obeying a savage, barbaric people than to live deprived of freedom under the shadow of the corrupt, bribe-taking government that subjected them to kinds of pains."

[p. 274] So in this text — even if his blind partisanship went as far as describing the Muslims as savage barbarians — at the same time he did not hesitate to acknowledge that the jizya which the Islamic government imposed was small in amount, easy to pay, and smaller and less by much than the crushing taxes which the emperors of Greece used to impose on them, which the writer expressed as plunder and robbery; so the unbelievers were happier and more content in the Islamic government than they were in a Greek empire; and that this was the factor that played a role in enabling the Muslims to widen the scope of their conquests and the extent of their influence.

Then Islam does not insist on taking it from them under the name of the jizya only — and especially if the unbelievers paying it disdained it; rather it permits — according to what some reports indicate — that it be taken from them in that case under another name too — specifically one that does not indicate humiliation and abasement. Among what indicates that is the story of ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him: when he resolved to impose the jizya on the Banū Taghlib — and they are a people of the Arabs — they refused and said: We are Arabs; we do not pay what the non-Arabs pay; but take from us as some of you take from others — meaning the *ṣadaqa* (alms). ʿUmar said: No, this is the obligation of the Muslims. They said: Then increase as you wish by this name, not by the name of the jizya. So he and they agreed that he would double the *ṣadaqa* upon them.¹

There is another report which indicates — if it is sound — that this ruling is not specific to the Banū Taghlib; rather it applies to others than them too, if the interest of the Muslims required it. It is as came in *Futūḥ al-Buldān* of al-Balādhurī: He narrated also that Jabala came to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb while he was upon his Christianity. ʿUmar offered him Islam and the payment of the *ṣadaqa*, but he refused that and said: I will remain upon my religion and pay the *ṣadaqa*. ʿUmar said: If you remain upon your religion, then pay the jizya. He disdained it. ʿUmar said: We have only three options for you: either Islam, or paying the jizya, or going wherever you wish.

So he entered the land of the Romans with thirty thousand. When that reached ʿUmar, he regretted [it]. ʿUbāda ibn

> ¹ *Fatḥ al-Qadīr* of al-Kamāl ibn al-Humām (2/201), Book of Zakat; and *Kitāb al-Amwāl* of Abū ʿUbayd, chapter on taking the jizya.
