[p. 275] al-Ṣāmit reproached him and said: If you had accepted the *ṣadaqa* from him and then conciliated him, he would have become Muslim. ʿUmar, may God be pleased with him, sent in the year twenty-one ʿUmayr ibn Saʿd al-Anṣārī to the land of the Romans in a great army and put him in charge of the summer campaign — and it was the first summer campaign there was — and ordered him to be gentle with Jabala ibn al-Ayham and to appeal to his sympathy by the kinship between them, and to call him to return to the land of Islam, on condition that he pay what he had offered of the *ṣadaqa* and remain upon his religion.¹

Some jurists held that it is not extended to other peoples besides the Banū Taghlib, with the reasoning that it is a ruling contrary to analogy; so they did not permit treating others than them like what was done with these, of changing the name of the jizya to the *ṣadaqa* after doubling it on them.² But what appears to me is that the prohibition applies only when the other term is one specific to the Muslims, like zakat and the *ṣadaqāt*. With any other name, it is most likely not prohibited. This is just as Imam al-Shāfiʿī (may God have mercy on him) permitted in some cases making peace with them without taking the jizya from them at all.³ [If that is permissible, though it forgoes the original entirely, then what we have said should be all the more permissible, since it merely changes the name while the substance remains.]

### 14 – Ministries and government departments

As we presented earlier, Islam did not legislate a special ruling and did not define a fixed position concerning the ministries and administrative departments in the government — their number and their powers and so on of those details which are connected to them; rather it entrusted to the people of insight in every age to choose from among the prevailing methods what they see as most fitting to their circumstances and most in the interest of the condition of the subjects.

Despite that, no era was empty of actually employing ministers to help the imam and support him in the affairs of the state; and the greatest witness of that is what came in the Noble Qur'an: that Moses, upon him be peace, asked his Lord for a minister, saying: **"And appoint for me a minister (wazīr) from my family — Aaron, my brother"** [Ṭā Hā,

> ¹ *Futūḥ al-Buldān* (1/161).
> ² *Kashf al-Asrār ʿan Uṣūl Fakhr al-Islām al-Bazdawī* (4/204), chapter on knowing the divisions of the cause.
> ³ *Kitāb al-Umm*, Book of Jihad and the Jizya, chapter on humiliation with the jizya, 1/214.

[p. 276] 20:29–30].

There are reports in which the Companions were called ministers of the Prophet ﷺ; among them what is narrated from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd, may God be pleased with him, that he said: "So He chose for him companions, and made them helpers of His religion and ministers of His Prophet ﷺ."¹

But that does not mean that the office of minister was formally entrusted to them as is the practice in this age of ours; rather the word "*wazīr*" (minister) was only applied to them because they were helpers supporting the Prophet ﷺ. In this meaning came the saying of the Prophet ﷺ: "And as for my two ministers from the people of the earth, they are Abū Bakr and ʿUmar."²

Likewise it is not established that anyone was put in charge of the office of the ministry or employed as a minister for the caliph in the era of the Rightly-Guided Caliphate either; rather many of the Companions used to undertake helping the caliph in the affairs of the state and performing what was entrusted to them of responsibilities and works; and the word "minister" used to be applied to them then — as is narrated that ʿAlī, may God be pleased with him, when he was charged with bearing the burdens of the caliphate, said: "It is better that I be a minister than a commander."³

In the eras after them, the office of the ministry accompanied the caliphate as a shadow accompanies its owner; and the imam continued formally to appoint someone to it, employing him as a minister who helps him and supports him in his works and his duties. The scholars of Islamic political jurisprudence divided the ministry into two kinds: the ministry of delegation (*wizārat al-tafwīḍ*) and the ministry of execution (*wizārat al-tanfīdh*). Here is their clarification in what follows:

As for the minister of delegation, he is the minister whom the caliph designates and delegates to him something of his powers and his competences, so that he uses them in managing matters by his opinion and executing them according to his ijtihad, without being committed in them to obtaining the agreement of the caliph and his approval.

As for the minister of execution, he is the minister who has no authority or jurisdiction of his own, and is not able

> ¹ *Sharḥ al-Sunna* of al-Baghawī (1/215).
> ² *Sunan al-Tirmidhī*, ed. Shākir (5/616).
> ³ *Tārīkh al-Ṭabarī* = *Tārīkh al-Rusul wa-l-Mulūk*, and *Ṣilat Tārīkh al-Ṭabarī* (4/427).

[p. 277] to execute matters by his opinion and his view; rather his role is confined to mediation between the caliph and the subjects and the governors: he conveys from him what he commanded, and executes from him what he mentioned, and carries out what he ruled, and the like of that of what relates to implementing the commands of the imam and his decrees only.

In Islamic history there are found examples of both these kinds which the scholars of Islamic politics mentioned, like al-Māwardī, Abū Yaʿlā and others. ʿAllāma Ibn Khaldūn (may God have mercy on him) saw that the ministry of delegation was obtained by a person as a result of the consolidation of his influence and his authorities over the caliph — in the sense that a person obtains a standing and sovereignty in the heart of the king, to the extent that the king remains trusting in him and in his management and his view; so he becomes a minister of delegation, and an authority is obtained for him similar to what is obtained today for the prime minister in the democratic system, where he is the one who manages and directs the affairs of the state and government — except that the caliph or the president at the same time retains the constitutional right to cancel his decisions and replace them with others.

Despite that, the kinds of ministries clarified above are not built on a text from the Qur'an or the Prophetic Sunna; they are only the offspring of situations and circumstances and the outcome of events and incidents. So it is not obligatory on the imam in the Sharia to take ministers of both these kinds. The principle in that is that the caliph has the right to grant someone all that he has of powers and authorities, or something of them; just as he has the right also not to delegate to anyone anything of that. The upshot is that employing ministers and appointing them is nothing but an administrative matter on which the Sharia did not decide a decisive matter; rather it permitted the caliph to follow in it the course which he sees as easiest administratively, as long as his government is administered on fixed Islamic foundations and principles.

It is regarded as most appropriate today, in view of contemporary circumstances and situations, that there be defined by way of the constitution or the law all that the kingdom needs of ministries and departments and the extent of their influence and the scope of their powers.

Likewise the matter of sub-departments: the Sharia has not decided for them a special number nor branches and special fields; rather throughout Islamic history such departments

[p. 278] and branches have arisen in response to the needs of the age and the requirements of the time. So it is permitted today also that Islamic government take of them whatever it wishes, on condition that it be out of real necessity and need.

### 14 [sic] – Regional autonomy in Islam

Among the burning issues that attract the attention of contemporary governments, and around whose solution the dispute intensifies and the clash increases, is the issue of the autonomy and local government of the regions and provinces belonging to a state. We would like to list in this regard also several basic matters:

**First** — and before everything — we repeat here also what we said more than once: that Islam has not legislated a decisive ruling concerning the type of government, whether federal or unitary. So the rulings of Islam do not form any obstacle in the way of implementing the method which the Muslims have chosen or will choose in view of the interests of their age and the requirements of their times.

**Second**: as we said in discussing nationalism, Islam does not support the idea of founding a state on the basis of sharing in language, lineage and colour; nevertheless regional autonomy and local government for provinces are not among what contradicts the principles of Islam altogether, if several conditions are observed in it.

It is unavoidable for any government — federal or unitary — that the various areas of the state and its regions be divided into different units for the purpose of facilitating the administration of the administrative affairs of the state and its executive tasks. Such administrative units existed in the first Islamic government that was founded at the hands of the Messenger of God ﷺ. So here is Mecca: as soon as it was conquered, the Prophet ﷺ put ʿAttāb ibn Asīd in charge of it and made him ruler over it. When Bahrain was conquered, he put al-ʿAlāʾ ibn al-Ḥaḍramī, may God be pleased with him, in charge of it. When Yemen submitted to the Islamic state, he divided it — because of its size — into two administrative units, each of which was called a "*mikhlāf*" (district), as its mention came in the hadith of Abū Burda, may God be pleased with him, which al-Bukhārī included in his *Ṣaḥīḥ*; he said: "The Messenger of God ﷺ sent Abū Mūsā and Muʿādh ibn Jabal to Yemen. He said: And he sent each one

[p. 279] of them over a *mikhlāf*. He said: And Yemen is two *mikhlāf*s."¹

Al-Ḥāfiẓ said: "The *makhālīf* of Yemen — the singular of it is *mikhlāf*, and it is like a region (*iqlīm*) for others than the people of Yemen."²

He said also: "And the side of Muʿādh was the upper, towards Aden, and among his territory was al-Janad (with *fatḥa* on the *jīm* and the *nūn*), and he has in it a mosque famous to this day."³

Al-ʿAynī (may God have mercy on him) said: "That he put each one of them in charge of a *mikhlāf*; and the *mikhlāf* is the district (*al-kūra*); and Yemen is two *mikhlāf*s. I said: The territory of Muʿādh was the uplands and what rose of the lands of Yemen, and the territory of Abū Mūsā the lowlands and what sank of it."⁴

Likewise in the era of the Rightly-Guided Caliphate: when the extent of the Islamic government widened and the scope of its influence stretched until it took control of half the inhabited world, it was divided at that time into administrative units like these, and each side had its ruler appointed by the caliph and imam.

It is self-evident that the powers and competences of the local and regional authorities must be defined, and the matters specified in which local governments act independently on their own judgement, and the matters in which they are still purely subordinate to the central government. What is not hidden in this field is that Islamic government — central or local — is entirely subject to the rulings of the Qur'an and the Prophetic Sunna and committed to not deviating from them a hand-span; and from this it is never permitted for it to lay down a law or issue an order contrary to the Islamic Sharia in anything, or clashing with its fixed principles. Since justice was among the most important Islamic principles and the ideals of its teachings for Islamic government, it is therefore enjoined upon it to fulfil its requirements in full, and that it not lay down any law nor take any administrative measure that exceeds its limits so as to become injustice and tyranny. Likewise it is necessary to make the Islamic directives the focus of attention in the sector of

> ¹ *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (5/161), Book of Military Expeditions, hadith no. 3431.
> ² *Fatḥ al-Bārī* of Ibn Ḥajar (1/186).
> ³ *Fatḥ al-Bārī* of Ibn Ḥajar (8/61).
> ⁴ *ʿUmdat al-Qārī Sharḥ Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (24/251), Book of Judgements, chapter 22.

[p. 280] education for each of the central and local governments. Just as there are several fields for which the state must adopt a deliberate plan through consultation among the representatives of all the regions and the territories belonging to it, so that the position of all those regions concerning them is unified, preserving the cohesion and unity of the state — and that is like international relations, foreign affairs, international trade and so on.

So, with observing these basic principles which are the most fundamental in this matter, there is no harm in granting the territories the greatest possible autonomy.

Hence the scholars of Islamic political jurisprudence divided the commanders of the regions and the rulers of the lands into two kinds: the special command [or: the special delegation], which is that the commander invest someone over a region while granting him authorities and powers within a limited scope; and the general command [or: the general delegation], which is that the sultan invest someone over a region of the state with wide authorities, such that he is regarded as a deputy of the caliph in all his authorities: of investing judges and governors, managing the armies, collecting the funds from all their sources and spending them in their objects of expenditure, and fighting the polytheists and those who wage war, and so on.¹

The idea of regional autonomy developed greatly in the Abbasid Caliphate, until there were granted in it to the governors and local commanders of the various regions general delegation in the aforementioned meaning of handing over all the authorities of the caliph to him within that region delegated to him.

Indeed, they — submitting to a realistic historical fact — divided the commanders with general delegation into two kinds: a commander by appointment (*amīr istikfāʾ*), who is the one to whom the general command or rule over a region or territory was entrusted by a contract of the imam's or the caliph's own choosing, so that he becomes a deputy of the imam in all his authorities and his powers, including managing the armies and undertaking jihad against whoever is next to him of the enemies.

A commander by seizure (*amīr istīlāʾ*), who is the one whom the imam did not invest with the command from choice on his part; rather he took control of a region and became a commander over it by his strength and his might. Then, for the purpose of his remaining in subordination to the Islamic caliphate —

> ¹ *Al-Aḥkām al-Sulṭāniyya* of al-Māwardī, p. 400; and *Taḥrīr al-Aḥkām fī Tadbīr Ahl al-Islām* of Ibn Jamāʿa, p. 60, Qatar printing.

[p. 281] even if symbolically only — he asked the caliph to annex his command to the caliphate. So the caliph would accept his request to preserve the unity of the Umma and protect it from division and tearing apart; and because he was a Muslim who governed the lands under the shade of the rulings of the Sharia and implements them to the extent of his authority. It has happened repeatedly that this seizing ruler did not have present in him those qualities and conditions necessary for taking up the grave office, like the command of the Muslims and ruling over them; but nevertheless, despite those defects of his, his incorporation into the caliphate and into the fold of the unified Umma were tolerated. To lessen the harm of the like of him upon the Umma and reduce what was expected from him of evils, the imam often undertook investing a righteous, qualified person with the office of deputyship of this seizing commander, to try to straighten his conduct as far as possible for him. Such commands were at first within the realm of exceptional rare cases; and with the progress of time and the weakening of the caliph's control, they increased extremely. ʿAllāma al-Māwardī (may God have mercy on him) says, while undertaking to explain the commanders of delegation in his time: "And if the caliph delegated the management of the regions to their governors and entrusted the consideration of them to those who had taken control of them — as the people of our time are upon…"¹

Usually the royal families of the Buwayhids, the Sāmānids, the Ghaznavids and the Seljuks were within this kind of command.

Since this situation was the offspring of circumstances of necessity and the product of the deteriorating situations which the Umma was passing through, and which threatened its unity and targeted its tearing apart and dismantling, it was never an ideal model of Islamic government for later governments to imitate. But it clearly and explicitly indicates the flexibility by which the rulings of Islam are distinguished in this regard: such that the commanders were granted all the authorities, even authority over the armies and jihad against the enemy!

After this quick survey of this important political issue from the point of view of

> ¹ *Al-Aḥkām al-Sulṭāniyya* of al-Māwardī (1/61). For more detail on this issue, see the book of Ḍiyāʾ al-Dīn al-Rayyis entitled *al-Naẓariyyāt al-Siyāsiyya al-Islāmiyya*, p. 278, Cairo printing 1976.

[p. 282] the Sharia and its stages through the history of the Islamic caliphate, let us cast a glance at the political atmospheres of our state and their positions towards this important, grave issue. First, it has become common among us to merge the call for regional autonomy with the call to nationalism (*al-qawmiyya*), so that anyone who raised this call or aspired to this demand was accused of treachery. In fact there is no connection between the requirements of regional autonomy and nationalism or the call to it in anything — as is accepted in the world today. For how many a region and territory belonging to a state has been allowed to issue its own special currency other than that of the state! Take Scotland, for example: its own special currency is issued, even though Britain's government is federal [sic: the UK is a unitary state, as the author himself says on p. 110] and Scotland represents a region and province of it.

On another side, regional autonomy means, from one aspect, that the region has first claim on its resources and products for its own benefit, before the other regions and provinces of the state exploit them. By contrast, many federal governments treat some of their regions in flagrant contradiction of this principle, exploiting their resources and supplying them to other regions while the province that produced them is deprived of benefiting from them for a long period. The clearest example of that is what we have witnessed in our state: that natural gas was found in Baluchistan, and was immediately sent to Karachi, Lahore, Islamabad and other cities, while Quetta — the capital of Baluchistan — remained deprived of it for a not short period, let alone its other cities. It was as a consequence of this unjust conduct that the call for regional autonomy turned into a call to nationalism and took the character of independence movements seeking secession from the state.

The gist of the words is that Islam does not see any harm in granting the units of the state and its regions wide authorities in administrative affairs to the furthest possible extent; indeed it encourages this to a degree, since it will help protect the state from being dismantled by regional currents or torn apart by destructive separatist calls — and it greatly eases the management of their administrative affairs and the performance of their basic duties.

---

## Chapter Five: Defence and Foreign Affairs

### Introduction

[p. 283] As becomes clear from the heading mentioned above, this chapter is devoted to studying the issues and questions which Islamic government faces concerning international relations, and clarifying the rulings of Islam which it will follow in laying down foreign policies. Before plunging into the depths of the details of the subject, it is fitting for us to give a brief account of the explanation of two terms which will recur often in this chapter, namely Dār al-Islām and Dār al-Ḥarb, into which the jurists divided all the states of the world.

### Dār al-Islām and Dār al-Ḥarb

**Dār al-Islām** (the Abode of Islam): it is the state which is under the control of the Muslims, and in which complete control has been obtained for them such that their word is manifest in it and their rulings are implemented in it. ʿAllāma al-Sarakhsī (may God have mercy on him) defined it with what is synonymous with this, saying: "For Dār al-Islām is a name for the place which is under the hand of the Muslims."¹

It is defined in *Jāmiʿ al-Rumūz*, citing *al-Kāfī*, as follows: Dār al-Islām is that in which the rule of the imam of the Muslims runs, and they are safe in it.²

So it is concluded that the abode only becomes Dār al-Islām if authority in it has been obtained for the Muslims, even if all the rulings of Islam are not actually being implemented in it. (This is another matter: that implementing the Sharia and applying all its rulings is in itself an important duty and a grave responsibility on the shoulders of the Muslims, especially in an Islamic state in which they have control over it and influence in it.)

The matter became confused for some, as they thought that the phrase "the rule of the imam of the Muslims" which came in the text of *Jāmiʿ al-Rumūz* mentioned just now [means]: implementing all the rulings of the Sharia; and from that they went

> ¹ *Sharḥ al-Siyar al-Kabīr* (1/1253).
> ² *Jāmiʿ al-Rumūz* 4/556.

[p. 284] to [holding] that the state in which the Sharia and its rulings are not actually implemented is not Dār al-Islām at all.

But it is an illusion that has no share of soundness; for the only element that makes the state Dār al-Islām is that its authority and its control be in the hands of the Muslims such that they are able in it to implement the Sharia and apply all its rulings. Then if, being able to do so, they fail to do it out of slackness and negligence, they commit a grave sin, and must immediately give up this negligence and undertake their responsibilities and perform their duty towards implementing the rulings of the Sharia in the areas of their influence and to the extent of their powers.

We do not see any contradiction between the two definitions of al-Sarakhsī and *Jāmiʿ al-Rumūz*; for the principle is what al-Sarakhsī (may God have mercy on him) said: that the state be under the hand of the Muslims — what was expressed in *al-Jāmiʿ al-Rumūz* by the running of the rule of the imam of the Muslims in it — regardless of whether his rulings and commands are in conformity with the Sharia or not. Since it was difficult at that time to conceive the Muslims' slackness to the limit of carelessness about implementing the Sharia in a state that is under their hands and in their authority, the earlier jurists therefore did not undertake to state its ruling expressly; rather they contented themselves in that with the affirmative side of the definition of Dār al-Islām: that it is the state in which the rule of the Muslims runs. As for the later ones among them, they stated its ruling expressly also after they witnessed this deteriorating situation and saw this evil day for Islam, when its own children neglect to implement it in lands under their control and authority! Among such explicit statements is what ʿAllāma Ibn ʿĀbidīn al-Shāmī (may God have mercy on him) said: "And by this it became apparent that what is in Syria of the mountain of Taym Allāh called Jabal al-Durūz and some of the lands belonging to it are all Dār al-Islām; because even if they have Druze or Christian rulers and they have judges on their religion, and some of them openly insult Islam and the Muslims — yet they are under the rule of our holders of authority, and the lands of Islam surround their lands from every side; and if the holder of authority wanted to implement our rulings among them, he would implement them."¹

This also makes clear what we said: that what counts in Dār al-Islām is that the Muslims have

> ¹ *Al-Durr al-Mukhtār* and the gloss of Ibn ʿĀbidīn (*Radd al-Muḥtār*) (4/175).

[p. 285] complete control over it and full influence, even if they did not actually implement in it all the rulings of the Sharia out of negligence and laziness in performing their duty and undertaking their responsibilities.

**Dār al-Ḥarb** (the Abode of War), by contrast, is the state which is under the hands of the unbelievers and in their control and their authority, as came in *Jāmiʿ al-Rumūz* also: "And Dār al-Ḥarb is that in which the command of the head of the unbelievers runs."¹

From this, if authority over a state were established for non-Muslims and their influence in it were completed, then it is Dār al-Kufr (the Abode of Unbelief), even if the Muslims were allowed in it to establish their rites and set up their judicial courts; because what counts — as we said — is authority, influence and control, and it is obtained here for the unbelievers.

Accordingly, ʿAllāma Shaykh Rashīd Aḥmad al-Gangohī (may God have mercy on him) gave a fatwa that India is Dār al-Ḥarb, even though the Muslims were free in it in establishing the Friday prayer, the ʿĪds and the other religious rites.

But it is necessary here to draw attention to the fact that the jurists often apply the word "Dār al-Ḥarb" to every state over which the unbelievers have taken control and in which they have extended their authority and in which their rulings are implemented. The truth is that it is not necessary that every state like this be Dār al-Ḥarb in the technical meaning also; and the detail of that is that the states of the unbelievers are of two kinds:

**Dār al-Ḥarb:** it is a state that has no peace treaty or truce with the Muslims, and in which Muslims are not permitted to establish their religious rites. This kind is Dār al-Ḥarb in every meaning of the word, and all its rulings apply to it — among them that the Muslims residing in it must emigrate from it to an Islamic state, or another unbelieving state in which they are able to establish religious rites and act by the teachings of the Sharia in a safe, peaceful atmosphere.

**And Dār al-Amn** (the Abode of Safety): it is a state ruled by unbelievers, but the Muslims are able in it to establish their religious rites and follow the commands of the Sharia in their personal and social life with all

> ¹ *Jāmiʿ al-Rumūz* 4/556.

[p. 286] freedom and without finding any obstacle from the government blocking them from it. The example of that in the early period of Islam is the kingdom of Abyssinia, to which a not small number of the Companions, may God be pleased with them, emigrated by the order of the Prophet ﷺ in the wake of the increase of afflictions and calamities upon them from the polytheists of Mecca. So the state was a state of unbelievers at that time; but it permitted the Muslims to act by their Sharia — which made it Dār al-Amn for them, and not Dār al-Ḥarb. Shaykh Shāh ʿAbd al-Ḥaqq al-Dihlawī (may God have mercy on him) says [in Persian]: "*Wa hijrat dar Islām bar do wajh wāqiʿ shuda: awwal intiqāl az dār-e khauf ba dār-e amān, chunānki baʿḍ-e ṣaḥāba dar ibtidā-e Islām ba Ḥabasha hijrat kardand, tā az khauf-e sharr wa fasād-e mushrikān-e Makka dar amān bāshand; wa chunānki baʿḍ az Makka ba Madīna raftand pīsh az hijrat-e Rasūl Allāh ﷺ wa istiqrār-e amr-e Islām. Wa thānī intiqāl az dār-e kufr ba dār-e Islām, wa īn baʿd az tamakkun wa istiqrār-e Āṅḥaḍrat ﷺ būd dar Madīna.*"¹

[In Arabic translation:] "Emigration in Islam occurred in two kinds. The first: emigration from the abode of fear to the abode of safety, as many of the Companions emigrated in the early period of Islam to Abyssinia, fleeing for their lives from the persecution of the polytheists of Mecca and their harm; and likewise the emigration of some of them to Medina before the establishment of the rulings of Islam in it is also of this kind. The second: emigration from the abode of unbelief to the abode of Islam, which is the emigration the Muslims made after the establishment of the rulings of Islam in Medina."

Thus His Eminence (may God have mercy on him) divided the abode of unbelief — or say the abode of war — into two kinds: the abode of fear (*dār al-khawf*), and the abode of safety (*dār al-amn*). Likewise Shaykh Mawlānā Muḥammad Sahūl (may God have mercy on him) said, in a treatise of his in which he clarified the fatwa of Shaykh Rashīd Aḥmad al-Gangohī concerning India being Dār al-Ḥarb, which was printed with it later; there came in it [in Urdu]:

> "*Yeh bāt yahān ẓāhir karnā bahut ẓarūrī hai ke āj kal Hindustān bāstiṡnāʾ-e Islāmī riyāsaton ke agarche Ḥaḍrat-e Mujīb aur Ḥaḍrat Shāh ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz aur baʿḍ dīgar akābir kī taṣrīḥ ke muṭābiq dār al-ḥarb hai, magar wāqiʿāt se maʿlūm hotā hai ke woh dār al-amān hai — yaʿnī jis ṭarḥ se Ḥabasha qabl-e hijrat-e sharīf ke bāwujūd dār al-ḥarb*

> ¹ *Ashiʿʿat al-Lamaʿāt* 1/35, in the explanation of the hadith "Actions are only by intentions".

[p. 287] *hone ke dār al-amān thā, isī ṭarḥ se Hindustān bhī āj kal dār al-amān hai. Yehī wajh hai ke yahān se Musalmānon ko hijrat ẓarūrī nahīn hai. Kātib al-ḥurūf ke istifsār ke baʿd Ḥaḍrat Gangohī ne aisā hī mushāfahatan farmāyā thā jo bande ko khūb achchhī ṭarḥ se yād hai.*"

"What must be drawn attention to here is that India — except what is in it of the Muslim princely states — is Dār al-Ḥarb according to the express statements of His Eminence the Respondent [i.e. Shaykh al-Gangohī] and His Eminence Shāh ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz and some other great ones; except that it becomes clear from external reality that it is not Dār al-Ḥarb but Dār al-Amān, exactly like Abyssinia: before the emigration it was regarded as Dār al-Amān despite its being Dār al-Kufr; so likewise India today is Dār al-Amān; and from this emigration from it is not obligatory on its Muslim people. This is what Shaykh al-Gangohī said orally upon the inquiry of the writer of these lines — what he still remembers well."

### Relations of the Islamic government with other states

Since it has become clear from the detail that has just passed that all states, in relation to the Islamic government, are divided into three kinds — Dār al-Islām, Dār al-Amn and Dār al-Khawf — we shall clarify below the details of its relations with each one of these three kinds.

### Relations with Muslim states

We mentioned earlier, in the discussion of the multiplicity of governments and their unity, that the ideal from the Sharia point of view is that all the Muslims throughout the length and breadth of the world have one government administered by one imam and caliph. But — regrettably — they have divided and separated in external reality into more than fifty units, or rather states. So first, all these states should strive to bring about unity among the Muslims of the world and to bring them together in a single, unified Islamic government. So every state must give attempts at establishing unity the place of precedence in its foreign policy, contributing its share towards founding a unified world Islamic state that gathers them all

[p. 288] in a single rank.

God has given the Islamic states great privileges which He did not give to others than them among the holders of other religions. For all their states — beginning from the Far Maghrib and ending with Indonesia — form a chain in which some of its links support others; just as they occupy the best geographical location in the world, where they are located in its heart, possessing the most important main international routes like the Bosphorus Strait, the Suez Canal, the Gulf of Aden, the Strait of Hormuz and so on — in addition to their containing in their interior the most important oil reserves, to the point that the current saying became famous about them: "Oil is found wherever the Muslims are found"; and likewise their wealth and their enormous human heritage. Add also their tremendous material wealth which they never possessed before in all their glorious history: billions upon billions of dollars which they pumped and piled up in the banks of America and the other Western states, for those states to enjoy to the full and profit from, even by lending at interest to the Islamic states if they are in need of them for fulfilling their necessary needs. So in view of these situations, Islamic unity does not guarantee for these states the loftiness of their standing politically only; rather it provides for them their interests from both aspects: economic and defensive also. So if God decreed for it that it be completed, it would emerge on the world stage as a nation with power and a lofty standing among the states of the world in defence and economy.

But, as we noted there as well, the formation of this Islamic unity perhaps cannot be completed and obtained except by the shedding of blood among the Muslims and their fighting one another — which would drag the Umma to a far worse and more evil situation. So until God enables their kings to sacrifice for the sake of unifying their word, so that they form from their states a unified Islamic state, or at least form among them a coalition and union, we find ourselves compelled to consider these dismantled units as abodes of Islam, because their rulers are Muslims. So at the least, there should be among these Islamic states connections of affection and relations of mutual support among one another.

Accordingly, the first priority in forming the foreign policy of the Islamic state is that it try to bring about a relationship and establish a good brotherly connection with its sister states

[p. 289] of the other Islamic states, built on the basis of solidarity and mutual support.

This includes that if the unbelievers launch an attack on one of those states, its sisters must support it and strengthen its position — indeed supply it with military support if its own resources fall short of protecting and defending it. The teachings of the Qur'an and the directives of the Prophetic Sunna and the texts of Islamic jurisprudence are explicit and clear in this field. God, glorified and exalted, said: **"And cooperate in righteousness and piety, but do not cooperate in sin and aggression"** [al-Māʾida, 5:2].

He, mighty is the Speaker, also said: **"The believers are but brothers"** [al-Ḥujurāt, 49:10].

The Prophet ﷺ said: "The Muslim is the brother of the Muslim; he does not wrong him nor hand him over [to the enemy]."¹

He, upon him be blessing and peace, said also: "The believer to the believer is like a building, part of which supports another part."²

Also: "You see the believers, in their mutual mercy, their mutual love and their mutual sympathy, like the body: if one limb complains, the rest of its body calls out to it with sleeplessness and fever."³

From this the jurists stated expressly that the unbelievers, if they attacked a town of the Muslims and there was not sufficiency in them for protecting their land and repelling their enemy, then it is obligatory on whoever is next to them of the Muslims to fight their enemy with them all together. ʿAllāma Ibn Nujaym (may God have mercy on him) said: "And it is an individual obligation (*farḍ ʿayn*) if the enemy attacks: so the woman goes out without the permission of her husband, and the slave without the permission of his master… What is meant is its attacking a specific town of the lands of the Muslims; so it is obligatory upon all the people of that town, and likewise whoever is near to them if there is not sufficiency in its people; and likewise whoever is near to whoever is near, if there was not sufficiency in whoever was near, or they were lazy and disobeyed — and so on until it becomes obligatory on all the people of Islam east and west."⁴

### Relations with non-Muslim states

The first thing we notice in this regard is that there is a vast gulf and a clear difference between

> ¹ *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (3/128), Book of Grievances, hadith no. 2442.
> ² *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (1/103).
> ³ *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (8/10).
> ⁴ *Al-Baḥr al-Rāʾiq Sharḥ Kanz al-Daqāʾiq* and *Minḥat al-Khāliq* and *Takmilat al-Ṭūrī* (5/78).

[p. 290] the purposes of life among the Muslims and among the unbelievers. For the Muslim — personally, or at the level of the state and government — aims, through his life, at attaining the pleasure of God, glorified and exalted, through spending it in obeying Him and submitting to His rulings, beginning with accepting the creedal principles like faith in God and His messengers, and specifically the one who is the loftiest of them in standing and the most eminent of them in rank even if the last of them in time, our master Muhammad ﷺ. While the one bare of this faith — person or government and state — will never strive to attain this aim. So if the purposes of their lives and the aims which each of them seeks from his life on earth differ, it is impossible for a Muslim to form with the unbelievers a connection of real affection, or to establish with them a firm heartfelt relationship such as there is between two persons whom the bond of sharing in purposes and union in aims and goals gathers. It is this kind of friendship that the Qur'an forbade with the most severe prohibition, saying: **"Let not believers take disbelievers as allies (awliyāʾ) rather than believers. Whoever [of you] does that has nothing with God"** [Āl ʿImrān, 3:28].

He said also: **"O you who have believed, do not take the Jews and the Christians as allies. They are [in fact] allies of one another. Whoever is an ally to them among you — then indeed, he is [one] of them"** [al-Māʾida, 5:51].

And: **"You will not find a people who believe in God and the Last Day having affection for those who oppose God and His Messenger, even if they were their fathers or their sons or their brothers or their kindred"** [al-Mujādila, 58:22].

And: **"O you who have believed, do not take My enemies and your enemies as allies, extending to them affection while they have disbelieved in what came to you of the truth, having driven out the Prophet and yourselves [only] because you believe in God, your Lord"** [al-Mumtaḥana, 60:1].

His Eminence Shaykh Mufti Muhammad Shafiʿ (may God have mercy on him), in the commentary on these aforementioned verses, undertook to shed light on the ruling of the relations of the Muslims with non-Muslims, in a weighty, comprehensive style covering all its forms and states. We see it as most fitting to transmit it to you in Arabic translation. He (may God have mercy on him) said: "Many of the Qur'anic verses have mentioned this meaning in summary and in detail: in them the Muslims are forbidden with the most severe prohibition from taking the unbelievers as allies, having affection for them and befriending them. So if the ignorant among the unbelievers looked at it without [knowing] the reality of the matter, they would think that there is no room in Islam for anything of being courteous to them or

[p. 291] being kind to them or dealing with them with good character. On another side, many of the Qur'anic verses and the Prophetic hadiths, in word and deed, and the stories of the dealing of the Rightly-Guided Caliphs and others than them of the Companions, may God be pleased with them all, clearly and explicitly indicate the permissibility of being kind to them and showing them goodwill, to which it is difficult to find a parallel in the nations and the other religions. So in view of these two aspects, some — even those of superficial view among the Muslims — may feel a contradiction and conflict between the rulings of the Qur'an and the Prophetic Sunna and its teachings. The truth is that both these ideas were only born from the passing look and the deficient tracing of the rulings of the Qur'an and the Sunna and its teachings. For if all the Qur'anic verses that have connection with this subject were gathered, then the view were deepened in them, non-Muslims would have no grounds for complaint, nor illusion of contradiction and inconsistency between the verses and the rulings established from them. So we explain this place with an explanation that clarifies the import of each of alliance (*muwālāt*) and kindness to someone and goodwill towards him, and distinguishes them from one another.

The truth is that there are several degrees of relations and connections between two persons or two groups. Among them: alliance (*al-muwālāt*), or heartfelt affection; and it is specific to the Muslims. It is categorically impermissible for a Muslim to form this kind of connection with an unbeliever.

Among them: goodwill (*al-muwāsāt*), and it is the relationship of mutual sympathy and sincere counsel and doing someone good. It is permissible for the Muslim to establish it with all the unbelievers except those waging war among them; and its detail came in the eighth verse of Sūrat al-Mumtaḥana, where God, glorified and exalted, said in it: **"God does not forbid you from those who do not fight you because of religion and do not expel you from your homes — from being righteous toward them and acting justly toward them"** [al-Mumtaḥana, 60:8].

The third degree: it is courtesy (*al-mudārāt*), and its meaning: gentleness, good character, companionship and social intercourse. Bringing it about is also permissible with all the unbelievers, if the aim from it was benefiting them religiously, or hosting them if they asked to be his guests, or protecting oneself from their strife and their harm. This third rank is what is meant in His words, the Exalted: **"Except when taking precaution against them in prudence"** [Āl ʿImrān, 3:28] — a phrase that comes within

[p. 292] the aforementioned verse of Sūrat Āl ʿImrān; i.e. alliance with the unbelievers is not permissible except in the form where you intended by it protecting yourselves. Since courtesy outwardly resembles alliance, it was properly excepted from it in the verse.

The fourth degree: it is transactions (*al-muʿāmalāt*); and its meaning: that transactions be concluded with the unbelievers in the sector of trade or leasing or employment, profession and industry and so on. Concluding them is also permissible with all the unbelievers — except if it causes harm to the generality of the Muslims. The action of the Prophet ﷺ and the dealing of the Rightly-Guided Caliphs and others than them of the Companions, may God be pleased with them all, bear witness to that. Hence they prohibited selling weapons to the unbelievers, while permitting trade and leasing, and employing them or being employed with them in their factories and their institutions.

So it became known from the foregoing detail that it is never permissible for the Muslim to conclude with an unbeliever alliance or heartfelt affection; while goodwill and sincere counsel are permissible with all of them except those waging war among them; and likewise the Sharia permits him to be courteous to them and to have good character towards them if it was for hosting them, or for the purpose of benefiting them religiously and providing religious information to them, or protecting oneself from their evil and their harm."¹

The gist of the words is that Islam has forbidden alliance and heartfelt affection, with the unbelievers — the kind that erases the difference in life's purposes between the two parties. As for other than that — of truce, the conclusion of peaceful agreements, human goodwill, being kind to them and counselling them on the basis of humanity, and cooperation and mutual support exchanged for realizing the common human interests — all this is permissible — indeed preferred and beloved — in Islam. We see it as best to present here several texts of the Qur'an and the Sunna related to the subject, and several examples from the eras of the Prophet and the Companions in this regard.

> ¹ *Maʿārif al-Qurʾān*, Sūrat Āl ʿImrān, verse 82 [sic: 28], part 2, pp. 50–51.

### Keeping covenants and agreements

[p. 293] First, here is the Qur'anic text which indicates the legitimacy of making peace and reconciliation with non-Muslim states if they do not conflict with the interest of the Muslims, and it is His words, the Exalted: **"And if they incline to peace, then incline to it [also] and rely upon God"** [al-Anfāl, 8:61].

And once a truce has been concluded, it strongly emphasizes fulfilling it, saying: **"O you who have believed, fulfil [all] contracts"** [al-Māʾida, 5:1].

And: **"And do not let the hatred of a people for having obstructed you from al-Masjid al-Ḥarām lead you to transgress"** [al-Māʾida, 5:2].

Even in the form where the Muslims feared from their enemy the breaking of the covenant, the Sharia also did not permit them to take any measure except after cancelling the treaty and openly repudiating it. God the Exalted said: **"If you [have reason to] fear from a people betrayal, throw [their treaty] back to them, [putting you] on equal terms. Indeed, God does not like traitors"** [al-Anfāl, 8:58].

The history of the two eras of the Message and the Companions is full of splendid stories and pioneering events showing how faithfully and well they carried out this command and applied this principle. Among those stories is what Muslim narrated in his *Ṣaḥīḥ* from Ḥudhayfa ibn al-Yamān; he said:

"Nothing prevented me from witnessing Badr except that I set out, I and my father Ḥusayl. He said: The unbelievers of Quraysh seized us; they said: You want Muhammad. We said: We do not want him; we only want Medina. So they took from us the covenant of God and His pledge that we would go away to Medina and not fight with him. So we came to the Messenger of God ﷺ and told him the news; he said: 'Go away; we will fulfil their covenant for them, and we will seek God's help against them.'"¹

Let us weigh the gravity of the situation by our own standards. It was the first battle the Muslims fought in defence of their religion, the battle by which God had decreed to judge between truth and falsehood and distinguish one from the other; those Muslims who took part in it would win reward, pride and honour in Islamic society such as none

> ¹ *Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim* (3/1414), Book of Jihad, chapter on fulfilling the covenant, hadith no. 4603.

[p. 294] after them would attain; and it was a struggle of 313 men, barely armed, against a thousand fully armed warriors, which left them in the direst need of manpower, even a single man. Moreover, Ḥudhayfa and his father had a plausible justification for breaking the covenant, since they had not taken the covenant and the pledge upon themselves willingly; rather it was under compulsion from the unbelievers and coercion. But the Prophet ﷺ forbade them from breaking this covenant — if it is correct to call it a covenant — with clarity and firmness, in avoidance of the slightest blemish of disloyalty and treachery. By that he set up a bright lamp and a pioneering model example for his Umma for avoiding treachery even in so critical and desperate a situation.

Likewise Abū Dāwūd narrated with a sound chain from Abū Rāfiʿ, may God be pleased with him; he said: "Quraysh sent me to the Messenger of God ﷺ. When I saw the Messenger of God ﷺ, Islam was cast into my heart. So I said: O Messenger of God, by God I will never return to them. So the Messenger of God ﷺ said: 'I do not break the covenant, nor do I detain messengers; but return, and if what is in your soul now is in your soul, then come back.' He said: So I went; then I came to the Prophet ﷺ and became Muslim."¹

Although there was at that time no contract or covenant between the Prophet ﷺ and the polytheists of Mecca, he nevertheless ordered him to return to them so that it would not be a breach of what is regarded as a covenant by custom and implication. That is because of what ʿAllāma al-Khaṭṭābī (may God have mercy on him) said: "The meaning is perhaps that the message requires an answer, and the answer does not reach the senders except upon the tongue of the messenger after his departure; so it became as if a covenant had been contracted for him for the duration of his coming and his return — and God knows best."²

As for the era of the Companions, read with me the following story which Imam Abū Dāwūd (may God have mercy on him) narrated: that there was between Muʿāwiya and the Romans a covenant, and he was travelling towards their lands so that when the covenant ended he would raid them. A man came on a horse or a nag, saying: God is greatest, God is greatest — fulfilment and not treachery! They looked, and it was ʿAmr ibn ʿAbasa. So Muʿāwiya sent to him and asked him; he said: I heard the Messenger of God ﷺ say: "Whoever has between him and a people a covenant, let him not tighten a knot nor untie it until

> ¹ *Sunan Abī Dāwūd* (3/82), Book of Jihad, chapter on the imam being consulted about covenants, hadith no. 2758; its chain is sound.
> ² *Maʿālim al-Sunan* (2/317).

[p. 295] its term ends, or he throws [it] back to them on equal terms." So Muʿāwiya returned.¹

Let us notice that Muʿāwiya, may God be pleased with him, did not actually break the covenant; because he entered the land of the enemy and attacked them after the end of the period of the ceasefire and the halting of war. But since he had begun marching towards them before it ended, ʿAmr ibn ʿAbasa, may God be pleased with him, regarded this as contrary to scrupulousness and caution, and Muʿāwiya, may God be pleased with him, yielded to him, for fear that it might be disobedience issued from him and opposition to the saying of the Prophet ﷺ. So perhaps it is the unique example in which the conqueror gave up what he had conquered of lands merely for fulfilling the requirement of caution and scrupulousness!

Just as the story indicated that what plunged them into the depths of the battles was not the greed to take control of lands nor the desire to prevail over servants; rather their aim from all that was attaining the pleasure of God, glorified and exalted. So when they saw that [God's pleasure] lay in giving up the conquered lands, they gave them up at once.

Muʿāwiya, may God be pleased with him, also has that splendid story which al-Qāsim ibn Sallām narrated with his chain as follows: "The Romans made peace with Muʿāwiya on [condition] that he pay them money, and Muʿāwiya took hostages from them and put them in Baalbek. Then the Romans acted treacherously. Muʿāwiya and the Muslims refused to consider it lawful to kill the hostages in their hands, and let them go, and sought [God's] victory over them by that [conduct], and said: Fulfilment in [the face of] treachery is better than treachery for treachery."²

### Pacts of joint defence

Alongside ceasefire agreements, there are found examples of the pacts which the Prophet ﷺ contracted and concluded with the unbelievers concerning common defence and common protection.

Among that is that Medina, when the Prophet ﷺ came to it, was dominated by the tribal system, and many tribes inhabited it — among them tribes of the Jews also — each one of which had its master and its head; and there was no central government uniting them. So the Prophet ﷺ called them to

> ¹ *Sunan Abī Dāwūd* (3/83).
> ² *Al-Amwāl* of al-Qāsim ibn Sallām (1/211), paragraph 446.

[p. 296] join a single government formed by mutual agreement — a matter which these welcomed. So at that time the Prophet ﷺ undertook laying down the constitution of the city-state in Medina in the form of a written document in which he included the rights and duties of all the citizens; and it is the first written constitution in human history, according to what Dr. Ḥamīdullāh (may God have mercy on him) verified. It contained 43 [sic] clauses, and clauses 37 and 44 of them were devoted to the alliance between the Muslims and the Jews concerning mutual support if an enemy attacked Medina. Here are their texts:

(37) "And that upon the Jews is their expenditure and upon the Muslims their expenditure; and that between them is help against whoever wars against the people of this document; and that between them is sincere counsel and good conduct, not sin."

(44) "And that between them is help against whoever attacks Yathrib."¹

Likewise it was in the Treaty of al-Ḥudaybiya: the contract between the Prophet ﷺ and the polytheists of Mecca was concerning ceasefire only; as for the Banū Khuzāʿa, there was between them and the Muslims an alliance concerning common defence, as a continuation of what they had been allied upon with the Banū Hāshim in the Jāhiliyya — and most of them had not yet become Muslim.

The detail of that is that when the Treaty of al-Ḥudaybiya took place between the Messenger of God ﷺ and Quraysh, among what they stipulated was that whoever liked to enter into the contract of the Messenger of God ﷺ and his pledge, let him enter into it; and whoever liked to enter into the contract of Quraysh and their pledge, let him enter into it. [So the Banū Bakr entered] into the pledge of Quraysh, and Khuzāʿa entered into the pledge of the Messenger of God ﷺ. This amounted to nothing other than an alliance for common defence: that the polytheists and the Banū Bakr, if they attacked one of these two allies, it would be binding on the other to help his ally and support him in protecting him and defending him.

Then the Banū Bakr rose and attacked Khuzāʿa by night, and struck from them a man; and they argued and fought, and the Banū Bakr were reinforced by Quraysh with weapons, and some of Quraysh fought with them at night in hiding.

When the Banū Bakr and Quraysh supported each other against Khuzāʿa [and struck from them what they struck] and broke what was between them and the Messenger of God ﷺ of covenant and pledge, ʿAmr ibn Sālim al-Khuzāʿī went out — Ibn Saʿd said: among forty riders — until he came to the Messenger of God ﷺ in Medina; and that was what stirred up

> ¹ *Majmūʿat al-Wathāʾiq al-Siyāsiyya li-l-ʿAhd al-Nabawī wa-l-Khilāfa al-Rāshida* (1/59–62).

[p. 297] the conquest of Mecca. So the Messenger of God ﷺ said: "You have been helped, O ʿAmr ibn Sālim." He informed the people that he was travelling to Mecca, and ordered them to make earnest preparations — which led to the conquest of Mecca, may God ennoble it.¹

So the scholars of Islamic politics argued by it that it is permissible for the Muslims to make alliances with the unbelievers and conclude with them treaties concerning common defence. Al-Ḥāfiẓ Ibn Ḥajar (may God have mercy on him) said: "And that is not counted among alliance with the unbelievers nor affection for the enemies of God; rather it is of the kind of employing them and reducing the force of their gathering and setting some of them against others."²

### Goodwill and sincere counsel

God loves goodwill and sincere counsel and good dealing with the unbelievers on the basis of humanity. At the individual level, this is shown by what came in the Qur'an of emphasizing good companionship and good social intercourse with the polytheist parents, except obeying them in polytheism and unbelief, where God, glorified and exalted, said: **"But if they endeavour to make you associate with Me that of which you have no knowledge, do not obey them but accompany them in [this] world with appropriate kindness"** [Luqmān, 31:15].

His general command in this regard has passed in the verse of Sūrat al-Mumtaḥana: **"God does not forbid you from those who do not fight you because of religion and do not expel you from your homes — from being righteous toward them and acting justly toward them"** [al-Mumtaḥana, 60:8].

This indicates [the permissibility of] good relations with the polytheists and keeping ties with them, individually and socially at the level of the state; and the researcher who studies the Prophetic biography, upon its owner be blessing and peace, finds abundant examples in it of ties of this kind. Among them is what al-Bukhārī narrated with his chain from Asmāʾ bint Abī Bakr, may God be pleased with them both; she said: My mother came to me while she was a polytheist in the time of the Messenger of God ﷺ. So I asked the Messenger of God ﷺ for a fatwa; I said: And she is desirous [of my kindness]; shall I keep ties with my mother? He said: "Yes, keep ties with your mother."³

> ¹ *ʿUyūn al-Athar* (2/212).
> ² *Fatḥ al-Bārī* of Ibn Ḥajar (5/338).
> ³ *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (3/164).

[p. 298] More explicit and more astonishing than that is what was of his ﷺ goodwill towards the polytheists of Mecca: they had rolled up their sleeves to war against him and show him enmity; they spared no effort in persecuting him and persecuting his Companions and bringing down upon them what was within their capacity of calamities and afflictions. They boycotted him and his family in the Shiʿb of Abū Ṭālib — a boycott that compelled them to live on eating the leaves of trees and the skins of animals. But despite all that, when the vicissitudes of fate turned and the days revolved against Quraysh, and a severe famine struck them in which they ate bones and skins — what the Qur'an expressed, according to the commentary on the verse, by the coming of the sky with a visible smoke — Abū Sufyān, still an unbeliever, came to the Messenger of God ﷺ and said: "O Muhammad! Your people have perished; so call upon God to remove it from them." So the Messenger of God ﷺ asked for rain for them, and rain came down upon them, and the affliction of the famine was removed. ʿAllāma al-Sarakhsī (may God have mercy on him) said that he also sent to them five hundred dinars to distribute among the needy among them.¹

He gave Abū Sufyān a large amount of ʿajwa dates as a gift, and asked him for a gift of leather.² And Dr. Ḥamīdullāh (may God have mercy on him) held that asking them for a gift of leather also was only from the motive of gentleness with them; perhaps the Prophet ﷺ thought that Quraysh would not find anyone to buy their leather because of the blocking of the road to Syria, and that it would rot and spoil if it remained with them unused; so he asked them for it in exchange for the dates, to fulfil their food need.

Read also the story of Thumāma ibn Uthāl, the master of the Banū Ḥanīfa: an expedition of the Prophet ﷺ directed to Najd captured him; then the Prophet let him go, and Islam had been made beloved to him in the wake of seeing the character of the Prophet ﷺ and his splendid traits and his virtuous dealing; so he became Muslim. Yamāma was at that time the road of grains and cereals for Quraysh; so Thumāma swore that no grain of wheat would come to them from Yamāma until the Prophet ﷺ gave permission for it — which troubled the unbelievers and exhausted them. So they wrote to the Prophet ﷺ: "You command keeping ties of kinship"; so he wrote to Thumāma that he leave [the way open] between them and the carrying [of grain] to them.³

> ¹ *Al-Mabsūṭ* of al-Sarakhsī (10/92), chapter on the peace of kings.
> ² *Al-Amwāl* of al-Qāsim ibn Sallām (1/328).
> ³ *Fatḥ al-Bārī* of Ibn Ḥajar (8/88).

[p. 299] Perhaps human history has not seen — over its whole extent — an example of goodwill and good companionship more splendid and more majestic than what came from the Prophet ﷺ when God enabled him, on the day of the conquest of Mecca, over his enemies who were thirsty for his blood, whose fierce enmities had stretched for a long period in which they harmed him in every way of harm possible for them. But he did not treat them harshly and did not take revenge from them; rather it was nothing from him except that he said to them: "No blame upon you today; you are the freed ones (*al-ṭulaqāʾ*)."

These events teach us that what is hateful is not the unbelievers themselves, but their false beliefs and their mischief (*fitna*). As for showing them goodwill and sincere concern on the basis of humanity, that is desirable in the Sharia. It is from this very principle of sincere concern for them that the command to invite them to Islam springs; and jihad likewise has no aim other than sincere concern for all humanity — preserving and protecting it from the corruption and strife that unbelief and tyranny bring upon it.

### Cooperation in good works

Above all that, Islam permits partnership and cooperation with the unbelievers in matters of good, righteousness and keeping ties — indeed urges it — as an explicit, clear Qur'anic text came concerning it: **"And cooperate in righteousness and piety, but do not cooperate in sin and aggression"** [al-Māʾida, 5:2].

Let it be known that this portion of the verse came in the context of [forbidding] aggression against the unbelievers; and the verse in its entirety is as follows: **"And do not let the hatred of a people for having obstructed you from al-Masjid al-Ḥarām lead you to transgress. Cooperate in righteousness and piety, but do not cooperate in sin and aggression. Fear God; indeed, God is severe in penalty"** [al-Māʾida, 5:2].

So it includes cooperation with the unbelievers too in righteousness and good works. From this, if the unbelievers put forward a project which aims at the interest of humanity, and in which there is not what conflicts with the interest of Islam and the Muslims, then it is permissible — indeed recommended — for the Muslims to contribute to it and take part in accomplishing it.

There are found in the biography of the Prophet ﷺ many examples of the relationship of partnership and participation with the unbelievers in matters of good, repelling evil and deterring from injustice — the most prominent of them the pact which the Prophet ﷺ contracted

[p. 300] in participation with the unbelievers, by the name "Ḥilf al-Fuḍūl" (the Pact of the Virtuous). Here are its details in what follows:

### Ḥilf al-Fuḍūl (the Pact of the Virtuous)

The pacts of mutual cooperation took place in the Jāhiliyya on the basis of the tribe, in the sense that a person was committed to helping whoever belonged to his tribe, whether in the right or the wrong, just or unjust, wrongdoer or wronged. In all these cases a person was bound to support his tribesman, even if he was upon clear injustice and evident aggression.

Ḥilf al-Fuḍūl was the noblest pact ever heard of and the most honourable among the Arabs. The first to speak of it and call to it was al-Zubayr ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. Its cause was that a man of Zubayd came to Mecca with goods, and al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil bought them from him; and he had power and nobility in Mecca, so he withheld his right from him. So the Zubaydī sought help against him from the allies: ʿAbd al-Dār, Makhzūm, Jumaḥ, Sahm and ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb; but they refused to help him against al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil and rebuked him — that is, they repelled him. When the Zubaydī saw this ill treatment, he went up on Abū Qubays at sunrise, while Quraysh were in their gatherings around the Kaʿba, and cried out at the top of his voice:

> *O family of Fihr, for one wronged in his goods,*
> *in the heart of Mecca, far from home and kin…*

So al-Zubayr ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib stood up for that and said: This cannot be left unanswered. So Hāshim, Zuhra and Taym ibn Murra gathered in the house of Ibn Judʿān, and he made food for them, and they allied in Dhū al-Qaʿda in a sacred month, standing; and they contracted and covenanted by God that they would be one hand with the wronged against the wrongdoer until his right is paid to him — as long as the sea wets a wool-tuft, and as long as Ḥirāʾ and Thabīr stand firm in their places — and on mutual support in livelihood. So Quraysh named that pact "Ḥilf al-Fuḍūl".¹ So they stood against al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil and rescued from him his right.

The Prophet ﷺ took part in it before he had been sent as a messenger; but after his mission he praised it, saying: "I would not exchange a pact I witnessed in the house of Ibn Judʿān for the finest red camels, nor would I break it. Hāshim, Zuhra and Taym allied that they would be with the wronged as long as the sea wets a wool-tuft; and if I were called to it

> ¹ *Al-Rawḍ al-Unuf*, ed. al-Salāmī (2/46).

[p. 301] I would answer."¹

It is also narrated — with a slight difference in wording — in each of al-Ḥākim's *Mustadrak*, and the *Sīra Nabawiyya* of Ibn Hishām and Ibn Kathīr.²

It was only named Ḥilf al-Fuḍūl because of its containing many virtues (*faḍāʾil*); and it was said: that Quraysh were preceded to the like of this pact by Jurhum, so three of them allied, they and those who followed them: one of them al-Faḍl ibn Faḍāla, the second al-Faḍl ibn Wadāʿa, and the third Fuḍayl ibn al-Ḥārith. So since the later pact of Quraysh resembled the action of these Jurhumīs, it was named Ḥilf al-Fuḍūl — and *al-fuḍūl* is the plural of *faḍl* — and they are the names of those whose mention preceded.³

At any rate, in view of the aforementioned hadith in which the Prophet ﷺ praised Ḥilf al-Fuḍūl and showed his resolve to answer [it] if he were called to it today, ʿAllāma al-Suhaylī (may God have mercy on him) and others of the jurists held that it is permissible for the Muslims — indeed recommended — that they enter with non-Muslims into a covenant aiming at helping the wronged and repelling injustice and the like of that of the good purposes beneficial to humanity.

### Jihad

It has become clear from the detail that has passed that the nature of the Islamic state's relationship with non-Muslims is determined by the conduct of the unbelievers, as God the Exalted said: **"So as long as they are upright toward you, be upright toward them"** [al-Tawba, 9:7].

But the elements which form a real threat to Islam and the Muslims, which have launched all their forces in hostility against them and made them a target for their aggression and persecution; and which try to obstruct the course of the Islamic call by putting obstacles in its way; or whose habit and occupation have become corruption and destruction against humanity — the Islamic government founded on straight foundations does not know, to deter them from their errors, any option except jihad and struggle, armed and unarmed.

> ¹ *Al-Ṭabaqāt al-Kubrā*, al-ʿIlmiyya ed. (1/103), and *ʿUyūn al-Athar* of Ibn Sayyid al-Nās 1/59.
> ² *Al-Mustadrak* of al-Ḥākim, end of the Book of the Mukātab 2/220, and al-Dhahabī confirmed it; and *al-Rawḍ al-Unuf* (1/56); and *al-Sīra al-Nabawiyya* of Ibn Kathīr 1/258.
> ³ *Al-Rawḍ al-Unuf*, ed. al-Salāmī (2/46).

[p. 302] As for fighting (*al-qitāl*) — or armed jihad — many of the Qur'anic verses have dealt with explaining the purposes of its legitimacy; among those is His words, glorified and exalted: **"Permission [to fight] has been given to those who are being fought, because they were wronged. Indeed, God is competent to give them victory"** [al-Ḥajj, 22:39].

So from it is known the first purpose of fighting, namely that it be for defending Islam or the Islamic state if the unbelievers attacked it. Let it be noted that the form of "permission" (*al-idhn*) used in the verse, which tells of permission and allowance only without emphasis on it, came by virtue of its being the first verse that speaks of the legitimacy of fighting for the Muslims, and that was in the wake of the rulings of patience and forbearance with which the Muslims had been charged in their Meccan life, without it being permitted for them in it to raise weapons or fight. Otherwise, other than it of the Qur'anic verses and the Prophetic hadiths indicate that fighting and armed struggle are obligatory on the Muslims in such a case — not merely permitted. God the Exalted said: **"Fight in the way of God those who fight you, but do not transgress. Indeed, God does not like transgressors"** [al-Baqara, 2:190].

The second purpose of fighting in the light of the Noble Qur'an is: rescuing the Muslims who face waves of injustice and persecution in an unbelieving state in which they reside. God — mighty is the Speaker — said: **"And what is [the matter] with you that you fight not in the cause of God and [for] the oppressed among men, women and children who say, 'Our Lord, take us out of this city of oppressive people and appoint for us from Yourself a protector and appoint for us from Yourself a helper'?"** [al-Nisāʾ, 4:75].

This also covers the case where the unbelievers set up obstacles and placed hindrances in the way of the Islamic call — what the Qur'an expressed in places by "obstruction from the way of God" (*al-ṣadd ʿan sabīl Allāh*). For God, and He is the most truthful of speakers, said: **"They ask you about the sacred month — about fighting therein. Say: 'Fighting therein is great [sin], but averting [people] from the way of God and disbelief in Him and [preventing access to] al-Masjid al-Ḥarām and the expulsion of its people therefrom are greater [evil] in the sight of God. Fitna is greater than

[p. 303] killing.' And they will continue to fight you until they turn you back from your religion if they are able"** [al-Baqara, 2:217].

The third aim which Islam aims at behind the legitimacy of fighting is: curbing *fitna* (persecution/oppression). The ruling of jihad for curbing *fitna* is not confined to cases where those persecuted are Muslims; rather whenever the unbelievers set up in the world a tyrannical, oppressive, despotic system, and began to bind God's servants in it with their man-made rulings and invented laws, the Islamic Sharia commands its followers to fight them in such a case, to rescue humanity from their injustice by breaking their power and ending their dominance, and striving to establish the just divine order in its place. This is what we mean to include among the aims which Islam seeks behind the legitimacy of fighting, and it is what is meant in His words, the Exalted: **"And fight them until there is no fitna and [until] the religion, all of it, is for God. If they cease — then indeed, God is Seeing of what they do"** [al-Anfāl, 8:39].

It is what Ribʿī ibn ʿĀmir, may God be pleased with him, expressed in the council of Rustam — commander of the army of Iran — when he asked him about what had brought them to Iran; he answered him, saying: "God has sent us to bring out whom He wills from the worship of servants to the worship of God, and from the narrowness of this world to its breadth, and from the tyranny of religions to the justice of Islam."¹

Whoever reviews the system of the tyrannical Chosroes and becomes acquainted with the forms of his enslaving of the subjects will grasp precisely how true these words of Ribʿī were.

At any rate, these are several purposes for whose realization jihad was legislated in Islam; and this brief discussion has no room for further detail on them. There are extended books and studies aiming at refuting the propaganda broadcast against the legitimacy of jihad in Islam, and specifically the claim that its aim is to compel people to embrace it and forcing them to accept it.

The wars people waged before the dawn of Islam, and those the great powers wage today, have no aim but control over lands and wealth, or preserving nationalist (*al-waṭaniyya*) or linguistic partisanship and the like of that of purely material purposes

> ¹ *Al-Bidāya wa-l-Nihāya*, Hajar ed. (9/622).

[p. 304] which serve their interests only. Their fires, when they flare, leave behind every law and moral regulation entirely discarded, without being restricted by them or committed to them in anything. Islam, by complete contrast, first established that fighting must be for a legitimate cause and a good purpose — which made it a war with honour and dignity, and protected it from being an instrument of enslavement and colonization and the ownership of lands and control over wealth, on the one hand. Second, it granted the world just rules for the methods followed during engaging in fighting, which the world had never known in the fields of wars. Proceeding from this, the Muslim jurists took the lead in the service of the subject of international relations with every elaboration and detail — classification and composition, rule-making and foundation-laying, ramification and application — with all precision and careful thought such as had never occurred to humanity before them — indeed humanity had not been able even to conceive of it. All the books of jurisprudence in general, and the books composed on the subject of the *siyar* [the law of war and peace] in particular, are full of the rulings of Islam and its teachings in this field. So Islamic government must take them into account and implement them in all its international dealings, whether in the state of truce or war.

---

## Chapter Six: On Removing the Imam and Overthrowing the Government

### Introduction

[p. 305] Many Western writers have expressed their opinion that Islam does not permit following any way to topple the government and remove it once it has been formed and established. Perhaps its source is nothing but the superficial, hasty look at the Islamic rulings which emphasize the prohibition of rebellion (*al-khurūj*) against the imam and forbid it categorically. But it is in reality a pure error which has no basis at all from argument or reason. Rather, just as Islam possesses within its folds safe ways for appointing the imam and designating the caliph, so too its teachings cover how to remove and depose him by calm, peaceful ways.

### 1 – Peaceful, safe removal

The following are the peaceful grounds on which the imam is removed from office under the Sharia:

**(1)** If he removed himself — that is, he submitted the resignation and showed his inability to continue the work as caliph and imam; for the Sharia judges in this form that he is removed, as is apparent.

**(2)** That something befall him which prevents him from performing the functions of the imam — as if madness overtook him, or illness struck him which prevented him from undertaking even his ordinary affairs and his daily occupations. So it is self-evident that his removal is judged in this form also.

**(3)** If he committed something of the matters of transgression (*fisq*), like drinking wine and taking bribes and imposing non-Sharia taxes on the subjects, and other than that of the matters of transgression, including injustice and tyranny in rule as well. Its ruling is that he deserves removal for his committing these matters; but fighting him and armed rebellion against him are not yet permissible. This means that if the Muslims are able to remove him by a safe way, they are obliged to do so, as al-Ḥāfiẓ Ibn Ḥajar (may God have mercy on him) said: "What the scholars hold concerning the commanders

[p. 306] of tyranny is that if he is able to depose him without strife (*fitna*) and without injustice, it is obligatory; otherwise the obligation is patience."¹

The meaning of patience here: the prohibition of raising weapons against him and removing him by acts of armed violence; this does not preclude efforts by safe, peaceful means — for example, the matter could be referred to the shūrā council or the court to determine whether the transgressions he has committed warrant his removal; and if they find that they do, they would remove him.

**(4)** And if there was not found any of these three matters, but his policies and his plans do not appear in agreement with the interests of the state, such that aversion occurs towards him among the people — does he deserve by this his removal or not? I did not find its ruling expressly stated in the words of the scholars, neither in negation nor in affirmation. What seems most correct to me, in view of the general rules, is that there would be no harm if the shūrā which chose him and put him in charge of the imamate removed him in such circumstances by peaceful constitutional means. It is supported by the hadith of Anas ibn Mālik, may God be pleased with him, that the Messenger of God ﷺ cursed three, among them: "A man who led a people [in prayer] while they disliked him."²

Let it be known that there is talk about the chain of this hadith; but the route by which al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī narrated it as mursal is sound, just as it is soundly narrated as mawqūf from ʿAmr ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Muṣṭaliq, may God be pleased with him; and al-Tirmidhī (may God have mercy on him) himself cited it after this hadith.

So the hadith indicated that it is not commendable for a person to insist on the imamate of Muslims who dislike him; so if it were possible to replace him with one better than him and more righteous, doing so would accord with the import of this Prophetic hadith, upon its owner be blessing and peace.

Also, ʿAwf ibn Mālik narrated from the Messenger of God ﷺ; he said: "The best of your imams are those whom you love and who love you, and who pray for you and you pray for them; and the worst of your imams are those whom you hate and who hate you, and whom you curse and who curse you."³

> ¹ *Fatḥ al-Bārī* of Ibn Ḥajar (13/8), Book of Trials, chapter on his saying ﷺ: The destruction of my Umma…
> ² *Sunan al-Tirmidhī*, ed. Shākir (2/191), Book of Prayer, chapter on one who leads a people…, hadith no. 358.
> ³ *Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim* (3/1481), Book of Leadership, hadith no. 4768.

[p. 307] It came in the same hadith that the Companions asked the Prophet ﷺ, saying: Shall we not oppose them with the sword?

So he answered them ﷺ, saying: "No, as long as they establish the prayer among you. If you see from your rulers something you dislike, then dislike his action, and do not withdraw a hand from obedience."

But — as we said before — its meaning is nothing but the prohibition of opposing them with the force of the sword and weapons, and what is in it of shedding blood and stirring up strife; and seeking his removal by a calm, peaceful way does not negate it.

Proceeding from this, if the Muslims wanted to lay down the constitution of the state on Islamic foundations, it is fitting that they provide in it for a clause concerned with defining the ways and mechanisms that will be adopted for his deposition in the like of these situations and states.

**(4)** [sic] It has prevailed in most states of the world today that the headship of the state — whether the head of state or the prime minister occupies it — is held for a fixed term, and its holder leaves office automatically merely by the passing of that period, so that another president fills his place. Although no fixed term existed for the caliph or chosen imam in Islamic history, Islam — as we said more than once — has granted its rulings which it legislated in the sector of politics a flexibility with which the needs of this age and its requirements are accommodated; for we do not find any ruling in the Qur'an or the Prophetic Sunna which contradicts defining a period for the imamate and the command. So if the shūrā which chooses and appoints the imam sets a fixed term for him, there is no harm in that from the Sharia point of view. But the term should be long enough for the president to carry out his policies and plans effectively. If this is accepted, it would be the fourth way for the removal of the imam, summarized in that he is removed by the passing of the period of the imamate defined for him.

### Rebellion against the imam [undertaking acts of armed violence against the imam]

It becomes clear from the teachings of the Prophet ﷺ and his directives that he regarded civil wars in the Islamic state as among the worst of matters and the most heinous of wrongs. It is the matter which he ﷺ strongly emphasized

[p. 308] in his sermon of the Farewell Pilgrimage, saying:
