"For your blood and your property — Muḥammad said: and I think he said — and your honour are sacred to you, like the sanctity of this day of yours, in this land of yours, in this month of yours; and you will meet your Lord, and He will ask you about your deeds. Lo, do not return after me as misguided ones [or: unbelievers], some of you striking the necks of others. Lo, let the present inform the absent; for perhaps some of those whom it reaches will be more retentive of it than some of those who heard it." Then he said: "Lo, have I conveyed?" twice.¹

Hence we see that the Islamic Sharia has sometimes tolerated grave wrongs merely for protecting the Muslims from plunging into civil wars. The Prophet ﷺ repeatedly forbade, with a categorical prohibition, in different ways and various styles, armed rebellion against the imam or the caliph whose rule has been established and whose authority is in place — so that ferocious wars do not break out and bloody battles do not erupt among the Muslims. There passed before us the hadith of ʿAwf ibn Mālik al-Ashjaʿī in which came: "Lo, whoever has a governor appointed over him, and he sees him do something of disobedience to God, let him dislike what he does of disobedience to God, and let him not withdraw a hand from obedience."²

It is even reported from Ḥudhayfa ibn al-Yamān, may God be pleased with him, that the Prophet ﷺ said once, in what he said about the trials (*fitan*) of the end of time: "And there will stand among them men whose hearts are the hearts of devils in the bodies of humans." So he asked him, saying: What should I do, O Messenger of God, if I reach that? So the Prophet ﷺ answered him by saying: "You hear and obey the commander; and if your back is struck and your property taken, then hear and obey."³

In a report: "If God has a caliph on the earth, and he struck your back and took your property, then obey him."⁴

These hadiths aim only at prohibiting rebellion against the imam, to avoid

> ¹ *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (5/177), chapter on the Farewell Pilgrimage, hadith no. 4406; *Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim*, chapter on al-Qasāma.
> ² *Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim* (3/1482), Book of Leadership, hadith 4768.
> ³ *Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim* (3/1476), Book of Leadership, hadith 4763.
> ⁴ *Jāmiʿ al-Uṣūl* (10/46).

[p. 309] bloodshed among the Muslims themselves; so it does not contradict what we said earlier: that it is obligatory on the Muslims to devote their efforts to adopting safe, peaceful ways to turn the like of this ruler away and remove him from the throne of rule. It is also permissible for a person to defend himself as far as possible for him, as its detail will come in what follows, God willing.

But there is one form in which the Prophet ﷺ permitted armed rebellion to topple the government of the imam and bring down his system — and it is what came in the hadith of ʿUbāda ibn al-Ṣāmit, may God be pleased with him: that the Prophet ﷺ took their pledge on hearing and obedience in what we are eager for and what we dislike, and in our hardship and our ease, even when others are preferred over us, and that we not dispute the matter with its people — "unless you see open unbelief (*kufran bawāḥan*), concerning which you have from God a proof."¹

The upshot of that is that the attempt to overturn the system of rule by raising weapons against it is only permissible if there issued from the ruler what is regarded as explicit, clear unbelief; because the Prophet ﷺ restricted it with "open" (*bawāḥ*) — meaning that what issued from him must be so explicit and clear in its unbelief that every Muslim would understand and regard it as unbelief. And they must have clear proof and argument concerning both matters: its being open unbelief in itself, and its issuing from that ruler. So if there were not valid with them a proof except what they received from the mouths of people, or they extracted from weak analogies and false conjectures, or the factor behind it were misguided propaganda against the ruler — then in all these forms, they are never permitted armed rebellion.

The Sage of the Umma, Shaykh Mawlānā Ashraf ʿAlī al-Thānawī (may God have mercy on him), made a fine point here, which in summary is that what is meant by seeing in his saying, upon him be blessing and peace: "unless you see open unbelief" is the seeing of the eye, not opinion and the seeing of the heart — by virtue of its having been made transitive here to one object; for if it meant the seeing of the heart, it would have made it transitive to two objects [Tr.: the Arabic text has "of the eye" here, evidently a slip]. The import of the seeing of the eye: that he witness with his eye and observe the issuing of open unbelief from the ruler. As for if it was not like that — if he merely had a suspicion or a groundless conjecture — then rebellion is not permissible for him. The upshot is that it is not permissible

> ¹ *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (9/47), Book of Trials, hadith 7056.

[p. 310] to rebel except by the establishment of both these two matters: its being unbelief in itself, and its issuing from the ruler.

In addition to that, there are two other conditions, which [he] did not mention because they are so obvious, namely: that they have the ability that enables them to remove him and turn him away from the government by weapons and force; and that it not be feared that rebellion would lead to the entanglement of the Umma in an evil more dangerous and a harm greater than the harm of this ruler's remaining. As if it were feared that the Muslims would not agree after his removal on one person, so wars would continue among those seeking the government — which would bring the oppressed people no gain or benefit, despite its struggle and its fighting, except the shedding of blood and the taking of souls. Or it were feared that the unbelieving enemy would use this situation in its interest, so it launched the raid on the state and took control of it — with the dire and miserable result that the state be transformed — because of the unbelievers' taking control of it — from Dār al-Islām to Dār al-Kufr. So their example would be nothing but like one who built a house and demolished a city, and their situation would be worse than that of Ḥunayn of the proverb, for they would come back from all their efforts and sacrifices without even his pair of slippers [i.e. utterly empty-handed].

There are two other matters to which it is fitting that we give our attention. As for the first: it is that there is a difference between the attempt to remove the imam and turn him away by the force of weapons, and between what is defence of the self or property. As for the first, it is not permissible except in the form of open unbelief, according to its details that have passed; while a person retains the right of defending himself and his property if the ruler wronged him concerning them; and even if he were compelled for its protection and defending himself and his property to raise weapons against the ruler, it is also permitted in the Sharia — and that is because of what the Prophet ﷺ said: "Whoever is killed defending his property is a martyr."¹

In a report of al-Tirmidhī, Abū Dāwūd and al-Nasāʾī: "Whoever's property is sought without right, and he fights and is killed, he is a martyr."²

Second: war which a person is compelled to for defending himself and his property usually does not take the character of strife among the Muslims; because in the general run of cases it is individual, personal, without

> ¹ *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (3/136), Book of Grievances, hadith 2480.
> ² *Jāmiʿ al-Uṣūl* of Ibn al-Athīr (2/742).

[p. 311] causing prolonged bloodshed at the level of the state. Since it does not count as *fitna*, it is permitted in the Sharia. By contrast, the acts of violence taken against the imam and for the purpose of toppling his government usually do not produce anything except the shedding of blood and their spilling on a wide scale — what is a great strife; so the Sharia did not permit it except in special exceptional cases.

As for what some jurists mentioned — that a group of the Muslims, if they rose against the imam over a grievance, and showed what entitles them to fight, as if [the imam] wronged them or wronged others than them with an injustice in which there is no doubt, then it is obligatory on the others to help them until he treats them fairly and returns from his tyranny¹ — its meaning is defence against injustice, and not rebellion to overturn the system of the government.²

There is another form which the Sage of the Umma, Shaykh al-Thānawī (may God have mercy on him), mentioned: namely, if the imam commits a transgression that affects people's religion, compelling them to disobey God. If this is done to one or two people, it is coercion, governed by the rules explained in their proper place. As for if he made it his policy and began to insist on applying the laws that clash with the Islamic Sharia — either preferring them over the law of God, and that is explicit unbelief; or out of slackness and laziness from applying the Sharia of God, in such a way that it is most likely that continual action contrary to the Sharia will breed contempt for it in people's hearts — then the like of this slackness and laziness, even if it is not an explicit unbelief by which its committer is declared an unbeliever, is nevertheless in the ruling of unbelief; so rebellion is also permitted in such a case. But it is necessary here to draw attention to two important matters:

**The first:** it is possible in this last form specifically that disagreement occur over whether his continual action against the Sharia counts as open unbelief or not. So some may affirm it and see rebellion against the imam as permissible, while some may negate it and then forbid rebellion against him. The difference of opinions like this is an ijtihad-based disagreement; it is not permissible for one to blame the other. It is in this light that the disagreement of the Companions, the Followers and those after them over

> ¹ *Al-Durr al-Mukhtār* and the gloss of Ibn ʿĀbidīn (*Radd al-Muḥtār*) (4/265).
> ² *Imdād al-Fatāwā* 5/121.

[p. 312] rebellion against the imams of their time is to be understood — like the rebellion of our master Ḥusayn, may God be pleased with him, or the people of al-Ḥarra against Yazīd and others of the commanders of the Banū Umayya; and likewise the support of Imam Abū Ḥanīfa (may God have mercy on him) for the rebellion of Zayd ibn ʿAlī and Ibrāhīm al-Nafs al-Zakiyya, in which others of the imams and mujtahids disagreed with him.

**The second:** the jurists have agreed altogether that rebellion, in all forms of its permissibility, requires two things: power and strength (*al-qudra wa-l-manʿa*), and that it not cause a harm greater than the harm of this imam's remaining. Here too opinions may differ. This is just as Imam Abū Ḥanīfa (may God have mercy on him), even if he saw rebellion as permissible in itself, nevertheless did not take part in it, giving as reason: "If one man rose for it, he would be killed, and the people's affair would not be set right. But if he found righteous helpers against it, and a man to lead them who is trusted in the religion of God… And this is an obligation which is not like the obligations which a man undertakes alone; and this, whenever the man undertook it alone, he would shed his blood and expose himself to being killed; so I fear that he would help in killing himself."¹

At any rate, Imam Abū Ḥanīfa and al-Ḥasan ibn Ṣāliḥ ibn Ḥayy (may God have mercy on them both) used to permit armed rebellion against the transgressing imams who administer the helm of government with what entails contempt of the Sharia; but the jurists after them — including the followers of Ḥanafī jurisprudence also — agreed, in the light of their seasoned experiences in this field, that acts of armed violence like this usually do not lead, for the Umma, to a better outcome; so it is better that one be careful of it and avoid it — as al-Ḥāfiẓ Ibn Ḥajar (may God have mercy on him) said in the biography of al-Ḥasan ibn Ṣāliḥ (may God have mercy on him):

"[Al-Ḥasan ibn Ṣāliḥ] used to see the sword — meaning rebellion against the imams of tyranny; and this is an old school of the predecessors; but the matter settled on abandoning that, because they saw that it led to what is more severe than it. For in the event of al-Ḥarra and the event of Ibn al-Ashʿath and others than them there is an admonition for whoever reflects… And al-Ḥasan, with that, did not rebel against anyone."²

Al-Ḥāfiẓ (may God have mercy on him) himself said, in the explanation of one of the hadiths narrated from Ibn ʿAbbās,

> ¹ *Al-Jawāhir al-Muḍiyya fī Ṭabaqāt al-Ḥanafiyya* (1/50), biography of Ibrāhīm ibn Maymūn al-Ṣāʾigh.
> ² *Tahdhīb al-Tahdhīb* (2/288), biography of al-Ḥasan ibn Ṣāliḥ.

[p. 313] may God be pleased with him: "Ibn Baṭṭāl said: In the hadith is an argument for abandoning rebellion against the sultan even if he is tyrannical. The jurists have agreed on the obligation of obeying the dominating (*mutaghallib*) sultan and on jihad with him, and that obeying him is better than rebelling against him, because of what is in that of sparing blood and calming the masses; and their argument is this report and others that support it. They made no exception except where open unbelief issued from the sultan; then obeying him in that is not permissible; rather fighting him is obligatory for whoever is able to."¹

But Imam Abū Bakr al-Jaṣṣāṣ (may God have mercy on him), building on the action of Imam Abū Ḥanīfa (may God have mercy on him), held, in the commentary on the noble verse **"My covenant does not include the wrongdoers"** [al-Baqara, 2:124], that it is permissible to rebel against the imams of tyranny even if this involves bloodshed and strife — on condition that there be ability for it, and that it not lead to a more dangerous harm and a greater corruption. Indeed he held that the government of transgressors has no legitimacy and their rulings are not to be implemented.

But what appears to me is that the later jurists chose the same position which passed in the aforementioned text of *Fatḥ al-Bārī*. ʿAllāma al-Sarakhsī (may God have mercy on him) said: "And by this wording argues whoever claims that the ruler is removed by tyranny; and this is not our school, and we have explained that in what we dictated of the commentary on *al-Ziyādāt* 'in the chapter on arbitration'."²

### Political movements

There is another issue worth mentioning and studying in this discussion of ours, namely: when rebellion against the imam is not permissible, is there a safe way that Islam permits to bring down a government and replace it with another, to meet the people's demands and recover their rights? How does Islam look at the political movements which are adopted in contemporary political circles to achieve this purpose?

It is a fact that Western ideas and theories have become — for centuries — the first bricks upon which we Muslims found the structure of our intellectual life and the edifice

> ¹ *Fatḥ al-Bārī* of Ibn Ḥajar (13/7).
> ² *Sharḥ al-Siyar al-Kabīr* (1/869), chapter on hiring in the land of war and the extra share [*nafl*], paragraph no. 1567.

[p. 314] of our civilizational existence in all branches of life in general, and politics among them in particular. Hence many political practices imported from the West have come to be regarded as virtually indispensable to political life, regardless of whether they are sound or corrupt. Among these practices: the methods of popular protest prevailing widely, like strikes and demonstrations accompanied by sabotage and destruction, and other than that of the ways people tread to pressure the government and carry it to yield and respond to their demands.

Concerning the ruling on these political practices from a Sharia perspective, we say: some of them are definitively forbidden, not permissible in the Sharia at all and absolutely — like a hunger strike to the point of starving oneself to death; and likewise any other method that leads to violating the sanctity of someone's blood or property or honour, or harming public governmental property, or damaging the funds of the state. Aggression against the properties of the state and its funds is among the gravest of sins, one for which it is extremely hard to make amends or escape punishment; by virtue of its not being a special personal property of the rulers only — rather in it is a right for all the citizens of the state and its inhabitants. So toying with it and inflicting any harm on it means injuring the right of the whole people. Since it relates to the rights of servants, release from it is not possible except by being absolved by all the holders of rights — and how is a person to be absolved, or to seek release, from all the inhabitants of the state?! So his shoulder then remains weighed down with the burden of this great, enormous disobedience.

As for the ruling on the general strike, we say: if someone appeals to the people and asks them to express their anger and resentment at a government measure by closing their businesses for a time, and then leaves them free to respond to this request or not — there is no harm in the Sharia in the mere request or in responding to it, provided the people are not coerced or compelled. For a strike like this is permissible in itself in the Sharia as a permissible measure for attaining the permissible goal — on condition that there be excepted in it what is indispensable for human beings, like medical care and others, and especially in emergencies.

But it has become an inseparable part of the strike that whoever appeals for it and calls to it does not content himself

[p. 315] with appealing and calling; rather the situation has deteriorated to the point that they compel the people to contribute to their strike and force them to suspend their works and close their commercial shops, and rain stones on whoever dared to take his car out into the street, and block the streets and public roads, and use violence against whoever does not contribute to the strike — or at least vent their fury on them. It is not hidden that all these works are definitively forbidden in the Sharia; besides that they cause several other evils as well: for the poor worker who earns his daily food is deprived, he and his family, of their provision; and the sick one who suffers from the severity of his illness — loss of treatment increases him in suffering and hardship; and some of them meet their death because of their inability to obtain treatment.

What one never ceases to be astonished and amazed at in this regard is: the double standard of democracy and its treatment with two faces: for it claims to support freedom of expression and opinion on one side, while denying those who do not join the strike any right to these freedoms, and forcing them to take part whether they like it or not. So this is among what clashes with the principles of Islam and contradicts moral values and principles, and specifically the principle of respecting freedom of opinion and thought.

Rarely is a strike free of these evils and harms in this age of ours; and that a strike of a safe, peaceful kind should occur in this atmosphere of ours, whose organizers act with decency and honour, leaving to the people the way to respond to their calls or not — that is extremely rare.

The permissible act, if it is taken as a means to forbidden things and a way to committing what is not permissible in the Sharia, then that permissible thing should be forbidden in keeping with the principle of blocking the means (*sadd al-dharāʾiʿ*). From this, the measure of the strike which produces destruction and sabotage, and the disturbance of security and peace, and obstructing people from continuing their works, does not fall within the measures permitted in the Sharia.

Since it is established that politics is not intended in itself — rather the original intent and the ultimate goal is attaining the pleasure of God, glorified and exalted — therefore let us take the ways and methods which our Lord, majestic and exalted, is pleased with, of what does not contradict the Sharia; otherwise there is no meaning to attempting

[p. 316] to implement Islam by way of contradicting its rulings one after the other.

Marches and demonstrations — their ruling likewise is that they are permissible in themselves if they do not cause severe harm to the people; but their nature, like the strike, rarely is free also of acts of violence, sabotage and causing problems for the people; and it is apparent that the saying of their permissibility from this aspect is not possible.

After this detail, the question which we put forward at the beginning of the discussion repeats itself here: is there a way other than them for pressuring the government and obtaining rights from it?

So in answer to it we say: the truth is that the Sharia has guided us to a way and taught us a principle — if we took it with regard, the tyrannical governments would surrender to it and the despotic authorities would submit to it — namely what the Prophet ﷺ expressed by his saying: "No obedience to a creature in disobedience to the Creator."

If the people once embraced it, kept it always before their eyes, and refused as one to carry out any government command or ruling that contradicts the Sharia — then by God tell me: what trick would the government have left, what arrow in its quiver?! Suppose that all the judges working in the courts announced their refusal to work as long as it is not permitted for them to rule by the rulings of the Sharia; and the lawyers, all of them, if they determined their resolve on not appearing before the courts in the capacity of advocacy as long as its laws are not dyed with a religious dye; and the employees of the banks, if they all refrained from providing their services in them, and the beneficiaries of them — the depositors — abstained, to the last of them, from depositing funds in them; and all the merchants from using their financing facilities as long as their system is not purified of usury — if all of these undertook this strike built on the basis of the aforementioned Sharia rule [no obedience to a creature in disobedience to the Creator], they would find it more useful for their purpose and more damaging to the governmental bodies — enough to bring them at once to yield to their demands and needs.

So this is the Sharia method for showing protest against the government; except that — since it needs something of sacrifice — people find it unpalatable and the public do not find it easy. For it is very easy for a person to shout "Long live Islam" and raise slogans on its behalf while

[p. 317] freely engaging in usurious transactions; and likewise it is easy — how easy it is! — that the judge and the lawyer be occupied with undertaking their professions according to man-made laws even if clashing with the rulings of the Sharia, and with that they contribute to the demonstrations in the right of Islam and shout "Long live Islam". As for using these professions and functions of theirs which they practise — of the judiciary, advocacy, banking and others — as a weapon to attain the goal of implementing Islam, it needs something of resolve and sacrifice; and how much we need them and how much they are lacking among us!

We grant that this approach will only work if the majority of the people is prepared for it, or at least a large number of them for whom the government reckons and gives weight, such that it suffices for pressuring it. It is known that preparing this amount is not completed except after directing the people and making it aware This requires organized planning by the political parties and groups — provided they first open their minds to this idea and wholeheartedly accept this principle — and then that they prepare a programme and planning a project aiming at the awareness of the people and its education in this field, beginning from the educational institutions and ending with the public mass meetings. So if this campaign undertook acquainting people with the harms of the jobs in which they are compelled to cooperate in forbidden matters, and their grasping the extent of the value of the lawful and its gravity — so that it made the majority of the people averse to them and dislike them, and then prepared to abandon them even at the cost of material sacrifice and of living a less comfortable and luxurious life than it was before — then this campaign would obtain a dazzling success and an overwhelming victory, and would pave the way for implementing the Sharia more than any strike or demonstration.

This is just as the army has often suspended or abrogated the constitution in Pakistan and other states, and succeeded because each time some judges of the higher courts lent its action legal legitimacy. But some of them refused to endorse any unconstitutional measure; and though they lost their posts and material benefits, they won the trust, esteem and respect of the people and praise for their role — so much so that people felt the other judges should have followed their example, and were certain that it would not have been within the power of

[p. 318] anyone to cancel the constitution if they all refused to document it legitimately. This actually happened when a large number of the judges of the Supreme Court in Pakistan agreed on opposing what General Pervez Musharraf undertook of cancelling the constitution; so the world saw, and witnessed with its own eyes, that success in the end was not except for those judges, not for the tyrannical despot.

As we presented in what has passed, the basic constitution of the Islamic state and its primary sources of legislation are nothing but the Qur'an and the Prophetic Sunna — and the constitution of Pakistan has provided for that, praise be to God. So if the motives for preserving them were aroused in the people, and the sentiments of protecting them were stirred in it as they were against Pervez Musharraf, this principle [no obedience to a creature in disobedience to the Creator] would play its role greatly, as a splendid and highly effective method of safe, peaceful protest.

### Political movements and the aspect of self-reform

This point too is of gravity and standing in the subject: that Islam does not aspire, from political efforts and movements, to obtaining authority or possessing power over lands and servants; rather the ultimate goal of them in Islam is: reforming the system of government, seeking the pleasure of God, glorified and exalted, by making it in agreement with His Sharia, and administering it according to what He loves and is pleased with. Since it is not necessary for achieving this goal that the holders of these efforts and movements themselves reach the seats of authority, there is no need, from the Sharia point of view, for them to take power themselves if the goal can be achieved by reforming those already in power, or through others — groups or individuals.

The first thing stipulated for this is that these workers in the field of Islamic politics belonging to its groups be sincere of intention, sound of disposition, free of the taints of love of status and position and seeking command and authority. But what actually happens is far from this; for when a person plunges into political battles and begins to engage in their activities, he is quickly — by the very nature of the atmosphere in which he works — infected with the vices of the ego: running after fame, and eagerness for the masses' turning towards him, and love of status

[p. 319] and position — which distracts him from his original goal, and makes him seek from every step of his the satisfaction of these psychological instincts, and aim by it that people return the credit to him alone for the deeds and achievements that he realized or will realize. As a result of all this, political leaders began to let themselves be carried along behind the desires of the common people and to be pushed towards realizing their demands and their wishes, and to ride the backs of their whims — instead of leading them with a sound leadership on straight lines — which made them lose sight of the real interests of the state and the Umma in their steps and plans. This is among the woes which secular democracy has dragged upon humanity. As for Islam and its politics, they firmly oppose these vices. If the working intention is not made sincere, and the idea urging the efforts and attempts is not purified, then naturally the movement and the group in its entirety go in the direction of error — which deprives them of the help of God and His aid, and then excludes them from success. So their condition does not resemble except the return of an echo in the air or a cry in the valleys. So proceeding from this, the leaders of the movements and the political groups must have passed through the stages of personal reform and completed the purification of the soul and spiritual education, and been able to rid themselves of being concerned with status, position, reputation and fame.

Let us turn the reins of our attention a little to the Meccan era, which comprised thirteen years which the Prophet ﷺ and his Companions spent holding fast to what God commanded them of patience and forgiveness [*And be patient, and your patience is not but through God*] — no jihad, no politics, no government. Rather they were charged with patience however much the unbelievers struck them with harm and brought down afflictions upon them. The ruling of patience was not from the motive of weakness nor the drive of cowardice; for they were capable of at least some struggle, resistance and self-defence — and among them were those for whom the world bore witness of heroism and bravery. If 313 barely armed men, with no more than two horses and eight swords between them, were able to resist a thousand fully armed heroes and triumph over them at Badr — was it not within their capacity in Mecca to repay them with the least of what was possible for them: a slap for a slap and a blow for a blow? Yes!¹ So know that the ruling of patience in the Meccan era aimed, behind it, at the making of men and straightening their moral conduct, and founding personal conduct on lofty values and human principles,

> ¹ A point which His Eminence Shaykh, the Sage of the Umma, Mawlānā Ashraf ʿAlī al-Thānawī (may God have mercy on him) gave.

[p. 320] and planting the idea of the otherworldly life in their souls and developing it and watering it, and purifying their hearts from the taints of love of wealth and status, and cleansing them of the other moral vices. Islam made this the starting point in forming the caravan of the Islamic call, so that it would be in complete readiness and full piety and spiritual education for plunging into the depths of the thorny battles of politics — which, as soon as a person enters them, begin to play with him and flip him among whims and vices: some base vice receives him and another snatches him, and another strife lies in wait for him and hunts him. So when He completed their spiritual education, and straightened their conduct, and reformed their character, it was no sooner than they plunged into the battle of politics that they were able to establish the first Islamic government, splendid to the utmost, the like of which the world has not witnessed throughout its history at all, nor will it ever see its equal again. That only became possible by virtue of their spiritual education which took thirteen years, so humanity reaped from its fruits what it had never reaped before.

So it is obligatory on whoever undertakes the burden of leading the Umma in the field of politics that they have passed the stage of spiritual education such that their hearts have become filled with virtuous morals and their conduct adorned with noble traits — pure with all purity from the taints of eagerness for status and position, and clear with all clarity from the turbidities of reputation and seeking acceptance among the masses. If those were not easy for them to perfection, then let vices and blameworthy psychological sentiments at least be overcome in them, entirely subject to the purpose of attaining the pleasure of God, such that it is always the focus of their attention in all their plans and their political steps. This is the original aim and the ultimate goal of politics in Islam, and it is impossible to attain except after ridding oneself of those psychological vices. How excellent is the Persian poet, saying:

> *Yā makun bā pīl-bānān dūstī — yā binā kun khāna bar andāz-e pīl*

Either refrain from the companionship of elephant-keepers, or build your house on the pattern of their dwellings [i.e. large enough for an elephant].

The last of our prayer is that praise belongs to God, Lord of the worlds.

---

## Appendix A: The Components of an Islamic Constitution

### Introduction

[p. 321] It has become [the case] today that most states of the world have their own special constitution. What is most fitting, in view of what we mentioned earlier of the principles of Islamic politics and its rulings, is that they be codified, so that they are included in the constitution of the state, so that they become clear and evident to the common people and the elite. Indeed many of them deserve inclusion in the constitution. More than forty years ago I wrote two studies: in one of them I clarified the components of the Islamic constitution in the Qur'an, and in the other I clarified the requirements of the Islamic constitution in Pakistan in view of its circumstances and situations. Because of their connection with the subject of our book, we add them to it as two appendices, A and B.

### A look at the constitution

The constitution of the state in reality forms that first brick on which the structure of the system of government in it is built, and on which its existence is founded. It is the pole around which the mill of the state's development and its flourishing, its consolidation and its prosperity revolves; and it is the root on which the people's coexistence in the state with safety, peace, calm and prosperity depends. So if this first basic brick of the state were laid crooked, then you may bid farewell to its order and progress!

It is known that the term constitution refers to the body of rules that clarify the form of the state and its basic goals and the system of rule in it, its authorities and the manner of distributing these authorities, and the clarification of their competences; and the clarification of the rights of citizens and their duties; and other than that of main rulings, like the system of forming the government, and the manner of electing the president and the members of parliament, and their conditions and their qualities and the way of removing them; and the clarification of who takes the authority of legislation and its powers; and the competences of the courts, and the definition of the laws by which it rules in pleadings and disputes; and the manner of appointing employees in the governmental departments, and the distribution of their functions among the units

[p. 322] of the state and its territories; and the clarification of the bases on which the budget of the state and its allocations will be arranged; and the principles of imposing taxes on the people; and how the regional governments are formed, and to what extent their powers will be, and which are the matters in which the reference will be entirely to the central government; and to what extent the structure of government may be altered in a state of emergency, and specifically the state of war with another state; and in such a state, what are the special privileges which the governmental bodies will be able to use.

So these matters are clarified and defined in the constitution of the state, and the state's organs are then run according to it for as long as it endures. After the constitution is implemented and applied to the state once, it is obligatory on the government that it perform all its works and its duties in the light of that constitution and in full subordination to it. So if it did not do that and undertook what contradicts the constitution of measures, or issued what contradicts it of rulings, the people would be able to cancel it by way of appeal in the Supreme Court. But if there were included in the constitution itself erroneous clauses that clash with the interests of the subjects and conflict with them, then the government, whatever it may be, would be compelled to act by it, without there being in the hands of the people the option to reform it or a way to get rid of its evil consequences and its miserable ends — except amendment in the constitution, and that is extraordinarily difficult, if not impossible. The Pakistani nation, which was struck because of the amendment in the 1962 constitution with heavy losses which cannot be compensated nor mended at all, estimates very well the extent of its difficulty and its hardship; and it still suffers crises and endures problems and faces difficulties because of that erroneous constitution, which was implemented once and continued its harming of the state and its interests repeatedly.

So the drafting of the state's constitution, due to begin on 3 March, requires that those undertaking it have an ample share of far-sightedness, breadth of horizon, cleverness and intelligence — alongside deliberation and reflection, arduous effort and serious attempt. The work of laying down the constitution, even if the direct contribution in it is for the members of parliament and the house of representatives, it is never in the people's interest to ignore it or turn a blind eye; rather it is touched by a need for complete alertness and full understanding

[p. 323] in all stages of its accomplishment.

For the people chose the members representing it only so that they would undertake, on its behalf, laying down a constitution that realizes its interests and fulfils all its requirements and meets its needs. So if they did not undertake that, the people's trust in them would be shaken, and they would have the right to question them, and then to carry them to perform their responsibilities by laying down a constitution to its satisfaction.

It is self-evident, needing no proof or evidence, that only a constitution in accordance with the teachings of the Sharia can succeed in Pakistan. This is what the head of state declared in a legal order for the constitution [Tr.: presumably the Legal Framework Order of 1970]; and all the political parties acknowledged the pressing need for it during the campaign for the last parliamentary elections, and promised the nation to contribute their share in founding it on pure Islamic foundations and principles. No wonder in this; for the state was created out of Islam, so there is no room in it nor scope for laying down or implementing a non-Islamic constitution.

What concerns us in this field is the role of the party that has won the majority of votes in parliament, and then has the main authority in the process of laying down the constitution and codifying it and arranging it: to what extent will it fulfil its covenant and its promises and undertake its responsibility and its duty?

In this discussion we shall study, in summary, the principles of the Islamic constitution — of its meaning and its import, its components and its privileges. We consider it a duty to put this before the people, so that they have knowledge, understanding and a correct conception of the Islamic constitution, and thus a touchstone by which to test the new constitution: whether it conforms to those qualities or not? Then: whether it deserves acceptance as a constitution for the Islamic state or not?

When we say that Islam is comprehensive and all-embracing for all the branches of life and its fields, we do not mean that every partial issue and secondary case of these fields has had its ruling expressly stated in the Qur'an and the Prophetic Sunna or the texts of Islamic jurisprudence. Rather the meaning: that Islam has legislated in every branch of the branches of life and its fields basic principles, and laid down main rulings sufficient

[p. 324] for extracting all their particulars from them. The constitution is like that: Islam has granted us concerning it essential directives and basic starting-points, fixed, not capable of change — even if all humanity agreed on issuing a decision contrary to them or clashing with them, they would have no right to do so. As for what is besides these principles, it has entrusted their matter to the people of insight among the Muslims, to choose by mutual consultation what they see as most useful for their interests and most fitting to their circumstances — on condition that it not contradict those basic principles in anything.

Many of the constitutional issues that are discussed when laying down the constitution for the Islamic state are of this kind: Islam places no obstacle to choosing them by consultation, provided the choice conforms to these basic principles — like the multiplicity of the legislative body and its unity, the official language of the state, and the administrative units into which the state will be divided, and the specification of the manner of its connection with the central federal government so as to preserve the cohesion of the state, and the number of ministers and members of the house of representatives, and the ideal way for the accounting and financial auditing of the state. These are matters for which Islam has not defined a special form; rather it has entrusted them to the collective consultation of the Umma; so whatever it chose in this regard would be permissible and capable of actual implementation from the Sharia point of view.

As for the main issues and basic principles concerning which Islam has taken a clear, decisive position, which will remain definitively fixed over the passage of ages and the succession of eras, without there being in it latitude for change or room for amendment — even if the whole people agreed upon it, let alone the members of parliament and the legislative body — these are what we regard as the components of the Islamic constitution, such that no constitution can be truly Islamic without them. Here below we clarify them and their basis from the Noble Qur'an.

### The components of the Islamic constitution

**(1)** Supreme sovereignty and absolute dominion in this universe belong to God the Exalted.

Its basis is His words, the Exalted: **"Judgement belongs to God alone"** [Yūsuf, 12:40; and al-Anʿām, 6:57].

It is the first clause with which the Islamic constitution opens, and it is what distinguishes it from the systems of

[p. 325] secular democracy which acknowledge for the people alone dominion and authority, and which permit for it legislation contrary to the rulings of God if the whole people or its majority agreed on it. As for Islam, it does not acknowledge supreme sovereignty except for God; and man rules over his fellow human beings under the shade of his vicegerency and his deputyship. God the Exalted said: **"Indeed, I will make upon the earth a vicegerent"** [al-Baqara, 2:30].

**(2)** No legislation is enacted, and no law is agreed upon, and no administrative measure is issued, contrary to the Book of God and the Prophetic Sunna; and any already taken shall not be retained.

**"And whoever does not judge by what God has revealed — then it is those who are the disbelievers"** [al-Māʾida, 5:44].

**(3)** The ultimate goal and the original intent of the government: that it establish justice and fairness, and uproot strife (*fitna*) and corruption from their roots, and provide Muslims with opportunities to perform their acts of worship, and undertake commanding right and forbidding wrong. God the Exalted said: **"[They are] those who, if We establish them in the land, establish prayer and give zakat and enjoin what is right and forbid what is wrong. To God belongs the outcome of [all] matters"** [al-Ḥajj, 22:41].

And: **"And when you judge between people, judge with justice"** [al-Nisāʾ, 4:58].

And: **"And if it were not for God checking [some] people by means of others, the earth would have been corrupted"** [al-Baqara, 2:251].

**(4)** All the offices of the government and its properties are a trust in the hands of the rulers, and it is the duty of government bodies to render them to those entitled to them. God the Exalted said: **"Indeed, God commands you to render trusts to whom they are due"** [al-Nisāʾ, 4:58].

**(5)** The government shall not be individually despotic; it shall only be consultative (*shūrawiyya*), and all its important affairs shall be administered in consultation with the people of the state and its inhabitants. God the Exalted said: **"And whose affair is [determined by] consultation among themselves"** [al-Shūrā, 42:38].

**(6)** Unity shall be on the basis of Islamic brotherhood, and it is upon the state to end partisanship (*al-ʿaṣabiyya*) among the Muslims on the basis of geography, tribe, language, race and the like, which contradicts the principles of the Sharia, and to strive earnestly to build and consolidate the unity of the Umma, and to establish social justice among all its Muslim citizens. God, glorified and exalted, said: **"The believers

[p. 326] are but brothers"** [al-Ḥujurāt, 49:10].

And: **"And made you peoples and tribes that you may know one another. Indeed, the most noble of you in the sight of God is the most righteous of you"** [al-Ḥujurāt, 49:13].

**(7)** It is stipulated for the head of state that he be a Muslim, truthful, trustworthy, bearing scholarly and practical abilities and qualifications. God the Exalted said: **"My covenant does not include the wrongdoers"** [al-Baqara, 2:124].

And: **"Indeed, God has chosen him over you and has increased him abundantly in knowledge and stature"** [al-Baqara, 2:247].

**(8)** The state guarantees to all its citizens the basic rights of the human being: that they be safe from aggression against them in life, honour or property; and it grants them the freedom of assembly for good purposes, and the freedom of constructive criticism. God, glorified and exalted, said: **"And do not kill the soul which God has forbidden, except by right"** [al-Isrāʾ, 17:33].

And: **"And do not consume one another's wealth unjustly"** [al-Baqara, 2:188].
And: **"O you who have believed, let not a people ridicule [another] people"** [al-Ḥujurāt, 49:11].
And: **"There is no compulsion in religion"** [al-Baqara, 2:256].
And: **"And let there be [arising] from you a nation inviting to [all that is] good"** [Āl ʿImrān, 3:104].
And: **"You are the best nation produced for mankind: you enjoin what is right and forbid what is wrong"** [Āl ʿImrān, 3:110].

**(9)** No one shall be sentenced to a punishment as long as his crime has not been established. God, glorified and exalted, said: **"O you who have believed, if there comes to you a disobedient one with information, investigate, lest you harm a people out of ignorance and become, over what you have done, regretful"** [al-Ḥujurāt, 49:6].

**(10)** The judiciary shall be free, in fulfilling the requirements of justice and equity, from any pressure and interference — except the law of the pure Sharia. God, glorified and exalted, said: **"O you who have believed, be persistently standing firm in justice, witnesses for God, even if it be against yourselves or parents and relatives"** [al-Nisāʾ, 4:135].

And: **"So judge between them by what God has revealed and do not follow their inclinations"** [al-Māʾida, 5:48].

[p. 327] **(11)** The state shall guarantee the provision of vital needs for the needy, the disabled and the bankrupt among the citizens, and supply their necessities; and it shall undertake the just distribution of collective wealth, which enables the whole people to benefit from it, and it shall not remain circulating among the rich among them. God the Exalted said: **"And what God restored to His Messenger from the people of the towns — it is for God and for the Messenger and for [his] near relatives and orphans and the needy and the stranded traveller — so that it will not be a perpetual distribution among the rich from among you"** [al-Ḥashr, 59:7].

And: **"And from their properties was [given] the right of the [needy] petitioner and the deprived"** [al-Dhāriyāt, 51:19].

**(12)** The government shall not confiscate the individual personal properties which their owners have owned by ways permitted in the Sharia. God the Exalted said: **"And do not consume one another's wealth unjustly"** [al-Baqara, 2:188].

**(13)** No tax shall be imposed on the citizens beyond their capacity, and they shall not be charged with any administrative matter that is not within their ability. God the Exalted said: **"And relieves them of their burden and the shackles which were upon them"** [al-Aʿrāf, 7:157].

And: **"God does not charge a soul except [with that within] its capacity"** [al-Baqara, 2:286].

**(14)** The government shall provide to all Muslim citizens the service of education and upbringing which enables them to learn the Noble Qur'an and to know the basic rulings of Islam, and to live a good life on earth in observance of the Islamic teachings and in their light; and likewise it shall make available to them the opportunity for excellence and mastery in the economic sciences also, so that they make the state self-sufficient by itself, not in need in that of anyone. God, glorified and exalted, said: **"And teach them the Book and wisdom"** [al-Baqara, 2:129].

**(15)** Non-Muslim citizens (except the apostates among them) shall in principle enjoy all the human rights which the Muslims among them obtain. God, glorified and exalted, said: **"But if he was from a people with whom you have a treaty — then a compensation payment presented to his family"** [al-Nisāʾ, 4:92].

And: **"O you who have believed, fulfil [all] contracts"** [al-Māʾida, 5:1].

**(16)** The state shall undertake the duty of jihad according to the Islamic rulings. God the Exalted said: **"And strive for God with the striving due to Him"** [al-Ḥajj, 22:78].

[p. 328] **(17)** Relations of peace, tolerance and kindness with non-Islamic states shall be strengthened — except those hostile to Islam and the Muslims. God the Exalted said: **"God does not forbid you from those who do not fight you because of religion and do not expel you from your homes — from being righteous toward them and acting justly toward them. Indeed, God loves those who act justly"** [al-Mumtaḥana, 60:8].

**(18)** The state shall be committed to fulfilling the covenants and permissible treaties which it contracted and concluded with other states, or to repudiate and terminate them openly and publicly. God the Exalted said: **"Except for those with whom you made a treaty among the polytheists and then they have not been deficient toward you in anything or supported anyone against you; so complete for them their treaty until their term [has ended]"** [al-Tawba, 9:4].

And: **"If you [have reason to] fear from a people betrayal, throw [their treaty] back to them, [putting you] on equal terms"** [al-Anfāl, 8:58].

**(19)** A non-Muslim shall not be put in charge of an executive office with access to the confidential matters of the Muslims and the secrets of government. God the Exalted said: **"O you who have believed, do not take as intimates those other than yourselves"** [Āl ʿImrān, 3:118].

**(20)** No change at all shall be permitted in the clauses of the constitution which have been taken from the Qur'an and the Prophetic Sunna directly. God the Exalted said: **"And the word of your Lord has been fulfilled in truth and in justice. None can alter His words"** [al-Anʿām, 6:115].

These are the basic points without which no constitution can be called Islamic. We have contented ourselves, in their bases, with what came concerning them of the Qur'anic verses, and did not undertake to explain the Prophetic hadiths which clarify them and detail them more. Indeed, the first written constitutional document in the world was the one the Prophet ﷺ drew up on establishing the Islamic government in Medina the Illuminated, in which he set out the civil rights of the citizens of Medina, Muslims and non-Muslims among them; and it contains 47 clauses, and ʿAllāma Ibn Hishām (may God have mercy on him) brought it in his *Sīra*.

---

## Appendix B: The Islamic Clauses in the Constitution

[p. 329] As we presented in what has passed, there are many political issues on which Islam has decided a definitive, decisive ruling, from which it is not permitted for the Umma to deviate, nor to oppose it or contradict it. To ensure compliance with them and their application at the level of the state, the constitution-making for the state must pass through the four steps which we shall clarify in this discussion of ours.

### The first step

That it make the Objectives Resolution which the legislative assembly adopted in the year 1951 [sic; 1949] a necessary part, not separable from the constitution in any case; because it is what defines the course of the basic policy of the state and specifies the direction of its main plan — in it is acknowledged that the true rule belongs to God, glorified and exalted, and that the elected representatives of the people will undertake using their authority within the framework of those limits and the commitment to those restrictions which the rulings of the Qur'an and the Sunna impose upon them. Let it be provided in the constitution itself that the law or the governmental measure which contradicts the points adopted in that resolution will be subject to review and appeal in the Supreme Court of the state.

### The second step

The second stage is of gravity and standing in the Islamization of the constitution, namely: specifying the sound principles and foundations for legislation and law-making in the state; and let the constitution guarantee that no law will be enacted contrary to any ruling of the Qur'an and the Sunna, that no administrative measure contrary to the Qur'an and the Sunna will be taken, and that existing laws of the state contrary to them will be amended until they agree with the rulings of the Sharia.

Let the following matters and points be noted in that with precision and mastery:

**(a)** In view of the express statement which the constitution includes of amending and changing

[p. 330] the laws which contradict the Sharia, let there be defined in the constitution itself an appropriate period sufficient for the process of Islamizing the laws in the new constitution — and it should not exceed five years in any case; and I am confident that it suffices for codifying the Islamic laws if one worked in it with seriousness and sincerity.

**(b)** And in this clause of the constitution, let both the Qur'an and the Sunna be stated clearly and explicitly; it is not enough to use the word "Islam" or "the Qur'an" alone — and that is in acceptance of and faith in the Prophetic Sunna as a source of legislation and a basis of law.

**(c)** And so that the benefit of this clause is completed, it is necessary to state in the constitution that the house of representatives, if it adopted a law contrary to the Qur'an and the Sunna, it may be challenged and overturned on appeal in the Supreme Court.

**(d)** It is necessary to specify the way which is followed in ruling on a law as being contrary to the rulings of the Qur'an and the Sunna; and for this purpose let the constitution provide that this task is entrusted to the scholars of the Sharia who learned the sciences of the Qur'an and the Sunna and studied them in a regular study. The best way for that is that a special judicial body belonging to the Supreme Court be designated, comprising a judge from its judges and five (or six) proficient scholars.

**(e)** And concerning the designation of the committee which undertakes reviewing the laws and making them in agreement with the Sharia, let the constitution provide with precision and careful thought for the qualities of the members of this committee which will perform the task of codifying the Islamic laws.

In view of these matters, we see that the clause concerned with legislation should be in the constitution as follows:

**(1)** No law shall be legislated contrary to the Qur'an and the Sunna; and if there were in the constitution of the country a law contrary to them, it shall be amended and changed according to what comes in the following paragraph.

**(2)** And to undertake the task mentioned in the first paragraph, the head of state shall form, within six months from the day of implementing the constitution, a committee called "the Islamic Law Committee", in which there shall be representatives of all the Muslim Islamic sects present in the state.

[p. 331] **(3)** Two thirds of the members of this committee shall be of the scholars who are characterized by the following qualities:

(a) That he has occupied the office of fatwa for a period of ten years at least in one of the well-known religious institutes or universities.

(b) Or he has remained the reference of fatwa in a region of the regions of the Muslims for a period of ten years at least.

(c) Or he has worked as a Sharia judge in one of the official Sharia judicial departments.

(d) Or he has undertaken teaching the subjects of the commentary on the Noble Qur'an and the Prophetic hadith or jurisprudence for a period of ten years at least in an institute or university among the religious institutes and universities.

**(4)** And the remaining third of the members of the Islamic Law Committee shall be of legal specialists who:

(a) Have occupied the office of judge in the High Court or the two High Courts in succession for a period of five years at least.

(b) Or have remained a lawyer for the High Court or the two High Courts in succession for a period of fifteen years.

**(5)** And the best arrangement for this appears to be that the head of state, before appointing them, request from all the religious and political parties in the state the names of members of this committee by way of proposal; he shall not omit any name common to all the proposals — unless that person does not meet the conditions mentioned in paragraphs 3 and 4.

**(6)** The Islamic Law Committee shall undertake the fitting codification of the Islamic rulings within five years from the day of its establishment, and its report — interim or final — shall be laid before the national parliament, which shall enact it as law within six months of receiving it.

**(7)** If objections were raised and remarks were brought against the laws which the legislative assembly legislated, that they are contrary to the rulings of the Qur'an and the Sunna, then the decisive word and the final

[p. 332] ruling in it shall be for the judicial body belonging to the Supreme Court, which the head of state designates for this purpose, and at least five of its members shall be from the scholars of the Sharia.

**(8)** And for the judicial body belonging to the Supreme Court mentioned in paragraph 7, there shall only be designated of the scholars those in whom the qualities mentioned in paragraph 3 are present; and all the regulations proposed for the other judges shall apply to them also.

### Explanatory points for this clause

**(1)** With regard to applying this clause to the personal laws of the sects among the Muslim Islamic sects, what counts in it is the interpretation which that particular sect accepts.

**(2)** No weight shall be given to any explanation and clarification of the Qur'an and the Prophetic Sunna contrary to what all the schools of jurisprudence agreed upon; so that explanation shall be regarded as abrogated, cancelled to the extent of the mentioned contradiction.

We see that the condition of restriction by the Qur'an and the Sunna in the work of legislation only bears fruit and produces good if it is mentioned in the constitution according to the details that have passed.

### The third step in Islamizing the constitution

It is: that the constitution provide for stipulating Islam for each of the head of state and the prime minister.

### The fourth step

It is: that the work of laying down the constitution put before its eyes those twenty-two points which 31 great scholars drew up and agreed upon [Tr.: in Karachi in January 1951], among them those belonging to every Muslim Islamic sect. So let the new constitution be founded on those principles and starting-points, and let whatever contradicts them of the clauses of the constitution be regarded as abrogated and void. Here below we present those principles in translation.

[p. 333]

### The basic principles of the Islamic state

The Islamic constitution must provide in it for the following principles:

**(1)** God, glorified and exalted, is the true Ruler of the universe — creatively and legislatively.

**(2)** The law of the state shall be built on the Book and the Sunna, and no law shall be enacted, and no administrative measure ordered, contrary to the Book and the Sunna.

(Explanatory note)

If laws of this kind were actually in force in the state, then it is necessary to state in the constitution that they will be abolished, or brought into conformity with the Sharia gradually within a defined period.

**(3)** The state shall not be built on a geographical, racial or linguistic conception; it shall only be built on the principles and goals that emanate from the constitution of Islamic life.

**(4)** It shall be the duty of the Islamic state to command what is right and establish it, and forbid what is wrong and remove it, and establish the Islamic rites and raise them high, and provide the service of basic religious education for every Muslim Islamic sect according to their schools.

**(5)** It shall also be its duty to strengthen the bonds of unity and brotherhood among all the Muslims of the world, and to block all the ways which call and incite to racial or regional partisanship or other material differences among the Muslims of the state — and that is for preserving Islamic unity and its consolidation.

**(6)** The state shall provide the basic human needs — of food, housing, clothing, medical care and education — for every disabled citizen incapable of earning a livelihood, or who has actually become temporarily incapable of it because of unemployment or illness or other causes, regardless of religion, race and the like.

**(7)** The citizens of the state shall obtain all the rights which the Sharia has granted them — of the protection of life, property and honour, and freedom of religion and belief, and of worship, and of the person, and freedom of opinion,

[p. 334] and of movement, and of assembly, and of earning a livelihood, and equality in social advancement, and the right to benefit from charitable institutions — all of that within the framework of the law.

**(8)** No right of these mentioned civil rights shall be stripped from any citizen except where Islamic law permits it; and no one shall be punished on a criminal charge unless he has been given an opportunity to defend himself and a court has convicted him.

**(9)** The recognized Muslim sects shall have, within the limits of the law, complete freedom in practising their religious affairs; and they shall have the right to give religious education to their followers, and to spread their ideas and their beliefs freely. They shall be judged in their personal affairs and their disputes according to their school of jurisprudence; and it shall be more fitting to provide a judge from among those belonging to that sect.

**(10)** Non-Muslim citizens shall likewise enjoy, within the limits of the law, the freedom of belief, worship, civilization and culture, and religious education; and they shall have the right to request that they be judged in their personal affairs according to their religious law or what has prevailed among them of rites and traditions.

**(11)** It is obligatory to fulfil the Sharia covenants which the state has contracted with the non-Muslim citizens, and they shall have all the civil rights mentioned in clause 7, equally between them and the Muslims.

**(12)** It is stipulated for the head of state that he be a male Muslim, trusted in his trustworthiness and his competence and the correctness of his opinion by the masses or their elected representatives.

**(13)** The head of state is the one who is responsible for the administration of the state, and it is permitted for him to delegate something of his authorities to an individual or a group.

**(14)** The government of the head of state shall not be an individual, despotic government; rather it shall be built on the basis of shūrā, in the sense that he undertakes his works and performs his responsibilities by consultation with the members of the government and the representatives of the people.

**(15)** The head of state shall never have the right of suspending the constitution, wholly or partially, nor of administering

[p. 335] the government without shūrā.

**(16)** The group which undertakes the authority of choosing the president shall have the authority to remove him also.

**(17)** The head of state shall be in civil rights like the generality of the Muslims, and shall be subject to the sovereignty of law.

**(18)** The same law and regulations shall apply to officials and members of the government and to ordinary citizens alike, and the courts shall enforce them on both.

**(19)** The judiciary shall be independent and entirely separate from the executive, so that the courts are not affected, in performing their duties and fulfilling their responsibilities, by any pressure or governmental influence.

**(20)** The spread of, and propaganda for, ideas and theories subversive of the basic principles, values and foundations of the Islamic state shall be categorically prohibited.

**(21)** The various provinces and territories of the state shall be treated as administrative units of the unified state; and their status shall not be as sectarian units on the basis of racial, linguistic or tribal [identity]. They are only territories into which the state has been divided for administrative convenience; and it is permitted that administrative authorities be delegated to them in subordination to the sovereignty of the centre; and it is not their right to secede from the centre at all.

**(22)** No weight shall be given to any explanation and clarification of the constitution contrary to the Book and the Sunna.

---

*[End of the book.]*
